Epilogue
BONUS CHAPTER
“Some stories are stitched from both ruin and rebirth.
A heart shatters, learns to breathe again, and discovers love waiting in places grief once claimed.
A child arrives as a quiet miracle, carrying the last soft echo of a man who ran out of time but not out of longing.
And somehow, in the space between goodbye and beginning, a broken life becomes a gentler one
— proof that even the most painful endings can leave behind a light that never stops living.”
Prologue
The city never sleeps , not for people like me, anyway.
The rain hits the window in a slow, steady rhythm, like a song that refuses to end. I sit by the crib, watching her tiny chest rise and fall beneath the soft pink blanket. My daughter. My miracle. My secret.
Her name is Race Faith Martins, but everyone calls her Ray. Two years old, with her father’s green eyes and my stubbornness. The kind of child who laughs with her whole body, who looks at the world like it’s hers already.
Sometimes I wonder what Isaiah would think if he saw her. Would he see himself in her smile? Would he even care?
I shake the thought away before it can grow roots. He doesn’t know ,and he never will.
New York gave me a chance to breathe again. The city swallowed my past like it was nothing. Here, I’m just Alyssa. The theatre actress. The single mother. The woman trying to piece herself back together.
The woman still blindly in love with her ex husband.
Broadway isn’t glamorous ,not the way Hollywood was. The lights here don’t blind you; they test you. They demand truth, not perfection. And for the first time, I think I’m ready for that.
Jordan Campbell found me again last year the producer who once saw me fall apart and never said a word about it.The man who witnessed the end of my ten years old marriage. Now
he’s everywhere: backstage, in my apartment fixing broken lightbulbs, in the audience pretending not to stare.
He loves Ray like she’s his own. And he loves me ,I can feel it in the quiet moments when neither of us dares to say it out loud.
But something in his eyes tells me he’s hiding something.
Something that feels too familiar. Too dangerous.
Something that can destroy my idea of love. Someone from his past, someone important.
And when the past knocks again , the one I’ve spent two years running from ,  I realize the truth I’ve avoided all along.
You can change your name.
You can cross oceans.
You can rewrite your story.
But secrets always find their way back home.
One
New York City. The city that never sleeps.
Every girl dreams of living here, wrapped in the glow of endless lights, chasing purpose under the hum of car horns and ambition. But for me, this was not a dream. It was a quiet exile dressed as a fresh start.
Once upon a time, I was a girl who dreamed of becoming a Hollywood A-lister. I wanted the applause, the stage lights, the sweet taste of recognition. Instead, I became the wife of one. I was the woman who gave up her dreams to stand in the shadow of someone else’s spotlight. And then, when the curtain fell, I became the punchline. The wife everyone pitied. The woman who wasn’t enough.
Now here I am, standing in a high-rise apartment overlooking the heart of Manhattan, trying to gather what’s left of my life. No career. No husband. Just a quiet settlement account with too many zeros and a six-month pregnant belly I managed to hide beneath oversized sweaters and long coats.
The city below me buzzes with life I no longer feel part of. I lean against the balcony rail, my breath visible in the crisp September air. Fall has already arrived, brushing the streets with gold and melancholy. The wind carries the faint scent of roasted chestnuts and rain, and somewhere below, a street musician is playing a familiar tune on a violin.
My apartment is spacious , too spacious for one person. Four bedrooms, though I asked the landlord to convert one into a nursery. A little room painted in soft creams and muted pinks, even though I promised myself I wouldn’t plan too far ahead. But I couldn’t help it. I needed something to hold on to.
Once upon a time, I imagined Isaiah and me building a nursery together. Picking out colors. Arguing over baby names. Laughing about who the baby might look like. But dreams die
quietly. They fade until all that’s left is the echo of what could have been.
Now, Isaiah is somewhere in Los Angeles, expecting a baby with his mistress. Parading around red carpets with her like royalty. Smiling for cameras, while I hide behind curtains, praying the world forgets I exist.
My heart aches thinking about it. That my child will grow up never knowing their father. That one day, I’ll have to tell them about a man who loved fame more than he ever loved me.
I decided not to find out the baby’s gender. It was easier that way. If I knew, I’d start imagining a future that included the other baby — the twin who didn’t make it. The grief still lingers like a shadow in my chest. Some nights, it feels like it will crush me.
The evening wind picks up, sweeping my hair across my face. I close my eyes, letting the city sounds drown out the noise inside me. When I open them again, I freeze. Across the street, two men stand with cameras. Lenses pointed directly at my balcony.
Click. Flash.
My pulse spikes. I step back instinctively. Another flash. My breath catches.
Oh no.
I turn and rush back inside, sliding the balcony doors shut and yanking the curtains together. My hands tremble as I press them against my chest.
“God,” I whisper. “Not again.”
I glance down at my oversized shirt , thank God it’s baggy enough to hide my belly. Still, my mind races. Did they see anything? Did they capture a glimpse of my stomach?
Why can’t they leave me alone? Haven’t I given them enough already?
That night, I call my mother. The clock reads past midnight, but she answers after the second ring, her voice heavy with sleep and worry.
“Alyssa?”
“Mom.” I sink into the couch, wrapping my arm around my stomach. “They found me again.”
Her sigh is soft but weighted. “Honey, I told you to get more security. You can’t stay there alone like this.”
“I thought it would stop after the divorce,” I murmur. “I thought if I left Los Angeles, they’d forget me.”
“They won’t forget you, sweetheart. Not when your name still sells stories.” Her tone is calm, maternal, the way it always is when I start to unravel. “You need to protect yourself and the baby. Let me send someone to handle it.”
I nod even though she can’t see me. “Okay.”
There’s a pause. “How are you feeling otherwise?”
I hesitate. How am I supposed to say that I feel hollow most days? That the silence in this apartment feels too heavy? That I wake up every morning hoping it was all a bad dream?
“I’m managing,” I whisper instead.
“I know it hurts,” she says softly. “But this pain won’t last forever. You’ll find yourself again.”
“I hope so.”
We talk for a few more minutes before she tells me to rest. I promise her I will, though I don’t sleep that night. I just stare at the ceiling, listening to the city and wondering if peace is something women like me ever really get to have.
The days blur into weeks. I keep to myself, barely stepping outside. The paparazzi don’t stop. Sometimes I hear them outside the building, waiting. Other times, they send drones near the balcony. I learn to live with the paranoia. The blinds stay closed, the lights dimmed. My world shrinks to the walls of my apartment and the soft thump of my baby’s heartbeat on the monitor.
In between doctor’s appointments and long silences, I spend hours on the phone with my sister, Amy. She’s going through her own hell , a messy, public divorce from Yulis, the Hollywood director everyone worships. Their “open marriage” became the tabloid’s favorite meal. Now, the same public that adored her is tearing her apart.
The headlines are brutal. The internet is worse. They call her every name imaginable, because people always need a villain. And Amy, despite her steel spine, is breaking. She can't even control her own narrative despite her being a publicist herself. It shows how powerful Yulis can be.
I listen to her cry some nights, her voice cracking between breaths. I want to be there for her, but I’m barely keeping myself together.
“It’s like they want me to bleed for their entertainment,” she told me once.
I knew exactly what she meant. I’d been bleeding in public for years.
A month passes. The leaves outside turn orange and brittle, falling like little fragments of time. I scroll through Instagram one quiet morning, curled up on the couch with tea gone cold beside me.
And then I see it.
A photo of Isaiah and Anya.
Engaged.
The caption reads: Hollywood’s golden couple take the next step.
My hand goes numb around the phone. My chest tightens until I can barely breathe.
One month. That’s all it took. One month after our divorce was finalized, and he’s already promising forever to someone else. The same man who swore he needed time. The same man who cried at thought of me leaving him.
I stare at the photo for a long time. They look perfect ,she’s glowing, he’s smiling that familiar camera smile. I almost don’t recognize him anymore.
My thoughts spiral. Did he ever love me, or was I just a prop in his movie? Did he start cheating before we even fell apart, or did he fall in love with her while lying next to me?
I press a hand against my chest, trying to breathe through the ache.
The tears come slowly, then all at once. They burn as they fall, hot against my cold skin. My body shakes with the kind of grief that feels bottomless. I think about the promises, the years I gave him, the dreams I let die for him. I think about how easily he replaced me.
And then I think about the baby. Our baby. The one I’m hiding from the world. The one he doesn’t even know exists.
A bitter laugh slips out between sobs. “You moved on, Isaiah. But I’m the one still carrying your heartbeat.”
I toss the phone aside and curl into myself on the couch. The pain builds in my chest, heavy and sharp. Then another kind of pain hits , low in my abdomen, sudden and cramping.
I freeze.
The pressure deepens. A wave of discomfort rolls through me, stronger this time.
No. No, no, no.
Not now.
I shift, trying to stand, but my legs tremble. Then I feel it , a strange warmth between my thighs, followed by a slow, unmistakable wetness.
Panic grips me. My breathing quickens. My palms press instinctively over my belly.
No
I am not due yet!
“Please,” I whisper, “not now.”
My phone is across the couch. I grab it, hands shaking, and scroll through contacts until I find the number.
“Jet,” I manage to choke out when my head of security answers. “You need to come now.”
“Ma’am? What’s wrong?”
“I—I think something’s happening. I think…” My words stumble. “My water just broke.”
Silence. Then his voice sharpens. “I’m on my way.”
The line goes dead. I clutch the phone to my chest, pacing the living room in slow circles, the pain coming in waves. My vision blurs with tears and fear.
The city lights outside flicker through the curtains, painting shadows across the floor. Somewhere below, life goes on , taxis honking, people laughing, the world spinning.
And here I am, standing in the middle of my living room, clutching my belly, praying that my baby holds on just a little longer. My time not even close. I am just two months away.
Then another contraction hits, hard enough to steal my breath. I bite down on a sob.
“Please,” I whisper to no one, “just hold on.”
The phone slips from my hand, hitting the floor with a dull thud as I double over, tears streaking down my face.
The sound of sirens begins faintly in the distance, growing closer with every passing second.
And for the first time since I came to New York, I am truly, terrifyingly alone.
Two
I wake up in the hospital feeling like I just got struck by lightning.
The scent of antiseptic fills my nose, sharp and almost nauseating. The bright white lights above me sting my eyes, and for a moment, I can't tell if I'm awake or still trapped in some kind of nightmare. My throat feels raw, burning, as though I've swallowed gravel. My lower half is heavy, aching, tight.
Then it hits me like a wave I can't swim through , why I'm here.
My baby.
I jolt up, panic rushing through my veins, but my body immediately betrays me. Pain shoots through me like a thousand knives, and I collapse back onto the bed with a groan that scrapes through my throat.
"Alyssa! Oh, thank goodness you're awake. You gave us a fright."
My mom's voice. Breathless, trembling, relieved.
But my mind is spiraling, my heart thundering. My baby. Where's my baby? Why is my mom here? What's happening?
"Allen, call the doctor back in! She's awake!"
My mom's voice again, louder this time, sharp with authority and fear.
Dad. My dad is here too. I can sense his presence before I see him, the quiet firmness that always surrounds him. How did they find out? Did Jacob call them?
"W... water," I whisper, my voice barely a breath.
Everything hurts , even speaking feels like a battle.
Then I smell her perfume before I see her face. Mom. She moves closer, sitting on the edge of the bed with her usual graceful
composure, but her hands are shaking as she lifts a bottle to my lips.
"Here, sip slowly," she murmurs, holding it for me.
The water hits my tongue, cool and refreshing, and I swallow greedily until my throat stops burning.
When I'm done, she puts the bottle aside and smooths a hand through my hair, just like she used to when I was a child waking up from bad dreams.
"My baby?" The words scrape out of me like sandpaper.
"She's fine. You both are fine." Mom exhales shakily, her voice soft but trembling. "God, Alyssa, do you want to kill me before I even meet my grandchild?"
I barely hear the rest. One word echoes in my mind - she.
"She?" My voice is barely audible.
My mom freezes for a second before realizing what she's said.
"Oh," she says softly, smiling now, "yes, darling. You're having a little girl."
I blink. A girl.
My baby is a girl.
My chest tightens, and my eyes sting before the tears even come. "I'm having a girl," I whisper, as if saying it out loud will make it real. "A little girl."
Tears spill over, and I can't stop them. My gaze drops to my bump, where a fetal monitor is attached, blinking rhythmically. The room fills with the soft, rapid sound of her heartbeat. It's the most beautiful sound I've ever heard.
Mom smiles through her own tears. "Yes, darling. A little princess." She leans in and kisses my forehead, gentle and warm.
The sound of that heartbeat grounds me, fills the sterile air with hope.
But then fear creeps back in, curling around my chest. "Is she okay? What did the doctor say? I need to see the doctor. Now."
"Be quiet, Alyssa," Mom scolds, her tone sharp again. "You're stressing yourself."
Before I can respond, the door opens, and the doctor walks in with a nurse, my dad right behind them and Amy.
Amy?
Why the hell is she here? She should be resting.
She waddles beside the doctor, looking very pregnant and very impatient.
The moment she sees me awake, she gasps dramatically and rushes over.
"Alyssa Marie Turner!" she yells, hands on her hips. "You scared the hell out of me! Don't ever do that again! And now there are rumors all over the internet because of the ambulance parked outside your apartment!"
My eyes widen. Rumors?
Oh no.
"Rumors?" I ask weakly, my heart skipping a beat. The monitor beside me starts beeping faster.
Dad's tone cuts through the tension like a knife. "Amyra Carson Turner. Not now."
Amy huffs but rolls her eyes, muttering under her breath. Sometimes I forget who's the older sister between us.
The doctor clears his throat awkwardly. "Okay, Miss Martins, I am doctor Cobbs, we'll raise your bed now."
Martins.
That name still feels foreign in my mouth, like something that used to belong to me but doesn't anymore. I'm not even legally using it now, but I haven't gotten around to changing it everywhere yet. Too many documents. Too much emotional weight.
"She prefers Miss Turner now," Amy corrects sharply, crossing her arms.
"Ah, apologies for the mistake," the doctor says, chuckling softly.
And Amy smiles , no, she blushes. I swear she actually blushes at the doctor.
Jesus Christ, Amyra.
I'll blame it on her pregnancy hormones because there's no other explanation. She's in love with her bodyguard, for crying out loud, and here she is flirting with the man in scrubs.
Once the nurses adjust my bed and I'm comfortably propped up, the doctor moves closer, flipping through his chart.
"You're stable, Miss... Turner," he says, glancing at Amy before continuing. "You had quite the scare."
"What exactly happened?" I ask, my voice a mixture of dread and curiosity. "Why did my water break so early?"
"Your water didn't exactly break," he explains gently. "You had what we call a leaking liquor - amniotic fluid. Because of your history of miscarriages and ectopic pregnancies, your cervix showed signs of incompetence, meaning it was dilating prematurely."
I blink at him, trying to absorb the words. "So I was... going into labor?"
"Potentially, yes," he replies. "A lot of factors can trigger premature contractions, but stress is one of the biggest. Have you experienced any recent emotional strain? Other than, well... your divorce."
The word hits me like a slap. I can't answer. I just stare out the window, the city lights blurry through my tears.
Amy groans beside me. "Oh, for God's sake, Alyssa. Not again."
The doctor clears his throat politely. "We performed a cerclage - a minor procedure to tie your cervix and stop it from dilating further. You'll need to stay in the hospital for observation until the baby is more viable for delivery. Strict bed rest."
Mom nods firmly before I can say a word. "She's not going anywhere. Not until the baby is safe."
"Mom-"
"No, Alyssa. Listen to your doctor."
Amy crosses her arms. "Oh, she's staying right here. I'll make sure of it."
"Stop it, Amy," I snap, glaring at her.
"I'm not stopping anything if you're being stupid," she fires back.
"Ladies," the doctor interjects awkwardly. "I'll need someone to sign a few consent forms."
Dad steps forward immediately. "I'll handle it."
They both walk out with the nurse, leaving the battlefield behind.
The silence that follows is heavy, until Amy finally turns to me.
"Show me some respect, Amyra. I'm your older sister," I say sharply.
""Then act like one!" she snaps. "I can't believe you're letting this fucking moron get to you from here! He's engaged, so fucking what? He's moved on. You should too! Do you even know what I had to do to manipulate TMZ into changing the story about the ambulance scene? Thank God for Dad's company jet, or we wouldn't have even made it here for you!"
"Fourteen years, Amy," I fire back, my voice shaking. "Fourteen fucking years of loving him! Of making him my everything! And within two months, he divorces me and is ready for a second marriage! Ten years of marriage-down the drain. I can't just fucking forget about him! Everything feels new, and it's like my world just disappeared overnight!"
"Well, he has moved on. So should you." Her tone is cold, cutting. "I also gave Yulis ten years of my life. I was more vulnerable than you when I met him. I was only fucking eighteen! But here I am, completely okay after everything. Sometimes you need to fucking get over your head!"
"You don't get to compare your marriage to mine!" I yell back, my throat tightening with rage. "I loved Isaiah, but you didn't love Yulis. That was a contract! So don't you dare compare us! You're carrying another man's child!"
Amy glares, unflinching. "You don't know what you're talking about. At least Yulis and I had an understanding.I wasn't blindsided by his behavior. You, on the other hand, enabled his bullshit. You became his puppet. You were stupid enough to make him your whole identity. And for what? You don't even think about your child, Alyssa, because if you did,you wouldn't be here breaking yourself apart over a man who clearly doesn't give a damn! So stop being stupid for once in your life and grow a spine. You let him treat you like a shadow. You became his puppet, and for what? Fame? Love? Pity. We won't always
be here to fight your battles. Isaiah has moved on! Focus on yourself!"
My breath catches, tears threatening to spill again.
"At least Yulis and I had an understanding," Amy continues, softer now but no less sharp. "I wasn't blindsided by his actions. You, on the other hand, kept pretending your perfect marriage wasn't burning. You forgot yourself, Alyssa. And that's the problem."
"Stop," I whisper.
"No, you need to hear this. Because one day, Mom and Dad won't be here to clean up your messes. And I won't be, either. You have a baby now. She needs a mother who's strong, not one stuck crying over a man who's already moved on."
"Enough, Amyra!" Mom's voice cuts in sharply. "Your sister's been through enough."
Amy crosses her arms, glaring at the window. "She needed to hear that, Mom."
Silence fills the room. Heavy. Stifling.
For once, I can't argue with her.
Because she's right.
I stare at the monitor, listening to the steady, comforting rhythm of my daughter's heartbeat. That tiny, fast pulse is the sound of life , her life , and I almost risked it for someone who stopped caring the moment he found a new spotlight.
Isaiah moved on. He's out there smiling for cameras, flashing engagement rings, pretending I never existed.
And I'm here, fighting for something far more important than him.
For her.
I reach down and press my palm to my belly, feeling the faint kick beneath my hand.
My baby girl.
My second chance.
Maybe Amy's right. Maybe it's time I stopped being the woman who loved Isaiah Martins and started being the woman who loves her daughter more than anything else.
The tears return, but this time they're quieter, softer, almost freeing.
I take a deep breath, letting it out slowly.
"Okay," I whisper, my voice trembling. "No more crying over him."
Amy looks at me, her face softening.
"Good," she says simply. "Because I'm not changing any more diapers for you either."
Despite everything, I laugh. It's weak and shaky, but it's real.
For the first time in months, I feel like I can breathe again.
Three
So apparently what the doctor meant by being monitored was that I wasn't going home until the baby arrived.
It has been a month since I was admitted, and I am still here. The walls are the same color of dull beige they were when I came in, and I have memorized every crack in the ceiling, every squeak the door makes when the nurses come in at midnight to check my vitals. I have been placed under strict supervision by a team of doctors who apparently think I'm too fragile to walk more than ten steps without something tragic happening. Bed rest, they call it. A luxury prison cell, if you ask me.
I am not allowed to leave the room. The only time I'm permitted to walk is when my legs swell from being still for too long, and even then, a nurse hovers near me like I'm some fragile antique ready to shatter. Sometimes the contractions start-sudden, sharp, and terrifying-but the doctors work their magic and somehow make them stop.
So I read. I write. I sleep. And sometimes, I just stare out the window watching people live their lives. My notebook is filled with things that don't make sense-half poems, half diary entries. Mostly the kind of thoughts that never make it to paper when life is normal. I write about the life I dreamt of having. The one that feels out of reach now.
Amy confiscated my phone a week after I got here, claiming I needed a digital detox. I'm not even allowed to scroll through Instagram, which, honestly, is both a curse and a mercy. The television in my private hospital suite only has Netflix, but even that is restricted to the kids' section. "Cartoons help," the doctor said once. I could tell he was lying through his teeth. It had Amy written all over it. She doesn't want me stumbling across Isaiah's face on some movie poster, reminding me of everything I'm trying to forget.
Living here feels like floating underwater. Days blur together. There's no noise, no laughter, no purpose. Just the rhythmic beeping of machines and the occasional shuffle of nurses outside my door. My life feels small.
I don't have any friends. Never really did. Isaiah used to tell me that women in Hollywood were fake, that they'd use me to get close to him. I believed him. I built my world around him, cut myself off from everything that wasn't him. Now he's gone, and so is everyone else.
My therapist comes twice a week. Mom insisted on hiring her privately, and she had to sign an NDA, just like every doctor and nurse who steps into this room. My therapist says I need to "rebuild social structures" and "reconnect with the outside world" once I'm discharged. I nodded when she said that, even though I have no idea what that world looks like anymore.
Amy was right about the paparazzi. A few days after my admission, photos of me being rushed into the hospital leaked online, and speculation began immediately. The headlines were brutal-"Isaiah Martins's ex-wife hospitalized!"-followed by wild theories about mental breakdowns, overdoses, even plastic surgery complications. Amy handled it. She spun the story before it spiraled, told the press I was recovering from exhaustion. Classic Hollywood cover-up.
But sometimes I still catch a glimpse of men with cameras outside the hospital gates, waiting like vultures. They're not just here for me. Amy is a headline magnet now too. Her divorce from Yulis has become the kind of tabloid circus that Hollywood feeds on. A famous director's wife pregnant by her bodyguard-how could they resist?
People love scandals more than they love truth.
Amy spends most of her time here, shuffling around my room in fuzzy slippers, eating my hospital snacks, and complaining about her swollen feet. Mom stays in my apartment most nights. Dad went back to Los Angeles to handle work at the firm, though he calls every evening to check in. My brother calls too, but it's really his daughter, Ophelia, that I look forward to hearing. She's like sunshine during a rainy season,always laughing, always reminding me that somewhere beyond this hospital window, life still exists.
Today is the first of November. Outside, the leaves are already turning red and gold, and the chill in the air feels sharper than it did yesterday. I'm sitting in bed, absently flipping through a magazine I've already read twice, when the door swings open.
Amy waddles in like she owns the place, one hand clutching a bag of candies and the other resting dramatically on her enormous belly. She's wearing what looks like a medieval costume, complete with a corset and velvet sleeves.
"You do know Halloween was yesterday, right?" I ask, raising a brow.
"Yep," she says, popping a candy into her mouth.
"Then why are you still dressed up? And where's your little boyfriend?" I tease.
A month ago, after my admission, Amy decided she would have her baby here in New York too. She bought the apartment right below mine and moved in with Nikolai like it was the most natural thing in the world. It amazes me sometimes how easily she moves on from chaos, how she just reinvents herself like nothing can touch her. The media, of course, is eating it up. Amy Turner, the scandalous PR queen, now playing house with her ex-bodyguard,it's the kind of thing that trends for weeks.
"Work," she sighs. "He has a contract with Yulis, and apparently, terminating it is about as possible as me fitting into my pre-pregnancy jeans. That contract is tighter than my Spanx. Even Dad can't find a loophole. So, he flew back to LA this morning."
"Oh," I say, frowning. "He should be careful. Yulis isn't exactly the forgiving type."
Amy waves her hand dismissively. "Yeah, yeah, I told him that. But you know men,they think they're untouchable." She plops down on the couch with a groan.
"That still doesn't explain the outfit," I say, smirking.
"Role playing this morning, baby," she says, grinning wickedly. "Too tired to change. Ugh. I need this baby out of me now."
Her groan turns into laughter, and for a second, the room feels light again. Her belly has grown enormous,she looks like she's smuggling a watermelon under her dress,and she's due any day now. Despite how heavy she must feel, she still manages to flirt shamelessly with my doctor whenever he checks in. He always blushes, flustered by her boldness, and she teases him until he stammers through his words. Amy is everything I'm not,confident, outspoken, unapologetically alive.
Later that afternoon, I drift off for a nap. The soft hum of machines and distant chatter of nurses lull me into sleep, but it doesn't last long.
I wake up to the sound of whimpering. At first, I think it's from the hallway, but then I notice Amy hunched over on the couch, clutching her stomach.
"Fuck. That hurts," she groans.
My heart skips. "Amy? Are you okay? Should I call a nurse?"
"Yes. I think I'm in labor," she grits out, trying to stand. The moment she does, her water breaks, splattering across the floor like a bad movie scene.
"Oh my God," I breathe. "Okay, okay-don't move!" I reach for the red call button on my bed and press it hard.
Within seconds, two nurses rush in.
"Miss Martins, are you alright?" one of them asks, looking alarmed.
"Yes, I'm fine, but my sister's water just broke!" I point to Amy, who's now panting and muttering curses under her breath.
They spring into action, calling for a doctor, gathering towels, and preparing to wheel her to a delivery room. But Amy, being Amy, has other plans.
"I'm not leaving her!" she shouts between contractions. "She's not supposed to move, and I'm not doing this without her!"
"Amy, don't be ridiculous," I say, already panicking. "I'll be fine."
"No," she insists, her eyes glassy but determined. "You're staying right here. They can bring me a damn bed in this room if they have to."
The nurses exchange uneasy glances, but within minutes, hospital staff are rearranging the space. They bring in another bed, separate us with a thin curtain, and set up everything they need. The doctor arrives,sweating, slightly out of breath,and immediately calls our mother.
Mom bursts in less than twenty minutes later, her hair still pinned in the messy bun she probably threw together mid-panic. "What happened? Is she okay?" she demands.
"She's in labor," I say, smiling weakly.
Amy screams just then, and Mom rushes to her side, taking her hand. "Breathe, sweetheart. You're okay. Just breathe."
The next few hours are a blur of shouting, encouragement, and tension. I stay on my side of the curtain, clutching my pillow, whispering prayers. Every time Amy screams, my chest tightens. I want to help her, hold her, do something. But all I can do is listen.
Then, finally, there's silence. A few seconds later, a new sound fills the room,the cry of a newborn baby. High-pitched. Fragile. Beautiful.
Tears spill down my cheeks before I even realize it.
The curtain is pulled back, and there's Amy, drenched in sweat, holding the tiniest baby boy I've ever seen. Her face glows with exhaustion and joy.
"Meet Emeric Turner Young," she says softly, her voice trembling.
He's perfect. A little pink bundle wrapped in white, eyes squinting as if already annoyed by the world.
Mom is crying, kissing Amy's forehead over and over. "He's beautiful, Amy. So beautiful."
We spend the next two hour taking turns holding him. Amy looks so at peace, it almost hurts to see. Her chaos, her scandals, her loud personality,all of it fades in this moment. She's just a mother holding her son.
Mom tries to reach Nikolai, but he's unreachable,calls go to voicemail, messages left unanswered. Amy's smile falters a few times, but she doesn't let herself cry. I can tell she's pretending to be okay, pretending it doesn't matter.
That night, when everyone leaves and the room is quiet again, I stare at her sleeping form. She's curled up beside her baby, her hair messy, face soft.
I feel something shift in my chest something that feels like hope.
Despite everything, despite heartbreak and betrayal and loneliness, life keeps moving. It doesn't wait for you to heal before it hands you another reason to smile.
And for the first time in months, I smile too.
Four
Three days. That's how long the walls of this hospital have felt like both a refuge and a prison. Three long, sleepless days since Amy gave birth, and I'm still confined to my bed, legs swollen, belly heavy, the faint beeping of monitors filling the quiet when we're too tired to talk.
The doctors still won't let me move much. "You're stable," they say, like that's supposed to comfort me. But stability feels like a cruel joke when everything else around me is falling apart.
Amy has been restless since morning. Her discharge papers sit on the tray table beside her bed, but she hasn't even looked
at them. Her hospital bag is open, half-packed, the little baby blanket folded neatly on top , a blanket Nikolai bought before he left for Los Angeles.
She's staring at her phone again. That same unread message screen. Her lips are trembling, but her pride won't let her cry.
"I've called him twenty-seven times," she says finally, voice breaking. "Twenty-seven, Alyssa. Who does that to someone who just gave birth to their child?"
I want to tell her to stop counting, to stop torturing herself with the silence. But I can't lie to her. I know what it feels like to wait for someone who doesn't call back. I know the taste of that slow, choking fear.
"Maybe he's just caught up somewhere," I whisper, though even as the words leave my mouth, they feel useless.
Amy's laugh comes out sharp, humorless. "Caught up? He's not lost in a grocery store, Alyssa. He's in Los Angeles. He's alive. He just doesn't care."
Mom walks in before I can respond. She looks exhausted , she hasn't left the hospital since we were both admitted. Her curls are flat, her eyes red. She tries to smile as she carries a paper cup of coffee to Amy's bedside.
"Sweetheart," she says softly, "try not to work yourself up. Stress isn't good right now. You need to rest, for the baby's sake."
Amy shakes her head. "How am I supposed to rest when I don't even know if he's okay? Or if he's-"
"Don't," I interrupt quietly. "Don't finish that sentence."
The silence that follows is unbearable.
Around noon, Amy's frustration boils over. She snatches her phone off the table and starts scrolling furiously. "Fine. If he won't answer, maybe his family will."
My heart sinks. "Amy, no. Don't call them. You know they-"
She's already pressing the call button. I reach out but stop midway. She deserves the truth, even if it hurts.
The call rings once. Twice. Then someone picks up.
"Hello?" an older woman's voice answers, sharp and cold.
"Mrs. Young, hi. This is Alyssa, Nikolai's-"
"I know exactly who this is," she cuts in, venom dripping from every word. "What the hell do you people want now?"
I freeze. Amy's eyes dart toward me.
"Ma'am," I begin carefully, "we've been trying to reach Nikolai for days. Amy just had the baby, and-"
"Oh, please spare me the pity story," she snaps. "You people dragged our son into your Hollywood filth. Look at the headlines. Look at the humiliation. Do you think we don't see what's being said about him?"
"Mrs. Young, that's not-"
"Don't you dare lie to me! He was a good boy before your sister seduced him. Before she broke her marriage vows and dragged his name through the mud."
Amy rips the phone from my hand. "I'm right here, you bitter old witch," she snarls. "If you have something to say, say it to me!"
There's a pause, then a hissed, "You disgust me. If anything happens to my son, Amy, you'll regret the day you met him."
The line goes dead.
Amy stands frozen, the phone still pressed to her ear, her breathing ragged.
Mom covers her mouth. "Dear God..."
I take the phone from Amy gently, but she snatches it back. "They think this is my fault," she mutters. "They think I destroyed him."
I want to tell her they're wrong, but the look in her eyes tells me she already believes them.
By evening, we try reaching Yulis. Every number we have goes straight to voicemail. The hospital room is dim, the television flickering silently in the corner. Amy is pacing now, the baby's soft cries filling the air as Mom rocks him gently.
Then, miraculously, one of the numbers connects.
Yulis's voice, calm and smug as ever. "Alyssa. How unexpected."
"Yulis," I say tightly, "we need to talk. Amy's been trying to reach Nikolai. Do you know where he is?"
There's a pause, long enough to make my stomach twist.
"I don't owe you or your sister any explanations," he says finally. "You both made your choices."
"Look Yulis,she just had his baby and he needs to know,he needs to be here" I say calmly and deadly.
"The whore final had the bastard. I don't really care.."he says bitterly.
Amy storms across the room, grabs the phone from my hand. "You bastard!" she screams. "Where is he? What did you do?"
"Watch your mouth, Amy," Yulis says evenly. "You're not in a position to-"
"Don't tell me what position I'm in! You think you can play God with people's lives because you have money? You think you can bury him like you bury your scandals?"
There's a faint click. He's gone.
Amy hurls the phone across the room. It hits the wall and shatters. Mom gasps. I close my eyes, fighting the sting in my throat.
That night, none of us sleep. Amy sits by the window, staring into the darkness. I lie awake, one hand resting on my stomach, the baby inside me shifting weakly, like she can feel the tension too.
When morning comes, I'm half-asleep when I hear Mom's gasp.
The television is on. A red "Breaking News" banner flashes across the screen.
Mass Shooting in Los Angeles. Multiple Casualties.
Amy sits up, her hair disheveled, her face pale. "What-what's happening?"
The reporter's voice trembles as she speaks. "The incident took place earlier this morning at a private event in downtown Los Angeles. Among the injured is renowned film director Yulis Mack..."
My heart stops.
Amy turns to me, wide-eyed. "No. No, no, no."
Her hands tremble as she reaches for the remote, turning the volume up.
The reporter continues, "Sources confirm that Yulis Mack remains in critical condition. Authorities have yet to identify several other victims."
The room goes silent.
Amy whispers, "If Yulis was there..." Then she stops, shaking her head violently. "No. No, he wasn't. Nikolai wouldn't-he wouldn't be there."
I don't answer. I can't.
The minutes stretch into an hour. Amy refuses to eat, refuses to lie down. Her baby starts crying again, and she doesn't even flinch. Mom tries to soothe both of them, her voice breaking as she hums an old lullaby.
Then, as if fate couldn't be crueler, the next headline drops.
"Authorities have confirmed one of the deceased as Nikolai Young, ex personal bodyguard and boyfriend of celebrity publicist Amy Turner."
Amy screams.
The sound doesn't even sound human. It's raw, guttural, ripped straight from her chest.
Mom rushes to her side, trying to hold her, but Amy shoves her away, falling to the floor. "No! No, that's not true! He promised,he promised he'd come back!"
Her voice echoes through the room.
I can't move. I can't breathe. My heart feels like it's caving in.
The baby in my belly kicks hard, and I press my hand there, whispering through tears, "Please, not now. Please."
Amy is sobbing uncontrollably, clutching the blanket Nikolai bought. Mom is crying too, trying to pull her up, whispering prayers through her tears.
The nurses rush in, alarmed, but even they can't calm her. It takes two of them to lift Amy onto the bed. She keeps screaming his name. Over and over.
I can't bear to watch, but I can't look away either.
The baby's cries mix with Amy's, filling the room with chaos and heartbreak.
Then, as suddenly as it started, Amy goes quiet. Too quiet.
Her body shakes. She stares at the wall, eyes glassy and distant.
"Amy?" I whisper.
No response.
Mom looks at me, panic written all over her face. "Alyssa, call the nurse again!"
I press the button repeatedly, my own vision blurring.
In that moment, the reality of it all hits me like a wave. Nikolai is gone. Yulis is fighting for his life. And my sister ,my strong, fiery, impossible sister ,is slipping away right before my eyes.
The nurses rush in again. The machines beep. Someone calls for a sedative.
I hold her hand, whispering, "It's okay, Amy. I'm here. I'm not going anywhere."
Her eyes flutter for a second. Then close.
The room falls into a hollow kind of silence, broken only by the faint hum of the machines and the distant cries of her newborn son.
And as I sit there, clutching my swollen belly and praying for the strength to hold us all together, I know deep down that nothing will ever be the same again.
Not after this.
Not after him.
Five
The hospital feels different today.
It is quieter, heavier, as if the walls themselves are mourning. The nurses walk softly, their shoes barely making a sound against the polished floor. Even the machines seem to hum more gently, as though afraid of breaking the fragile silence that has settled after yesterday's storm.
Nikolai is dead.
I still can't say it out loud. The words refuse to come out of my mouth. When they echo in my head, they sound unreal, like someone else's tragedy. But it happened. He is gone.
Amy has not spoken since they told her. She screamed once, a sound so raw and violent it didn't sound human, and then she just... shut down. The doctors sedated her after she tried to leave the hospital barefoot, insisting she had to find him. Now she lies still in her hospital bed two doors away from mine, her baby
sleeping in the neonatal ward until she is stable enough to hold him again.
I should rest. I am supposed to rest. But I can't.
All morning I have been shuttling between my room and hers, checking on her, checking on Mom, telling everyone I am fine. I am not fine. I haven't been fine for a long time. But pretending is easier than admitting I am falling apart.
The doctor came in earlier to check my vitals. His frown told me what I already knew. My blood pressure is too high. The baby's movements are strong but irregular. He told me I need to stay calm, to breathe deeply, to "avoid emotional stress."
I almost laughed. How do you avoid emotional stress when your sister is barely holding onto her sanity and the entire world is watching your family's downfall like it is some sick television show?
Outside the hospital, the chaos has already begun. The paparazzi found out about the shooting and Nikolai's death. They are camped outside the entrance with cameras flashing, hungry for photos of Amy, or me, or anyone related to the story. I heard one of the nurses whisper that TMZ and Page Six are running pieces titled "Tragedy Follows the Turner Sisters."
That one stung.
Around noon, my father's assistant called from Los Angeles, warning me to stay indoors. He said the headlines were twisting the story, that people were speculating about whether Amy's affair had somehow led to what happened. The words made my stomach twist. How cruel people can be when they are desperate for a scandal.
When I enter Amy's room, it smells faintly of antiseptic and baby lotion. She is lying on her side, her face pale and hollow.
Her eyes are open, unfocused, fixed somewhere far away that I cannot reach.
Mom is sitting in the chair beside her bed, gently stroking Amy's hair, humming a tune I recognize from when we were children. Her voice trembles on the last note.
I stand by the door, unsure if I should speak. The air feels fragile, like one wrong word could shatter it.
"Did she eat?" I ask softly.
Mom shakes her head. "She won't touch anything. The doctor said it's normal. Shock, grief, exhaustion. They're monitoring her closely."
I nod and step closer. "Maybe she'll eat if I stay with her for a bit."
Mom gives me a tired look. "You need to rest too, sweetheart. You're not strong enough to be moving around like this."
"I'll be fine," I whisper.
She sighs but doesn't argue. She knows it's pointless.
I take the chair on the other side of Amy's bed. For a long time, I just sit there, watching her breathe. Her chest rises and falls slowly, each breath shallow. I want to tell her it will be okay, that she will get through this, but the words feel dishonest. I don't even believe them myself.
After a while, her eyes flick toward me. They look glassy and strange, like she's seeing me but not really.
"Alyssa," she whispers. Her voice sounds like gravel, rough and broken. "He was here."
I blink. "Who?"
"Nikolai."
My heart stumbles. "Amy..."
She sits up suddenly, her hands trembling. "I saw him. He was standing right there. By the door. He looked at me and smiled."
I glance at the doorway. It's empty. The light flickers slightly from the ceiling.
"You were dreaming," I say gently. "You're tired. You've been through so much."
Her eyes widen, panic flooding them. "No, I wasn't dreaming! He was here. He said he couldn't leave yet. That he was waiting for me."
Mom stands quickly and reaches for the nurse call button, but Amy swats her hand away.
"Don't treat me like I'm crazy!" she shouts, tears spilling down her face. "I know what I saw!"
The baby starts crying softly in the corner bassinet,he was brought here to breastfeed and mom wanted him to stay for a while, maybe that will help with Amy.. The sound pierces through the tension. Amy looks toward it, her expression collapsing into despair.
"I've lost everything," she whispers, her voice trembling. "You still have something to fight for, Alyssa. You still have your dignity. You don't know what it's like to lose the person you love and still have to breathe."
Her words hit me like a slap.
I swallow hard. "Amy, you have your son. You have Mom. You have all of us. You are not alone."
She looks at me with eyes full of rage and sorrow. "Don't you dare talk to me about not being alone. You have no idea what this feels like."
The nurse enters quietly, and Amy turns away from me, pulling the blanket over her head.
Mom gives me a pleading look, silently asking me to go.
I nod and step out of the room before the tears can escape.
Back in my room, I close the door and lean against it. My hands are shaking. My chest feels tight, my breaths shallow. The baby kicks hard, sharp pain radiating across my abdomen. I grip the edge of the bed and try to steady myself.
"Calm down," I whisper to my belly. "You're okay. Mommy's okay."
But it's a lie.
By evening, the sky outside has turned a deep shade of blue, streaked with orange light from the setting sun. The city looks almost peaceful from my window, but I can hear the faint buzz of sirens and traffic below.
Mom comes in briefly with some soup and a small smile that doesn't reach her eyes. "Try to eat, sweetheart."
I nod, but the moment she leaves, I push the tray aside. My stomach feels like a knot.
I open my journal and flip to a clean page. The pen feels heavy in my hand, but I force myself to write. Words spill out ,messy, uneven, desperate.
I write about Amy. About how fragile she looks. About Nikolai's death. About how the world keeps spinning even when ours has stopped. I write until my vision starts to blur.
My handwriting wobbles as the room begins to tilt slightly. I blink a few times, trying to steady my sight. My chest tightens again, and a sharp pain spreads low in my stomach.
"Not now," I whisper.
I try to stand, but the floor moves beneath me. My heart races. My breath comes in shallow bursts. I reach for the call button,
but my fingers slip. The pain grows stronger - blinding, tearing, consuming.
The journal falls to the floor.
I try to call out, but no sound comes. My knees give way, and I collapse beside the bed. The cold tiles press against my cheek. My vision fades in and out. I hear the faint echo of a nurse's voice somewhere down the hall, but it feels distant, unreachable.
Then there is silence.
Not the peaceful kind, but the heavy, terrifying kind that lives between heartbeats.
The baby kicks once, hard, as if protesting.
And then everything goes black.
Six
The world felt quiet before it broke.
I floated somewhere between sleep and something deeper, something darker. It was the kind of silence that hums inside your skull, heavy and distant, like the pause before thunder. I could hear faint echoes around me-voices slipping through fog, machines beeping somewhere close, a woman crying from what felt like a different lifetime. My body felt both heavy and
weightless, like I was sinking underwater while trying to breathe through glass.
And then I heard a word that sliced through the blur.
"Placental abruption."
Another voice followed, sharp and urgent. "We need to move now."
For a moment, I didn't know if they were talking about me. The room tilted, and I could taste metal on my tongue. My chest rose and fell, but it didn't feel like my own doing. My mind flickered,Amy's face, tear-stained and broken, her arms clutching her newborn son. Nikolai's name echoed in the corridors. My mother's voice trembled somewhere near me, praying in short gasps.
And then, in that strange space between panic and peace, Isaiah appeared.
He stood in a white room, the same tuxedo he wore the night he won his first award. Cameras flashed behind him, a thousand faces chanting his name. He turned toward me and smiled the way he used to,soft, magnetic, cruelly beautiful. My heart clenched.
"Alyssa," he said. "You're shaking."
I tried to answer but my lips barely moved. Everything felt slippery, unreal. I wanted to tell him that I was scared, that I couldn't lose this baby, that I didn't want to die in a hospital room under fluorescent lights. But my words wouldn't come. All I could do was reach out,and touch nothing.
The dream faded.
Voices rushed in like a storm. My world became motion and noise,hands on my arms, someone shouting for anesthesia, the ceiling lights passing one by one as they wheeled me down a
corridor. My body burned from the inside, sharp pains ripping through my abdomen. I could hear my mother's voice begging them to save me, her prayers tumbling into sobs.
Then everything went white.
For a long while, there was nothing but the sound of my own heartbeat,slow, tired, stubborn.
When I opened my eyes again, I thought I was still dreaming. The room was dim. The air smelled like antiseptic and something faintly sweet, like powdered milk. A soft wail cut through the silence,a baby's cry, small but alive.
I didn't move. I was terrified that if I did, the sound would disappear.
Then someone whispered beside me. "She's here, Alyssa. Your daughter's here."
The nurse leaned close, her face blurry through the tears clouding my vision. She placed a tiny bundle against my chest. Warm. Fragile. Real. My arms felt too weak to hold her, but I did anyway.
Her cry softened as her cheek pressed against me. I looked down, and there she was,my daughter. A perfect, trembling miracle.
Her skin was pink, her lips moving softly as if she already wanted to speak. Her eyes fluttered open for a second, dark and curious, before closing again.
I whispered, "It's just us now, okay? Just us."
The nurse smiled faintly and took her away for observation. "She's doing well," she said. "She's premature but strong. We'll take her to the NICU just for a while."
When the door closed, the silence felt unbearable again.
I stared at the empty space where my baby had been. I wanted to cry, but I was too exhausted. My body felt stitched together with invisible thread, my heart both hollow and too full. Somewhere deep inside, relief and grief were fighting for space, and I didn't know which one would win.
Hours passed, or maybe minutes. The clock meant nothing anymore. When the door opened again, it was my mother. Her eyes were swollen, her hair messy, her hands trembling as she held a cup of water for me.
"She's beautiful," she said softly. "She looks like you."
I nodded. My throat burned too much to speak.
Then I saw her,Amy.
She was sitting on the second bed, pale and fragile, her son asleep in her arms. The sight almost made me cry again. She looked smaller somehow, like a piece of her had vanished.
Her eyes met mine. For a long time, neither of us said anything.
Finally, she whispered, "You were right."
I frowned.
She adjusted her baby in her arms, her voice trembling. "About me needing to be here. For him. For Emeric. He's all I have left now. I am he has"
Her words cracked something open in me. I tried to smile, but it came out crooked. "He's lucky to have you,and you have us Amy,you both do" I managed.
Amy looked down at her son, brushing a strand of hair from his face. "Maybe. I don't feel lucky."
"You will," I whispered.
Her gaze lifted again, and for the first time in days, I saw something human flicker behind her grief. It wasn't joy, not yet,but it was the first fragile step toward it.
The room was silent except for the soft breathing of two newborns in two different wards. The weight of everything,Nikolai's death, Amy's pain, my near-death,hung heavy in the air, pressing against the walls like it wanted to burst out.
I closed my eyes, listening to the faint hum of hospital machines. Somewhere beyond the window, New York glowed,neon signs, traffic lights, a world still spinning despite the wreckage of ours.
By the next morning, the chaos outside had found us.
The hospital lobby was swarmed with paparazzi, their cameras flashing like lightning whenever someone opened a door. They were there for Amy hungry for details, headlines, and heartbreak. "CELEBRITY PUBLICIST AMY TURNER LOSES PARTNER DAYS AFTER CHILDBIRTH" was already splashed across every news outlet.
But they didn't know about me. Not yet.
I had been careful, quiet. I never announced my pregnancy, never left my home without coverage. Still, the moment they saw our parents arriving together, whispers started spreading like wildfire.
When I looked out the window from my hospital bed, I could see them,the lenses pointed at every nurse who walked by, every shadow that crossed a windowpane. It was suffocating.
My mother tried to draw the curtains, but I stopped her. "It's fine," I said, though my voice trembled. "They'll find out eventually."
She turned to me, her eyes tired. "Let them find out when you're ready. Not before."
I wanted to believe that. But in my world, privacy was always an illusion.
Later that afternoon, the nurse wheeled me to the NICU. Mom made sure there was no parent visiting during my visit. The air inside was cool and still, filled with the rhythmic beeping of monitors. Tiny bodies lay in incubators, wrapped in pastel blankets. It was like stepping into a fragile, floating universe where every breath mattered.
And then I saw her.
My daughter.
Race Faith Martins.
The name came to me that morning, whispered like a promise. Race-because she came from everything I tried to outrun. Faith-because she was proof that even broken things could survive.
She lay there with her tiny hands curled up near her face, a small tube running along her nose, her chest rising and falling with gentle persistence. My heart cracked open all over again.
I pressed my hand against the glass and whispered, "You came from all my breaking points, Race. Maybe that's why you're the only thing that feels real."
Her little fingers twitched, and I smiled through tears.
For a long time, I just stood there, listening to the machines, watching the faint rise and fall of her chest.
Outside, the world was still loud, cruel, and hungry for stories. But in that small, sterile room, everything else fell away. It was just me and her,and the quiet promise of something new beginning in the ruins.
Seven
The world feels softer now, as if someone turned down the volume on everything that used to be too loud. The machines. The footsteps. The whisper of nurses behind curtains. Two weeks have passed since I gave birth, and yet the hospital still feels like it belongs to another lifetime,one I'm still trying to climb out of.
The corridors that once buzzed with urgency now echo in quiet hums. Only my mom, Amy, and the nurses ever came in. No crying babies in the halls, no chatter from visitors, just the soft hiss of oxygen and the muted sound of footsteps that always stopped outside my door.
I should have been home by now, but Race had to stay. Premature. Fragile. Perfectly tiny. The doctors said she just needed more time to grow, to breathe on her own without the machines. I tried to be patient. I told myself that patience was just another form of love.
Every morning I came here, wrapped in a cardigan too big for me, notebook pressed against my chest like a shield. I sat by the incubator and wrote letters to her,letters I'll give her one day when she's old enough to ask me who I was before I became her mother. Letters about survival and what it means to love something so much that it terrifies you.
Her name, Race Faith Martins, still feels too big for her tiny body. But it fits in the way that names do when they're meant to carry both your pain and your hope.
My scar still aches when I move too fast. Sometimes, it burns when I laugh, so I've learned to smile instead. My body feels unfamiliar. My reflection in the bathroom mirror looks like someone I used to know faded, smaller, quieter. But every time I see Race's fingers twitch inside the incubator, something inside me stitches itself back together.
Amy moved into my apartment the same day I was discharged. She came in with three suitcases, a diaper bag, and a look that told me not to argue. She said it was temporary. I didn't believe her. Because I knew she couldn't stay in the apartment without Nikolai's presence haunting her. Plus it would be good for mom. We've stressed out that woman alot. We both needed each other too much to pretend otherwise.
Her energy is different now. She still carries herself like the woman who once walked through premieres in six-inch heels and sunglasses that cost more than rent, but her eyes tell another story. She's softer now, in a way that feels both broken and brave. She clings to her son, Emeric, like he's her anchor. Sometimes, she forgets to eat until I remind her. Other times, I find her sitting by the window, whispering to him about a man who loved her and couldn't stay.
The paparazzi are relentless. They've turned her pain into a circus. Every day, they wait outside the building, shouting her name, twisting her silence into scandal. They say she was involved in Nikolai's death. They say she's unstable. They say a lot of things, but none of them are true. Yulis has gone quiet,out of the radar.
They still have no idea that I've already given birth. That secret belongs to Amy and me. She made sure of it. Everyone who stepped foot in that hospital signed NDAs. The doctors,
the nurses, even the cleaners. Amy made sure my daughter's existence stayed invisible to the world.
She uses her own chaos to protect me. Every tabloid headline with her name is a shield in disguise. Every photo of her walking with Emeric keeps the cameras away from me. I tell her she doesn't need to do it, but she just shrugs and says, "You'd do the same for me."
Inside the apartment, we've built a small world that feels almost safe. Amy has occupied one of my guest rooms, and mom the other. Then with the help of dad, who flew in the day of Nikolai's death, helped turn my tiny office space that was filled with my books to a cute nursery for Emeric.
Emeric's room is painted in soft blue with toy airplanes hanging from the ceiling. Race's room is all cream and sunlight, her name written in gold letters on the wall. Amy still wakes up in the middle of the night, crying quietly. I pretend not to hear, but sometimes I sit outside her door, listening to the sound of her trying to piece herself back together.
Our parents are still in New York. My mother refuses to leave us alone, while my father also says the same, my brother handles things in California . His presence changes everything. He has this quiet authority that makes the noise outside feel small.
He handles the logistics, the building security, the deliveries, everything that could possibly leak to the press. My mother divides her time between Amy and me, always with a cup of tea and a voice that trembles when she says she's proud.
I tell everyone I'm fine. I smile when I have to. I eat when they watch. I even laugh when Amy makes a joke about how both our lives could be turned into a soap opera. But deep down, I know I'm still split open in ways that no surgery could fix. My body came home, but my heart is still somewhere between the NICU
and that sterile delivery room where I first heard my daughter cry.
Then one morning, a week after being discharged, my phone rang. It was the hospital.
They told me Race was ready to come home.
I didn't even realize I'd stopped breathing until the nurse repeated herself. My knees went weak. I cried, but quietly, as if even my tears might wake her too soon.
When I told Amy, she froze mid-sentence. Then she laughed,a real laugh this time,and said, "We're doing this."
She planned everything within hours. That morning, she left the apartment with Emeric in her arms, knowing the cameras would follow her every step. It was her distraction. Her way of creating noise so I could slip through the silence unnoticed.
My father's security team handled the rest. They pulled the car into the hospital's back entrance. No photographers. No reporters. Just the sound of my shoes echoing softly down the corridor.
The nurse met me at the NICU doors, holding a bundle wrapped in a pale yellow blanket. My heart clenched when I saw her face again. Her cheeks had filled out, her lips pinker, her tiny chest rising with steady breaths.
When the nurse placed her in my arms, the world tilted. My hands trembled. My throat burned. I whispered, "We're going home, baby."
For a moment, everything else disappeared.
The drive back felt endless. Every stoplight was torture. I kept glancing down at her to make sure she was still breathing. My father sat beside me, silent but protective, his eyes flickering to the rearview mirror every few seconds.
When we finally reached the apartment, we didn't use the main entrance. We went through the underground garage, away from the chaos outside. My father carried the car seat while I followed, clutching the baby bag to my chest like it held my soul.
Inside, the air smelled faintly of vanilla and baby powder. Amy's laughter drifted from upstairs, tired but genuine. Emeric was asleep in his bassinet, his tiny fist tucked beneath his chin. My mother stood by the door, her hands trembling as she saw me carrying Race.
"Welcome home," she whispered, her voice breaking.
I didn't speak. I just walked straight to Race's nursery. The cream curtains were drawn, sunlight spilling gently across the room. The rocking chair sat by the window, draped in one of my old blankets. I lowered her into the crib and just stared.
For the first time, it felt real.
No cameras. No crowds. No scandals. Just me and my child.
That night, the city hummed outside our walls, but inside, everything was still. I sat by the crib, listening to her soft breathing. The air smelled like new beginnings and something sacred.
Amy came in quietly, her hair messy, eyes red but kind. She didn't say anything at first. She just sat beside me, holding a cup of tea.
"She's beautiful," she said softly.
"She's perfect," I whispered.
Amy smiled faintly. "You know everyone is going to lose its mind when they find out."
"I know," I said, brushing a finger against Race's tiny hand. "But not yet. They don't get to have this part of her. Not yet."
"I think you should Zay about her. As much as I hate him,he deserves to know he has a daughter. He'll find out one way or other. We can't keep her hidden forever" she says quietly
"I know,but I am not ready for him to know her yet. He already has his dream family,this is mine. I'll tell him when I am ready" I reply looking at my world.
We sat there in silence for a long time. Just two sisters and two babies learning how to exist in a world that had already taken so much from them.
When Amy left the room, I stayed. The hours passed in slow motion. The city lights flickered faintly through the curtains, tiny flashes of paparazzi cameras in the distance. They looked like ghosts,reminders of the life I used to live, the one I left behind.
I leaned closer to the crib, watching Race's chest rise and fall, and whispered, "You're home now. No one knows you exist yet, and maybe that's a good thing. For now, you're mine. Only mine." She stirred slightly, her lips parting in her sleep, and I smiled.
"The world doesn't know you yet," I said softly. "But one day they will. And when they do, it'll be on our terms, not theirs. One day, I'll tell you why I gave you your father's last name. Maybe by then, I'll have forgiven him. Or maybe I'll just be ready to tell you the truth."
I reached out and turned off the lamp. The room fell into gentle darkness, the only sound of the rhythm of her breathing.
Outside, the world was waiting. But tonight, it could wait a little longer.
Because in this small apartment, in this fragile quiet, I finally had something worth protecting.
And for the first time in a very long time, I wasn't afraid.
Eight
Isaiah
It has been six months since the divorce, and I am still searching for air. I spend most of my nights at Anya's house in Beverly Hills, though calling it home would be a stretch. It feels more like a movie set that never wraps.
Cameras. Publicists. Assistants whispering about photo ops. Everyone pretending we are happy. I sleep there sometimes, but I live somewhere else entirely.
The house Alyssa and I bought together still stands in the hills above Los Angeles. It has become my secret sanctuary. I keep the place locked. Curtains drawn. I never let Anya near it. She thinks I keep it for "space." The truth is, I cannot bear to let another woman inside the walls where Alyssa once slept beside me. That house is the only place where she still feels real.
Her perfume lingers faintly in the hallway. Sometimes, when I open the bedroom door, I can still imagine her standing there with that soft look in her eyes, her hair falling down her shoulders, her voice saying my name like a prayer. I have tried to wash the sheets a thousand times, but the scent refuses to leave. Maybe it never will.
I come here when I cannot breathe. I drink too much. Smoke until the air thickens. I play old interviews where Alyssa and I are smiling, pretending we were not already slipping away from each other. I still have all our text messages saved, even the ones that end in silence. I never deleted her number. I just renamed it "Don't."
There is a part of me that knows I destroyed everything, but I do not know how to stop watching the ruins burn.
I can trace the first crack back to one night. The night Alyssa told me she wanted to act again. The night I brought her home from Amy's house after our ten days argument . She said she missed the stage, the lights, the feeling of being seen for who she really was. I heard something else. I heard that I was going to lose her.
When we had arguments and she went to Amy after that scandal with Anya and stayed ten days. Ten days of silence. Ten days that felt like the world ending in slow motion. I spent the first three nights pacing our bedroom, replaying every word she said. By the fifth night, I stopped eating. By the seventh, I was drinking.
Then came Anya. My co-star. Always around. Always too close. She asked if I was alright, and I told her no. She said I was brilliant and misunderstood.
She told me that Alyssa was too quiet, too selfless, that I needed someone who could match my fire. I wanted to believe her. I wanted to believe anyone who told me I was still good.
That night we kissed. It was not romantic. It was desperate. I pulled away almost immediately, disgusted with myself. But guilt is a disease that spreads fast. I told myself it was just a mistake. That I could bury it. But Anya was not finished.
She introduced me to her dealer the next week. "Just something to help you calm down," she said. It started small. A little powder before a long shoot, a pill before an event. But once the guilt set in, I needed more.
Leo, my manager, and Julian, my publicist,and Tasha ,saw what was happening and turned it into strategy. They told me Anya and I had chemistry. That Hollywood loved new blood. That Alyssa's quiet elegance didn't sell. "You and Anya are the next big thing," they said.
The drugs blurred everything. The guilt, the noise, the truth. One night I got too high, too lost, and I crossed the line for good. I remember flashes of that night like broken glass. Her hand in my hair. Her voice in my ear as I ate her out.
Fuck I was too high,I thought it was Alyssa.
My own disgust afterward, crawling into the bathroom and staring at myself in the mirror until I wanted to break it.
That was the point of no return. I told myself I had already become the man Alyssa feared I would. The villain. And once you start to believe you are the villain, it becomes easier to live like one.
Now six months later,I am divorced and soon to be father with my girlfriend carrying my child.
Here I am, in this strange half-life. I am still acting, still smiling, still pretending I am happy. But everything inside me feels hollow.
Anya tries to keep the illusion alive. She calls it love. I call it survival. She tells me I am the father of her child and that we should be grateful. And she is right about one thing. I do love my child. More than I ever thought I could love anything again.
Sometimes I sit beside her at night, her hand resting on her swollen belly, and I feel the baby move. Those small kicks, those faint flutters,they make me feel alive again. For a few seconds, I imagine holding the baby. Teaching them to walk. Protecting them from everything I failed to protect Alyssa from. The love I could not give her, I want to pour into this child.
But then the guilt returns, stronger than ever. The thought of what I have done, of who I became, of the family I destroyed to create this one.
Leo threatens to send me to rehab if I keep showing up late to shoots or skipping interviews. TMZ has caught me stumbling out of clubs at four in the morning, eyes red, shirt half unbuttoned. The headlines write themselves. "Isaiah Martins Parties Before Fatherhood." "Inside Isaiah and Anya's Turbulent Love."
The devil is fast but Julian and Tasha are faster every time. They leak "exclusive" updates about our wedding plans. They post glowing articles about how "fatherhood has changed me."
Anya plays along, always smiling, always tagging me in her posts. I watch her from across the room, talking to photographers and stylists as if our love were a brand partnership. Maybe it is.
I do not hate her, but I cannot stand being near her for long. Her laughter feels artificial, rehearsed. Her affection feels like another performance. When she touches me, it feels like a scene we have both memorized.
She wants to believe this is real. I want to believe I can still be saved.
.....
Later in the evening,
When the nights grow too long, I go back to our old home. I light a cigarette and sit in the dark, listening to the creak of the floorboards, the sound of my own breathing. I think about Alyssa. Where she might be. If she ever thinks of me.
I hired a private investigator months ago. I told him to find her, to tell me if she was okay. If she was happy. He has come back three times with nothing.
"She's completely off-grid, Mr. Martins," he says. Every time.
Each time he says it, something inside me snaps. I have thrown bottles at the wall. Torn photographs. Smashed a lamp once. The silence she has built feels like a punishment I deserve. She has erased herself from the world I ruined. She found peace, and it hurts that I cannot.
Some nights, I scroll through her old photos online. The ones from film school. The ones from our wedding. I press my thumb against the screen as if I could reach through and touch her face. I start to dial her number, just to hear the ring. But I never let it go to voicemail. I just listen until the call ends on its own.
That sound,the ring stopping,feels like losing her all over again.
....
On the surface, everything looks perfect. Anya is seven months pregnant now. She posts maternity photos almost every day. She insists we attend every gala, every premiere. She tells me the world is watching. I tell her I do not care.
We still sleep in the same bed, but I spend most nights staring at the ceiling, counting the seconds until morning. Our conversations have become business meetings. She talks about nursery colors, baby names, interviews, and sponsorships. I talk about nothing at all.
I am tired of pretending. The drugs do not calm me anymore. They just quiet the noise for a few hours before it comes roaring back. When I look at myself in the mirror, I barely recognize the man staring back. My eyes look older. My face is drawn. My smile looks forced.
I do not see the man Alyssa once loved. I see a headline waiting to happen.
......
It is a Wednesday morning when Anya asks me to come with her to the ultrasound. She says she wants me there to hear our baby's heartbeat. I tell her I will come. She looks so hopeful that I almost believe we can still fix this.
The clinic is private. Discreet. She wears a flowing white dress, her makeup flawless as always. The paparazzi are outside, but her assistant handles them before we enter. Inside, the air is cold and sterile. I sit beside her on the examination bed, my hands clasped tightly together.
She talks about how the nursery is almost finished, how she wants to name the baby something "beautiful and timeless." I smile weakly, pretending to listen, but my heart is pounding.
When the doctor walks in, everything feels too bright. She spreads the gel over Anya's belly and moves the probe in slow circles. The screen flickers. I hold my breath, waiting for that familiar thump, that sound I have come to love more than anything else.
The doctor adjusts the probe again. Then again. The room grows quiet. Too quiet.
"Let's check again," she says softly.
Anya's smile falters. Her hand grips mine. I can feel her nails digging into my skin.
The monitor hums. Static fills the room. No sound. No rhythm.
"Sometimes it takes a moment," the doctor murmurs. But her eyes have already changed. She knows. We all know.
"Please," Anya whispers. "Please find it."
The doctor turns up the volume. Nothing.
The silence that follows feels like it stretches forever.
When she finally speaks, her voice is barely above a whisper. "Mr. Martins... I'm sorry. There's no heartbeat."
Anya screams. The sound rips through the room, sharp and raw. I can't move. I just stare at the screen. The world fades around me. All I see is the dark stillness where life should have been.
Something inside me collapses. The guilt, the drugs, the lies,all of it comes crashing down in one unbearable moment.
I loved that baby. I loved that tiny heartbeat. I loved the promise that maybe, somehow, I could make something good out of the wreckage I created.
Now there is nothing. Just silence. Just ghosts. Just me, sitting in another room where love used to live, watching it die all over again.
Nine
Letters To Race Faith Martins
Week One
The house feels too big. Too quiet. Too alive and too still all at once.
There's the faint hum of the baby monitor, the soft rhythmic sound of the refrigerator, the whisper of the city outside my windows that I never open. Every breath I take feels like I'm holding it for the both of us.
Race sleeps beside me in the bassinet, her chest rising and falling with fragile grace. I watch the tiny flutter of her eyelids, the little sighs she makes in her sleep. I check her breathing so often that it feels like an obsession, but I am terrified to stop. I am terrified to lose her before she's even had the chance to live.
I have not been outside since the hospital. The curtains stay closed. My hair is always tied up, and my face has forgotten makeup, perfume, and mirrors. I have not looked at myself properly in weeks, maybe months. I look at Race instead. She is what I want to remember.
Amy comes every morning with Emeric. She sits on the couch with her baby at her breast and makes jokes just loud enough to keep me from drifting too far into silence. Sometimes she talks about the most random things, like celebrity scandals or a recipe she ruined, and sometimes she says nothing at all. Her presence fills the space that used to echo with my fear.
Our mother moves like a quiet spirit through the apartment. She folds laundry, hums softly, makes soup that I rarely finish. She doesn't ask questions. She just stays. I think that's her way of saying she understands.
I sleep in short bursts, between feedings and cries, and when the house finally stills, I start to write.
Letter 1
Dear Race,
You are so small. Sometimes I am afraid to touch you. Your fingers curl around mine like you're trying to remind me that I'm still here. You breathe so softly that I press my ear to your chest just to make sure the world hasn't stopped. I don't know if I'm a good mother yet. I only know that I love you in a way that hurts. You've made silence feel safe again. You've given me something I didn't think I could ever find again,
peace.
---
Month One
By the end of the first month, I start to learn the rhythm of our new life. Mornings blur into afternoons, nights melt into dawns, but I begin to feel less like I'm drowning and more like I'm floating.
Every morning, I open the curtains for a few minutes. The light startles me. The city looks alive in a way I haven't felt in a long time. I sit in the rocking chair with Race, her tiny body pressed against me, and hum old songs I used to sing at Yale. My voice is softer now, but it still carries enough to make her eyes flutter.
Amy and I joke that Race and Emeric are practically twins. They sleep at the same time, cry at the same time, and manage to soil their diapers within the same minute. We laugh like we're kids again, even when we're both covered in baby spit and exhaustion.
One afternoon, Amy suggests we dress the babies in matching onesies. She takes a picture of us holding them and says, "We survived the first month. That deserves a trophy." I laugh, really laugh, for the first time in a long while.
That evening, I finally step onto the balcony. It's cold, but the air feels like a promise. I hold Race close, her tiny hat covering
most of her face, and I whisper to her about all the things I want us to do someday,walks in the park, birthdays, mornings that don't hurt.
Letter 2
Dear Race,
You sleep with your hand over your heart. I think you already know how to protect yourself. You and Emeric make Amy and me laugh until our eyes sting. The world still feels fragile, but it's starting to hum softly, like a song I used to know. You're the music in the background of every breath I take
.
---
Month Two
The first time you smiled, I cried so hard Amy thought something was wrong. But nothing was wrong. Everything, for a moment, was right. I held my phone, shaking, and captured that tiny burst of sunlight you gave me. I sent it to Mom, and she cried too.
I've started keeping a journal just for you. Every giggle, every nap, every expression you make. I don't want to forget any of it. I even write down the silly things Amy says when she visits, because someday you'll want to know how we laughed our way through exhaustion.
Amy is trying to heal. Some days she smiles, other days she goes quiet, lost in thought. I catch her staring at Emeric sometimes like she's both grateful and broken.
