She doesn't talk about Nikolai much, but I see him in the way she holds their son,like she's holding on to what's left of him. I help when she can't. She helps when I can't. We're learning to mother and mourn in the same breath.
Letter 3
Dear Race,
Today you smiled, and it felt like sunlight through rain. I thought of your father. He used to make me laugh when I didn't want to. I hope you inherit that from him-his light, not his shadows. I wish he could see your smile. It's the kind that makes the world stop for a second.
---
Month Three
I take you outside for the first time. Amy comes with me, pushing Emeric beside us. I wear a large hat and sunglasses, but my hands still tremble. I'm afraid someone will recognize me. I'm afraid someone will see you.
But no one does. It's quiet, safe, almost peaceful. You sleep the whole way, bundled in blankets, unaware of the courage it took for me to step back into the world. For the first time in months, I breathe without fear.
That night, I write my first song in a year. The melody is simple, inspired by your breathing. It's the sound of life beginning again.
Letter 4
Dear Race,
We went outside today. You slept through it, of course, but for me, it felt like stepping back into life. The city didn't feel cruel or loud. It just felt alive. You make me brave in quiet ways. Every small thing I do feels possible because of you
.
---
Month Four
I start singing again. It happens accidentally-one evening while rocking you to sleep. My voice cracks at first, but I keep going.
Amy hears me and stands in the doorway, smiling. "You sound like yourself again," she says softly.
That night, I realize she's right. Somewhere between sleepless nights and spilled milk, I've begun to feel like Alyssa again. Not the actress, not the wife, not the woman who fell apart-but the one who's still here.
Amy and I start talking about her moving back downstairs soon. She wants to reclaim her space, to rebuild something new. I can see the hesitation in her eyes,guilt, fear, the strange loyalty that comes with loss,but she's getting there.
Letter 5
Dear Race,
Your laugh is my favorite sound. It comes from deep inside you, unguarded and free. I used to forget what joy without reason felt like. You're teaching me again. Every time you laugh, something in me heals.
---
Month Five
Life starts to feel familiar again. There's structure, noise, the smell of coffee, and piles of laundry that never seem to end. You and Emeric are teething now, which means we sleep less and laugh more at our shared misery. Amy calls it "twin parenting," and it fits.
I've started writing again small notes, fragments of thoughts, tiny reflections about motherhood. I'm learning to find beauty in simplicity. The days are repetitive, but there's peace in that repetition. It feels like a heartbeat.
Letter 6
Dear Race,
You're five months old today. You grab at everything,my hair, spoons, even my heart. You've made this home feel alive again. I used to think love had to be loud to be real, but you've shown me how it can whisper. Every time you reach for me, the world makes sense again.
---
Month Six
Amy finally moves downstairs. The apartment suddenly feels too quiet, but not empty. Our parents return home too, and for the first time since your birth, it's just you and me again.
I thought I would fall apart, but I don't. The silence is different now. It's peaceful. It's sacred. I fill it with your giggles, your babbling, your toys scattered everywhere. I talk to you constantly. About the weather. About dreams. About your father. You blink as if you understand every word.
Letter 7
Dear Race,
It's just you and me now. I was afraid of this silence before, but it doesn't hurt anymore. I talk to you about everything, and you listen with your wide, serious eyes. I think you know more than you let on.
---
Months Seven to Ten
Time begins to move faster. You start crawling, babbling, discovering the world with fearless hands. You say "ma" one afternoon and I nearly drop my coffee. Amy screams from across the room, "She said it! She said it!" Emeric claps like he understands.
I start taking short acting classes online, just to feel that old fire again. At night, when you're asleep on my chest, I write songs about the way you breathe, the way you dream, the way I've found pieces of myself through you.
Amy visits every day. She's doing well, though I can see the ache in her eyes when she talks about Nikolai being gone. We don't talk about it often. We don't have to. Our silence understands what words can't.
Letters 8-10
Dear Race,
You're growing faster than I can write. Every day you surprise me with a new sound, a new look, a new part of yourself. You've started saying "ma," and it sounds like a promise. I don't know what the future will bring, but I know this: love doesn't vanish when life gets quiet. It only grows deeper
.
---
Month Eleven
You take your first steps today. Amy and I both burst into tears. Emeric follows five days later, and we laugh through our tears again. The house is filled with clapping, laughter, the sound of two mothers who survived heartbreak and found grace in their children.
I print the photos and tape them above my desk. Beneath them, my journal grows thicker with pages. I finish a full song tonight. It's the first complete thing I've written since before everything fell apart.
Letter 11
Dear Race,
You took your first steps today. You looked so proud, so sure, like you had conquered the world. Watching you, I realized that I've been learning to walk again too-not with my feet, but with my heart.
---
Month Twelve
The morning of your first birthday smells like vanilla and laughter. The apartment is glowing with balloons and flowers. Amy and I dress you and Emeric in matching white outfits. The sunlight through the windows makes everything golden.
Alec arrives with Daisy and their daughter Ophelia, who squeals when she sees you both. "My cousins!" she shouts, hugging you so tight that you both giggle.
For the first time in a year, the house is filled with real joy. No shadows. No pain. Just life. Just love.
When the laughter fades and the day grows quiet again, I take out my final letter.
Final Letter
Dear Race
,
You're one. I can't believe how much life can fit into a single year. You've grown teeth, taken steps, and filled every empty space inside me with light. Every scar I carry feels lighter when you smile.
If you ever read these letters someday, I want you to know that you saved me. Not in the way stories say love saves people, but in the quiet, ordinary way,a way that keeps a heart beating when it wants to stop. You are my miracle, Race. My beginning. My reason.
Happy birthday, my love.
Ten
Jordan
Waking up each morning feels like signing myself up for a war I never volunteered for. Life has been like this for as long as I can remember, a constant battle of surviving in a world that refuses to be kind. Since I took over my father's legacy, it has felt like I handed my soul straight to the devil. Technically, I did. That's what they say about Hollywood-you can't rise to the top without trading a piece of yourself. You learn to lie, manipulate, and sacrifice whatever innocence you once had.
Growing up here isn't something I would wish on anyone, especially a child. Parents should be safe havens, anchors in the storm, but mine were always performing, always in front of cameras, living their lives as if the world's attention mattered more than me. My father was the owner and CEO of Avalon Universal Group, a sprawling empire of music labels, film studios, and talent management. Everything he touched was about influence, power, and control. And now, somehow, I am at the helm of it all-the CEO, the owner. The man hated me. I
hated him too. We never understood each other. I was his only son, his firstborn, yet the person who mattered least.
My biological mother was just a simple hairstylist. She married my father hoping for love, for a family. But exposure to Hollywood changed her, and his constant cheating drove her into despair. Eventually, she couldn't take it anymore. She killed herself. I found her that day, fresh from school, my heart brimming with excitement because I had just made the soccer team. I wanted to tell her, wanted to see her smile. But there was only silence, only blood, only the void she left behind. That moment shattered me, and I was pulled into darkness far earlier than any child should ever know.
A year later, my father married my stepmother Reina, and they had Mabel. Two years after her birth, they divorced, and my father remained single until I left for college. During my senior year, I returned home one day to find a new woman in his house. He introduced her as his wife, Kaya. She was years younger than him, barely older than me. I was twenty-three. I didn't hate her. I pitied her. Another woman stepping into the path of a man who had already destroyed everything around him.
After college, I tried working for him, trying to prove myself, but I wasn't ready. I was too young, too naïve, and the world he lived in was darker than I could handle. Drugs and alcohol became my escape. They were my friends when family and life could not be. I don't remember a single day in college when I was truly sober, and after college, it only got worse. I was always late to work, partying, drinking, disappearing into nights I cannot fully recall. My father yelled at me, scolded me, tried to push me into responsibility, but I blamed him. He had taken away my childhood. We never saw eye to eye. Eventually, he fired me. I became a useless piece of shit living in his house, depending on
his money, doing nothing of value. He didn't care. He had Kaya. Life seemed fine to him, but for me, it was a spiral.
I started gambling-not for money, not really-but to escape. To feel in control of something in a life that was spinning out of control. I bet on hands, on numbers, on chances that felt as unfair as life itself. Winning gave me a rush, losing gave me a lesson, and the cycle consumed me. I spoke of it vaguely, in parables even to myself, because the truth hurt too much to say out loud. Gambling, drinking, running from everything-it became my way of surviving a world I hadn't chosen.
Three years ago, I hit rock bottom. I got out of rehab, and I decided to take responsibility. I wanted to prove I could survive without the crutches I had leaned on for too long. That's when my father handed the company to me. Not out of pride, not entirely out of trust, but necessity. He was dying. His prostate cancer had left him with a few years to live, and suddenly he needed someone to carry the empire forward. He told me I was ready. Deep down, I knew he wanted me to take over because he couldn't keep going himself. The man who had been my tormentor, my teacher, my nightmare, was now relying on me.
Now I run Avalon Universal, directing Broadway shows, occasionally stepping onto the stage, and managing the empire he built. Mabel is the only family left that matters. She is a little whirlwind, a teenage princess with tantrums and attitude, but she is mine, the only one left I can call family. There is also my son, hidden from the world, a secret no one can touch. He is the anchor I keep safe, a piece of normalcy in a life that has rarely known it.
I drag myself into the bathroom, the space so pristine and expensive it could be in a magazine. I stare at my reflection, seeing a man thirty years old but exhausted beyond measure, a man who looks like he has been dragged through decades of
mistakes and loss in just a few years. I shave, brush my teeth, trace my jawline, trying to recognize the face staring back at me. This is me, I remind myself. All of it-past, present, mistakes, victories, failures-it has made me who I am.
Suit on, I walk downstairs. "Good morning, Jordan. Breakfast is ready," Jylia, my nanny since I was nine, says with her familiar cheer.
"Thank you, Jylia, but I'll have just my coffee. I have a meeting at nine and I am already late," I tell her, forcing a small smile.
"Jordan! What did I tell you about missing breakfast? At least have the pancakes," she scolds, wagging a finger at me.
"Jylia, I truly wish I could stay, but I promise not to miss dinner," I say, kissing her forehead before walking out.
Outside, my phone buzzes. I glance at the caller ID and immediately hang up when I see Kaya's name. I am not in the mood for her nonsense today.
In the car, scrolling through social media, I see news on Isaiah's and Anya's pages: Miss Johnson and Mr Martin wish to Inform their fans a public about the death of the baby.
Under the post read;
It is with a heavy heart that we announce the death of a baby girl. Yes it was a girl and we found out on the day she died. Anya and I went for our usual antenatal check up and upon checking the babies heart wasn't heard. The next day Anya was scheduled for an induced labor where she delivered our still born baby girl.
We hope you give us the privacy and support we need at this emotional time of life.
Thank you,
Love Zay
My chest tightens. My mind immediately goes to Alyssa.
Fuck. She was the most beautiful woman I had ever seen. I remember the first time I saw her at the studio. It was instant. A connection that made my chest constrict and my mind spin. The universe was cruel, I thought, punishing me by letting me meet the most perfect woman, only for her to belong to someone else. Zay Martins, that arrogant, untouchable bastard. He didn't deserve her. I didn't deserve her either, but there was something about her that made me want to try anyway.
I remember the day she confided in me, humiliated and vulnerable. I comforted her, feeling a pull I had never felt for anyone. She was meant to be mine. And yet, I hesitate. Could she accept me if she knew my darkest truths? My past? That I had been a nightmare for another woman, reckless, selfish, dangerous in ways she had escaped once before? Could she accept that version of me?
She is in New York now. I cannot rush her. I need to give her time, space. But I know fate has a way of nudging people back together. I have a project on Broadway. Soon, very soon, I will see her again. And this time, I will not let her slip away.
Eleven
For Race and Emeric's first birthday, my sister and I decided to go all in. Even though they are only five days apart and Race probably should not have been born within the same month, we decided it would be easiest to celebrate them on the same day, the weekend of their birthday week. On their actual birthdays, we had done little private celebrations, just the two of us with our babies. Today, though, was going to be something entirely different.
I finally got to buy things with my card without fear. I am back to being a Turner. The freedom to shop, to plan, to celebrate-it was intoxicating. Even better, I was so glad to have my dad and Alec with me, their presence grounding me, reminding me that I was no longer a Martins, that my identity and my family were firmly mine again.
For her birthday last Wednesday, I had bought Race a dollhouse. I knew she would not yet understand how to play with it, but I wanted to buy her anything and everything. My heart swelled at the thought of spoiling her a little. Amy had taken Emeric out to Disney World on his actual birthday. I
had wanted to go, of course, but the logistics of explaining two babies out in public was more than I could manage. Besides, it gave Amy and Emeric their own private memory to treasure.
Race, though, was changing so quickly. Each day, she took on more of Isaiah's features, and it made my chest ache. His eyes alone were not enough to mark him in her, now she carried his nose, the curve of his lips. I fought the wave of sadness that hit me every time I looked at her, every time I noticed her hands fidgeting the way his had. The last thing I could allow was anyone seeing her before I was ready to reveal her identity to the world.
So today, we were celebrating them together. Mom, Dad, Alec, Daisy, and Ophelia had arrived yesterday to help with preparations. It was going to be a small gathering. Only us, the babies, and some children of our security personnel-no strangers, no extra guests. Just family and a handful of little friends.
I woke up this morning with Race pressed close against my chest. I would never get tired of this feeling. Some people may joke about having a Velcro baby, but I am a Velcro mom. Race had never wanted to sleep alone, and I could not bring myself to put her back in her crib during the middle of the night. She whined softly in her sleep, her tiny hands reaching for me, sighing when she found my chest again.
I decided to linger in bed for a while, just the two of us. We cuddled for what felt like another hour, and I reminisced about the past months,the fear of losing Race, the nights spent staring at her tiny fingers, praying she would make it through. I allowed myself the fleeting thought of what might have been if the other twin had survived.
Would she have been another girl? How different would our lives be? But dwelling on the past had never helped me, and it certainly would not help me now. My future was Race and reclaiming the life I had put on hold, including my acting career.
The past thirteen years did not exist in this household. Isaiah's name was never spoken.
My sister had taught me to move on, and I had, firmly. Now seeing him online did not affect me anymore. I did not follow him, I blocked every account that might show me updates, and the not interested button on TikTok was a lifeline.
Occasionally, I admitted to myself, I would sneak a peek at him. Curiosity, obsession, habit,it did not matter. The heart has a mind of its own.
I also heard about their tragedy even tho I didn't want to. It was all over the Internet.
Am I bad person for not caring??
I had also returned to the internet world, cautiously. After questions about my disappearance and rampant conspiracy theories, I posted something playful, a little bit shady toward him and his girlfriend, subtle enough to keep it from being obvious.
I stood in front of the mirror in a two-piece swimsuit, taking the picture, and captioned it: "Some people chase the perfect life while others actually live it. But hey, you do you, I'll do me."
The comment section flooded immediately with haters and supporters, and I could not help but laugh at how sharp people were, reading between lines I hadn't even tried to spell out.
Amy had encouraged me that day. We were having a little pool day with the kids, and she wanted me to reclaim a small piece
of myself, to remind me that I was still Alyssa Turner, not just someone's shadow.
But in reality, I only posted about me, never about the kids. Amy and I were united in our vow never to expose Race or Emeric to the greedy eyes of the media.
Back to reality, I looked down at Race, wide awake and flashing me a toothy grin.
"Ma... mama," she cooed. I picked her up, pressed her soft little body to my chest, and kissed her face a hundred times over.
"Happy birthday, darling. The past year with you has been a blessing, and I am blessed to be your mom," I whispered, my voice catching with emotion.
From downstairs came a symphony of noise: voices, the blender, the clatter of breakfast preparations.
"Seems like everyone is awake. Time to go," I said softly, though my heart wanted to linger in this moment just a little longer.
I carried Race to the nursery and sat her on the floor with her binky while I went through my morning routine. Afterwards, we headed into the walk-in closet where I changed into joggers and a basic backless top. My hair was piled into a messy bun, concealer lightly dabbed to hide dark circles, and a little lip gloss to bring life back to my face. I finished dressing Race in a tiny outfit I had picked out just for the occasion, making sure she looked adorable without being uncomfortable.
When we headed downstairs, the smell of breakfast hit me immediately. My family was gathered in the kitchen. Amy always came upstairs for breakfast with Emeric since cooking was not her strongest skill, and Emeric was sprawled in his high chair, eggs smeared everywhere. But as soon as he heard me, he froze, looked up, and his face broke into the most delighted
expression. He started clapping and bouncing in his chair when he saw Race. Race, in turn, squealed and reached for him, and I felt a wave of warmth watching their interaction.
"Aunt Lyssa, can I play with Race and Emeric before the party? I want to paint their nails," Ophelia's voice rang out, giggling with excitement.
"Okay, but fake polish only. They are too young for the real thing," I replied, smiling. Ophelia nodded eagerly.
Mom handed me a glass of bloom mix and my breakfast. We settled at the table, and for a while, we just watched the children. Emeric grabbed Race's tiny hands and tried to make her clap. Race squealed with laughter, the sound filling the room. Alec teased them, making silly faces, while Daisy helped Ophelia apply soft fake polish. My father chuckled, teasing us all about how fast they were growing.
Once the kids were distracted, I turned to my mom. "Now that Race is one, I feel like it's time. I am ready to get back to my career. Broadway is what I want, Mom. I have already started applying for auditions. I want to do shows, nothing else."
Her eyes lit up, her hand reaching across the table to squeeze mine. "That is wonderful, Alyssa. I know a few people who could make things move faster for you. If you want, I can contact them on your behalf."
The weight of her support settled warmly on my chest. "You would do that?" I asked, my voice soft.
"Of course. You've worked too hard to wait any longer." She smiled, and I felt a little surge of courage. Maybe this was the moment I could finally reclaim my dreams.
It was time to turn attention to the celebration. The living room had been transformed into a cheerful, pastel wonderland, with
balloons, streamers, and banners proclaiming "Happy First Birthday Race and Emeric." Two cakes sat on the table: Race's pink and gold unicorn theme, Emeric's blue and green dinosaur. My heart ached with love and pride as the children crawled toward them, eyes wide.
I lifted Race onto my lap and she reached for the unicorn cake with tiny fingers. Emeric mirrored her, fascinated by the colors and decorations. They giggled at each other, racing to tap the frosting. Ophelia and Daisy helped guide them to sit, and Alec was teasing them relentlessly, making both babies squeal with delight.
At one point, Race leaned into me, resting her head against my chest, and I whispered, "You are my everything, little one. Never forget how loved you are." Her tiny hand squeezed mine, and I felt like I could dissolve into pure happiness.
Amy sat nearby with Emeric, helping him grasp the fork. "Can you say dada?" she encouraged softly, the hope in her voice making me ache a little for what Nikolai would never see. And then, in the quietest, most perfect moment, he did.
"Dada," Emeric said, his little face breaking into a huge smile. Amy gasped, tears brimming in her eyes. The sound carried weight, love, and memory all at once. Everyone hushed, letting the moment linger. Alec wiped a tear from his eye, and even Daisy looked choked up, clutching Ophelia's hand.
Race, oblivious to the gravity of the moment, reached for Emeric, their hands clasping, and they both erupted into giggles again. My heart swelled until it threatened to burst.
The rest of the morning passed in a blur of laughter, food smears, and tiny clumsy hands digging into cake. Race and Emeric shared toys, chased each other around the living room, and even attempted to crawl together, giggling with pure
delight. Alec kept sneaking cupcakes to them when no one was looking, while my father lifted Race up into the air, making her squeal. Daisy and Ophelia danced around with ribbons, twirling the children in games of make-believe.
Finally, when the sugar crash began to hit, we settled everyone down. Race nestled against me, exhausted but glowing, and Emeric fell asleep in Amy's arms, his head resting on her shoulder. I looked around at my family, at the mess, at the chaos, and I felt a kind of perfect contentment.
My mother leaned close, whispering, "Your Broadway career will be amazing, Alyssa. You've got everything it takes, just like with your parenting. You do everything with your heart, and that will take you far."
I nodded, swallowing a lump in my throat. "I hope so, Mom. I really hope so."
And for that moment, watching my baby sleep, laughing with my family, feeling their warmth and love surrounding me, I allowed myself to believe it would all work out. That I could have my daughter and my dreams. That I could finally live the life I had always wanted, and still keep the little hands that held my heart.
Twelve
I sighed as I watched Race stomp her tiny feet on the living room rug, crossing her arms and pouting like a little diva. One year and three months old, and already she knew exactly how to push my buttons. Amy had flown back to Los Angeles with Emeric for work a few days ago, which left me juggling the impossible task of leaving Race with a babysitter while I went to my Broadway audition. And of course, the little stinker was having none of it.
“Come on, sweetie,” I said, crouching down to her level. “I’ll be back really soon. I promise. You just stay with Miss Clara, okay?”
Race shook her head violently, hair bouncing. “No!” she squealed, and then threw herself onto the rug dramatically, the kind of tantrum that could rival the most seasoned actor.
I ran a hand over my face, suppressing a laugh and a groan at the same time. “I know, I know. You don’t want to stay, but you’re going to be fine. Miss Clara is really nice, and she’s going to play with you while I’m gone.”
Miss Clara, the babysitter, gave me a polite, patient smile from across the room. “It’s fine, Ms. Alyssa. She’ll settle once she sees I’m here. And don’t worry, I’ve signed the NDA, so your little one’s world stays just between us.”
I nodded, feeling a mix of relief and guilt. I hated leaving her. But this was my moment, the first step back into a world I had postponed for years. “Thank you, Clara. Really. You’re a lifesaver.”
Race crawled toward me, clinging to my leg as I bent down to pick her up. Her tiny hands pressed against my cheek, and I leaned into her warmth. “I’ll be back soon, my love. Just a little bit, okay? Mommy has work today, but I’ll come right back.”
She rested her head against my shoulder, quiet for a moment, and I could see the understanding flicker in her eyes. She was still learning the words, but sometimes, the little ones understood more than we gave them credit for. I pressed a soft kiss to her hair. “Be good, okay? Mommy loves you so much.”
Finally, with a deep breath, I handed her over to Miss Clara. Race clung for a brief second longer, then, distracted by a toy, she finally let go. I exhaled sharply and straightened, mentally preparing myself for the day ahead.
I slid into my car, feeling a rush of nervous energy. The streets felt lighter than I expected—no swarming paparazzi at every corner like a few months ago. Of course, I wasn’t completely invisible. A few cameras here and there caught me as I drove, and I knew the news feeds were quick to follow every move, especially with Isaiah trending again. Allegedly hooking up with someone new, his name circulating in the tabloids. But as always, his PR team—Tasha and Julian, faster than the devil himself—debunked everything by the next day with staged pictures of him and Anya looking like the power couple in every
Star font imaginable. I rolled my eyes, half exasperated, half amused. Let them have their headlines. I had bigger things to worry about.
The Broadway theater loomed ahead, and I parked, taking a moment to inhale deeply. I straightened my top, tugged at my joggers, and brushed a strand of hair out of my face. This was it. The audition my mom had helped set up through her connections, the one chance to step back into the spotlight without hesitation.
I entered the theater, greeted by the faint smell of wood, paint, and a lingering trace of stage lights. A man approached, clipboard in hand, introducing himself with a warm, professional smile. “You must be Alyssa,” he said. “I’m Sammy, the director. It’s a pleasure to finally meet you.”
I smiled, nerves bubbling beneath the surface but controlled. “Thank you, Mr. Sammy. It’s great to meet you too.”
He gestured toward a small sitting area. “Please, make yourself comfortable. I’ve heard wonderful things from your mother. She’s very excited about your audition, and so am I. I can’t promise you anything huge, but I look forward to seeing your talent in action.”
I nodded, appreciation and anxiety mingling in my chest. “I understand, and I appreciate the opportunity.”
Sammy’s easy demeanor helped calm some of my jitters, and we chatted briefly—small talk about the theater, the upcoming musical, and my recent return to acting. Then he led me to a side room to meet the producer team. My stomach dropped the second I stepped in and saw him.
Jordan Campbell.
I froze for half a second, just long enough to make him raise an eyebrow and smile politely. The man who had accidentally bumped into me twice, the stranger who had witnessed my marriage unravel, the one who had held me through the aftermath of betrayal and heartbreak, was now in front of me. Sitting on the producer’s panel, all business and calm charm.
Fuck my life.
Thirteen
Jordan stood just inside the door, leaning casually against the frame, one hand in his pocket. Even from a distance, my stomach knotted at the sight of him.
He wasn't smiling broadly, but there was a warmth in his eyes, that familiar recognition that made my chest tighten. "Alyssa," he said softly, holding out his hand. "It's... really nice to see you again."
I swallowed hard, trying to steady my voice, trying to make it sound professional and calm, though my heart was hammering.
"Jordan. Nice to see you too," I said, forcing a smile that felt fragile.
He returned it with a small, encouraging grin, the kind that seemed to reach into my chest and calm the tight coils of anxiety there. I felt a strange mixture of comfort and nerves, memories of him comforting me, holding me as I'd fallen apart, washing over me unexpectedly. For a moment, the audition room, the scripts, and the other actors faded. It was just him. Just Jordan.
"Hey... um, I mean, I didn't expect to see you here," I admitted, glancing at him nervously.
He shrugged casually, but there was a light in his eyes that told me he wasn't joking. "I work here, you know. And it seems fate has a funny way of putting us in the same place again."
I laughed, a short, nervous burst. "Yeah. Fate, huh? More like bad luck sometimes," I muttered, feeling the blush creeping up my neck.
He chuckled softly. "I don't think so. You're stronger than you think, Alyssa. You've been through a lot, but you're here, auditioning. That counts for something."
I tried to hold his gaze, trying to focus on the present, on the fact that I was supposed to be auditioning. "Thanks... it's just... nerves, I guess."
Jordan nodded knowingly. "Of course. But nerves aren't bad. They mean you care. Just let that energy flow through you, not against you."
I took a shaky breath and nodded, finally letting go of some of the tension. Sammy, the director, waved me over. "Alright, everyone. Let's get started. Alyssa, you'll be reading for Sophie, the quirky best friend. Keep it light, funny, and real. Let's see some personality."
I adjusted my script, my hands trembling slightly as I walked to the center of the small audition space. The other actors were already there, some humming tunes, some whispering lines to themselves. My stomach twisted. I could feel every eye-or so I imagined-on me.
Jordan gave me a subtle nod, almost imperceptible, but it was enough to steady me. I let my breath slow and reminded myself why I was here. I could do this. I had done hard things before. This was just one more.
"Okay," I murmured under my breath, practicing my lines quietly before the music cue. "Honestly, I don't see why you're so nervous. It's just a party, not a presidential debate." I said the words aloud this time, letting the rhythm fall naturally, as if I were speaking to a real person across from me.
I straightened my shoulders and added the next line: "I'm not nervous! I just... I just don't want to make a fool of myself." My voice wavered slightly, but I held my chin up, letting Sophie's quirkiness flow through me.
"You'll be fine. You always are," I added with a laugh, letting my hands gesture toward the empty space as if the imaginary scene partner could see me.
The music started, a soft jazz-infused number, and I let the movement carry me. I tapped my foot to the rhythm, spun lightly, and delivered: "Come on! Life is too short to sit in the corner and sulk. We're here to have fun!" I flung my arms wide, letting the lines sound natural, infusing humor where it belonged.
I could feel the eyes of the producers on me-Jordan in particular. I caught a glimpse of him, leaning slightly forward, eyebrows raised ever so slightly. It wasn't criticism; it was attentiveness, focus, and perhaps... encouragement. My heart
jumped in my chest, and I forced myself to keep moving, keep speaking, keep being Sophie.
"Honestly, if you don't loosen up, you'll miss everything that makes life... life!" I added, feeling the energy rise with the rhythm of the music. I twirled, I leaned into the beat, and I smiled at the imagined audience, hoping the humor and lightness of Sophie came through.
When the song ended, I paused for a heartbeat, chest heaving, script still in hand, and looked at Jordan. He gave me a small, approving nod, and I felt a flutter of relief and something deeper, something I hadn't anticipated.
After everyone finished, Sammy and the producers huddled briefly, murmuring among themselves. My stomach twisted into tight knots, anticipation and anxiety blending into an almost unbearable cocktail. Finally, Sammy turned toward me.
"Alyssa," he said, his tone warm, professional, yet encouraging, "we've decided on the roles. You'll be playing Sophie, the quirky best friend to the lead character. You'll have some fun musical numbers, and your presence will be key to the story's lighter, humorous moments."
Relief washed over me like a wave, but I forced myself to maintain composure. I smiled politely, nodding. "Thank you. I won't disappoint."
Jordan stepped forward immediately, as if drawn to me naturally. He held out his phone, and my chest tightened again. "Can I get your number? I'd like to discuss some scheduling notes and maybe keep in touch about other opportunities."
I hesitated for the briefest fraction of a second, that familiar caution rising, but it melted under the weight of his sincerity. "Alright," I said, handing over my number.
His smile was wide now, the kind that made your chest ache because it felt so familiar and grounding. "Perfect. You did amazing today. I can't wait to see more of you on stage."
I exhaled, a small, relieved laugh escaping me. "Thank you, Jordan. Really."
He leaned casually against a table nearby, hands tucked into his pockets, giving me that calm energy that I could never quite put into words. "You're going to do great things, Alyssa. I know it."
Driving home, my mind refused to slow down. Every movement, every glance, every word replayed in my head. Jordan's presence, the brief touch of his hand, the way his eyes seemed to recognize not just the performer but the person behind her,it was dizzying.
My phone buzzed mid-drive. I pulled it over just long enough to read the message from Jordan: You did great today. Can't wait to see more of you.
I smiled, warmth flooding me, not just from the audition, but from him.
And as I imagined Race at home, blissfully unaware, waiting for me to walk through the door and scoop her up into my arms, a spark of hope lit inside me. I could see a future unfolding a future where I was not just surviving but thriving, where my life and career, my stage and my home, could coexist. For the first time in a long while, I felt ready to embrace it fully, and the possibility of joy didn't terrify me anymore.
Fourteen
The past few weeks had been relentless. Lines, rehearsals, memorization, pacing,every hour of my day had been consumed by the theater. Exhaustion and exhilaration collided inside me in a way that was both exhausting and addictive. Every day I left feeling drained yet impossibly alive, clinging to the small victories of a perfectly delivered line, a well-timed gesture, or a laugh that came exactly where it should.
I had to keep my life outside the theater orderly, structured, because outside those walls, there was Ray, my daughter, my secret heartbeat, the part of my world no one else could touch. She demanded a rhythm all her own, one that often conflicted with the rigid schedules and expectations of my rehearsals.
Mornings at home were a delicate balancing act. I would sit cross-legged on the living room rug with her bouncing near me, her tiny fingers reaching for my scripts with more curiosity than comprehension. "Read, Mama," she would demand, eyes wide
and bright, the kind of serious expression you'd expect from a tiny director commanding a room.
Her words were messy and imperfect, but they were also wonderful. "No dooo!" she'd exclaim, her pronunciation mangled, mimicking my dramatic pauses and exaggerations as best she could.
I would laugh, scooping her into my lap. "Okay, baby girl. You tell me how, then." I let her tap at the pages with her crayon, pat the script with her chubby hands, or even attempt to turn the pages herself. "Good job, Ray," I whispered into her hair, feeling the warmth of her trust curl through me like sunlight. She would snuggle against me, arms tight around my neck, and I could feel her little heartbeat against mine. "You're the best audience in the world," I said quietly, holding her closer, marveling at the way she could make the exhaustion of my day melt in a heartbeat.
Evenings were filled with small, domestic rituals. Emeric would play with Ray nearby, giggling and babbling at each other's nonsense, or quietly stacking blocks with solemn concentration. Amy watched them both, her calm presence filling the room with warmth. Motherhood had indeed made her softer. But don't let that deceive you. There was a strength beneath her gentleness, a careful composure that could surprise anyone who thought they could push her.
Ray would sometimes point at me, her tiny hands jabbing the air as she declared, "Mama work! Lines!" She clapped her hands when I exaggerated a scene, her baby enthusiasm fueling something deep inside me. Occasionally, she tried to imitate my words: "No... Mama bad!" I would laugh, tousling her soft hair, the sound of her giggle lifting me in a way no applause ever could. She didn't understand the theater, the stakes of my
professional life, or the weight of what it meant to perform. But she was my anchor, my tiny, shining light.
Back in the theater, rehearsals in front of everyone were nerve-wracking, especially when Jordan was present. His calm, steady gaze could be both comforting and suffocating, a reminder of how carefully I had to move through the world. Every fumble of a line felt magnified under the scrutiny of expectant eyes. Every whisper behind my back made me hyper-aware of how easily a single misstep could spiral into a headline.
Jordan never failed to attempt calming me. Lately, he seemed intent on making conversation whenever he could. Sometimes it made me uneasy, aware of the eyes that lingered on us, the soft whispers from cast members. I was already a public figure, a woman under a microscope ever since being Isaiah's wife, now ex-wife. My every movement could be magnified, twisted, dissected. Any slip could become news, any innocuous gesture fodder for gossip.
It was difficult enough keeping Ray a secret from the world when the media wasn't constantly hovering. I couldn't afford another headline, couldn't risk anyone learning the one truth I would protect with every breath: my daughter's existence. That was why I sometimes felt a flicker of irritation when Jordan sat close to me during rehearsal or offered to walk me to my car. I didn't want the media seeing something that didn't exist.
I enjoyed his presence. His words were easy, grounding. But I was not ready for the world to know me in that context. Not yet.
If Isaiah had been famous, Jordan was a whole echelon above. A permanent A-lister, an Oscar-winning producer, with influence that ran through half of Hollywood. Yes, I had stalked him on Instagram, glanced into his world, the way anyone curious about such a figure 0might.
But I had never gone deep. My life wasn't Hollywood anymore. I just wanted my space and my career, my dignity intact.
Offering friendship, companionship, or anything close to that, was not on my list. I was done with the world that had nearly consumed me. Jordan was Hollywood personified, and I wanted none of it.
Back to reality, today was the show. According to Sammy, the theater was packed. He had told us a week ago that tickets had sold out in less than seven days after the poster's release, and the buzz had been nonstop online. My face on the poster had sent the Internet into a frenzy, debates and excitement alike. Praise flooded in, the occasional negative comment like a small ripple in a tidal wave of support.
I slipped into my costume in the dressing room, smoothing the fabric, adjusting my hair, checking the subtle details. Jordan had stopped by earlier, quiet and steady, offering a presence that grounded me even as nerves threatened to coil in my chest. He did not know about Ray, and he never could. The relief that brought me was intertwined with an unease I couldn't shake.
"Break a leg," he whispered, brushing a stray strand of hair behind my ear. I forced a smile, hiding the flutter of emotions that his nearness always stirred. "Thanks," I said, though my chest tightened with anticipation and anxiety.
The curtain rose, and the world of the theater consumed me. Every line, gesture, and pause carried the weight of weeks of preparation. Each moment was a fragile thread, a connection between me and the audience, between my fear and my triumph. I caught Jordan's gaze in the crowd, unwavering, proud, and it ignited a warmth inside me that I couldn't quite name, a pulse quickening in ways I hadn't anticipated.
By the final scene, my throat was raw, my muscles trembling, my body buzzing from the adrenaline and relief. The standing ovation hit me like a wave, leaving me breathless, my eyes stinging with unshed tears. Voices called my name from every angle.
Someone even threw a bouquet of roses my way.
I couldn't hold back anymore. I let the tears fall, small and quiet, in the safety of the backstage shadows. For a moment, I allowed myself to believe all the hard work, the pain, and the sacrifices had culminated in this perfect, fleeting triumph.
This is the life
Fifteen
Backstage, I went through the familiar ritual of getting ready to leave. My phone buzzed with messages from Mom, Dad, and Alec, all overflowing with pride, warmth, and encouragement. I had just finished a call with Amy, who told me that Ray hadn't stopped asking for me since the moment I left the apartment that morning.
I was packing my things when I felt a presence at my side.
I turned sharply to see Jordan, hands in his pockets, a wide, confident smile lighting up his face.
"You really did wow me, Alyssa. You had potential. Such a shame you turned down the role in that fantasy movie," he said casually, but there was an edge of sincerity that made my chest tighten.
"No... wait. What?" My voice caught, confusion and disbelief tangling together.
"Yeah. I was really surprised when Coleman told me you turned it down," he repeated, calm and steady.
"What? That's a mistake. I didn't turn down any role. Coleman dropped me for no reason at all," I said, letting the truth spill out.
"Figured. Well, we had a call from your agent, saying you were too busy to work with us. Now I understand everything," he said.
I blinked at him. Agent? I didn't even have one anymore. That had been Isaiah's doing, long before our marriage ended.
"Isaiah! Fucking asshole!" I hissed under my breath, anger flaring hotter than the adrenaline still coursing through me from the stage.
"Finally. I thought I'd have to destroy your marriage myself to tell you, but it seems he made it easier. Good thing you divorced him, Lys," he said softly, stepping closer.
"No. Don't call me Lys," I snapped. The word cut through the tension like a knife. He stepped back, visibly hurt. That name was for Isaiah alone.
"Sorry... didn't mean to... I'll go," I murmured, grabbing my purse. Rage still throbbed beneath my skin. How could he sabotage another opportunity in my career?
"Wait, sorry for upsetting you. I didn't come here for that. We're going to celebrate. Everything's on me. Come on," he said, smiling as though I hadn't just been harsh.
"Oh... that's really kind, but I can't. I'm babysitting Amy's son tonight," I lied, thinking of Ray and the little chaos waiting for me at home.
"Oh, it won't be long," he replied softly, coaxing me.
"I truly wish I could come, but I can't. I promised her. Maybe next time?" I insisted.
His blue eyes held mine, locking me in place for what felt like an eternity. A flicker of disappointment passed over his face, but he didn't push. I felt the pull, the temptation to let him in, to let this moment stretch longer-but my priorities were clear: my daughter, my family, my home.
"Okay, enjoy your evening," he said finally. Sammy appeared at that exact moment, his excitement palpable.
"Alyssa! You were incredible tonight. I hope to see you in my other projects. Welcome to Broadway, Alyssa Turner!" He kissed my cheek and moved on, leaving me smiling long after his retreating figure.
I turned back to Jordan. "Guess I'll be seeing more of you. You did excellent, Alyssa Turner. Goodnight," he said, stepping closer as if weighing some unspoken thought.
Then he leaned down. My breath hitched, eyes closing instinctively, unprepared for the contact. His lips brushed my cheek in a fleeting kiss. The warmth, the scent, the shock-it all hit me in a dizzying rush.
And just like that, he was gone. I watched his back, touching my cheek, the moment lingering in a way that left my chest aching long after he had disappeared.
The city was quiet as I drove home, the hum of tires on asphalt blending with the soft glow of streetlights reflecting off the wet pavement. Even the occasional distant siren felt muffled tonight, as if the world itself had paused to give me this one small hour of peace. My chest still thrummed from the performance. Every step on that stage, every line delivered, every applause, had left me exhilarated and exhausted all at once.
When I unlocked the apartment and stepped inside, the familiar scent of home hit me-vanilla from the candle Amy always kept lit in the living room, a faint whiff of laundry detergent, and the soft tang of baby lotion. Ray was still awake, curled up in the middle of the living room with her little stuffed bunny tucked under her arm. Her wide, bright eyes locked onto me the instant she saw me.
"Mama!" she squealed, her voice cracking with joy. She lurched forward, taking unsteady steps. I dropped my bag and swept her into my arms, burying my face in her soft, fragrant hair. "Mama's home, baby girl," I whispered, pressing my lips to the top of her head. "The show's over."
Her giggles vibrated against me as she wrapped her tiny arms around my neck, clinging like I was the only anchor she had in the world. Her curls tickled my cheek, warm and alive. "I... I missed you, Mama!" she said between bursts of laughter. Her words were messy and slurred, the way they always were after a long day of running, exploring, and discovering, but they carried more love than any perfectly enunciated sentence could.
"We need to celebrate, sister. You've got the internet buzzing! Look, even your following is going up by the second, and people are in the comments section bashing him and comparing you with them. God, I live for this kind of drama," Amy said
happily, emerging from the kitchen, holding Emeric on her hip, supporting him with one hand and holding her phone with the other.
"No, Amy. You know I don't want that. I don't like comparisons. I want to be me. I want to bask in my glory alone," I smirked.
Oh my goodness, my sister is rubbing off on me.
"Ahh, I see what you did there. You almost got me," she laughed, kissing Emeric's cheeks as he tugged at her hair.
"Mama... sparkles," Ray said, squeezing my cheeks. I kissed her cute little nose, and she smiled before snuggling into my chest.
"Why didn't you go out to celebrate? Jordan said the crew would be going out," Amy asked, heading to the couch to sit down.
I joined her with a tired sigh and plopped Ray on my lap. She laid her head on my chest, giving a soft, sleepy yawn. My baby girl was tired.
"I still can't wrap my head around the fact that you two are friends. And it's already weird enough that he tries to make conversation with me whenever we're in the same room. I don't want unnecessary gossip," I groaned.
"It's just a celebration, Alyssa. You might have been the main cast, but you were the star of the show. Plus, Jordan is a really chill guy who's been through a lot. It's time to move on. You're still young and pretty-you'll be 34 next month-you need to be out there," she said.
"But not with another Hollywood person. So done with them, and men in general. I just want to focus on my career and raising Race out of the spotlight."
She sighed and shook her head before muttering something under her breath.
"Speaking of Race, have you thought about contacting him? She is growing and will start asking questions. You can't keep her in this apartment forever."
I groaned. I'd been dodging these questions for months now.
"Can't I keep her hidden forever? He doesn't deserve to know her. I don't want her exploited by the media. And if I know his PR team, they'll want to use my daughter to clean up his image," I replied.
"We'll draw up a contract. He can take you to court and fight over custody if you don't tell him now," she said, standing up.
"I still need time to get everything together."
"Okay, but you need to hurry. Mom called me-she said she's already found a manager and a talent agency ready to sign you, only if you're ready," she added, walking toward the door.
"Tell Mom to relax. Also... did you know it was him who sabotaged my audition a year ago?" I suddenly remembered.
"Yeah. Jordan told me," she replied casually.
"What? Why... why didn't you tell me?" I asked, perplexed.
"Because what would you have done? You were so deep in love with him, so into the marriage that you couldn't see what was in front of your eyes. You would have just forgiven him with one sorry-ass apology and makeup sex. So it was good you found out after the divorce," she said. I instantly went quiet.
She was right.
"I gotta go. Emy's getting fussy, and I need to put him to sleep. Aren't you sleepy, bubby?" she cooed. He smacked her in return.
I laughed and got up, still carrying my wide-awake Ray.
Sixteen
Now, back in the stillness of our home, the quiet was comforting but fragile. I had a small bedtime ritual with Ray, brushing her curls, adjusting her pajamas, humming a soft lullaby, and tucking her into her little bed. She clutched her stuffed bunny tightly, murmuring little sounds as she settled in. I lingered for a moment, watching her chest rise and fall, the tiny weight of her trust pressing softly against me.
Just as I turned to leave her room, the doorbell rang. My chest tightened. Visitors at this hour were rare. Amy had a spare key and she just left, so she can't be back right now. Plus our security system and team are the best money could afford, so this is really odd. My curiosity, mixed with a flicker of unease, made me pause.
"Stay here, Ray," I whispered, handing her the stuffed bunny. "Be quiet. Mama will be right back." She clutched it tightly, murmuring a soft "okie" to herself, the innocence of her trust making my chest ache.
I opened the door.
Jordan.
What the fuck! How did he get in here? How did he get past my security because only my parents and brother plus his wife and daughter are allowed up into this building.
He stood casually, hands in his pockets, leaning slightly against the frame as if this were an ordinary visit. His gaze swept over me, unreadable, his smile small but careful. Something in his posture betrayed a flicker of uncertainty, like he didn't quite know what to expect.
My mind raced. He had no idea. Absolutely no idea.
"What are you doing here? How did you get past my security? What are you here period!" I asked with a stern voice and a glare.
"I..I didn't,sorry. You left this there and I thought maybe you might need it" he said raising my purse. He voice sounds a bit hurt but I don't care.
This is weird and an invasion of privacy.
Fuck,I thought I placed it inside my bag.
Before I could form words, a tiny voice rang out.
"Mama! Mama!"
Ray had wriggled free from her bed, toddling downstairs and toward the door, hair tousled, cheeks flushed, clutching her bunny as if it were her anchor in this strange, sudden moment. She had no concept of who this man was, only that someone new had appeared in her world, someone interesting.
Jordan froze. His eyes widened, mouth falling slightly open, as his gaze fell on her. "Wait... what?" His voice was quiet, incredulous, caught somewhere between disbelief and awe.
"Is that... Is she...??" He looks confused,
My heart hammered in my chest, a mix of fear and protectiveness flooding me. "Jordan..." I began, forcing calm into my voice, though my pulse raced like wild horses. "This is... I..." The words faltered, the weight of the secret I had carried for so long pressing down on me.
Ray toddled closer, humming softly, oblivious to the tension thickening the air. I stepped in front of her, gently guiding her back, the soft rustle of her pajamas brushing my legs. Jordan's gaze shifted between us, trying to process. "She... she's yours? And his"
I nodded, steadying my voice, though it trembled slightly. "Yes. This is Race but she prefers Ray," I said softly, motioning for him to step inside carefully, keeping myself between them.
The air hung heavy, charged and unsteady. Jordan bent slightly, curiosity and shock warring in his expression. Ray looked up at him, eyes wide, completely unaware that she had just altered the course of his understanding forever. She clutched her bunny, her tiny presence a paradox of innocence and revelation.
I could feel my pulse in my throat, in my chest, in every nerve. The moment was monumental. The first time Jordan saw her, and I had to navigate it with care, balancing revelation with protection, letting him process the impossible truth without exposing Ray to confusion or fear.And even as the shock in his eyes was palpable, I knew with certainty that nothing would ever be the same again.
Ray toddled forward a little, reaching a hand toward him, her tiny voice bright. "Hi! Mama!"
Jordan blinked, caught between awe and hesitation, before offering a tentative, slow smile. "Hi... Ray," he said, his voice
low, careful. He took a small step forward, hands open, trying not to startle her. She stared at him, curious, then looked back at me, her safe anchor, before cautiously patting his hand with her bunny.
The tension in the room softened, just enough to breathe, and I let out a quiet exhale I hadn't realized I'd been holding. Jordan's eyes never left her, a mixture of wonder and disbelief still flickering there, as if the reality of what I'd kept hidden was still settling around him.
I watched the way he adjusted his posture, how every careful movement seemed designed not to alarm her. I felt a strange mix of relief and lingering anxiety. He didn't know her yet, but he would remember this moment forever.
And I knew that while the revelation had been mine to control, the ripples it would send were already beginning. The world outside this apartment might never know her, might never see her, but in that small space, her presence had already shifted everything.
I took a small step toward him, voice firm yet soft. "She's my world," I said simply. "I... I hope you understand."
Jordan nodded slowly, a flicker of respect and disbelief in his eyes. "I... I get it," he murmured. "I really do."
And for the first time that night, I felt a small, tenuous thread of trust forming. Not between him and me, but between him and her, fragile yet undeniable, the beginning of something that would change everything without ever forcing her into the chaos of the world outside these walls.
Ray yawned softly, rubbing her eyes, her attention drifting from him back to the warmth of home. I scooped her up, cradling her against me, feeling the small weight of her tiny body and the immense weight of the secret I had protected for so long.
Jordan stayed still for a moment longer, watching us, before he finally stepped back, offering a tentative, understanding smile. I nodded once, almost imperceptibly, then gently closed the door, shutting the world out behind me.
In the silence that followed, I held Ray tighter, inhaling her scent, feeling the life and joy in her small, perfect presence. The apartment felt warm and safe, filled with the quiet rhythm of bedtime and the lingering echoes of celebration.
And somewhere deep in my chest, I knew that the night's revelations were only the beginning.
Seventeen
Jordan
I don’t know what I expected when I came here. Maybe a quick drop-off, maybe a nod, a “thanks” and a polite goodbye. Maybe Alyssa would shoot me a glare, maybe she’d ignore me entirely. Anything but this. But standing on her doorstep, the cool night air brushing my face, I felt every plan I had unravel before it even started.
The moment the door opened, I saw her. Alyssa. But not just Alyssa,the version of her I hadn’t been allowed to see for years. The one who carried herself with that quiet, effortless strength. Her brow furrowed slightly, eyes narrowed, voice sharp. “What
are you doing here? How did you get past my security? What are you here for, period?”
I froze. Her words should have felt like a punch. Maybe they did. But all I could do was hold up the purse she’d left behind. “I… I didn’t. Sorry. You left this there and I thought maybe you might need it,” I said, raising the purse like some peace offering, though my voice probably sounded more hesitant than I intended. I hated that. I hated hesitating around her. Always had.
Before I could brace myself for the scolding, for the storm I expected, I heard a soft, tiny voice.
“Mama! Mama!”
A baby, little girl toddles down the stairs, hair tousled, cheeks flushed, holding her stuffed bunny like it could protect her from anything in the world. There was a curious spark in her eyes, a boldness I hadn’t expected from a child so small. And for the first time in a long while, my chest actually ached.
I froze again, feeling exposed, caught somewhere between awe and disbelief. “Wait… what?” I whispered, caught off guard by her presence. My eyes flicked from her to Alyssa, then back to the girl, trying to process this new, impossible reality.
“She’s yours?” I asked, voice low, careful, though the disbelief lingered. “And… his?”
I watched Alyssa stiffen, her hands moving instinctively to shield Ray, to keep that tiny barrier between us. She nodded, a tremor in her voice as she said, “Yes. This is Race, but she prefers Ray.”
I swallowed hard. Of course, I’d imagined something like this might exist, somewhere in the quiet corners of my mind when I thought about what could have been. But seeing it, feeling it,
that tiny human being here, in front of me, in Alyssa’s arms,it changed everything.
Ray toddled closer, humming softly, oblivious to the tension thickening the air around us. I took a careful step forward, hands open, soft, careful. I couldn’t let her be scared of me. Not for a second. “Hi… Ray,” I said again, slower this time, letting my voice carry gentleness, offering her the smallest, safest smile I could muster.
She stared at me for a moment, curious, then looked back at Alyssa, her anchor, before tentatively patting my hand with her bunny. And that tiny gesture,it should have been simple, harmless, yet it hit me in the chest like a hammer.
I’d always wanted to be part of her world, to be there for Alyssa in any way possible, but now the stakes were different. Her tiny hand reminded me that I couldn’t just step in. Not without permission. Not without respect.
I never believed in love at first sight but with Alyssa I believed that concept was true. I couldn't just get her beautiful face and alluring scent out of my head. I fell in love with her at first sight.
I took another step, closer this time, slow, deliberate. My chest felt tight. I wanted to reach out, to say all the words I’d stored away for months,the “I love you”s, the “I was there all along”s,but none of that would work here. Not in this moment. Not when Ray existed, when Alyssa existed, when I had to navigate the unknown with care.
Alyssa’s gaze never left me. Not her angry, protective glare,not her wary, measured assessment,but a look that said she was still in control, still the keeper of this space, this world. I respected that. I always had. And yet, every fiber of me wanted to defy it. To lean in and tell her she didn’t have to carry everything alone.
Ray yawned softly, rubbing her eyes, and I watched as Alyssa scooped her up, cradling her against her chest. There was an intimacy there I’d never been allowed to share, a quiet rhythm that reminded me I was on the outside looking in. I wanted that rhythm. I wanted to belong to it. But I knew better than to reach for it just yet.
I found myself studying them both, memorizing the small details, the way Ray’s curls bounced as she rested her head against her mom, the softness in Alyssa’s eyes when she looked down at her daughter.
I had seen Alyssa before, briefly, during an audition and at a moment that would stay with me for years,but never like this. Never so raw, so unguarded, so undeniably… human. And it hit me all at once,the years I’d been holding back, the love I’d buried, the quiet longing that had never dissipated.
“I… I get it,” I said finally, my voice low, careful. “I really do.”
It was the truth. I did get it. I understood that this wasn’t just Alyssa’s life anymore. It was Ray’s life, too. And any misstep, any false move on my part, could change everything. Could damage the fragile trust she’d built around her daughter. I had to tread lightly.
She took a small step back, adjusting Ray in her arms, reinforcing that invisible line between us. And I respected it. But inside, something shifted. The awareness that Alyssa’s world was no longer solely hers,it included Ray now,made me feel both terrified and exhilarated. I had loved Alyssa for years, long before this moment, long before I had any right to even hope,but now, seeing her like this, I realized my feelings hadn’t dulled. If anything, they had sharpened.
Ray’s tiny voice pulled me out of my thoughts. “Mama!” she said again, looking up at Alyssa. Her innocence, her trust, the
way she anchored Alyssa in a reality that had never been mine,it was overwhelming. I wanted to lean down, to talk to her, to be part of this tiny, perfect world, but I also wanted to respect the boundaries Alyssa had so clearly set.
I took another careful step forward. “I won’t startle her,” I said quietly to Alyssa, my tone more of a promise than an explanation. She gave me a small nod, not relaxed, but a sign that I could proceed cautiously. And so I did.
“Hi, Ray,” I said again, kneeling slightly to lower myself to her level. My hands stayed open, non-threatening, and I offered the smallest of smiles. She stared at me, tilted her head, and then, with an almost imperceptible movement, touched my hand with her bunny. That gesture,simple, tentative, and trusting,made my chest tighten in a way I couldn’t control.
Alyssa’s gaze remained fixed on me, steady, assessing, like she was weighing my every move. And in that moment, I realized how much trust she had to give,and how much I had to earn. It wasn’t about feelings anymore. Not just mine, anyway. It was about proving I could exist in this space without disrupting it, without harming it.
The silence that stretched between us was thick, charged with every emotion I’d been carrying for years. Fear, longing, restraint, hope,they all collided in a quiet storm inside me. I had loved Alyssa from afar, watched as her life unfolded in ways I could never touch. And now, here I was, standing in her apartment, witnessing a world I’d been shut out of. But I was here, and I wasn’t leaving. Not yet.
Ray’s yawn reminded me that she was still a child, still vulnerable, and still needed her mother more than she needed me. I forced myself to take a breath, slow and steady, and let the
moment settle. I didn’t need to rush. I didn’t need to push. All I needed was presence,quiet, patient, careful.
“Can I… can I just… watch for a minute?” I asked finally, my voice low, careful. Alyssa’s eyes met mine, sharp, piercing, assessing. I could see the questions there,why, how, what I wanted. I held her gaze and added softly, “I’m not here to interfere. I just… I want to understand. That’s all.”
A flicker of something passed over her face. Hesitation, curiosity, a trace of the trust I hadn’t expected to earn so easily. She nodded once, almost imperceptibly, and I exhaled quietly, relief and tension mingling in my chest.
I watched her, watched the way she held Ray, the quiet grace in the simplest of gestures. And I realized that this,this small, intimate world,was what I’d been longing for all along. Not the drama, not the fame, not the recognition. Just this. Just being allowed to exist here, quietly, without overstepping, without forcing my feelings onto a child or the woman I’d loved for longer than I cared to admit.
Ray yawned again, nuzzling into Alyssa’s chest, and I felt a strange mix of awe and reverence. She was perfect in all her tiny, chaotic, living ways. And I had to protect that perfection, respect it, honor it,even if it meant keeping my distance.
I stayed there for a few more moments, just observing, letting the reality sink in. The secret I had never known, the daughter I hadn’t met, the woman I had loved for years,now they existed together, and I had a place in their orbit, even if it was just at the edge.
Finally, I straightened slowly, offering a small, tentative smile. Alyssa’s eyes met mine again, and for the first time, there was no anger, no suspicion just a quiet understanding. “I… I understand,” I murmured. “I get it. And I’ll respect it. Always.”
She gave me a slight nod, holding Ray a little tighter, and I knew that was enough for now. I stepped back, keeping my distance, and let her close the door between us. The apartment quieted, leaving me standing in the hall, chest tight, heart racing, but also… strangely hopeful.
Because now I knew. I knew her world, I knew the stakes, and I knew the truth. And even if I had to wait, even if I had to tread carefully, I would. I would be there, in whatever way Alyssa allowed, because nothing,no mistake, no past heartbreak, no secret,would stop me from seeing her, protecting her, and eventually, maybe even sharing the life she had built with the daughter I had never met.
And as I walked back to the street, feeling the night air on my face, I realized that nothing would ever be the same again. But for the first time in years, that thought didn’t terrify me. It excited me. Because I had seen her. And her daughter. And I had a chance. Just a small, careful, fragile chance. But a chance nonetheless.
And I wasn’t letting it go.
Eighteen
Isaiah
I walk into Leo's hotel room with a lit cigar perched between my lips.
I really needed this high before walking down the red carpet tonight.
With everything happening on the Internet lately, it could drive anyone to a mental asylum.
I've been going crazy for the past few days.
Everywhere I go, anywhere I pass, I see her or her name.
She is everywhere. She couldn't stay in my dreams-now she is invading reality.
In theatres, she's become a Broadway star, and all it took was a mini play or some show, whatever they call it.
I was never interested in Broadway to begin with, so I had my agent turn down every offer. I thought it was beneath me.
But Alyssa took it-and now she's shining.
I keep thinking about what might have happened if she had taken the audition that made me famous. Or the last audition I sabotaged.
Yes, I admit it. I called Coleman, told him not to give her the offer, and asked him to make some excuses.
But I had my reasons, so don't come for me.
I was afraid. Afraid of the exposure she would get. Afraid the spotlight would be stolen from me. I knew her skills. I knew her capabilities. I couldn't let that happen. I was insecure about her outshining me.
I didn't want to share the spotlight, though there was another way for things to go-we could have been Hollywood's greatest couple.
But jealousy and fear got the better of me, and I sabotaged her audition.
My second reason was that our marriage was already drifting apart. I was afraid that if she started acting, it would collapse entirely.
It did anyway. And she didn't even lift a finger. I single-handedly destroyed the only good thing in my life.
Now, I am just a shell of my former self. I don't answer my mom or any of my siblings anymore.
I want them to live their lives without me. I work hard enough to ensure they don't suffer financially.
Besides, I have nothing left to lose. No child, no wife.
Just a clingy girlfriend.
I can't wrap my head around the fact that she became famous from just one Broadway show, and people are praising her acting skills. If she were cast in that fantasy movie, it would have been hell for me.
"What are you doing here, Zay? You should be in the room preparing with Julian and Tasha. And throw away that cigarette-remember what the doctor said," Leo said, taking a big bite of his cheesy burger.
"The doctor doesn't know what I need and doesn't know what the hell I go through," I tell them, plopping onto the couch.
Before he can respond, the hotel room door opens. Voices step in.
One voice that always irritates me.
Tasha, Julian, and Anya enter, along with her publicist.
"What are you guys doing? I left so I could get away from you all," I groan, taking another swing of the cigar.
"Don't say that, Zay. You're hurting my feelings, and I'm already stressed out with all the media negativity!"
"Zay, you need to come back and get ready. The premiere starts in a few hours, and you aren't prepared. We need this time because the questions will be brutal. Your ex-wife has created a big scene online, and we need to get you in order," Tasha says, pacing around, her pregnant belly slightly visible in the dress.
I immediately take my eyes off her, memories biting.
"Yes. I can't believe people think she's a better actress than I am. She's just mediocre. My little sister could be better," Anya whines.
She leans closer to me, and I scoot away slightly. I hate her clinginess.
"Despite what you think, we still want you guys to be together. Don't answer any questions about her, and just wish her luck," Julian says.
"It isn't that bad. It will go away in a few days. This is the Internet for you," I reply casually, taking a hit from my blunt.
"No, it won't go away in a few days. Do you know how many people are talking about her? They're calling her performance Oscar-worthy. Fucking Oscar-worthy. We can't let that happen. You've sacrificed enough!" Tasha insists.
"Oscar-worthy? Oh, please. I have two Oscars. Do you know the strings my dad pulled to get me nominated? The things I had to do to win? I bet it was sisters doing. Yeah, she's the only one who pulled this off," Anya replies, hooking her arms with mine.
I pull away slightly and throw my blunt in the ashtray.
I can feel a headache forming.
"Can you just stop talking about her? I'm really tired and just need some peace before I go," I say and stand up.
Anya also stands, but my glare sends her straight back to her seat.
"I can help you relax, you know. You need to stop pushing me away, Zay. We can use this opportunity to try for another baby," she says softly.
"Yeah. Perfect. Anya's right. Another baby will take the spotlight off her," Tasha states as if it's the brightest idea ever.
I give her a confused look.
Is she serious right now?
She knows how I've been spiraling these past few days, and she still wants me to have a baby with Anya? My life can't even fathom a child right now.
I feel sick, literally and metaphorically.
"I'm going to pretend you didn't just suggest using a baby as competition for something that isn't even that important," I reply, and walk out of the room.
I enter my suite, lock the door, and collapse onto my bed.
I feel weak. I am tired-so tired-but both physically and mentally.
Bringing a baby into my life right now would be the worst possible idea.
Yes, Anya and I still have sex. I won't lie about that. Sex is one way to distract myself. And Anya would do anything to have me.
But I can't and don't want a baby again, especially with her.
If I die without a child or wife, my fortune will be split three ways: half to Alyssa, though she hates me, half to my family, and the rest to charity. I've told my lawyer everything.
Alyssa.
Tears instantly well up in my eyes. I take my phone from my pocket and open my gallery.
My album is filled with her pictures. The new PI I hired is better than the previous one.
This one actually brought me good news. I knew she had entered Broadway, I just hadn't confirmed it yet.
I bring the picture to my heart and let the tears fall.
She is my lifeline, and I don't know how I'm still alive. My mind is my enemy.
I miss her. I can't breathe without her.
"Alyssa... baby... I am so sorry. Please come back to me," I whisper into thin air as the sobs break free.
I sob like a child.
---
Immediately, Anya and I step out of the car, hand in hand. The moment our feet hit the red carpet, cameras explode around us. Flashes pulse like lightning, bouncing off the sequins on her dress, reflecting in my Chanel suit.
I force a smile, my signature, practiced for situations exactly like this. I hold Anya closer, projecting the image of a perfect couple. Inside, my chest feels tight, my thoughts scattered, my mind screaming her name.
"Isaiah! Over here!"
"Anya! Over here!"
The calls blur together, but I wave anyway, moving forward. The crowd's excitement, the lights, the lenses-none of it touches me except as a background noise to the storm in my head.
We start posing for photos, smiles, kisses, careful hand placements. Every movement is calculated. I pull Anya in for a kiss on the cheek, just enough to convince everyone watching that we're still connected, still in love. I can feel her warmth, her hand resting on mine, and yet it only makes the emptiness inside me sharper.
After the pictures, we're whisked into a series of interviews.
"Zay Martins and Anya Johnson, you two look stunning as always. What are you wearing tonight?" an interviewer asks.
"Thank you. I'm wearing a custom Ralph Lauren, and Zay is in a Chanel two-piece," Anya responds, her smile smooth, flawless.
"Oh, that's gorgeous. So when are you two tying the knot? Everything's ready, right?"
Anya answers before I can. My head starts to feel heavy, a pressure behind my eyes. The lights feel sharper, hotter. My heart beats faster.
"Oh, soon. We're just coming out of our grief, and we want to take our time with everything," she says, her voice like honey, smooth and careful.
"Okay, that sounds great. And what are your expectations for tonight's premiere?" the interviewer continues.
"Nothing but the best. Zay worked hard on this movie, and we hope it meets the expectations we've set," she replies again.
I feel my stomach twist. Lies. Every word is a lie. I regret taking this movie. Every scene, every line, every premiere has been a battlefield. This film, this night-it's a monument to everything I've lost.
I start to feel dizzy. My heart hammers against my ribs. My breathing shortens. The bright lights, the questions, the cameras-they start to close in, press against me.
"That's great. So, one last controversial question: Zay, what's your take on Alyssa, your ex-wife, and her sudden rise in Broadway?"
The words cut through me. My ears burn, my chest tightens. My vision blurs. I let go of Anya's hand.
"Oh, we wish her nothing but the best," Anya says softly, sweetly, completely unaware of how my body is betraying me.
Fuck.
Everything is spinning. The crowd's cheers, the flashbulbs, her voice-it all merges into a distant roar. I bend over, hands gripping my knees, trying to inhale enough air to stay upright.
"Zay... are you okay, baby?" Anya's voice comes from far away, distorted.
"Mr. Martins!" someone else calls, panicked.
I try to walk, but darkness creeps across my vision. Spots explode in my eyes. My legs give way.
I hit the carpet with a heavy thud. The world tilts and then shatters.
The last thing I hear is my name, over and over, before everything goes black.
Nineteen
Six months later
I wake up to noise and giggling coming from downstairs. Chatters and the clinking of utensils echo up the staircase. I groan and roll over on my bed, burying my face deeper into the pillow. I am so tired and drained from yesterday's show. I barely made it home in time to put Ray in bed. Sometimes I am so grateful Amy moved to New York. Despite her busy schedule, she still has time to babysit Ray during show days. When we both get too busy, we call our usual nanny, Clara. She is the only person aside from my family and now Jordan that Ray is comfortable with. My little girl has trust issues, so when she loves you, she loves you. Period.
Speaking of love and trust, I am still wrapping my head around how Ray adores Jordan now and vice versa. Ever since Jordan found out about my baby-or as I now call it, the moment they discovered each other-he has always been here, almost twenty-four seven, just to see Ray. He absolutely adores her and spends
most of his time here rather than at the studio. It took me three months to adjust to the idea of him being around her constantly, but I still have my guard up to this day. I do not care who he was or what he did. I still had him sign an NDA, and he didn't even protest. He was almost too willing. My little daughter with the heart of gold and an innocence that could melt titanium is so smitten by him. She waits at the door every evening for his arrival. They are best friends. They bond so well you would think they are a father-and-daughter duo.
I open my eyes slowly and stare at the ceiling, realizing how wild that sentence sounds-father and daughter-but it is the honest truth. Jordan slid into that role so naturally it scared me at first. I did not know what to do with that level of devotion. I was convinced life had already taken too much good from me to ever hand me something soft again. But the universe must have had a spare miracle lying around because somehow I got this weird little trio dynamic-me, Jordan, and Ray-that feels suspiciously close to a family.
I stretch lazily and stare at the sunlight dancing along my curtains. The city feels different these days. I feel different these days.
