My life flipped overnight when Broadway embraced me-when the show blew up-when my name started trending like wildfire. It is still surreal to think about the fact that I am now under a major talent agency. I remember signing those papers while my hands trembled. My new manager, Hanna, is a force of nature-strict when she needs to be, protective in a way that makes me feel like she genuinely cares. She has been handling the spotlight, interview schedules, magazine offers, future Broadway auditions, script readings, and all the other chaotic details that would have driven me insane on my own.
I am working with Sammy on another show next season, and there are rumors I might be cast in one of Jordan's new theatre
projects too. Any time someone online mentions my name these days, it usually comes with capital letters, viral clips, and screenshot edits-people dissecting my vocal choices or my acting expressions, as though I am some rare form of art. It is flattering and terrifying all at once.
I reach for my phone lazily, half tempted to scroll, but then something in my brain clicks violently.
Wait.
Why do the numbers on the screen look like that?
It shows today's date.
Today.
It is Ray's second birthday.
Panic hits me like cold water. I sit up in an instant, heart racing, shame spiking through my veins. How could I almost forget? How could I almost get lost in my ridiculous exhaustion and fame and barely remember my own daughter's birthday?
I jump out of bed and hurry to the bathroom to splash water on my face. The giggling downstairs becomes louder, brighter, sweeter. I hear her tiny laugh-her little squeal. The kind that sounds like sunshine hugging glass. I do not even bother brushing my hair properly. I pull it into a terrible messy bun and rush out of my room, nearly tripping as I storm down the stairs.
When I reach the kitchen entrance, I stop.
Jordan is standing there.
He is in my apartment. At this hour. Wearing a black t-shirt, sleeves pushed up slightly, tattoos peeking from his wrist as he flips pancakes with one hand and holds a mixing bowl with the other. Ray is sitting on the kitchen counter beside him, impossibly tiny in her pastel pajamas, and swinging her legs
as she giggles at something he just said. Her curls are wild, her cheeks flushed with morning excitement.
My heart softens instantly.
He looks up at me, like he sensed me. "Good morning, Alyssa," he says, smiling in that gentle way that always disarms me.
I blink, still half startled. "What are you doing here this early?"
He shrugs,casual,like this is normal. "I didn't want to miss her birthday."
Ray squeals and taps his arm. "Jo-jo! Jo-jo look! Mommy awake!"
That nickname still kills me softly every time. She could not pronounce Jordan when she started talking, so Jo-jo stuck. And it stuck in a way that feels like destiny now.
Jordan smiles at her affectionately. "Yes, munchkin, Mommy's awake."
That nickname still kills me softly every time. She could not pronounce Jordan when she started talking, so Jo-jo stuck. And it stuck in a way that feels like destiny now.
Jordan smiles at her affectionately. "Yes, munchkin, Mommy's awake."
I step forward, kneeling beside Ray, and she reaches out her tiny arms to me. I hug her close and feel her warmth. "Happy birthday, baby," I whisper, kissing her cheek.
Ray giggles again, turning back to Jo-jo. "Jo-jo flip! Flip!" She points to the pancakes he is flipping, eyes wide.
Jordan lifts the pancake and shows it to her like a trophy. "Here you go, birthday girl. One pancake for my princess."
She claps and shouts, "Yay! Jo-jo!" Then reaches out for me to hand her syrup. I smear a tiny bit on her plate and watch her carefully devour the first bite.
"Look at her, Jo-jo," I say, laughing softly. "She loves breakfast as much as cake."
Jordan glances at me, eyes soft. He stays back, letting me engage. "She's smiling. That's enough for me."
Ray talks to him like he understands every word. "Jo-jo! More flip! Flip!"
I sit back, smiling at the two of them. My heart feels full, fragile, and terrified all at once.
After a few minutes, my phone rings. I glance at the screen. It is my father. I take a deep breath and answer.
"Happy birthday, baby girl!" His voice booms through the speaker, so loud it makes Ray giggle. "Two years old already! I can't believe it!"
"Thanks, Dad," I say, smiling as Ray waves at the phone. "She's excited to see you soon. We'll take lots of pictures."
"I can't wait! Make sure she eats something sweet for me. And don't forget a hug from me!" He laughs, smitten as always.
Ray claps, screeches, and waves her tiny hands. "gan-pa!gan-pa!"
I hang up smiling, then my mother calls. She is emotional as ever, practically cooing through the phone. "Alyssa, my darling, I can't believe she's two. Two! I wish I could be there this morning. Please tell me she has her favorite breakfast."
"She does, Mom," I say gently, keeping my tone patient. "We'll celebrate quietly for now. I promise she'll have lots of love from you when we visit."
Then Alec calls. His voice is teasing, gentle. "Happy birthday, Ray-Ray!" He says, making her giggle even harder. Ophelia appears in the background, waving frantically and blowing exaggerated kisses. "We'll make up for missing the day soon, I promise."
Ray claps and screeches, "Op-Op! Op-Op hi!" She points at the phone, too excited to sit still.
"Thank you, Alec. I know she'll be thrilled," I say warmly, feeling my chest swell.
Later, Amy calls from Spain. Emeric yells happily in the background, his tiny voice blending with hers. Ray screams, "Emi! Emi!" and nearly tumbles trying to hug the phone. Amy laughs softly, promising stories and presents when she returns.
Jordan quietly watches, staying back, letting me handle the calls while also keeping Ray entertained. He leans slightly on the counter, hand on the back of her chair, eyes calm and steady.
After the calls, the morning settles into a gentle rhythm. Jordan helps Ray blow out her little cupcake candles-just two bright flames-nothing flashy. Just home. Just peace. Just love. Ray claps and screeches as the waxy flame dies.
We eat the small cupcakes together. I sip my coffee, Jordan hands Ray more syrup-covered pancakes, and Ray giggles every time she accidentally smears it on her cheeks. She babble-shouts "Jo-jo! Jo-jo!" constantly, trying to share her joy with him. I laugh softly, watching my daughter and the man who has become her steadfast anchor.
And it hits me,maybe this is enough.
Maybe this tiny life,this tiny moment,is more valuable than every camera flash I have ever stood under.
Jordan catches my eyes across the table, his gaze steady, patient, unspoken. There is something real there, something I am terrified to define and equally terrified to lose.
And for the first time that morning, I let myself breathe.
Twenty
Isaiah
I stand on the balcony of my old home with a glass of alcohol in one hand and a lit cigarette in the other. The smoke curls lazily upward, twisting into the thick evening air. The sky is heavy with clouds, and the sinking sun bleeds across the horizon, painting it in fiery shades of orange and gold. It's beautiful , heartbreakingly beautiful , the kind of beauty that feels cruel when your world has gone gray.
The horizon glows bright and alive, yet my life feels like the opposite , dark, heavy, and drained of all light.
I've been here since my release from the hospital. Alone. Away from everyone. The doctor recommended peace and quiet, and I took that advice literally. I didn't want visitors. I didn't want noise. I didn't even want pity. I just wanted to disappear quietly inside the silence.
The only people allowed through these doors are Leo, Julian, my nurse, and the doctor.
No family.
No Tasha.
And definitely no Anya.
Anya is her own story , a story I no longer have the energy to keep writing. I think it's time Julian and Tasha find a way to publicly announce our breakup. I need to let her go ,not for her, but for my own peace. Because, honestly, she already has. I didn't need Leo to confirm the rumors I've been hearing since last week, but of course, he did anyway. Late-night parties. Leaving hotels at dawn. Not coming home while I was still in the hospital. I don't even see the point of pretending anymore. Whatever this relationship was, it's long dead. The only thing keeping us tied together was our child , and when we lost that... the last thread between us snapped.
I stayed because of guilt, maybe. Or habit. And because the sex was convenient , another distraction to numb the noise in my head. But now? Now it all feels pointless.
Still, the irony doesn't escape me. Just yesterday, the Oscar nominations were announced. My name appeared not once but six times. Six categories. A record. People are calling me a phenomenon, a living legend, a once-in-a-generation artist. The probability of me winning at least one is almost guaranteed. But I can't bring myself to care.
I have no joy left. No hunger. No ambition. I feel nothing.
Absolutely nothing.
It's like something inside me died, and the rest of me just refused to get the message.
I take another drag of my cigarette just as the door behind me creaks open. I don't need to turn around to know who it is. Only one person enters my room without knocking , Leo.
He walks in with that familiar calm authority, his steps echoing across the hardwood floor.
"What are you doing here?" I mutter without looking back. "I told you to leave me alone unless it's an emergency."
"You need a friend, Isaiah," he replies, voice steady but edged with frustration. "You can't keep locking yourself in here. This isn't healthy - not mentally, not physically."
He walks closer, his hand reaching for the cigarette between my fingers. "And stop doing that. The drinking and smoking will only make things worse."
Before I can react, he plucks the cigarette from my hand and snuffs it out on the balcony rail. He succeeds in taking that, but not the glass of whiskey in my other hand.
"The harm's already been done," I say flatly, downing what's left of the drink. The burn trails down my throat, sharp and welcome. "So what more can it do? I'm already a goner."
Leo exhales sharply, frustration flickering in his eyes. "Your case isn't hopeless, Isaiah. Stop being so damn morbid. We're talking to some of the best doctors , in and out of state. There are new treatments, options worth exploring."
"No need," I say with a humorless laugh. "Why try when there's no survival chance? I've got not one, but two life-draining diseases. One's rare, and the other's incurable. So tell me, Leo, how the fuck am I supposed to be happy?"
He folds his arms, refusing to rise to my bitterness. "There are treatment options," he insists. "Medications to slow the symptoms, therapies that can ease things. You have money, fame, connections. We can get you the best care in the world. But you need to cooperate. Tell me what you need, Isaiah. Anything. I'll make it happen."
I turn to face him finally, the sunset's last light reflecting off the bottle on the table behind me. I study him for a moment, then let out a broken laugh. "There's nothing you can do for me, Leo."
I pause, then continue quietly, "Actually, no ,there are a few things you can do. Start by publicly announcing my breakup with Anya. Then, take me back eight years. Back when my marriage with Alyssa was still real. When I wasn't... this." I gesture at myself. "Then bring Alyssa back to me. Do that, and maybe I'll thank you."
Leo's expression softens , the way people look at terminal patients when they don't know what else to say. "You know I can't do any of that," he says quietly. "The only thing I can do is stage the breakup with Anya. It won't be hard, especially since she's already moved on with that actor,what's his name again? Matthew Moore. Yeah, him."
I scoff. "Good riddance."
He sighs, stepping closer. "Come inside, Isaiah. The cold isn't good for you."
I hesitate but eventually follow him back into the dimly lit bedroom. The curtains are half drawn, the furniture untouched, the air stale with neglect. The sheets are the same ones from two years ago ,unwashed, heavy with dust, and still faintly smelling of her. Of Alyssa.
"So," I say as I collapse onto the edge of the bed, "why are you really here?"
"I'm here to make sure you take your painkillers," Leo says matter-of-factly. He opens the nightstand drawer and starts sorting through the small mountain of pill bottles. "Your nurse said you skipped yesterday's doses."
I roll my eyes. "You're not my nurse, Leo."
"I wouldn't have to be if you didn't give her such a hard time," he fires back. "She almost quit yesterday. I had to offer her a raise just to get her to stay."
"Well, she doesn't need to be here either. I'm perfectly capable of taking care of myself."
Leo gives me a long, unimpressed look. "You sure about that, champ? Because right now, you look like a ghost clinging to his last cigarette."
I glare at him but say nothing.
He sighs, handing me a small cup of pills and a glass of juice. "At least take these."
I stare at the pills for a moment, then swallow them all at once, chasing them down with the juice. "Happy now?" I mutter.
"Not even close," he replies softly. "Have you eaten anything?"
"Yes. Now go."
He doesn't move. "You sure?"
"Yes, Leo," I say, sliding under the sheets. "Go."
He studies me for a moment, then nods. "Fine. But I'm staying downstairs. If you need anything-"
"I won't."
He opens his mouth like he wants to argue but then decides against it. With a quiet sigh, he turns and walks out, closing the door gently behind him.
Silence fills the room again.
It's the only sound that ever stays.
I lie back on the bed and stare at the ceiling. The faint scent of Alyssa lingers in the sheets ,a ghost of perfume and warmth that hasn't faded even after all this time. It's almost cruel how memory clings harder than people do.
I close my eyes, and for a moment, I let myself remember. Her laughter echoed down the hallway. Her voice saying my name like it meant something. The smell of her shampoo on my pillow. The way she'd smile when I walked into a room, before everything between us shattered.
She was my home long before I ever owned one.
And now that she's gone, everything else just feels... empty.
The tears come without warning, hot and silent. I don't fight them. I let them fall, soaking into the pillow. For years, I've been the man the world worships , the face on billboards, the name in headlines, the actor everyone calls "untouchable." But lying here now, surrounded by silence and shadows, I've never felt smaller.
The fame. The money. The awards. None of it matters. Because when the cameras stop flashing and the world stops clapping, the truth hits like a bullet , I traded love for legacy, and now I have neither.
Outside, the last light of sunset fades completely, leaving only darkness.
Inside, I'm drowning in it.
And for the first time in a long time, I whispered into the empty room , so soft even I can barely hear it.
"I miss you, Alyssa."
Twenty One
And that brings us to the end of another successful show. I feel alive as I take my final bow, the stage lights warming my skin while the audience roars like a living, breathing wave. For a moment, it feels like they're lifting me off my feet. Their applause becomes a rhythm in my chest, steady and familiar, almost like a heartbeat I've borrowed for the night. I'm still not used to hearing my name chanted like a favorite melody, but I'm learning not to flinch when the spotlight lands on me.
This is my third time leading a Broadway show, yet my first time doing it under Jordan's direction. Somehow, that tiny fact makes it mean more. I still remember the moment I got his call, the way I froze with my phone in my hand, staring at his name like it might vanish if I blinked. Being chosen among other well-known actresses felt surreal. I spent five minutes convincing myself he didn't dial me by mistake.
Backstage, the cheers fade into a warm hum behind me. People are hugging, congratulating, celebrating. Castmates brush
past with glitter still on their cheeks, tech crews are wrapping cables, and someone plays music too loudly from a speaker. It smells like sweat, roses, hairspray, and victory. My body aches beautifully-the kind of ache that tells me I did something worthwhile.
I slip into the dressing room, closing the door softly behind me. Home. Or at least the closest thing to home I have when I'm here. I can already feel the magnetic pull toward my baby girl. A few hours away from her, and my heart is already whining like an abandoned puppy. I really am a Velcro mom, clinging to every second with her before she grows up and decides I'm embarrassing.
I start removing my makeup, wiping away layers of foundation, eyeliner, and the stage version of myself. Under the bright bulbs, the real me stares back-tired, yes, but glowing in a way I don't fully recognize yet. Maybe it's contentment. Or maybe it's the fact that I know I'll be home soon, reading Ray her bedtime stories.
As I pack my things into my bag, there's a knock at the door. Light. Familiar. My stomach does a stupid flip. I don't need a mirror to know I'm smiling already.
I know exactly who it is.
The man who has been living rent-free in both my home and my head. The man my daughter adores with an intensity that should honestly terrify me. The man I... shouldn't be thinking about the way I do.
The door cracks open, and his head pokes through. His eyes are closed like a kid peeking into a room he expects to be booby-trapped.
"Are you decent? Can I come in?" His voice is gentle, teasing, already warm.
"Yes, come in," I reply, and he opens his eyes before stepping fully inside.
And there it is-the smile. The one that could power half of New York if we could bottle it. I blush immediately. It's ridiculous how easily he turns me into a soft, babbling mess. Isaiah being the only man I was ever with did something to me, I swear. I'm both too experienced and not experienced at all, and somehow both fail me when Jordan is involved.
"You going home now?" he asks, nodding at my packed bag.
"Yes. I promised Ray I'd read her two bedtime stories tonight." I sling the bag over my shoulder, but he stops me with a small raise of his hand.
"Oh. Okay. But... what if you join us for drinks? Just a couple. It'll only take a minute." He trails off like he expects me to shut him down.
I open my mouth to do exactly that, but he lifts a hand again, gently, like he's saying hear me out.
"I know, I know," he continues. "But how about this-we stop for a quick drink, just the two of us, and when we get back, we both tuck her in and read her a bedtime story? You know she can't resist one from her favorite uncle." The smirk he adds is maddening and adorable all at once.
"Alec will lose his mind if he hears you calling yourself that," I laugh. "And I thought you didn't drink. You didn't touch anything at Amy's birthday party."
"Yes, well," he says, shoving his hands into his pockets, "one beer wouldn't hurt. I only drink beer."
His tone is casual, but there's something underneath it,something studied, something careful. I know a performance when I see one, and Jordan is performing for me. It's subtle, but
it's there. He's not a man who drinks for fun. I've pieced that much together without him ever admitting it. But he's offering anyway, because he wants time with me.
The thought warms me more than it should.
I sigh softly. "You're really determined, aren't you?"
He steps closer, close enough that the scent of his cologne wraps around me-clean, warm, faintly woody. It's become something like a grounding force in my life.
"I'm determined about certain things," he says, his voice low. "And right now, I'm determined to convince you to let yourself breathe for one evening. Just one."
"That's manipulative," I whisper, my pulse quickening.
"That's honest," he counters.
I hate that he's right. I hate that it feels good that he's right.
I cave with a smile. "Fine. One drink. And then we go home."
His answering grin could melt steel.
"Deal. Let's go before you change your mind."
We walk out together, weaving through the remaining cast and crew. People greet him, congratulate him, tease him about being "the favorite director of the season," and he takes it all in stride. He always does. Jordan has this way of fitting into any room, any conversation, any space,except the walls he builds around himself.
Outside, the night air is cool, the city buzzing with neon lights and impatient taxis. He walks a little closer than necessary, his arm brushing mine every few steps, and each time feels like a spark I'm not supposed to acknowledge.
We stop at a quiet bar two blocks away, the kind with dim lights and old jazz floating through the speakers. It's cozy, warm, intimate. A place where secrets feel safe.
We sit near the window. Jordan orders a beer he won't actually drink, and I order something fruitier, lighter. He wraps his hands around the bottle but never lifts it. I pretend not to notice, but I do. I notice everything about him. The way he listens. The way he watches me like he's memorizing me. The way he stays in the moment as if nothing outside this booth matters.
We talk about the show, about Ray, about Amy's latest chaos. We laugh. We tease. There's a moment where I almost forget I'm a divorced woman with a complicated past. A moment where I feel young again. Wanted again. Seen again.
Then he asks, quietly, "Are you happy, Alyssa?"
The question lands heavily between us.
For a second, I forget how to breathe.
"That's a loaded question," I say softly, tracing my fingertip around the rim of my glass.
"I know," he admits. "But I want to know."
"I'm... trying," I answered honestly. "Some days are good. Some are harder. But I'm trying."
His eyes warm, soften. "You're doing more than trying. You're living again."
The words hit deeper than I expect. I swallow around the sudden tightness in my throat.
We leave the bar shortly after, walking back toward home. The city feels calmer now, like it's exhaling along with us. He walks
beside me in silence for a while, not awkward, just thoughtful. Comfortable.
When we reach my building, we pause by the front door. The hallway light casts a soft glow over him, making him look impossibly gentle and impossibly dangerous at the same time.
"Alyssa," he murmurs, and my heart stutters.
"Yes?" I whisper.
He looks at me like he wants to tell me something. Something big. Something that might change everything.
But instead he exhales slowly and says, "Let's go read Ray her story."
It's the safest thing he could have said.
And the most dangerous.
We head upstairs, and the moment the door opens, Ray squeals from the living room and runs toward us, tiny arms outstretched. She launches straight into Jordan's arms, babbling excitedly, her curls bouncing wildly.
"Uncle Jo-jo! You came!"
His face transforms completely-soft, bright, full of a love he doesn't try to hide.
"Of course I came, sunshine."
I watch them, my chest tightening. They look like a little family. They move like a little family. And for a terrifying second, I want what I'm looking at.
I want it too much.
Later, after stories and giggles and a quiet bedtime, after Ray falls asleep clutching her tiny rabbit toy, Jordan stands by the door, ready to leave.
"Thank you for tonight," he says softly.
"Thank you," I reply. "For... everything."
We stare at each other, something unspoken stretching between us.
Then he nods once, like he's afraid to say more, and slips out the door before either of us can make a mistake.
I stand there long after he's gone, heart pounding, breath uneven, wondering when exactly my life became something I wanted again.
And wondering when exactly he became the reason.
Twenty two
Ray was in one of her morning moods  giggly, messy, and completely unbothered by the fact that her oatmeal was now more on the table than in her bowl. I watched her from across the counter, my own cup of coffee cooling between my palms. She swung her little legs under the chair, humming a tune she learned God-knows-where, her hair sticking out in soft curls that made her look like she’d just rolled out of a cartoon.
“Mommy, look! Bubbles,” she said proudly, tapping her spoon into the oatmeal and sending another splash onto the table.
“Yes, sweetheart… that is very impressive chaos.”
She laughed like I’d told the funniest joke in the world.
That sound , that small, bright, perfect laugh , had the power to quiet every piece of me that still ached. Amy and Emeric had flown to England the night before for a job, so it was just me and Ray this morning. No cousin chaos. No backup. No Amy stealing the coffee before I could get to it.
Just me and my daughter, our little bubble , peaceful, warm, and wonderfully ours.
I was still sipping my coffee when I heard the knock. Not a hesitant one, not a professional one. A familiar rhythm, like someone knocking to a song only we knew.
Jordan.
Of course.
Ray’s head snapped up instantly, her whole face lighting. “Jo-Jo!”
She slid off the chair before I could stop her and sprinted toward the door on wobbly legs.
“Sweetheart, careful—!”
Too late. She was already grabbing the doorknob with both hands, dragging it open with all the dramatic effort of a two-year-old greeting her favorite person.
Jordan stood there in a dark hoodie and sweats, hair damp like he’d run here straight from the shower. He always looked good when he wasn’t trying. Actually… he always looked good. Period.
“Morning, tiny trouble,” he said, scooping Ray up as she squealed.
She wrapped her arms around his neck and nuzzled her cheek against him. “Jo-Jo! Breakfast!”
He laughed softly. “Oh yeah? Did Mommy make her famous gourmet… oatmeal explosion?”
I groaned. “Please don’t encourage her. We’re trying to learn how to use the spoon on the food, not on the walls.”
Jordan carried her inside like he had every right to be here , and maybe he did. He spent half his life in this apartment anyway. He knew where the extra bibs were, which cup Ray refused to drink from, and which seat I liked at the counter. He didn’t ask permission to step inside anymore. He didn’t have to.
It had become normal , terrifyingly, dangerously normal.
When he set Ray back into her high chair and kissed her hair (which sent her into delighted giggles), he finally turned to me.
“You look tired,” he murmured.
“Wow. What a beautiful compliment for a single mom.”
He smiled at that , the soft, lazy one that warmed me faster than the coffee ever could. “You look tired… and gorgeous,” he corrected.
And just like that, my heart betrayed me again.
I rolled my eyes, pretending it didn’t affect me. “Better.”
He took the mug from my hand without asking, poured himself coffee, added exactly one and a half teaspoons of sugar ,because he’d memorized it , then leaned against the counter beside me.
It was annoyingly easy between us.
Too easy.
“So,” he said, taking a sip, “big day today?”
“Just rehearsals,” I replied. “Sammy wants to rework scene twelve. Again.”
Jordan snorted. “He’s rewriting that scene for the sixth time. I love the man, but at this point I’d like to glue his script shut.”
I laughed , really laughed ,and it felt good, warm, landing somewhere in my ribs where joy had been missing for too long.
Then he said it.
“You should let loose tonight.”
I blinked. “What?”
“You heard me.”
He moved closer, not enough to touch me, but enough that the space between us suddenly mattered. My breath stilled, the room shrinking around us.
“Jordan—”
“Alyssa,” he said gently, with that patience he reserved only for me. “You’ve rebuilt everything. Your career. Your home. Your life with Ray. But you haven’t done anything for you.”
I wanted to deny it. I wanted to tell him I didn’t have time, didn’t have energy, didn’t have space in my chest for something new. But the truth lived behind my ribs, pulsing quietly.
I loved Isaiah once. Deeply. Fiercely. Enough to build a life with him, enough to break when that life cracked apart. But that love now sat like a faded photograph ,something I still treasured, but no longer clung to. I missed who we were, not who we became.
And what I felt when Jordan looked at me…
It wasn’t a photograph.
It was a beginning.
“You don’t have to pretend forever,” he said quietly. “You’re allowed to want things again. You’re allowed to breathe.”
He was too close now. My chest tight. My thoughts loud.
“I am breathing,” I whispered, but even I didn’t believe it.
He looked away for a breath, then back at me , and his eyes were soft but steady.
“Come out with me tonight, again.”
I swallowed. “Jordan…”
“Not a date,” he said quickly. “Unless you want it to be.”
My heart stuttered.
Ray banged her spoon on the tray like a tiny drunk drummer, startling us both. Jordan laughed and reached out to wipe oatmeal off her nose.
Watching him with her , the softness, the ease, the affection , tugged something unbearably tender inside me. He wasn’t just good with Ray.
He was safe with her.
Safe for me.
Which made him infinitely more dangerous.
“I don’t know if I’m ready,” I said quietly.
He met my eyes again. “You don’t have to be ready. Just willing.”
I should’ve said no. I should’ve ended the moment before it became something I couldn’t undo. But instead, my voice came out in a whisper.
“Okay.”
His breath hitched ,barely, but enough for me to hear it. Enough for me to feel it in my chest.
We spent the day rehearsing and pretending nothing had changed, but everything had. Every glance felt heavier. Every touch ,even when accidental , lingered longer. Every time he laughed, I felt it too deeply. Every time he stood beside me, I became aware of how warm he was, how close.
By the time we got home that evening, Ray already asleep, the apartment felt too quiet, too dim, too charged.
I set my keys on the table. Jordan stood behind me, not touching, but close enough that I felt the heat of him on my spine.
“Alyssa,” he murmured, my name a warm thread down my back.
I turned.
That was all it took.
The space between us collapsed.
He moved first , or maybe I did , but suddenly his hand was cupping my jaw, his thumb brushing my cheek like he’d been waiting years to touch me. My breath stilled.
“Alyssa,” he whispered again, “tell me to stop if—”
“I’m not telling you to stop.”
He inhaled sharply.
Then his mouth was on mine.
The kiss wasn’t rushed. It wasn’t frantic. It was slow, searching, like he was asking me a question with every movement and I was answering with the trembling of my hands against his chest.
Heat curled through me, but underneath it was something softer. Something terrifying. Something hopeful.
When we finally broke apart, breath mingling, foreheads touching, my voice barely held steady.
“Jordan…”
He gave me that small, soft smile , the one that always made me feel seen.
“I’ve got you,” he whispered. “Only if you want me.”
And for the first time in years, I didn’t feel afraid of wanting something new.
I felt alive.
Twenty three
It had been a month since the night Jordan kissed me in my living room, a month since everything quietly shifted. We didn't label anything, didn't declare anything, didn't have that defining "what are we?" talk. Yet somehow, he was everywhere in my life. In my home. In my thoughts. In the subtle warmth that lingered on my skin long after he touched me.
We existed in a strange, delicate space , something between friendship and romance, something soft and intoxicating, something neither of us dared to name for fear it would break if we looked at it too closely.
Every morning, Ray woke up asking for "Jo-Jo." Every night, he kissed my forehead before leaving, only to be back the next day like he never left. Some evenings he fell asleep on the couch beside me while we watched a show.
Other nights, we ended up tangled in each other , kissing deeply, touching gently, exploring each other slowly , but always
stopping before we went too far. It wasn't about restraint. It was about reverence. He treated every moment with me like something fragile, something precious.
And yet, under all of it, beneath every kiss and every soft night spent in the quiet of my living room, lay a thread of guilt I couldn't shake.
I loved Isaiah once. I didn't deny that. A part of me always would. And sometimes, when Jordan's hands moved over my waist or his lips brushed the curve of my neck, this quiet fear whispered in the back of my mind:
Are you just using him?
Using his warmth?
His touch?
His body?
His presence?
Are you healing with him or hiding in him?
I hated that voice.
But it was real.
Ray's delighted squeal pulled me back into the present.
We were in the living room that morning, sunlight pouring across the floor like liquid gold. Ray sat between us with her toys spread out in chaotic territory. Jordan was lying on the carpet, letting Ray climb onto his back like he had been hired as her personal jungle gym.
"Up, Jo-Jo! Up!" she demanded, tugging on his hoodie.
"You're lucky you're cute," he groaned dramatically before lifting her onto his shoulders like he weighed nothing. She giggled, clutching his hair with tiny fists.
"Jo-Jo tall!" she announced, proud.
"Yeah?" he said, glancing at me with a smirk. "Mommy doesn't think so."
I raised a brow. "Mommy thinks you exaggerate your height by two inches."
He scoffed like I'd insulted his entire existence. "First of all, I am exactly six-foot-four. Second of all, you think everyone is short because you wear heels the size of buildings."
I snorted. "They're three inches."
"Exactly. Towers."
Ray placed her little hands on both his cheeks, forcing him to look at her. "Jo-Jo silly."
"Thank you," he whispered, defeated.
She kissed his nose, and he melted like warm butter. Watching them together did something strange to my chest , something I wasn't ready to name either.
Later, while Ray napped, the apartment fell into a soft hush. Jordan and I ended up on the couch again, side by side, knees touching, an unspoken invitation lingering in the space between us.
He turned slightly toward me. "You're quiet today."
"I'm allowed to be quiet," I said, trying to sound light.
"You're allowed," he agreed softly, "but that's not what I asked."
His hand brushed my thigh, warm and gentle, and every nerve under my skin came alive. My breath caught, betraying me. This was how it had been all month ,touches that turned my bones to fire, moments that unraveled me.
"I'm thinking," I finally said.
"About?"
"You."
It slipped out before I could stop it.
His eyes flickered ,a spark, a softness, a quiet hunger. "What about me?"
"That I don't know what we're doing," I whispered.
"We're taking our time," he replied. "We're going slow. We're... figuring this out." His thumb traced small circles on my leg. "With no pressure."
"No pressure," I echoed, even though everything inside me felt pressurized.
I wanted him.
I wanted the way he touched me.
I wanted the way he saw me.
I wanted the way he held Ray like she was something sacred.
But I also felt the ghost of a man I once loved , a man I wasn't sure I had fully let go of.
"Sometimes," I whispered, staring at my hands, "I feel like I'm using you."
His fingers stilled. "Using me how?"
"To forget," I said, voice low. "To feel something else. Anything else."
"Alyssa." His voice was soft, steady, but laced with something warmer. "Look at me."
I did.
He leaned closer, so close I could feel his breath against my lips. "You aren't using me. You're letting me in. There's a difference."
I swallowed hard. "Is there?"
"There is," he murmured, brushing a strand of hair behind my ear. His fingertips lingered, trailing down the curve of my neck. "You don't touch me like someone filling a void. You touch me like someone afraid of wanting too much."
My heart thudded painfully.
He knew me too well.
"And," he added with a faint smile, "if you were using me, trust me, I'd know. I've been used before. You don't feel like that."
"What do I feel like then?" I whispered.
He leaned in slowly, giving me every chance to pull back. I didn't.
"You feel like someone I'm falling for," he whispered into my skin.
My breath trembled ,partly from warmth, partly from fear. But when his lips touched mine, everything inside me quieted. The kiss was slow, soft at first, then deeper as I sank into him. His hand cupped my cheek, thumb brushing under my eye as if he was memorizing me, piece by piece.
I slid my fingers into his hair, pulling him closer, the kiss growing heavier, warmer, more urgent. My body pressed against his, and he let out a low sound in his throat that sent heat through me.
We didn't rush. We never did.
But the softness of it, the ache of it, the way his hands traveled down my waist to pull me onto his lap , it was enough to make every thought scatter.
His lips moved down my neck, slow and reverent, and I exhaled shakily. Every touch made me feel alive, wanted, seen.
But even as passion tangled with tenderness, guilt whispered again at the back of my mind.
You still love Isaiah.
You are still healing.
Are you being fair?
Are you ready?
Are you breaking someone new while stitching yourself back together?
Jordan must have sensed the shift, because he kissed the corner of my mouth softly before whispering, "Hey... I'm right here."
I nodded, leaning my forehead against his. "I know."
"You don't have to choose anything right now," he murmured, brushing his nose against mine. "Not a label. Not a future. Not even me."
"But you're here anyway," I whispered.
"Always," he said simply.
My chest tightened , painfully and beautifully.
Before either of us could say more, we heard a small voice from the hallway:
"Mommy?"
We pulled apart gently as Ray appeared rubbing her eyes, hair a wild halo of curls.
Jordan smiled softly. "Nap cut short?"
She toddled over and climbed right into his lap like it was hers by law. He wrapped his arms around her instinctively. I watched them , the ease, the warmth , and something inside me softened and sharpened all at once.
Ray rested her head on his chest, tiny hand gripping the fabric of his shirt.
"Jo-Jo stay," she mumbled sleepily.
He kissed the top of her head. "I'm not going anywhere, tiny trouble."
And as I watched them, my heart whispered a truth I wasn't ready to say out loud but could no longer deny:
I think I'm falling for him too.
Twenty four
Jordan
I tried to keep my expression neutral when Alyssa opened the door for me that morning, but my chest betrayed me long before she spoke. It did that now,every time I saw her. Every time I smelled the warmth of her home. Every time Ray squealed my name from somewhere inside like I was the best part of her day.
I wished I could pretend that I wasn’t jealous. I wished I could deny the way it crawled beneath my ribs whenever I caught Alyssa staring off into a memory that didn’t belong to me. She loved Isaiah. Even if that love was fading,slowly, painfully,its
shadow still lived inside her. And I was just a man standing in the doorway, hoping someday that shadow would shrink enough to make room for me.
Not that I’d ever say it out loud.
Her smile was soft this morning. A little tired, a little dreamy, and far too pretty for someone who claimed she hadn’t slept well. “You’re early,” she said, brushing a loose curl behind her ear.
My eyes dipped down her body before I could stop them. She wore a simple, oversized T-shirt of mine,one I’d forgotten I owned,and leggings that clung to her hips like sin. She didn’t even realize she’d kept the shirt. Or maybe she did. Either way, the sight of her in something that smelled like me made my throat tighten.
“I didn’t think you’d mind,” I answered casually, even though I minded everything these days. Everything she didn’t say. Everything she still held back.
Ray barreled toward me from the living room. “Jow-dennnn!” she shouted, arms up, demanding to be carried like always.
I scooped her into my arms and kissed her cheek. “Good morning, tiny trouble.”
She giggled, gripping my shirt with both fists like she intended to permanently attach herself.
Alyssa watched us with that soft expression she tried to hide. It made my pulse kick every time,this quiet domestic picture we kept stumbling into. We hadn’t named whatever existed between us. We danced around it. We let ourselves kiss and touch behind closed doors. We let heat happen when nights grew too heavy. But she still carried guilt like a second skin, and I could feel it every time she hesitated before kissing me. Every
time she pulled back as if love were a fire she wasn’t sure she deserved to step into again.
And I—God help me—I accepted all of it just to be near her.
She poured me coffee and passed it over without meeting my eyes. “You’re traveling today,” she said lightly, as if she hadn’t spent the whole week dodging the topic.
“Yeah,” I replied, setting Ray down so she could chase a stuffed bunny across the carpet. “I told you I’d be gone for the weekend.”
She nodded. “To Illinois.”
Her voice dipped. She was curious, but too respectful to push. Too gentle to pry.
Too trusting.
Trust I didn’t deserve.
“It’s family stuff.” Short, clipped, just like always. I hated myself for lying to her, but I hated the truth more. If Alyssa knew that I had a son… that he was born from an affair that never should have happened… that the woman in that story was still technically my stepmother at the time… she’d run. She’d look at me the way people looked at news scandals and whispered the word monster under their breath.
I couldn’t stand the thought of her eyes changing when they looked at me.
So I kept the truth caged.
She forced a small smile. “You’ll come back?”
The question hit deeper than it should have. “Always.”
And I meant it. No matter where I went, I always came back to her.
Even if I didn’t deserve to.
---
Illinois looked the same as always,quiet, gray, and heavy with memories I didn’t ask for.
Kaya opened the door before I knocked. She looked annoyed, but then again, she always did. “You’re late.”
I exhaled slowly. “My flight landed twenty minutes ago.”
“That still makes you late.”
I stepped inside, brushing past her perfume,the same scent she used to wear on purpose. The same one she’d used to mark me once upon a stupid, regret-filled time.
Her heels clicked behind me as she followed. “You know, Jordan, some fathers actually want to visit their sons.”
I clenched my jaw. “I’m here, aren’t I?”
“Barely.” She crossed her arms and leaned against the kitchen doorway. “You come once a month, sometimes less. You never stay long. And you still expect me to keep your secret?”
There it was. The threat. She wielded it like a knife,always raised, always ready.
“You’re not exposing anything.” My voice hardened. “This is your reputation too.”
“Oh, darling, my reputation died the moment your father married me.” She smirked, stepping closer. “But yours? You’ve worked so hard to create a clean image for yourself. A respectable one. A successful one.” Her nails trailed across my sleeve. “It would be such a shame to ruin it.”
I stepped back sharply. “Kaya.”
She rolled her eyes. “Relax. You’re no fun anymore.”
“Because I learned from my mistakes.”
She laughed softly. “You say that every time, and yet here you are… back in my house.”
“Because my son is here,” I snapped.
Her smile thinned. “Yes. Our son.”
Before I could fire back, small footsteps pattered down the hallway.
“Daddy!”
My anger vanished instantly. I turned, and there he was,six years old, wide brown eyes, messy curls, running at me full speed.
I dropped to my knees and caught him. “Hey, buddy.”
His arms wrapped around my neck with total trust—trust I hadn’t earned, but he gave anyway. Every time. Without question.
Guilt, heavy and familiar, pressed down on me.
“I missed you,” he murmured.
“Missed you more.”
We spent the afternoon together,drawing, talking, building a toy fort he insisted had “dragon-proof walls.” I stayed until he fell asleep, tiny hand still clutching my wrist. Moments like this always tore something open in me. I wanted to do better. Be better. But my life was a mess of secrets, lies, and consequences.
And I wasn’t brave enough to pull Alyssa into this storm.
When I headed for the door, Kaya followed. “You know,” she said, leaning against the frame, “you could make things easier for yourself. For all of us.”
“No.”
“You didn’t even hear my suggestion.”
“I don’t need to.”
She touched my jaw lightly, and revulsion clawed up my spine. “You were happier when you were with me.”
“I was drowning.”
“You were mine.”
“I was stupid.” I lifted her hand off me. “And I’m not repeating that mistake.”
Her expression hardened. “Then don’t expect me to protect you forever.”
I walked out before the anger inside me turned into something uglier.
---
It was past midnight when I reached Alyssa’s apartment. The moment she opened the door, everything in me exhaled. Soft light spilled from the kitchen. She wore pajama shorts and one of those soft cotton tops that made her look warm and impossibly reachable.
She blinked. “You’re back early.”
“I couldn’t stay there.”
Her brows pulled together gently. “Are you okay?”
“Not really.”
She stepped aside. “Come in.”
I did. I always did.
The moment the door closed, I felt the silence shift between us. She wasn’t judging. She wasn’t demanding answers. She just… looked at me. Like she saw something breaking beneath the surface.
“Do you want to talk about it?” she asked quietly.
“No.”
I moved in closer. “I just… need something else.”
Her breath caught. “Jordan.”
I stopped inches away from her, searching her eyes, the curve of her mouth, the hesitation trembling through her body. She still carried guilt. She still loved a man she’d lost. But right now,right here,she was looking at me, not him.
“Alyssa.” My voice dropped, rough with everything I couldn’t say. “I need you.”
Her lips parted,soft, unsure, wanting.
“You’re upset,” she whispered.
“Yeah. And I still know exactly what I’m asking.”
She swallowed. “We shouldn’t—”
“We already did,” I murmured. “And we keep doing it. And we keep coming back.”
She closed her eyes like the truth was too much. “I don’t know what this is,” she breathed.
“I don’t either.” I lifted my hand, brushing her jaw lightly, giving her room to pull away. “But I know what I feel.”
Her eyes opened slowly, and whatever lived inside them stole the rest of my restraint.
She stepped closer first.
I kissed her second.
It wasn’t gentle. It wasn’t careful. It was months of tension, weeks of quiet yearning, and an entire night of jealousy and secrets ripping their way out of my chest. Her hands gripped my shirt. My mouth claimed hers with a hunger I’d tried too long
to bury. She tasted like wanting and fear and the beginning of something neither of us had the courage to name.
She broke the kiss only long enough to whisper, “Jordan…”
I pressed my forehead to hers. “I’m here. I’m not going anywhere.”
Her lips found mine again,desperate, searching, full of a need she no longer had the strength to deny.
And just like that, the line we’d been tiptoeing around dissolved beneath us.
We crossed it together.
Twenty Five
I didn’t know who moved first.
Maybe it was me.
Maybe it was him.
Maybe it was everything we’d been swallowing for months finally breaking loose.
But when Jordan lifted me,hands firm around my waist, body pressed tightly against mine,I forgot how to breathe. My legs wrapped around him naturally, instinctively, like my body had been rehearsing this moment in secret long before my mind admitted it.
He kissed me like he needed air and I was the only source he trusted.
I felt the wall behind my back before I realized we’d moved at all. His mouth trailed to my throat, slow and worshipful, and something inside me broke open,something I’d been holding together for far too long. My fingers slid into his hair. His breath shook against my skin.
“Tell me you want this,” he whispered, voice low, raw, pleading in a way he probably hated.
“I do,” I breathed. “I really do.”
Guilt flickered faintly at the edges of my mind, like a shadow passing over sunlight. A reminder that a part of me still loved a man who no longer existed in my world. But the guilt didn’t stop me. Not tonight. Not when Jordan looked at me like I wasn’t broken, like I wasn’t someone’s past, like I wasn’t a widow of a marriage that had died slowly long before the papers were signed.
He kissed me again,deeper this time. Messier. More certain.
Then he carried me to my bedroom.
The air shifted the moment we crossed the doorway. It felt intimate and terrifying and right all at once. My sheets still smelled faintly of lavender detergent and motherhood and exhaustion. His presence rewrote the space instantly,turned it warm, heavy, expectant.
He laid me down gently, as if afraid I might disappear if he pressed too hard.
But when he hovered over me, when his fingers traced the line of my hip, when his forehead rested against mine, I could feel the restraint burning through him like a barely held storm.
“Lyssa,” he whispered, voice breaking in all the places he never let anyone hear. “If I touch you right now, I won’t stop.”
“Then don’t stop.”
He closed his eyes like the words wrecked him.
I lifted his face to mine and kissed him,slow at first, then hungry, then desperate.
The rest of our clothes followed gravity and impatience. Skin met skin. Heat tangled with heat. Every breath was a shiver. Every touch was a confession we weren’t brave enough to speak aloud.
He touched me like he wanted to memorize every curve, every sound, every heartbeat. Like he wanted to rebuild something inside me brick by fragile brick. And I let him. I let myself be seen. I let myself be wanted.
When he finally moved with me,slow at first, then deeper, then impossibly close,I gasped into his shoulder, fingers clutching his back.
He murmured my name against my ear like a prayer he never learned how to say properly.
I held onto him like he was the only steady thing in a life that had spun out of control too many times.
The room dissolved. Time loosened. The world shrank down to breath and warmth and movement and the way his hand found mine in the dark.
We came undone together,quietly, breathlessly, like two people who had been holding their pain behind their teeth for far too long.
And when the trembling stopped and our breaths leveled and the world settled back into place, he didn’t move away.
He stayed.
Chest-to-chest.
Legs tangled.
Arms wrapped around my waist like I was something precious rather than something recovering.
I didn’t speak at first. I couldn’t. My heartbeat was still trying to make sense of itself. My body felt like melted sugar and fire all at once. My mind,my mind was a storm of contradictions.
I still loved Isaiah.
Yes.
But not with the same ache.
Not with the same desperation.
Not with the same hope.
What I felt now was nostalgia and grief and gratitude for a chapter that had already ended.
But what I felt with Jordan…
That was something else entirely.
Dangerous.
Unsteady.
Alive.
His thumb stroked the small of my back in slow circles that made my muscles relax against my will. He kissed the top of my head, breath soft and warm. I felt him fighting sleep but refusing to drift off before I did.
“Hey,” he whispered eventually, voice rough from everything we’d just shared. “Talk to me.”
I swallowed slowly. “I don’t know what to say.”
“Then tell me what you’re thinking.”
I shifted slightly so I could see his face. His eyes were tired but soft, open in a way that scared me more than anything else in my life.
“I’m… scared,” I admitted quietly.
“Of me?”
“No. Never you.”
I exhaled shakily. “I’m scared of how easy this feels. How good this feels. How good you feel.”
His jaw tightened slightly. “Because of him.”
“Because of me,” I corrected gently. “Because I’m still sorting through things. Because I don’t want to hurt you.”
He stared at me with an expression that felt like a hand pressed firmly over my heart. “Alyssa, you’re not using me.”
I looked away. “Sometimes I think I am.”
“You’re not,” he repeated, firmer this time. “Trust me—I’d know if you were.”
“What if I’m wrong? What if I ruin what we have because I’m still grieving something else?”
He lifted my chin so I had no choice but to look at him.
“Then we take it slow,” he said softly. “We don’t rush. We don’t label anything. We don’t force anything. We just… stay in this. Whatever this is. And if it grows, it grows.”
“And if it doesn’t?”
He smiled faintly, almost painfully. “It already has.”
My breath caught.
He didn’t kiss me.
He didn’t push.
He didn’t ask for more.
He just pulled me against his chest again and held me like something worth waiting for.
And for the first time in a very long time…
I let myself rest there.
Twenty Six
Morning light always has this rude habit of exposing everything you thought you hid well in the dark.
Especially when you wake up tangled in the arms of a man who is not your ex-husband…yet somehow feels more familiar than your own heartbeat.
Jordan was still asleep when I opened my eyes,his breathing steady, his face relaxed, his hand warm on my hip like it
belonged there. His hair was messy and his mouth slightly parted, and for a terrifying second, I forgot how to inhale properly.
Because he looked… peaceful.
And I hadn’t given anyone peace in years.
I lay still, listening to the soft rise and fall of his chest, feeling the quiet hum of something dangerous beneath my skin. Last night floated back through my mind—not the words, not the explanations, but the way he touched me. The way he held me. The way he stayed.
A warmth spread through my stomach, followed immediately by a cold flicker of guilt.
Isaiah’s name brushed the edges of my mind the way a memory brushes your fingertips when you open an old drawer. Not sharp. Not devastating. Just… there. A presence I no longer knew how to place.
I closed my eyes and exhaled slowly.
I wasn’t betraying a husband.
I wasn’t cheating.
I wasn’t doing anything wrong.
But my heart still had old wiring connected to a life I’d already walked away from. And breaking habits, especially emotional ones, takes longer than a single night.
Jordan shifted behind me, pulling me closer unconsciously. His face nestled into the back of my shoulder like he’d done it a thousand times, and something in me melted in a way that was almost embarrassing.
He didn’t love lightly. That was the terrifying part.
He loved with intention.
He loved with commitment.
He loved with a history that I still didn’t fully know.
And I was… not light to love. Not simple. Not uncomplicated.
I brushed a strand of his hair off his forehead and studied his sleeping face like a coward,because it was safer to feel things when he wasn’t looking back.
A soft knock suddenly echoed from down the hall,tiny, impatient, and followed by the sound of a little fist banging rhythmically.
“Mommy!”
Ray.
Of course.
Her internal clock was more accurate than NASA’s.
I gently eased myself out of Jordan’s arms, careful not to wake him. He murmured something incoherent and reached for me instinctively, fingers brushing my waist before falling away when he realized I was gone.
My heart squeezed.
I slipped out of bed, grabbed my robe, and padded into the hallway where my two-year-old stood with her curls wild and her stuffed unicorn hanging from her hand like a hostage.
“Good morning, baby,” I whispered, scooping her up.
She cupped my cheeks dramatically. “You s’eepy, Mommy.”
I smiled and kissed her forehead. “A little.”
I carried her toward the kitchen, whisper-singing her morning song, trying to calm the storm still swirling in my chest.
Last night changed us.
Not officially.
Not publicly.
But privately, between sheets and shadows, something shifted.
Ray giggled when I set her in her booster seat and handed her a banana. She kicked her little feet happily, humming to herself, blissfully unaware that her mother was having a quiet emotional crisis at 7:45 in the morning.
I was cutting slices of strawberries when footsteps padded softly into the doorway.
I didn’t have to turn to know it was him.
Jordan leaned against the frame, hair a mess, shirt in his hand, looking like he walked straight out of a dream I shouldn’t be having but absolutely did.
His eyes softened when he saw us,me at the counter, Ray in her seat, the morning sun spilling across the kitchen tiles. Something warm flickered through his expression, something that looked suspiciously like belonging.
“Good morning,” he murmured, voice still rough with sleep.
Ray squealed. “Jow-den!”
She still couldn’t pronounce his name right. And he pretended he hated it but secretly adored it.
He walked over and kissed her cheek, letting her grab his face with syrupy fingers without flinching.
“Hey, bug,” he whispered, rubbing her back. “Missed me already?”
She nodded so hard her curls bounced. “Uh-huh.”
He smiled. And then he glanced at me.
Not in a casual way.
Not in a “we’re just friends” way.
Not in a way I could ignore or pretend was something else.
Heat bloomed under my skin.
I hated that I blushed like a teenager around him.
I hated it even more that he noticed every time.
“You sleep okay?” he asked softly.
“I did,” I replied, trying not to sound breathless. “You?”
He gave me a small, crooked smile that made my stomach tighten. “Better than I have in a long time.”
I swallowed hard.
He stepped closer, stopping just behind me,close enough to feel his presence but far enough that he wasn’t assuming anything.
It was his way of saying,
I’m here. But you decide everything.
And God… that made me want to kiss him again.
Ray banged her spoon on the table. “Story! Mommy, story!”
Her timing was perfect. Children have a sixth sense for interrupting tension.
Jordan laughed softly and sat beside her, launching into a dramatic retelling of Goldilocks that involved unexpected plot twists, terrible accents, and exaggerated gestures. Ray was laughing so hard she nearly fell off her seat.
Watching them like that,my daughter glowing, Jordan glowing, my home feeling warmer than it had in years,did something to me.
A part of me whispered,
This could be real.
Another part whispered,
You’re not ready.
But when Jordan glanced at me over Ray’s head,eyes soft, smile small, expression vulnerable,
I realized I was already halfway in, whether I wanted to be or not.
After breakfast, Ray insisted on building a pillow fort in the living room. Jordan helped her, crawling on the floor, letting her order him around like a tiny general. He kept looking back at me as he worked,as if checking that I was okay, that last night hadn’t scared me off, that this morning wasn’t too much.
And maybe that’s what undid me the most.
He was gentle with me.
He was patient with me.
He was steady in all the places I wasn’t.
Ray eventually fell asleep against his chest, tiny hands tangled in his shirt. Jordan just sat there, holding her, breathing in sync with her, looking like the moment was enough to rebuild him.
Something in my chest tightened painfully.
Because I knew
I knew
this was so much deeper than a casual kiss or a physical release or a temporary escape.
This man was slipping into my life like he had always belonged there.
And I was terrified of how good that felt.
I sank onto the couch beside him, watching both of them.
“Jordan,” I whispered.
He looked over slowly, arm tightening protectively around Ray.
“Yeah?”
I hesitated, words stuck between fear and wanting.
But then he reached for my hand,carefully, gently, like asking permission.
I let him take it.
And for the first time since everything in my life fell apart…
I didn’t pull away.
Twenty Seven
Morning sunlight spills across my bedroom like warm honey, soft and lazy, as if the universe has decided to take its time today. For once, I want to follow its lead. No rushing, no running, no fear of being seen or photographed. Just a normal morning with my daughter,my safe place, my entire world,wrapped in a blanket beside me.
Ray is awake before I even open my eyes. I feel her little fingers tapping my cheek, her voice trying to whisper but failing adorably. “Mama… mamaaaa… wake up, I hungry.” Her big green eyes blink at me with all the seriousness a two-year-old can muster, which isn’t much, because she bursts into giggles the moment I groan.
I roll to my side and pull her on top of me, burying my face into her soft curls. She smells like baby shampoo and sleep, and I swear my entire heart becomes liquid. “Good morning, princess,” I mumble. She responds by patting my face again,roughly, because she still hasn’t figured out her strength,and announces, “Mama, I want pan-cates.”
“Pancakes,” I correct softly, but she shakes her head like I’m the crazy one. “Pan-cates,” she repeats with conviction. I kiss her nose and give up. “Okay. Pan-cates it is.”
Today was supposed to be a quiet day. That was the plan. No costumes. No crowds. No risks. Halloween has always felt like a neon sign flashing EXPOSED over my head, and now that I have a child whose existence the world is one whisper away from discovering, I’ve avoided it like the plague. The paparazzi haven't stopped circling like vultures since I moved to New York, and stepping outside in a costume-filled night felt like tempting fate.
So the plan was simple: cartoons, blanket forts, snacks, and maybe a quiet dinner. Amy could go on her little sexcapade with her British boxer-slash new flavor of the month,God bless that chaotic woman,and Jordan and I… well, later tonight we’d probably end up in each other’s arms again, doing the things we’re still pretending don’t mean anything.
We’re a complicated pair. Touching, kissing, exploring each other like we’re trying to learn the taste of new air, but still acting like titles ruin magic. He’s careful with me. Gentle. But there’s a fire beneath his gentleness that I pretend not to feel every time he presses me to a wall or whispers my name against my throat.
Today was supposed to be simple.
I lift Ray off me and slide out of bed as she toddles behind me toward the kitchen, dragging her unicorn plushie like she’s reenacting a dramatic breakup scene. I start the coffee. She climbs into her seat and bangs the table until I mix her batter. This is us. Our little bubble. Perfectly unremarkable. Perfectly safe.
Except safe doesn't last long in this house.
The moment I flip the second pancake, the front door opens without knocking. Ray gasps like someone just announced Christmas. “Jodannnn!” She squeals the name in her usual mispronunciation,Jodan, not Jordan,and instantly abandons her breakfast.
I sigh because of course he has a key. I blame myself for that. One emergency turned into two, which turned into the casual normality of him walking into my home like it was his. And deep down, I don’t mind it. Actually, I love it far more than I should.
He appears in the doorway, hair a bit messy, sweater sleeves pushed up, smile warm enough to melt glaciers. Ray barrels into his legs before he even finishes stepping in. He laughs and scoops her up, lifting her like she’s weightless. “Good morning, princess,” he murmurs, kissing her cheek loudly until she squeals.
I watch them, my heart betraying me as usual, glowing painfully. I hate how easy this feels. How natural. How much it looks like a picture I was never supposed to want again.
“You’re early,” I say, plating pancakes to distract myself. “We weren’t expecting you until ten.”
“That’s because Amy texted me at six in the morning,” he says, rolling his eyes. “Something about getting ‘the sexy man’s opinion’ on Halloween costumes.” His tone carries mock suffering, but his grin gives him away. “And she said you’re refusing to celebrate today.”
I stiffen immediately. He notices. He always does.
“It’s not refusal,” I say quietly. “It’s safety.”
He nods like he understands, then sets Ray in her chair. “Okay. But hear me out. What if we all stayed inside, dressed the kids
up, let them have fun, maybe buy candy for a fake trick-or-treating around the apartment?”
“That’s actually not a bad—”
“—or,” he interrupts gently, his voice dipping low in that way that makes my spine react before my brain does, “you could trust me to keep you both safe outside. Just once. Just tonight.”
I freeze, spatula mid-air.
He moves closer, his voice soft enough that only I can hear him. “You don’t live in hiding anymore, Lys. You deserve to have fun too.”
I hate how much those words hit me. I hate how much he wants me to live. But I hate most how much I want to say yes.
Before I can respond, Amy bursts into the apartment like a tornado in leggings, holding Emeric on one hip and a red lace bag on the other. “ALRIGHT, PEOPLE!” she shouts dramatically. “It’s Halloween and nobody is sitting at home crying over their ex-husband tonight! Except maybe the ex-husband!”
I glare. Jordan chuckles. Ray claps like this is entertainment.
“I told you,” I groan. “I’m not—”
“Oh shut it,” she snaps, tossing a costume onto my counter. “You’re Wonder Woman, Jordan’s Superman, I’m Catwoman, the kids are adorable, and we are going trick-or-treating whether you like it or not.”
“I didn’t agree to—”
“You will.” She points at Jordan. “Use your charm.”
He doesn’t even hesitate. He walks behind me, close enough that his chest brushes my back, close enough that I can smell his cologne, close enough that I know he knows what he’s doing.
His fingers graze the counter near mine, and his voice dips into something sinful.
“We’ll keep you safe,” he murmurs, his breath warm at my ear. “I promise.”
My entire body betrays me with a shiver. He feels it. Of course he feels it.
Amy’s eyes go wide. “Oooooh he’s good.”
I fan myself exaggeratedly in self-defense. “Absolutely not. Manipulation is not allowed before breakfast.”
Jordan steps back with a smirk, smug and devastating.
I try to resist. I do. But Ray tugs at my shirt, whispering, “Mama… I want pwetty dress.”
And that’s it. That’s the end of me.
Two hours later, I’m in a Wonder Woman costume, staring at myself in the mirror and wondering how the hell peer pressure still works on grown adults. Amy slinks out in her Catwoman suit, yelling “MEOW BITCHES!” like she’s possessed. Jordan emerges as Superman, and I swear my lungs forget their primary job. Ray twirls in her Princess Sofia gown, looking like an angel. Emeric proudly does the Paw Patrol salute. The whole scene is so cute it hurts.
Despite every nerve in my body screaming anxiety, the kids’ excitement wins. We step outside into the cool October night, bundled in warmth, surrounded by lights and decorations. For the first thirty minutes, everything feels perfect. Children run past dressed as skeletons and witches. Parents laugh. Amy and I gossip. Jordan keeps a protective hand hovering near my back. Ray refuses to let go of his thumb. It feels like a family. It feels like something I shouldn’t want.
Then it happens.
A group of teenagers walking past slows down, squints at me, then at Ray, then at Jordan. My heart drops. One of the girls points, whispering rapidly, her phone already rising like a gun being cocked.
“Oh my God… is that—”
I grab Ray instinctively.
Jordan steps forward immediately. “No pictures.”
But it’s too late. One flash goes off. Then another. Then screaming. Then running. Someone yells my full name. Someone else says “whose baby.” Another shouts “Is that her new boyfriend?!”
Everything spirals in seconds.
Jordan grabs my waist. “We’re leaving. Now.”
Security forms around us instantly, but the damage is done. Phones are up everywhere. Flashes blind me. Ray starts crying, terrified by the noise. My heart cracks open at the sound. Emeric cries next. Amy curses loudly. Jordan picks Ray up and shields her head as he pushes us toward the SUV waiting nearby.
We pile in. Doors slam. The car launches forward.
My breathing is shaky. My hands tremble uncontrollably. Jordan hands Ray to me and grabs my face gently, forcing my eyes to meet his.
“You’re safe,” he whispers firmly. “Both of you are safe.”
Ray sobs into my chest. I hold her so tightly I almost crush her. Amy tries to soothe Emeric, but even she looks shaken.
My phone explodes with notifications. I don’t open them. I already know what’s happening. My name is trending. Photos of
us are everywhere. The costume didn’t hide me. The night didn’t protect me. I walked into the world and it swallowed us whole.
My throat tightens painfully. “Jordan… I shouldn’t have gone out. I shouldn’t have listened. I just put her in danger. I should have—”
“Stop,” he says, voice rough with emotion. “This is not your fault. I promised to protect you and I will. I always will.”
Something in me breaks open then,terror, relief, longing, everything tangled into one overwhelming spiral. I press my forehead to Ray’s hair and let myself breathe.
Tonight was supposed to be simple.
Instead, it changed everything.
And deep down, I know this is only the beginning.
Twenty Eight
The moment the car doors slammed behind us, I felt my world fracture into a thousand pieces. Even with the SUV pulling into the garage, the flashes from the paparazzi followed us like relentless fireflies. They had positioned themselves perfectly, as though anticipating every movement, every breath. My
stomach lurched, a cold, heavy weight settling like cement. Ray whimpered against me, clutching at my sweater with tiny fists, and I pressed her closer, trying to make myself smaller, invisible, untouchable.
Jordan was already out, moving with controlled fury, his long legs cutting through the corridor between the flashes, body shielding Ray like she was a priceless gem. “Follow me,” he ordered, and I obeyed instinctively, heart hammering, fingers clutching the straps of my bag as if that would anchor me. Emeric clung to Amy, his sleepy little arms tightening around her neck, and I glimpsed Amy’s face,flushed but composed, already in publicist-mode.
“You idiots,” she muttered under her breath as we passed, a low growl, fingers dancing over her phone, fingers flying faster than I could follow. I caught a glimpse of her screen: multiple browser tabs open, messages being sent, calls being made, tweets being flagged for deletion. She was fighting a war, and the battlefield was our lives.
I wanted to run. I wanted to vanish. I wanted to press my palms to my eyes and make the world stop. Instead, I was forced forward, shoved into the hallway by security and Jordan’s firm grip. My phone vibrated relentlessly in my pocket, a chorus of doom. Notifications.
Tweets. Instagram posts. Every single one containing my face, Ray’s face, my name splashed across headlines. Mystery Baby Revealed. Alyssa Spotted with Daughter: Is Isaiah the Father or Jordan? Secret Child Exposed.
“Oh God,” I whispered, my voice breaking as tears welled in my eyes. “Oh God, Jordan… they’ll see… Isaiah…” My fingers trembled, reaching for my face, trying to shield it from the chaos.
Jordan dropped to one knee beside me, catching my chin gently. His thumb brushed across my cheek, and his eyes held mine with a steady, unshakable force. “He won’t see anything yet,” he said, firm. “Amy’s on it. I’m on it. You are not alone. Ray is safe. Emeric is safe. You are safe. Nothing else matters right now.”
But my mind raced uncontrollably. I imagined Isaiah scrolling through his phone, spotting Ray’s face, piecing together timelines, thinking, accusing. My chest tightened as if someone had wrapped a steel band around it. The panic rose like a tide I couldn’t fight. My knees buckled slightly, and I felt Jordan’s strong hands anchoring me, holding me upright against the tidal wave of dread.
Amy’s voice cut sharply through the room. “We need to get control now!” she snapped, urgency layered over calm efficiency. “I’m flagging posts, contacting editors, calling social media managers. If these images don’t come down in the next twenty minutes, it’s going viral, and we have no control.” She barked orders into her phone, multitasking like a seasoned general, her other hand brushing Emeric’s hair back from his forehead.
Jordan’s jaw tightened. He stepped into the fray, pulling out his phone and tapping into a network of contacts I barely even knew existed. “I’ve got three editors on standby. We can pull the most damaging photos off the feeds before they trend nationwide. I need your full cooperation, Lys. Stay calm, stay put,” he instructed, and I nodded, though trembling inside.
I could feel my pulse in my temples. Panic clawed through me, hot and suffocating. Images of Ray being recognized flooded my mind: toddlers pointed at on playgrounds, strangers whispering, fans posting every photo online. I felt like I’d been exposed, raw and bleeding, in front of the world I’d tried to keep
at bay for two years. Every instinct screamed at me to protect her, to shield her from this spotlight, but there was no hiding anymore.
Amy finally dropped her phone, breathing heavily, but still working through contacts, speaking rapidly. “I’ve got the legal team ready. Jordan, your connections,push them now. We need takedown notices and media control immediately. Lys, stay with the kids, do not move until I say so.”
I nodded numbly, gripping Ray tightly as she buried her face into my chest, tiny tears soaking into my sweater. Emeric stirred but didn’t wake fully, lulled by Amy’s calm presence. I felt utterly helpless, and the guilt was immediate and crushing. Isaiah. Oh God, Isaiah. I pictured him seeing this chaos, seeing my child, the world dissecting our lives while he was left out,or worse, thinking I had betrayed something sacred in keeping Ray hidden from him. My fingers twitched helplessly as if I could claw back the images.
Jordan crouched beside me again, his strong presence radiating calm in a room that had gone mad. He pressed a finger to my lips, firm yet gentle. “Lys, listen. None of this changes who you are or who she is. We contain this now, together. I’ve got this. Amy has this. You have us. That’s all that matters.”
I leaned against him, shuddering, heart hammering violently in my chest. His scent, that intoxicating mix of leather and wood and something uniquely Jordan, grounded me. Desire mingled with fear, and for a moment, I wanted to escape the chaos by clinging to him, by letting every rational thought dissolve into the warmth of his arms.
The children, sensing tension but too young to understand its gravity, clung to us both. Ray’s tiny fingers kneaded into my shirt, and I whispered, “It’s okay, princess. Mama’s here.
You’re safe.” Emeric stirred again, letting out a little sigh, and Amy smoothed his hair back with a practiced hand. The chaos outside seemed momentarily distant, swallowed by the need to protect these tiny, perfect lives.
Jordan leaned in, his lips brushing the top of my head, and whispered against my hair, “You did nothing wrong. You are not the story. The world doesn’t get to touch her. Not now. Not ever.”
I closed my eyes, letting his words wash over me, but panic clawed at the edges. My mind spun with images of headlines, of comments speculating about Ray’s father, about me being a mother in hiding. I could feel the eyes of the world pressing through every wall, through every shuttered window, into our home, into our lives.
Amy’s phone rang, shrill and urgent. She answered quickly, nodding and speaking in rapid bursts. “Yes… yes… take it down… immediately… flag the post… contact your manager… we need every editor on standby… yes, I understand…” She hung up and turned to us, eyes blazing with controlled fury. “We’ve slowed it. Not fully contained, but slowed. We need to get you all inside, secured, now. Jordan, make sure you lock this down.”
Jordan rose, placing one last reassuring hand on my shoulder. “Come on,” he said, and we moved toward the inner apartment, Ray pressed tightly against me. Each flash outside felt like knives slicing through my chest. Every phone recording felt like betrayal.
Once inside, doors locked, blinds drawn, I finally allowed myself to sink into the sofa, Ray curled against my side, Emeric still asleep on Amy’s shoulder. The adrenaline that had kept me
upright melted, leaving only raw, trembling exhaustion. Tears rolled down my cheeks freely, unrestrained.
Jordan knelt beside me, brushing wet strands of hair from my face. “You’re safe now,” he said quietly. “We’ve got this under control. Nothing can touch you or her inside these walls.”
I clutched Ray to me, feeling the guilt and panic still swirling inside. “It’s everywhere,” I whispered. “It’s online. People… they’ve already seen her face. They’ll know… they’ll figure out who she is. What if Isaiah… what if…” My voice faltered entirely.
Jordan tipped my chin up, forcing my gaze to meet his. “None of that matters right now. We can control this. We will control this. Amy’s working magic, I’m using my contacts, and you,stay with Ray. You keep her safe. That’s your job. The rest is mine.”
I let myself cling to him, heart racing, body trembling. Desire, fear, guilt, and relief tangled together. I wanted to kiss him, to anchor myself to him in the only way that felt real amid the chaos. And when I did, his lips met mine with a heat that drew out all the fear, if only momentarily, replacing it with urgency, need, and a strange, grounding comfort.
The storm outside still raged, but inside these walls, Jordan’s presence, Amy’s fierce competence, and Ray’s innocent sleep created a bubble of fragile calm. And yet, I couldn’t shake the gnawing fear that Isaiah might already know, that the world would continue to intrude, that the fragile bubble could shatter in the next moment. I pressed my lips to Jordan’s again, needing the only safety I could control: him, his warmth, his certainty.
In that moment, amidst chaos, panic, and media frenzy, I realized something terrifying and liberating: the world could expose me, they could dissect me, they could scream my secrets
into the void,but in this room, in his arms, I could still breathe. And for the first time tonight, I let myself do just that.
Twenty Nine
Isaiah
I puked all my gut into the bowl again, groaning as the acidic taste burned the back of my throat. I just wanted everything to end. One peaceful moment, one second of quiet, seemed impossible. The chemo days were hellish, a war my body fought without mercy, leaving me battered and raw.
“Drink the water,” Leo said softly, holding the bottle out to me. I opened my mouth to give him a crude remark, but before a word could leave my lips, another wave of nausea hit, and I vomited again into the bowl. My throat burned from the acid, my chest tightened, and every muscle in my body screamed. Painkillers did little to dull the torment.
I hate this. I hate cancer. I hate chemo. I hate the helplessness that accompanies every breath. I hate feeling my body betray me, turning against me in ways I cannot control. My lungs, once strong, wheezed with every shallow intake of air. My legs felt like lead. My skin, clammy and pale, was slick with sweat.
I could feel my nerves fraying, my composure slipping in thin, ragged strips.
“There is blood—is it normal?” Julian’s voice came, muffled yet insistent.
“Yes. There is nothing wrong; he’ll be okay,” the nurse replied, calm and professional.
I waited, lying on the sterile hospital sheets, stomach churning, hoping the nausea would finally subside. After five minutes of relative quiet, I handed the bowl to the nurse for disposal and leaned back, letting the bed cradle my trembling body.
Pain radiated through me like a constant electric current, and the only relief came from the brief pause between nausea waves. I could feel the disease inside me, gnawing relentlessly. I had known this day would come, but knowing it didn’t make it any easier.
Finally, I exhaled slowly, letting my mind wander to the unspoken truths I’d been avoiding for months. Parkinson’s disease. A rare, aggressive form of lung cancer,small-cell neuroendocrine carcinoma,that had spread to my brain. The doctors had been honest, brutal in their words: there was no cure. None.
