Only aggressive chemotherapy, experimental treatments, and a hope so thin it felt like holding onto smoke.
I hated being honest about it, even to myself. I hated the vulnerability. But I couldn’t hide it anymore. Not from the sterile lights, not from the cold hospital walls, and certainly not from my own consciousness, which refused to let me pretend.
I thought about the tremors, the stiffness, the way my body had started betraying me long before the diagnosis became official. I thought about the cancer cells that didn’t respect my plans or my history, the ones now inching into my brain, rewriting the
rules of my life without permission. The chemotherapy might slow it,maybe,but there were no guarantees. No promises.
I let myself feel it now. The fear. The exhaustion. The sorrow. The rage. All of it swirled inside me, heavy and suffocating.
An hour later, I woke from a brief nap, restless and still nauseated, though the worst of it had passed. My phone lay beside me, screen lit, Netflix paused mid-scene. I scrolled absentmindedly, trying to distract myself. There was nothing else to do. I couldn’t walk far; every movement left my breath ragged, shallow, desperate. The oxygen machine hissed softly beside my bed, a lifeline I had come to rely on in a way I hated.
The quiet was broken when Leo entered the room. His eyes widened slightly, a mixture of surprise and urgency etched on his face. “Isaiah… you need to see this,” he said, holding his phone like it was a detonator. My chest tightened instinctively, the premonition twisting in my gut.
“What is it?” I croaked, voice hoarse, throat raw.
He gestured for me to focus on the screen. I did, even though my mind fought, even though every instinct screamed to hide. The headlines blurred at first: Alyssa Spotted with Mystery Baby  Who’s the Father? Alyssa’s Daughter Revealed, Speculation Explodes Online. Screenshots of tweets and Instagram posts flooded my vision.
Comments, thousands of them, rolled in at a speed I couldn’t comprehend. People were speculating, pointing fingers, twisting the story in every direction.
My heart stopped when I saw the photograph. My breath hitched painfully, like someone had reached into my chest and squeezed everything tight. There she was. A little girl,tiny, maybe two years old,with wild brown curls and eyes so green they practically glowed from the screen. She was perched on
Alyssa’s hip, her small hand gripping Alyssa’s blouse, her round cheeks flushed as she squinted at the camera.
I didn’t know her name.
I didn’t know anything about her.
I hadn’t even known Alyssa was pregnant.
But the second I looked at her… something in me fractured.
Those eyes.
God,those eyes.
My lungs forgot how to work. My fingers twitched violently. I leaned forward as if getting closer would change the reality staring me in the face. The shape of her eyes, the soft curl of her lashes, the exact shade of bright, startling green.
It was me.
She looked like me.
Down to the little crease at the corner of her left eye.
Down to the same stubborn, determined stare I’ve had all my life.
I didn’t need a DNA test. Not for this. Not for her. Some truths lived in the blood. Some truths punched you straight in the soul the moment you saw them.
This one did.
My hands shook so hard I had to grip the blanket. My voice barely crawled out of my chest.
“Leo…”
He didn’t answer at first.
He was staring just as hard, jaw clenched, eyes wide with disbelief.
“Holy shit,” he whispered. “Isaiah.”
I swallowed, or tried to. My throat felt scraped raw. “Alyssa… has a child?”
“She never announced anything,” Leo murmured. “There were no pictures, no rumors, nothing for the past two years. But—” His eyes flicked to me again. “Man… she looks like you.”
My stomach twisted, churned, collapsed in on itself. My chest trembled under the weight of something too big to name.
“I… I didn’t know,” I whispered. My voice cracked in the middle, thin and broken. “I didn’t even know she was pregnant.”
The guilt hit next,sharp, catastrophic.
A tidal wave with knives in it.
My mind raced backward: the divorce, the silence, the distance I’d let grow between us like a wall I was too tired to climb. I’d been drowning in work, in fame, in pressure, in sickness,God, the sickness,and she’d been carrying this alone?
Carrying her alone?
My chest locked up completely.
If I wasn’t already leaning against the pillows, I would’ve collapsed.
My breath stuttered, harsh and painful, as if my lungs were made of gravel.
Leo placed a hand on my arm, like he thought I’d pass out.
“Isaiah. Hey. Breathe, okay? Slow, slow.”
But slow wasn’t an option.
Nothing about this moment was slow.
Everything was hitting me too fast,like the universe had waited for the exact second my body was at its weakest to drop the heaviest truth of my life onto my chest.
“I have… a daughter,” I said, the words tasting foreign and electric and devastating. “Leo, I have a daughter.”
The sentence shattered me.
It felt unreal, surreal, impossible,except it wasn’t.
She was right there on the screen.
Real.
Alive.
Two years old.
Two years of first laughs, first steps, first words…
Two years I missed.
Two years I didn’t know.
A tremor started in my hands and spread through my arms, my chest, my spine. Leo saw it instantly.
“Isaiah, relax. Hey. You’re going to trigger the spasms again—”
“I missed everything,” I choked out. “Her whole life. Leo—Alyssa never told me. She didn’t tell me. Why didn’t she tell me?”
Leo didn’t answer.
Maybe he couldn’t.
The picture was still glowing from his phone.
The little girl was looking into the camera with a half-smile, her green eyes cutting straight through me.
God, she looked like me.
Down to the exact shade.
Down to the tiny stubborn tilt of her chin.
“She’s mine,” I whispered, voice breaking in half. “I—I know she is. That’s my child.”
Leo inhaled sharply but didn’t disagree.
How could he?
The proof was staring both of us down.
And that’s when the panic hit full force.
Not the frantic kind.
Not the loud kind.
The deep kind.
The crushing kind.
The kind that steals the air from your lungs and leaves nothing but ache and regret behind.
My breath shortened.
My chest tightened.
The room spun slightly, tilting like a sinking ship.
Not now, I begged my own body. Not now, not now,please not now.
Leo moved fast, adjusting the oxygen tubes near my bed.
“Isaiah, look at me. Breathe through your nose. Slow it down. Stay with me.”
But I could barely hear him.
All I could see was her.
A little girl who didn’t know her father existed.
A little girl I had never held.
Never kissed.
Never protected.
A little girl who might grow up without me if this cancer kept spreading.
My vision blurred again,tears or dizziness, I couldn’t tell.
Probably both.
“Why didn’t she tell me?” I whispered again, voice small, broken, afraid. “Leo… why didn’t Alyssa tell me?”
Leo hesitated, then spoke gently, “Maybe she thought you wouldn’t want the child. Maybe she was protecting herself. Maybe she didn’t want a scandal. Maybe… she was just hurt.”
I shook my head violently. “No. No, Leo,I should’ve known. I should’ve been there. I should’ve…” My voice faltered, collapsed. “I should’ve protected them.”
He squeezed my arm.
“You didn’t know, Zay.”
“But I should have,” I said again, softer now, a whisper soaked in regret.
I leaned back slowly, letting my head hit the pillow because I couldn’t hold myself up anymore. The nausea was back. The tremors were worse. And the ache spreading through my chest was nothing chemo could explain.
But under all the shock and panic and heartbreak, something else flickered,weak but stubborn.
A spark.
A promise forming in the middle of the chaos.
My daughter exists.
And I will not leave this world without knowing her.
I stared at the screen again, my breath finally evening out.
Somewhere deep inside my exhausted, failing body… resolve settled in my bones.
I didn’t know her name.
I didn’t know her voice.
I didn’t know what she liked, what she laughed at, what she was afraid of.
But I knew one thing with absolute certainty:
That little girl was mine.
And I was going to fight for the chance to be her father,even if it killed me.
Thirty
As I expected, I received a message , a formal note, through Dad, who had flown in from LA with Mom the day after the Incident.
When he went back to LA, he was greeted at the door by a letter from Isaiah’s lawyer.
It didn’t “begin,” really. It demanded.
It demanded that I schedule a formal meeting with Isaiah’s team. Not Isaiah himself , because God forbid the man actually act like a father or a human being , but his team. And that team wanted a paternity test.
After all these years, he was still the same pathetic coward being puppeteered by people who only cared about his image.
And the fact that Julian and Leo were the ones who contacted me? Not Isaiah?
It made my blood boil.
I told Dad to tell them I declined the meeting. My daughter is mine. Alone. Period.
I have been silent enough. I have been used enough. For ten years of marriage, I let them control me because I didn’t want to ruin his reputation.
But not anymore.
Now I am my own person, and I’ll protect my daughter from their hungry eyes.
Ray won’t be paraded as some PR miracle baby.
That was two weeks ago.
They haven’t said anything since.
Good.
But peace hasn’t existed in my world since Ray’s identity went public. I thought the press would calm down after a week.
Instead, they multiplied like demons with cameras.
Amy and I were trapped in our apartments for three entire days because someone breached our security system,and photographers were literally standing at our doors.
We had to increase security and hire more personnel.
The only person allowed in or out without chaos was Jordan.
Sometimes he even stayed overnight because my paranoia had me checking every window and door at ridiculous hours.
And Ray… my sweet, tiny chaos queen…
She is an adorable attention seeker.
Every time we go out on the balcony for fresh air and the paparazzi start snapping photos, she waves like she’s greeting her imaginary subjects. Then she giggles, proud of herself as if she just won an award.
I can genuinely see her in the spotlight one day , that confidence is in her bones.
Now she’s with me everywhere. Her nanny, Clara, too. I take her to rehearsals, to the studio, everywhere except interviews.
She loves going because, well… her favorite human exists there.
Jordan.
And now she has Sammy wrapped around her little fingers, which is hilarious because Sammy used to claim he “doesn’t do kids.”
I’m always alert though , wary of paparazzi and wary of Isaiah’s silence.
His silence has always been the loudest warning.
---
We finished our biggest show yet, the entire theatre packed. Every seat sold out the moment tickets dropped — even with the chaos swirling around my life.
I bowed, waved, and picked up the single rose tossed onto the stage.
When the curtains closed, I sighed, letting the adrenaline melt off my skin as I walked backstage.
The first thing I heard was a squeal.
“Mummy! So pretty!”
Ray barreled toward me, tiny arms raised, her curls bouncing. Clara was behind her, trying to keep up.
I scooped my girl into my arms, showering her cheeks with kisses as she giggled.
“You think I was pretty, baby?”
“Yes! Pwitty like pwetty princess!” She clapped her hands excitedly.
If joy had a sound, it would be her laugh.
I twirled her gently, and she shrieked happily, gripping my shoulders with her tiny hands.
Jordan appeared from the side, leaning against the wall, smiling at us like we were his whole world.
The man had the audacity to look soft.
“Did Mummy do well tonight?” he asked her.
Ray nodded so hard her curls slapped her face. “Mummy best! Best in da whool world!”
I laughed. “You’re going to make me spoil you even more.”
“You alweady spoil me,” she declared proudly.
Jordan snorted. “She’s not wrong.”
I shot him a look, and he walked closer, placing a kiss on Ray’s forehead before brushing his fingers lightly down my cheek.
“You were beautiful out there,” he murmured.
His hand slipped behind my waist, and he leaned in. I didn’t resist.
His lips brushed mine , soft at first, then deeper , the kind that made warmth rush straight to my stomach.
Ray giggled behind her little fingers. “Mummy kissing!”
Jordan laughed against my lips. “Your daughter is a narc.”
“I heard that,” Ray said dramatically, even though she definitely didn’t know what “narc” meant.
I held her on my hip as Jordan leaned in again.
This kiss was hotter, hungrier. My fingers curled into his shirt, and my heart thudded.
God, if we weren’t in public, I’d drag him home and climb him like a tree—
“DADDY!”
A little voice cut through everything.
Jordan stiffened instantly.
We turned.
A small boy , maybe six , sprinted toward us. Blond hair. Brown eyes. The same shade as Jordan’s. The resemblance slapped me.
He wrapped himself around Jordan’s leg.
Jordan froze.
“Daddy! I found you!”
My lungs stopped working.
Jordan’s face drained of color.
“Colton… hey, buddy… what are you doing here?”
Son?
My arms tightened around Ray instinctively. She sensed my body shift and tucked herself closer to me.
Before Jordan could say anything else, a woman appeared behind the boy.
Tall.
Gorgeous.
Dressed like she stepped out of a magazine shoot.
Her eyes swept over Jordan… then me… then Ray…
And her smirk sharpened.
“Well,” she drawled, “I see you ran straight to your little show after the performance.”
Jordan’s jaw clenched. “Why are you here? And why did you bring him without telling me?”
She scoffed. “Our son wanted to see his father. You know… his father? The one who barely visits?”
Ray frowned, burying her face in my neck.
I swallowed hard. “Jordan…?”
He turned quickly. “Alyssa, it’s not what she’s implying—”
“Oh, don’t humiliate yourself,” the woman cut in sharply.
She looked at me with fake sympathy.
“Did he not tell you? About me?”
Jordan snapped, “Stop.”
She ignored him and kept going...
“He loved to pretend he was single. He loved pretending he wasn’t sleeping in my bed while chasing someone else.”
Jordan inhaled sharply. “That’s a lie. Alyssa, she’s twisting everything.”
The woman raised an eyebrow. “Oh? Did you tell her how you ghosted your son? Or how you show up maybe twice a month , on good weeks?”
Colton looked up at Jordan with sad eyes. “Daddy… you don’t want to see me?”
Jordan’s voice cracked. “Of course I do. Buddy, I—”
“You’re always with her now,” the woman continued, gesturing at me as if I were something stuck to her shoe.
“The actress. The one with the baby. Playing house with someone else’s man.”
My pulse thudded painfully.
I stepped back, Ray clinging to me.
Jordan moved quickly. “Alyssa, please don’t listen to her. She’s doing this on purpose.”
The woman smirked , a wicked, victorious curve.
The kind of smile that only appears when someone knows they’ve successfully delivered a fatal blow.
“It’s always the same type,” she said casually.
“The mistress pretending she didn’t know.”
My throat burned.
My chest tightened.
Ray whimpered softly, confused and scared.
Jordan reached for me.
“Alyssa, please—”
I stepped back.
“No,” I whispered. “Not right now.”
His face shattered. “Let me explain—”
I shook my head. “I need to go.”
The woman folded her arms smugly.
“Run along, sweetheart. Next time choose a man who doesn’t already have a family.”
Jordan barked her name...
“ Kaya!Stop. Enough.”
So now I knew her name.
But I didn’t stay.
I turned and walked away, clutching my daughter like she was the last piece of me not falling apart.
Ray’s tiny voice trembled.
“Mummy… don’t cry…”
I didn’t even realize I was.
Clara rushed to us, eyes widening.
“We’re leaving,” I said, barely a whisper.
She nodded instantly, guiding us out the back exit as Jordan yelled my name somewhere behind us.
I didn’t turn around.
I couldn’t.
I walked out of the theater with my daughter in my arms, tears burning down my cheeks, while the man I was beginning to trust , beginning to fall for ,broke apart behind me.
And just like that…
My fragile new beginning shattered in one public, heartbreaking moment.
Thirty One
I stepped out into the back parking lot, my pulse still hammering in my ears, but even out here I could hear Jordan calling after me. His voice carried across the asphalt, closer than I wanted it to be. I didn’t even need to turn around to know he was chasing me, but I did anyway,and there he was, actually running toward me.
And behind him?
The woman.
Kaya.
Carrying the little boy whose name I’d barely managed to remember,Colton.
My stomach clenched so hard it felt like someone had tied a fist around my ribs and squeezed. My mind kept circling one horrifying thought: He has a child. And not just that,he kept it hidden. For months. While he looked me in the eyes, held me, told me the kind of truths that felt like promises.
I had been honest with him. I had given him all the soft, tender, trembling parts of me. And he,he’d been lying right to my face.
God, I really was a fool.
Here I was thinking he was different from these Hollywood men.
Different from the glamorous, self-obsessed narcissists who cheated their way through promotions and parties and PR scandals.
But apparently, he fit right in. Perfectly.
Jordan reached me, out of breath, eyes wide with panic. “Alyssa, you can’t leave. I promise you—what she’s saying is all lies.”
He stepped closer, but I held my ground. My voice shook, but not with weakness. “So the boy isn’t your son?”
He gave me a look,pleading, cautious, terrified. But not truthful.
“You don’t get it,” he whispered. “Please, baby… let me explain everything.”
I jerked away when he reached to touch my arm. “Don’t touch me. And don’t call me baby. Explain what, Jordan? That you’ve been lying to me all this time while I trusted you? You can’t even answer my question. Is he or is he not your son?”
Before he could respond, she caught up.
Kaya arrived with a click of expensive heels and a cloud of perfume strong enough to resurrect the dead. She set Colton on the ground, smoothing his hair as he tugged at Jordan’s pants. Then she gave me a smile so sweet it should’ve come with a health warning.
“I’m Kaya,” she said brightly. “Jordan’s wife—or should I say, baby mama. Didn’t get to introduce myself earlier.”
Jordan stiffened. “No, you’re not. Kaya, I told you—”
“Oh relax,” she cut in, waving a hand. “I’m just introducing myself. You must be Alyssa.” Her gaze dragged down my outfit like she was reading the price tags. “I’ve heard so much about you. From the internet… and the messy scandals.”
Ray pressed her face into my shoulder, her tiny fingers curling into my sleeve. “Mommy, who’s that lady?” she whispered.
Kaya perked up. “And this must be the little baby the internet keeps talking about.” She reached out as if to touch Ray’s cheek.
Mistake number one.
I stepped back so fast she nearly touched the air instead. “Don’t.”
Ray buried deeper into me, smart girl that she was. Even at two years old, she could smell fake sugar coating a rotten core.
Jordan immediately stepped between us, jaw locked tight. “Kaya, stop. What are you doing here? I told you to call before—”
“Well,” she said with an innocent blink that couldn’t fool a blind man, “if you ever answered your phone, maybe I wouldn’t need to show up unannounced.”
He swallowed. Hard. “I’ve been busy.”
“With her?” Kaya tilted her chin in my direction like I was some charity case he’d picked up on the street. “And her baby?”
"Daddy" Colton called out
Jordan knelt to Colton, resting his hands on the boy’s shoulders. “Hey, buddy. I told you—you weren’t supposed to call me that out loud here.”
Kaya let out a soft laugh. “Jordan, please. He’s a child, not a trained guard dog. He misses you. Let him call his father what he wants.”
Ray tensed again. She peeked up over my shoulder, her big green eyes studying Jordan, studying the boy, studying everything.
Then she whispered something that nearly cracked me open.
“Mommy… Daddy?”
Everything inside me froze.
We didn’t say that word in my home.
Amy and I had shielded both Emeric and Ray from that word because it came loaded with too much pain. Too many memories. Too many scars we weren’t ready to reopen.
And now Ray had heard it.
Now she was looking at Jordan like he was a hero in a story she didn’t understand yet.
Jordan’s eyes instantly softened. He reached a little toward her. “No, sweetheart,” he said gently. “I’m not your—”
“She knows who her father is,” Kaya cut in, sugary sweet. “And clearly so does your son.”
Oh, she was playing dirty.
And she was enjoying every second.
Jordan snapped toward her. “Enough, Kaya.”
“Why?” She shrugged, all faux innocence. “Alyssa deserves to know you’ve been lying to her. You’re good at compartmentalizing, but this?” She smiled, brittle and poisonous. “Even for you, this is impressive.”
Ray looked between all of us, confused and frightened.
I straightened, tightening my hold around her small body. My own pulse thudded painfully.
“Jordan,” I said softly but firmly. “Is any of this true?”
His lips parted, and he looked absolutely terrified. “Alyssa… please. Let me explain. It’s not what she’s making it sound like.”
Kaya raised her brows. “Really? So you didn’t have an affair with your father’s wife? You didn’t get me pregnant? You didn’t drop off the face of the earth while you played house with her in New York?”
For a second, my world tilted.
“What?” I whispered.
His father’s wife?
What in God’s name…?
That explained her maturity, her energy, her entire smug performance.
Jordan’s hands trembled. “Kaya—”
Colton frowned up at him. “Dad? Are you in trouble?”
Jordan flinched like he’d been shot.
And that was it.
That was the moment everything clicked painfully, like bones snapping out of place.
The tension.
The hesitation.
The secrets he kept dodging.
The nights where something haunted him but he’d never say what.
I had spent months choosing him.
Trusting him.
Allowing myself to fall, knowing how badly I could break.
And he didn’t tell me about this.
Jordan reached for me again. “Alyssa, please don’t walk away. Not like this. Just give me a second—”
But I was already opening the car door. I gently handed Ray to Clara, who immediately lifted her into the seat to buckle her in. Ray pressed her forehead against my collarbone, sensing every ounce of my unraveling.
“Mommy home?” she whispered, voice trembling.
“Yes, sweetheart,” I managed, though my throat was tight and aching. “We’re going home.”
“Jo-jo coming?” she asked softly, trying to peek around me to see him.
My heart twisted, but I kept my voice steady. “No, baby. Jo-jo’s not coming.”
“Alyssa,” Jordan pleaded, sounding absolutely broken. “Don’t go. Just let me talk. I’m begging you.”
“I asked you,” I whispered, “if you were keeping anything from me. You said no.”
He looked like I’d stabbed him. “I didn’t tell you because I was scared. I didn’t want to lose you.”
“Well,” I said painfully, “you have now.”
He moved toward me again. “Alyssa, I swear—”
“Oh please,” Kaya muttered, rolling her eyes. “Let her go, Jordan. She’s just your latest distraction. She’ll find another man to play baby daddy soon enough.”
Something cold and lethal shifted inside me.
Jordan snapped. “Shut your mouth.”
But I was already done.
I got into the car, carefully, making sure not to push him too hard while his son was right next to him. I closed the door, started the engine, and rolled down the window.
“I don’t ever want to see you anywhere near my daughter or me,” I told him, my voice low, steady, final.
“No—Alyssa, no! Please, just let me explain!” he said, stepping toward the window.
But I was already pulling away.
“We are not done, Alyssa!” he shouted across the parking lot. “You’re my girl! I’m not letting you go that easily!”
Ray sniffled softly in the back seat. “No cry, Mommy.”
“I’m sorry, baby,” I whispered. “Mommy’s just sad.”
“Ice cream make it go ‘way,” she offered gently, hopeful.
I let out a broken laugh. “Thank you, sweetheart.”
“Luv you, Mama.”
“I love you too, baby,” I whispered, my voice cracking for real this time.
And just like that, I drove toward the city lights with yet another breaking heart.
Thirty two
Jordan
I didn’t move.
Not when her taillights disappeared.
Not when the sound of her engine faded.
Not even when the cold slammed into my chest like someone had carved me open and left my ribs exposed.
I just stood there.
Kaya was saying something behind me , I didn’t care. Colton was tugging my sleeve asking if I was okay , I couldn’t answer. The parking lot, the people, the damn buzzing streetlights… everything blurred.
All I could see was her.
Alyssa.
Leaving me.
Driving away with her daughter , with our daughter in all the ways that mattered , while I stood frozen like a coward who’d run out of excuses.
God.
I messed this up.
I messed everything up.
I pressed my hands to my face. My palms were shaking. My throat burned. My stomach twisted so violently I thought I’d throw up right there on the pavement. The air felt too thin, too sharp, like I wasn’t allowed to breathe it anymore.
Because the only person who made breathing feel easy had just told me she never wanted to see me again.
I deserved it.
But fuck, it still ripped me apart.
“Jordan,” Kaya said somewhere behind me, voice fake-sweet and grating. “You can drop the dramatics now. She’s gone.”
I turned around so fast she flinched. “Get out,” I said quietly.
She blinked. “Excuse me?”
“I said get out. Now. Leave.”
Colton looked up at me with wide eyes. “Daddy… you’re scaring me.”
The word “Daddy” stabbed through me again — but now for a completely different reason.
I crouched down to him, brushing a hand through his hair. “Hey, buddy. It’s okay. I’m not mad at you. I’m just…”
Breaking.
Dying.
Losing the one person I’ve ever loved without needing to pretend.
“I’m just having a rough moment,” I said instead.
Kaya rolled her eyes. “Really? Over her? You barely know—”
“Get. Out,” I repeated, standing.
She scoffed, grabbed Colton, and strutted off toward the elevator, mumbling something about me being dramatic. I didn’t watch them leave.
I couldn’t.
Because the second they were gone, I folded.
My hands braced on the hood of the nearest car as everything I’d been holding in, years of shame, guilt, panic, every secret I buried , detonated inside my chest. The sound that came out of me wasn’t human. It was a broken, strangled thing I didn’t know I was capable of making.
She left.
She left.
The one person I thought I might finally be worthy of…
And it was my own damn fault.
I slammed my fist into the metal. The car shook. Pain shot up my arm. I didn’t stop. I kept hitting until the skin split and blood smeared across silver paint.
I deserved worse.
I deserved much worse.
When I couldn’t lift my arm anymore, I stumbled back and sank to the curb. My vision blurred. Tears I hadn’t cried since I was a kid burned down my face. I didn’t even wipe them.
Let them fall.
Let them drown me.
“Alyssa…” I whispered into the empty lot. “Please don’t leave me.”
She wasn’t here.
She wasn’t coming back.
Not tonight. Not soon. Maybe not ever.
The thought hollowed out my chest.
I leaned back against the wall and closed my eyes, replaying everything. Every moment with her. Every laugh. Every soft morning. Every kiss that felt like a promise. Ray calling me “Jo-jo” like it was the sweetest name in the world. The way Alyssa looked at me like I could be more than the mistakes I’ve spent years trying to outrun.
And then , Kaya’s bomb dropping in the middle of everything.
Her timing wasn’t an accident.
She came to ruin this.
To ruin me.
But that didn’t matter.
Because the real problem wasn’t her.
It was me.
I should’ve told Alyssa the truth months ago.
Not the twisted, edited version I fed her.
Not the softened pieces I hid behind.
The real truth , the ugly one.
That I was twenty-three and She was thirty-four.That she was my father’s wife.
That I got pulled into something I didn’t fully understand until it was too late.
That it wasn’t love, it wasn’t a romance , it was manipulation and guilt and pressure I didn’t see because I was desperate for someone to tell me I wasn’t worthless.
And worst of all , Colton, didn’t ask for any of this.
How could I tell Alyssa that story without making her look at me differently? Without losing the fragile, beautiful thing we’d begun?
I couldn’t. So I didn’t.
And that cowardice…
That fear…
Just cost me the woman I love.
God, I love her.
Not the surface kind.
Not the “you’re pretty and convenient” kind.
No — the bone-deep kind.
The-
I-breathe-better-when-I’m-near-you  kind.
The kind where you start imagining futures before you even realize you’re doing it.
I saw myself in her life.I saw myself making pancakes with Ray.
I saw myself fixing the back porch of her New York house.
I saw myself backstage on opening night, holding flowers like a fool, waiting for her to glow under the lights again.
I saw a family.
A real one.
Something I’ve never truly had.
And now?
I might’ve lost all of it.
I dropped my head into my hands and breathed through the ache ripping me apart.
“Alyssa,” I whispered again. “Baby, please… don’t let this be the end.”
But the parking lot didn’t answer.
My phone buzzed. Once. Twice. Then continuously.
Amy.
Probably screaming at me.
Rightfully so.
I ignored it.
No.
No, I couldn’t stay away.
I’d already lost too much.
And I wasn’t going to lose them too.
I pulled my phone and texted her. Telling her I wasn't leaving her without a fight,and I was going to explain everything. That she was mine and mine alone. I'll never give up on her.
I pressed send and walked to my car.
I got in my car and slammed the door shut. My hands shook the whole drive. The city lights smeared past my windshield. I ran every red light, cursed every slow car, punched the steering wheel every time I thought about the look on her face when she whispered:
“You said no.”
“You lied.”
She was right.
I did.
And I swear to God, if she lets me close again, even for a second , I’ll tell her everything.
Ugly.
Raw.
Unfiltered.
The whole truth.
And pray she still sees the man I’ve been trying to become…not the scared boy I used to be.
Thirty three
Isaiah
Alyssa denied the formal meeting between her and Leo.
I knew she would. She had hated him ever since he became my manager when we were married and told me to get rid of him,but like the asshole I was, I didn’t listen.
I wanted fame. Plain and simple. And Leo had the power to make it happen. He was my best shot in Hollywood, my ticket to everything I ever thought I wanted, and I wasn’t going to throw it away.
I can’t blame her entirely, though. Leo could be insufferable. A power-hungry, money-hungry monster who cared about no one’s opinion but his own.
Yet, right now, he was the only one I had. Even though I would never admit it aloud, he had been my anchor through this hell. He had been the one keeping me alive, keeping me fed, keeping
me shielded from a world that had suddenly stopped giving a damn once the cameras stopped flashing.
My family hadn’t come to see me since Julian told them about my illness. Not that I wanted them here.
Mom had found a new man to occupy her time, my siblings were chasing their own lives. No one cared enough to check on me, to see me as Isaiah, the person, not Isaiah, the success story or the cash machine. I had destroyed the one person who had loved me willingly, unconditionally,who had never seen me as a business venture.
This is why I needed to meet my daughter. She was the only reason to wake up in the morning, the only light I could see at the end of this tunnel.
I knew I should have contacted Alyssa myself, but I was still undergoing chemo. I looked like a ghost. Worse than a ghost. Julian and Tasha had been tirelessly shielding me from the paparazzi, and I was grateful, but it wasn’t enough to make me look human. I was disappearing, thinning, fading… growing smaller with each passing day.
The world didn’t know. The world couldn’t know.
When news of my breakup with Anya spread, it caused tension, but it faded when she announced her pregnancy with her new man. I couldn’t believe it. Two months. Two months, and she was already expecting. Meanwhile, I was fighting for life and dying quietly. I was glad for that,glad the focus wasn’t on me this time.
I had just finished my fifteenth round of chemo. My body was battered, my energy depleted. I was waiting for the results, the verdict that would decide whether I could move forward with surgery to remove the tumors,yes, tumors, plural,or if I would be left with nothing but dwindling time.
If the chemo worked, there was hope for surgery, for radiation to shrink the ones that couldn’t be removed.
If it hadn’t… I was staring at the end.
Leo kept trying to keep my mind afloat. “Stay positive, Zay,” he said. But how could I? How could anyone be positive when the enemy was inside, gnawing at you from the inside out, when survival depended on chance and cruel science?
Still, despite everything, I was going to New York. I needed to see my daughter. I would surprise Alyssa, because telling her would likely mean I’d never see her at all.
I didn’t even know her name.
So many questions tormented me:
“Does she know me?”
“Does she know I love her?”
“Why is that Jordan kid even near her?”
I remembered him at our anniversary, the heat in his gaze. I had been right to be possessive. I would protect my daughter from him, even if she resented me for it later. Alyssa might hate me, but I wouldn’t allow my daughter to grow up around that scumbag.
---
The car ride to the hospital was silent. I watched snow fall against the window. Snow in Los Angeles was rare, but this year it had come early. It was beautiful, pure, and it reminded me of her.
Alyssa had always loved the snow. Christmas was her favorite holiday.
We would ski, decorate our home, buy toys for our neighbors’ children. On Christmas, our mornings were sacred,hot
chocolate, presents, movies. Evenings were for family dinners, then home again, where we closed the day wrapped in each other’s warmth, making love like the world had no tomorrow.
But as the years passed and my ambitions grew, the traditions slowly died. My Christmas mornings became consumed with preparation for lavish parties, leaving no room for us. Alyssa wanted time, real time together, but I kept promising I’d do better, and every year I failed,until she finally stopped asking.
I had been a monster in our marriage. The man she feared. The man she had promised she wouldn’t see me become.
I hoped, somewhere deep down, she would forgive me one day.
“Hey, Zay… you’re shaking,” Leo said, gripping my hands.
“Sorry… just nerves. The spasms are worse since the chemo stopped,” I muttered, leaning against the seat, feeling my body betray me yet again.
I was nervous, trembling for reasons more than my Parkinson’s. What the doctor said would determine everything. My entire life,or what remained of it,hung in the balance.
I had one wish left: to live long enough to meet my daughter.
“The doctor said it will go away. But we have good news. We haven’t been able to locate Alyssa’s home, so we’ve booked the next flight to New York before it gets canceled. The hotel is ready. Everything has been arranged. No paparazzi,” Leo said, tapping on his phone.
“Thanks,” I whispered before closing my eyes and trying to brace myself for what was coming next.
---
The hospital was stark, cold, antiseptic,the kind of place that smelled of despair if you were paying attention. Leo guided me inside, steadying me when my legs faltered.
We passed through a corridor that seemed endless, the fluorescent lights buzzing overhead. There were a few nurses, a couple of patients, but mostly silence.
“Ready?” Leo asked.
“I have to be,” I said.
The oncologist’s office was small but clinical. Dr. Marcus, my oncologist, looked up from his desk with a calm that I envied. He gestured to the chair across from him. I sank into it, Leo beside me, his hand hovering near mine.
“We need to discuss your results, Isaiah,” Dr. Marcus said quietly.
I nodded, my throat tight. My body felt hollow.
“The chemo… it hasn’t worked as we had hoped. The cancer is still spreading, though at a slower pace. We’ve exhausted all viable treatment options,” he continued, each word falling like stones on my chest. “I’m sorry. At this stage… hospice is the only path. Realistically… you have less than a year.”
I heard the words, but they barely registered. It was surreal. My body felt weightless, like I was watching someone else hear the news, someone else’s life being measured in months.
Leo leaned forward. “Dr. Marcus, there has to be something else. Experimental treatments, clinical trials,anything! We can’t just accept this!”
Dr. Marcus shook his head. “We’ve considered everything. Nothing is viable. I’m very sorry.”
Leo’s jaw tightened, frustration and fear flashing in his eyes. “Isaiah—please. Get a second opinion. Anywhere, someone might have something. You can’t just—”
“I know,” I said softly, cutting him off. My voice was barely audible. “I know, Leo. But I’m done fighting what can’t be fought. My body is tired. My time… it’s no longer about survival. It’s about what matters.”
“What matters?” Leo asked, his voice breaking.
“My daughter,” I whispered. “I don’t have much time. Less than a year, maybe… but I want to spend every second I have with her. That’s what I need to do. Nothing else matters now.”
Dr. Marcus nodded slowly. “I understand, Isaiah. Your decision is valid. Hospice care will focus on your comfort and dignity. And if spending your remaining time with your daughter is your priority, that’s what you must follow.”
Leo exhaled and squeezed my shoulder. “Then that’s what we’ll do. I’ll make sure you’re protected, Zay. You’ll get to her.”
A strange calm settled over me. It wasn’t surrender. It was clarity. My fight was no longer against disease. It was for connection, for legacy, for love in its purest, most essential form.
We finished with Dr. Marcus, discussing hospice arrangements and necessary support for the trip. Leo ensured everything was in place,privacy, security, medical care en route.
Snow still dusted the city streets as we left. My hands no longer trembled as violently. My heart was steady.
Leo helped me into the car. “We’ll get you to your flight. Soon, you’ll see her.”
“Yes,” I said, my voice thick with hope and fear. “Soon, I’ll meet her.”
At the airport, he guided me through security, guarding against the paparazzi, ensuring a smooth path.
“This is it,” Leo said softly. “Your daughter is waiting.”
I clutched his hand. “Thank you… for everything.”
The plane’s engines hummed beneath us. First-class offered space, comfort, quiet. I closed my eyes, imagining her small hands, her fragile voice, the tiny heartbeat that had been mine in part long before I could hold her.
Less than a year. Perhaps months, perhaps weeks. But my final chapter would be written with her in my arms.
I reached for Leo, squeezing his hand. “Protect her while I’m there. Protect her after. But let me live this one dream fully,just this once.”
He nodded, voice soft. “Go, Zay. Be her father. She deserves you. And you… you deserve this, too.”
I exhaled slowly, letting the hum of the engines lull me into determination. My life was measured, finite,but my love, my presence, would be boundless.
New York waited. My daughter waited. And soon, I would finally meet her.
Thirty four
Two days.
That’s how long it’s been since the truth blew up in my face like a grenade I never saw coming. Two days since the world tilted a little to the left and stayed there, refusing to straighten out, because apparently my heart really enjoys being a punching bag.
Honestly, if I ever find out which ancestor of mine pissed off the universe, I’m digging them up and asking why. Why curse your descendants with men who lie like they’re auditioning for a soap opera?
When I came home that night, still shaky from the emotional earthquake Jordan caused, I found Amy sitting in my living room with Emeric asleep in her arms. She was crying silently, clutching him like he was her life raft.
I didn’t even think ,I took Ray upstairs, tucked her in, then rushed back down. I held Amy as tightly as she was holding her son. Her tears soaked my sweater, and she kept whispering, “I miss him… I miss him so much.”
And suddenly I understood the pain behind her silence these past few weeks. Emeric has Nikolai’s eyes. His smile. His dimples. Loving him must feel like holding both heaven and hell in the same breath.
We cried together until our throats hurt. Two broken women sitting on the couch, taking turns apologizing for crying too loudly.
Eventually, between hiccups, I told her everything ,Jordan’s secret, the betrayal, the way I felt stupid for falling for someone who told me he wouldn’t hurt me. Amy went nuclear. Full nuclear.
She called him every profanity known to humankind.
She sent him a chain of messages so brutal I’m pretty sure they violated at least three international treaties.
She even threatened to break his kneecaps, which… honestly, was comforting.
Then we did what two heartbroken women do best , we binged Friends and ate ice cream directly out of the tubs like civilized adults.
But now Amy is back at her place, and I’m alone. Alone with my thoughts. Alone with a toddler who woke up and chose violence today.
Ray has been in a foul mood since sunrise.
Not just cranky ,no, she is in full meltdown mode.
Classic toddler hurricane with a sprinkle of heartbreak.
I’m sitting on the floor in the living room, hair sticking to my face, wearing a shirt that might actually be inside-out, watching my daughter roll dramatically across the carpet like a soccer player faking an injury.
“Mommyyyy!” she wails, face squished against her stuffed elephant. “I want talk to Jowdan!”
There it is.
The dagger.
Right to the heart.
I inhale slowly. “Baby, honey—Jordan’s busy right now.”
That is a lie.
He’s called.
He’s texted.
He’s apologized in paragraphs so long they could qualify as novellas.
He said he’d give me space, but every hour, a new message pops up saying something like:
“I’m here. Whenever you’re ready.”
“I miss you both.”
“Please talk to me.”
I’ve ignored every single one.
But Ray? She doesn’t care about adult heartbreak. She only knows that her favorite playmate, the man who lifts her like she weighs as much as a feather, isn’t here.
She sits up, cheeks streaked with tears, hair sticking up in angry curls. “He pwomiseeee,” she sniffles. “He pwomise watch cartoons wiff me.”
My chest tightens.
He did promise.
He promised a lot of things.
“Baby, come here,” I say gently.
She refuses, turning her back to me with the dramatic flair of a diva who just didn’t win an award.
“Don’t touch me,” she mutters.
Oh.
Okay.
She’s in her teenager era already. Perfect.
I crawl closer and sit beside her. Not touching. Just waiting.
After a minute, she scoots half an inch in my direction. Then another. And another. Until finally she leans her tiny body against my arm with a shaky sigh.
“There you go,” I whisper, brushing her hair back. “It’s okay to be mad.”
She presses her forehead into my shoulder. “I mad at you.”
“I know.”
“I mad at him.”
“I know, baby.”
“I mad at everybody.”
“Oh honey,” I say softly. “Me too.”
She lets out a hiccuping sob, and I hold her close. Her little heart is beating fast, her breath warm against my neck.
God, she doesn’t deserve this.
She didn’t sign up for adult complications.
She didn’t ask to care about someone who ended up lying to both of us.
She sniffles. “Can I call him?”
My heart cracks. Cleanly. Quietly.
“Not today,” I say, voice softer than a breath. “We’re taking a break.”
She scrunches her face. “What’s a bwake?”
“It’s like…” I search for toddler-friendly words. “It’s when two people need time to think.”
She considers this. “You thinking?”
“All the time,” I admit with a rueful smile.
She pokes my cheek. “You think too much.”
“Trust me, baby, I know.”
She finally relaxes against me, exhaustion settling in her limbs like a heavy blanket. After a while, her breathing evens out, and she falls asleep in my lap.
I carry her to her room, tuck her in with her favorite blanket, and kiss her forehead. She’s still frowning even in her sleep.
When I return to the living room, the silence hits hard.
Too loud.
Too aware.
Too filled with thoughts I don’t want to face.
Two days.
Two days of replaying Jordan’s confession.
Two days of wondering how a six-year-old boy fits into his life.
Two days of missing him and hating myself for missing him.
I sink onto the couch, elbows on my knees, hands gripping my hair.
I loved him.
I still do.
And that’s the problem.
My phone vibrates.
Jordan: Please. I don’t want to fight. Just let me know you’re okay.
I flip the phone over and shove it under a pillow.
A scream bubbles up in my chest , not loud, not dramatic, just a quiet plea for the universe to stop messing with me like I’m a telenovela character.
I lean back, close my eyes, inhale slowly.
Then…
A knock at the door.
My eyes snap open.
Jordan.
Has to be.
He said he’d give me space, but he’s impulsive when he’s desperate. He shows up. He knocks. He waits.
My heart races.
My palms sweat.
I walk to the door slowly, mind flashing with every possible version of him standing there—
tearful
apologetic
stubborn
hopeful
broken
I grip the handle.
“Jordan,” I whisper under my breath, already rehearsing the speech I told myself I wouldn’t give him.
I open the door.
And freeze.
Because it’s not Jordan.
It’s Isaiah.
Isaiah.
Standing in the hallway.
He is wearing a hoodie and a cap.
Face thinner,
Skin pale,
Eyes sunken but unmistakably green , the same green Ray sees in the mirror.
My breath stops.
Literally stops.
He looks at me like he’s memorizing me, like he’s terrified I’ll disappear if he blinks.
“Alyssa,” he whispers.
My world detonates.
Thirty Five
Isaiah stands there, trembling slightly, breathing like every inhale costs him something he can’t afford to lose.
And I don’t move.
I physically can’t.
My hand stays frozen on the door, fingers curled stiffly around the edge like my body forgot how to function under the weight of his existence. My heartbeat stutters, then trips over itself, a messy, panicked rhythm that makes my chest tighten.
For a long, suffocating moment, the hallway is silent except for his unsteady breathing and my own disbelief.
He looks… different.
Not actor-chiseled.
Not red-carpet polished.
Not the man the world worships or the man I once vowed to love forever.
He looks fragile.
Human.
Breakable.
And I hate , hate , that some old part of me still reacts. That some small buried muscle in my heart remembers how to beat for him. That the sight of him triggers a thousand memories I thought I’d buried in the grave of our marriage.
“Hi…” he tries again, voice cracking like the word is too heavy for him to carry.
My throat tightens. “What are you doing here?”
The words come out sharp. Stiff. Cold.
He flinches , not dramatically, not like he used to when he wanted sympathy , just barely. A small twitch of pain.
“I… I needed to see you.” He swallows, and his voice cracks again. “I needed to see her.”
Of course he did.
Of course he shows up now , when my life is already doing Olympic-level backflips off cliffs. When I’m barely holding myself together with caffeine, stress, and sheer stubbornness. When Ray has been crying for Jordan all day, screaming his name like I’m denying her air. When I am already bruised emotionally from every angle: heartbreak, betrayal, motherhood, loneliness.
Of course he picks now.
Of course.
“Isaiah, you shouldn’t be here. Not now, not ever.” My voice drops to a harsh whisper. “There is no ‘her.’ You are not her father.”
His eyes flicker , pain, regret, something else I don’t dare name , but he steps back instead of pushing forward. At least he remembers that line. The one that says: Alyssa will burn you alive if you cross this boundary.
“I know.” He nods slowly, swallowing hard. “I know you don’t want to see me. I know I don’t deserve to be here. But I—” His voice breaks again. “I need to see her for myself. I know she’s my daughter. Please don’t deny me this.”
Guilt shouldn’t hit me.
But it does.
Sharp. Unfair. And unwelcome.
Because he looks exhausted.
Not celebrity exhausted.
Not long-day-on-set exhausted.
Soul exhausted.
Bone exhausted.
Like even standing is a battle.
I fold my arms over my chest, grounding myself so I don’t crumble. “Isaiah, you need to leave. This isn’t—”
A soft cry echoes from inside the apartment.
Ray.
Oh God.
I freeze.
Isaiah’s head snaps up at the sound. His jaw tightens. His breath shudders. His eyes go glossy , that dangerous shine of a man barely keeping it together.
“Alyssa…” His voice is a whisper. A prayer. A plea. “Was that her?”
“No.”
The word slices out of me instantly, sharp as a blade.
He doesn’t get to have hope.
He doesn’t get to hear her.
He doesn’t get to imagine fatherhood when he walked away from the marriage that carried her.
“She’s not coming out,” I say firmly. “And you’re not seeing her.”
His face collapses quietly. No theatrics. No performance. Just a slow, devastating deflation , like every piece of him is folding inward.
He stands there shaking slightly, hands shoved into the pockets of a jacket that hangs too loosely on him. Or maybe he’s just… smaller now.
I almost ask if he’s eaten.
I almost ask if he’s okay.
I almost reach for the part of me that once loved him.
But I don’t.
He lost the right to my care the moment he chose fame over our marriage. Over me. Over the life we built. The moment he kissed another woman in a trailer while I sat home praying for us. The moment he asked for a divorce as if wiping me away would free him.
“Look, Isaiah,” I say, voice shaking with anger I refuse to unleash. “You can’t come here unannounced. I am being very, very calm right now, but inside?” I tap my chest harshly. “I am a raging tornado. So hear me clearly…”
I glance back to make sure Ray isn’t coming down the stairs.
“She is my daughter. Mine. She stopped being yours the moment you abandoned us. You don’t deserve to be called her father. Don’t ever show your face here again.”
His lips tremble.
He opens his mouth , maybe to apologize, maybe to beg ...
Ray cries again, louder, startled by her own frustration and probably ready to throw her stuffed elephant across the room for the fifth time today.
Isaiah’s shoulders shake.
He presses his eyes shut like the sound physically hurts him.
“I just need to see her,” he whispers. “I need to believe it with my own eyes. I never knew you were pregnant, Alyssa. Please. I’m begging you. I… don’t have much time left.”
My heartbeat stops.
“What?” My voice comes out thin.
He looks at me like he’s about to crumble. “I… I don’t have much time left to live.”
The words punch the air out of my lungs.
“What do you mean?” I whisper, barely managing to form the words. “What do you mean you don’t have much time to live?”
“I…” He tries to speak, but Ray wails again, louder, more desperate.
My poor baby.
Isaiah glances over my shoulder, instinctively trying to see inside. I block the doorway with my body.
“Can you—” he swallows, eyes softening painfully, “can you go attend to her? Please. I don’t want anything to happen to her.”
“I know how to care for my daughter,” I snap.
He nods quickly, apologetically.
I sigh, exhausted beyond measure.
“Isaiah… you need to leave.”
He nods again, defeated. “I’m staying in New York for a little while. I won’t bother you. But… please come see me. This is my address. We need to talk about her. I just wanted you to know.”
He holds out a small folded slip of paper.
His handwriting.
His address.
His shaking hand.
I take it with the tips of my fingers, refusing to let the contact mean anything.
He steps back.
Another step.
Another.
Like each one is ripping something out of him.I grip the door harder than necessary, fingers digging into the wood.
And right before he turns away completely, he murmurs,
“Tell her… tell her I’m sorry.”
I close the door.
Gently.
Not slamming.
Not violently.
Just… closing.
The lock clicks.
And something in my chest cracks with it.
I slide down onto the floor, burying my face in my hands because everything is too much.
Because Jordan shattered me two days ago with lies that gutted me.
Because Ray is crying for a man who is not her father.
Because her father is standing in hallways looking like a ghost carved out of regret.
Because I am exhausted. Bone-deep exhausted.
Because I don’t know how to hold all this pain and still be a mother.
And the worst part?
The moment I saw Isaiah —
I knew something was very, very wrong.
Not actor wrong.
Not guilt wrong.
Sick wrong.
The kind no amount of prayer or time can fix.
And suddenly the world feels heavier.
Like I’m standing in the eye of a storm that’s about to tear my life apart all over again.
Thirty Six
I sipped my lukewarm coffee while leaning against the kitchen counter, staring into absolutely nothing — my brain doing that thing where it shuts down to avoid dealing with life. It’s a survival tactic at this point.
Amy sat across from me at the small dining table, sipping her coffee like she was trying to vacuum the entire liquid through the mug. Loud. Dramatic. Annoying.
At least the house was tantrum-free. For the first time in three days.
Ray was actually happy today. She and Emeric had been running around the living room, babbling in some toddler language that only the two of them , and maybe aliens , could understand.
No Jo-jo crying.
No crawling onto my lap clutching her stuffed bunny like her soul depended on it.
No throwing herself on the floor screaming to “Call Jodwann!!”
Small miracles.
But the universe, being the spiteful little gremlin it is, left me with a second problem.
Isaiah.
Just thinking his name sent a sharp, hot ache right behind my ribs.
After he left yesterday, I called Amy and told her what had happened.
She laughed.
Laughed.
Then said, “I told you so,” in that tone siblings use when they’re thrilled you’ve finally stepped in the mess they predicted.
But beneath all the irritation, I needed her word of advice. Ray deserved the truth. She deserved to know who her father was someday — and I couldn’t just wing it the way I did everything else in life.
Amy, unfortunately, was my only compass right now.
And she was getting on my nerves with her loud sipping.
“Will you quit doing that and talk? You know I hate that,” I snapped.
“Finally!” she groaned dramatically. “I thought I might have to swallow the whole cup before you said something. Anyway, I’m your little sister , it’s literally my job to annoy you.”
She set her mug down, smirking.
“I thought you came here to help me, not annoy me,” I muttered.
“What is there to help with?” she shrugged casually. “You didn’t take my advice when I begged you for months to tell Isaiah about Ray. You ignored me — as usual — and now you’re shocked by the consequences.”
“Ugh, Amy, I don’t need a lecture right now. I need help. Isaiah was here. At my apartment. I don’t even know how he got past security — probably because they assumed I invited him, which
means I’m firing them tomorrow — but he was HERE. And he HEARD her. I need to do something.”
“Do what exactly?” she rolled her eyes. “Take Ray and move to another country? Fake your death? Learn how to teleport? Will that solve anything? No. You can’t keep running away from your problems. Ray will grow up and ask about her dad. What are you going to tell her then? Be bold for once, Lyss.”
I groaned through my teeth.
She wasn’t wrong. That was the worst part.
“I’m so mad he gets to be Ray’s father without ever caring about us,” I muttered. “He doesn’t deserve her.”
“But he is her father,” Amy said gently. “Nothing will erase that. The best thing you can do is let them meet eventually. And make him pay child support. That’s the least he owes you.”
“I don’t need his money,” I shot back. “I can take care of my daughter. Ray is priceless.”
“So is my Emeric,” she shrugged, “but if his dad existed anywhere on this planet and tried to leave me with 100% responsibility, I’d take child support, emotional support, spiritual support — everything.”
I didn’t even crack a smile.
“All I’m saying,” she continued, “is that Ray and Isaiah need to know each other. Whether you like it or not, that day will come. Might as well start somewhere. Go see him today.”
“What? Why? NOW?” I groaned.
“Yes, now,” she said cheerfully. “The earlier you face it, the faster you can stop spiraling.”
Before I could argue, a shrill giggle erupted from the living room.
Ray and Emeric barreled past the kitchen doorway, each wearing one of my fluffy socks on their hands like puppets.
Emeric looked at his sock-covered hand and declared, “Dino roar!”
Ray gasped in awe — genuine, heart-shaped awe — and copied him.
“Dino waaaah!”
Which… close enough.
Then they both roared.
Loudly.
Ferociously.
Like tiny dragons high off pure chaos.
They ran in a circle, roaring at the couch, the pillows, the air, and then at each other.
Emeric tripped and rolled onto the carpet in dramatic slow motion.
Ray screamed like he’d been shot.
Dove onto the floor next to him.
Patted his head.
Then yelled, “Again!”
And the cycle restarted.
Amy smiled proudly. “See? We make good babies.”
I sighed, my heart melting despite the chaos. “I know.”
But peace never lasts long in this household.
Ray barreled into view wearing Emeric’s tiny sweater like a cape. It trailed behind her as she ran, completely tripping her up. She landed face-first on the carpet.
Emeric gasped like he’d just witnessed a Shakespearean tragedy. “Waaay!” he cried, toddling to her with his small hands cupping her cheeks. “Ah you okie?”
Ray blinked, sat up, rubbed her face, then burst into tears.
And chaos began.
I sighed. “Here we go.”
Ray’s tantrum hit instantly — flailing limbs, red cheeks, dramatic wailing. Full toddler meltdown. Emeric, loyal little soldier that he was, patted her hair and said, “No cwy. No cwy. O-tay, Way.”
She looked up at him with tear-filled eyes and wailed louder.
Amy hid her smile behind her cup. “Oh, the drama. You raised a main-character child.”
“I blame her father,” I muttered, picking Ray up.
Ray clutched my shirt, still sobbing. “Jo-Jo… wan’ Jo-Jo…”
Of course.
My chest tightened. “Sweetheart, Jo-Jo isn’t here.”
She cried harder.
Emeric touched her arm softly. “Way, I pway wif you. No Jo-Jo. Emi here.”
Ray hiccupped, looked at him, then wrapped her little arms around him in a tight hug. He patted her back proudly, like a tiny emotional support boyfriend.
Amy cooed. “Oh my God, they’re in love.”
“They’re two and ewww... they are cousins” I said flatly.
“And already having healthier communication than you and Jordan,” she shot back.
I glared at her. She grinned unrepentantly.
After a few minutes, Ray calmed down enough to be bribed with a snack. I handed both kids some fruit and switched on their favorite cartoon. When they settled down, I grabbed my bag and jacket.
Amy raised a brow. “So… you’re going?”
“I guess,” I muttered. “You stay here with them. If Ray asks for me, distract her.”
“With what?”
I shrugged. “Lie. She’s two. She’ll believe anything.”
...
Ten minutes later I am standing in front of my apartment building complex, staring at the address in my hand.
I stared at the paper, then looked at the building across the street.
A luxury apartment complex.
Right across from mine.
I checked the address again.
And again.
My jaw dropped.
“Oh, absolutely not,” I muttered.
He was staying directly across from my apartment building.
Directly.
Across.
The street.
“The heck?” I whispered. “Why is he being a freak?”
He was close.
Too close.
Close enough that if Ray screamed “Jo-Jo!” from the balcony, he might hear it.
I swallowed hard and crossed the street, every step heavy. My heart pounded loud enough to drown out traffic noise.
Inside the lobby, I told the receptionist Isaiah’s unit number. She pointed me toward the elevators with a sympathetic smile, which made me wonder how pathetic I looked.
Probably very.
By the time I reached his floor, my palms were sweating, and my chest felt wrapped in barbed wire.
I didn’t want to pity him.
I didn’t want to feel anything.
But I couldn’t ignore the trembling in my hands , the mix of fear, resentment, heartbreak, and something I desperately didn’t want to name.
I knocked.
The door opened almost immediately.
Isaiah stood there.
Hair damp from a shower. T-shirt wrinkled. Eyes exhausted. Body thinner than I remembered. A slight shake in his left hand.
He looked… older.
Fragile.
Human.
“Alyssa,” he breathed.
I crossed my arms, needing armor. “You’re staying here? Right across from my place? Seriously?”
“I didn’t know,” he said softly. "Leo booked it. I wasn’t trying to be invasive.”
“You being anywhere near my daughter is already invasive,” I shot back.
He flinched.
“I know,” he whispered. “But… I didn’t come here to start a fight.”
My arms tightened across my chest. “Then why did you come? Why are you even in New York, Isaiah? Why are you living across the damn street from me?”
His breath stuttered. He looked… cornered. “Because I didn’t know how else to get close enough to ask.”
“To ask what?”
His eyes flickered with something raw. “To ask if I could meet her.”
My pulse punched through my skull.
He lowered his gaze, voice barely steady. “I know you won’t just hand me that chance. I know I don’t deserve it. But I had to try. Before I ran out of time.”
My heart lurched , not with sympathy, but with confusion. “What does that even mean? ‘Run out of time’?”
He hesitated.
Too long.
Too heavy.
Then he lifted his eyes, and the truth was sitting there, trembling.
“Alyssa… I came to New York because I’m sick.”
Something in me stilled. “Sick how?”
He swallowed, throat tight. “The doctors found more tumors. The cancer spread. The treatment isn’t working anymore.”
I stared at him.
He kept going, voice thinner by the second. “And I have Parkinson’s… it’s progressing faster than they expected. My hands, my speech, the muscle spasms — everything’s worsening.”
I blinked, hard, taking a step back as if the air suddenly changed.
“How long?” I whispered.
He let out a breath that sounded like surrender. “They think… a year. Maybe. If the new meds don’t help.”
I pressed a hand to my forehead, dizzy. “So you just — what? You just show up? Move across the street? Drop this on me like it’s some casual update?”
“I didn’t know another way,” he said, voice cracking. “I didn’t want to text you something this big. And I couldn’t keep lying to myself. I couldn’t die without—” His breath shook. “—without trying to make things right with you. And without knowing her.”
I felt anger rise like a flash fire.
“You don’t get to say her name,” I snapped. “Not like that. Not like you’ve been here. Not like you’ve earned anything.”
He winced.
“Alyssa—”
“No,” I cut in. “You think your illness gives you access? You think pain suddenly makes you a father? You left me alone. You humiliated me publicly. You let me go through hell while you were planning a proposal with someone else.”
His shoulders caved.
“I know,” he whispered. “I know what I did. I know I destroyed what we had. I know I hurt you in ways I can’t undo.” His voice broke. “But when the doctors told me what was happening… the only thing I could think about was you. And her. About what I missed. About what I threw away.”
My chest tightened painfully.
“Don’t do that,” I said sharply. “Don’t stand there and make me feel sorry for you.”
“I’m not asking for pity,” he said quietly. “I’m asking for honesty. I came because I don’t have a year to waste. And I don’t want to leave this world without trying to face what I ruined. Without knowing if… if she even looks like you.”
My throat bobbed.
God.
I hated him.
I missed him.
I hurt for him.
I hated that I hurt for him.
“You don’t get to ask that,” I whispered. “You don’t get to want things now.”
He nodded slowly. “I know. I know I don’t deserve the chance. But I’m here anyway. Because she’s mine. And because you deserved better than the man I became.”
Something inside me cracked, just a hairline fracture , pity, grief, love, anger tangled together until I didn’t know what was what.
But I held my ground.
“I’m not promising anything,” I said. “You won’t meet her today. Maybe not tomorrow. Maybe not this week. But eventually a. But I’ll talk to you. And you will respect my boundaries.”
Relief washed over his face so fast it hurt to look at.
“Thank you,” he murmured.
“This isn’t for you,” I said stiffly. “This is for Ray. She deserves the truth one day. Not the version the tabloids printed.”
His eyes filled again, softer now. “I know.”
I stepped back, needing air, distance, logic , anything but him.
“I have to go.”
He nodded. “Okay.”
“One conversation at a time,” I added. “That’s it.”
“Alyssa…” His voice trembled. “That’s more than I ever expected.”
I didn’t answer.
I couldn’t.
Because despite everything he’d done…
Despite all the scars…
A tiny, stubborn part of me still recognized the man I once loved.
And that terrified me more than his illness ever could.
Thirty Seven
Jordan
I’d spent the past week trying to convince myself that giving Alyssa space was the noble, respectable, emotionally mature thing to do.
It wasn’t.
It was torture.
Every day felt like walking barefoot across broken glass, pretending I wasn’t bleeding. Every hour was an argument between my pride and the part of me that loved her so stupidly, so recklessly, it felt like drowning with my eyes open.
And every time I even thought about calling her, I remembered the look on her face that night , hurt, betrayed, shattered ,when she realized I’d been keeping parts of my past buried under lock and key.
So I stayed away.
Did my job.
Slept badly.
Ate worse.
Checked my phone like a lunatic.
And tried  emphasis on tried , not to hate myself.
Today, though… today I couldn’t stay away anymore.
Today, I needed to see her. Even if it meant losing her for good.
Her building came into view as I crossed the street, my hands shoved deep in my jacket pockets because if I didn’t, they’d shake. I wasn’t scared of much , I’d survived addiction, public humiliation, scandals, betrayal, rehab, and my own demons.
But facing Alyssa when she’d been hurt by me?
Yeah. That terrified the hell out of me.
I texted her before buzzing up.
— I’m downstairs. If you don’t want to see me, just say the word and I’ll leave.
No response for ten seconds.
Twenty.
Thirty.
Then:
— Come up.
My heart slammed against my ribs.
She opened the door before I could knock.
Her hair was tied up in a messy bun, the kind she only did when she was overwhelmed or bone-tired. She looked softer today… but not warm. Not open.
Guarded.
And maybe rightfully so.
“Alyssa,” I breathed.
“Come in,” she said, voice calm but distant.
I stepped inside.
Her apartment smelled like vanilla and something warm , the scent I always associated with safety. With her. Ray’s toys were scattered in a small, colorful explosion across the living room. A blanket draped over the couch where she probably napped during long rehearsals. A mug half-full on the coffee table.
A home.
A life I wanted to be part of.
A future I was dangerously close to losing.
She didn’t sit. Didn’t offer me water. Didn’t smile.
She crossed her arms and leaned against the counter, studying me like she was preparing for impact.
“We need to talk,” she said.
My stomach dropped. “I know.”
“Good. Because I have questions.”
I nodded. “Ask anything.”
She inhaled slowly, steadying herself. “How, Jordan? How did you end up in that… situation with her? How did your stepmother get pregnant with your son?”
My throat closed for a moment.
Because yes ,she deserved the truth.
And yes I’d avoided saying it for years.
And yes,I remembered every disgusting detail too clearly.
I moved to the edge of the couch and sat, elbows on my knees, my hands clasped. If I didn’t stay grounded, I’d spiral.
“It didn’t start with the pregnancy,” I said quietly. “It started with… everything falling apart at once.”
She stayed silent, watching. Waiting. Bracing.
I forced myself to continue.
“I was twenty-two. My acting career was crashing before it even started. I’d gotten kicked out of two auditions for showing up hungover. I was failing my classes. My father wouldn’t speak to me unless it was to remind me I was wasting his money and ruining the family name.”
A bitter smile tugged at my mouth.
“And my grandma,the only person who cared about me, had just died.”
Alyssa’s expression softened for a second, but she held her silence.
“I was a mess,” I said. “Drinking every day. Smoking weed like it was air. Doing lines to stay awake. Doing lines to sleep. I don’t even know which was which anymore.”
My hands trembled, but I didn’t hide it.
“That’s when Kaya started being ‘nice’ to me. Asking if I’d eaten. Bringing me food. Talking to me like I wasn’t a disappointment. Like I mattered.”
Alyssa flinched.
“Jordan…”
“I know what you’re thinking,” I said, voice rough. “But it wasn’t that. Not at first. She didn’t touch me. Not then. She just… watched me fall apart. Then offered herself as the one person who didn’t mind holding the pieces.”
I exhaled shakily.
“That’s grooming, Alyssa. I didn’t know it then. I thought she was the only person in the house who gave a damn about me.”
Silence stretched, heavy and suffocating.
“What happened?” she whispered.
“She kissed me.”
I swallowed, feeling the old shame burn hot under my skin.
“She came into my room one night. I’d been drinking. I was so out of it I could barely stand. She told me I looked like I needed comfort. That I deserved affection. That I shouldn’t be alone.”
Alyssa covered her mouth with her hand, eyes glistening.
“I didn’t stop her,” I said. “Because I was drunk. Because I was grieving. Because I was stupid. Because I thought a woman wanting me meant I wasn’t completely worthless.”
My voice cracked.
“And because I thought saying no would get twisted into something else. She already had the upper hand. She was sober. Married to my father. Older. Smarter. Manipulative. I was drowning. She knew it.”
A tear slid down Alyssa’s cheek.
I hadn’t even reached the worst part yet.
“When my father found out,” I said, “it was already too late. Kaya was pregnant.”
Alyssa’s eyes widened. “He found out?”
“Oh, yeah.” A humorless laugh escaped. “He screamed so loud the whole damn neighborhood probably heard. But not at her. Never at her. She cried and said I manipulated her. Said I was using drugs and taking advantage of her.”
Alyssa’s jaw dropped. “What?!”
“Yeah. Suddenly I was the villain. The addict. The emotionally unstable son. And she was the poor, confused wife who fell into temptation.” I rubbed my face. “My father believed her. Of
course he did. Why wouldn’t he? She was his perfect wife. And I was the son who disappointed him at every turn.”
Alyssa shook her head slowly, horror in her eyes. “Jordan…”
“He fixed everything,” I said bitterly. “Paid people. Threatened others. Hid the story. No one outside the house knew. He kept her image spotless. And mine dirty. Because that was easier.”
I felt something splinter in my chest.
“And I hated him for it. Hated him for choosing her. Hated that he made me feel like what happened was my fault. Hated the guilt. Hated the silence. Hated myself. But eventually he divorced her.”
A long pause.
“When my son was born,” I continued quietly, “I didn’t want him.”
Alyssa inhaled sharply.
“I wanted him gone. I didn’t want to see him. I didn’t want to hold him. I thought he was the living, breathing proof of my biggest mistake.”
My voice faltered. “I became a deadbeat father before the term even applied.”
Her eyes were filled with pain — not disgust, just sadness.
“But one day,” I said, “I showed up at her house drunk. Kaya was threatening to take everything public unless I paid her. My father was there,you know even after they divorce,he still hooked up with after they he remarried,he was begging me to keep quiet. I wanted to scream at both of them.”
I swallowed hard.
“Then the boy — he toddled toward me. Smiling. Reaching. Saying ‘dada’ like he knew me. Like he didn’t care what I’d done. Like he wasn’t supposed to hate me.”
Alyssa pressed a hand to her heart.
“And I fell apart,” I whispered. “Something cracked open. I checked myself into rehab the next week. Tried to be better. Tried to fix what I’d broken. I stayed there for two years but when I came out,I couldn't do it .”
“But you couldn’t bond with him,” she whispered.
“No matter how hard I tried,” I said, feeling the old shame coil around my ribs, “part of me still saw him as the product of something that ruined my life. And that’s not fair to him. He didn’t ask to be here.”
I looked at her directly.
“He deserves better than the version of me who raised him.”
Alyssa’s expression changed , empathy turning into something sharper. Harder.
“So tell me something,” she said softly. “How can you show so much love to Ray — my daughter — when you couldn’t do the same for your own?”
I opened my mouth and closed it again.
Because I knew where this was going.
And I hated it.
But I also knew she was right.
“Because Ray is innocent to me,” I whispered. “She isn’t tied to trauma. She isn’t tied to shame. She isn’t tied to the worst years of my life.”
Alyssa shook her head, tears falling freely.
“I can’t do this,” she said. “I can’t be with someone who hasn’t fixed his own house. Someone who can love my child but push his own aside. Jordan… that’s not who I thought you were.”
Her voice broke.
“And that scares me.”
I felt the floor tilt under me.
“No, Alyssa—please. Please don’t say that. I just… I need time. A chance to—”
She cut me off gently.
“I’m not finished.”
I froze.
“There’s something else,” she said quietly. “Isaiah is  in New York.”
Everything inside me stilled.
“And,” she continued, “he wants to meet Ray.”
My lungs locked.
“Alyssa,what? Why? Why now?”
“Because he’s dying,” she whispered. "I went to see him. And he is sick Jordan, Parkinson's and Cancer, he is dying only a year to live. . Hear it. And Ray deserves to know who her father is.”
My stomach twisted sharply.
“So you’re going back to him?” I said before I could stop myself.
She recoiled. “No. God, Jordan — no. I’m doing this for Ray. Because she deserves her father in her life, even if only for a short time.”
My chest ached.
“But I need to get my life together,” she said softly. “For her. For me. For everyone involved. And you… you need time too.”
Silence swallowed the room.
“Alyssa,” I whispered, voice cracking, “don’t leave me.”
“I’m not leaving you,” she said. “But we need space. Real space. If you love me ,truly love me — use this time to fix what’s broken. Bond with your son. Heal. Grow. And when life settles… if it’s meant to be…”
Her eyes softened.
“I’ll be here.”
My heart shattered.
But somehow… it also steadied.
Because this wasn’t punishment.
This was a lifeline.
And maybe the only way forward.
I nodded slowly.
“Okay,” I whispered. “I’ll do it. For you. For him. For us.”
Her gaze flickered, tender and sad.
“For yourself too,” she said.
And for the first time in weeks, I let myself breathe.
Thirty Eight
The house was quiet in the kind of way that made every sound feel louder. The clink of Ray’s spoon against her cereal bowl. The soft hum of the dishwasher. The faint buzzing of my phone on the counter.
I didn’t have to check it to know who it was.
Jordan had texted every morning for the past two days. Nothing heavy. Nothing dramatic. Nothing begging for me to come back or talk or explain. Just little updates, pieces of his day that he clearly wanted to share, even if he wasn’t expecting anything back.
Sometimes they came with pictures , a blurry shot of a playground slide, a tiny hand gripping his shirt, a lopsided Lego tower.
Today’s first message came in just before breakfast.
“We made pancakes. He insisted on pouring the batter himself. My kitchen is… unrecognizable.”
Then, right after:
“He called me ‘Dad’ again. Still feels unreal.”
I didn’t reply.
I just stared at the messages for a long moment, then placed my phone facedown and focused on Ray, who was now using her spoon to pretend she was “stirring soup” instead of eating breakfast.
“Sweetheart,” I said gently, “you’re going to make it all mushy.”
She looked at me with that guilty-toddler expression , the wide eyes, the half-smile, the who, me? innocence , then giggled.
“Mushy soup,” she announced proudly.
I sighed, defeated. “Fine. Mushy soup it is.”
She burst into laughter, and I couldn’t help smiling. It was impossible not to. When she laughed, all the heaviness in my chest loosened just a little.
Two days since the talk with Jordan.
Two days since I told him we needed time.
And somehow, even with the ache of everything between us, I felt… lighter. Not healed. Not okay. But grounded, the way you feel when you finally let a breath out you didn’t know you’d been holding.
“Ada-ven-cha?” Ray asked suddenly, stabbing her spoon in the air like a sword.
“Adventure,” I corrected softly. “Yes. After we finish breakfast. A whole adventure.”
Her eyes widened. “We go OUT-out?”
That made me smile. “Yes, out-out.”
She clapped her hands, cereal splashing onto the table.
We hadn’t gone out as normal people , truly normal people , in a long time. Even here in New York, where celebrities walked around like pigeons, there was always one paparazzo lurking behind a café door or pretending to read a newspaper on a bench.
They weren’t as aggressive as the Los Angeles ones. But they were still there.
Always watching.
Always waiting.
Always waiting for us to slip, to fall, to cry.
But today, I didn’t want any of that to matter.
Today, I wanted to be a mother taking her little girl out for fresh air and sunlight and ice cream without feeling like the world had claws in our clothes.
....
The first camera flash didn’t come until we were halfway down the street, which was practically a blessing.
I spotted him immediately ,cap pulled low, trying to blend in, holding a camera like he was “just taking architecture shots.”
Right.
I tightened my grip on Ray’s hand.
“Stay close, sweetie.”
Ray nodded, completely unbothered, because recently she is used to seeing cameras and strangers calling her name like it was normal.
I hated that part the most.
But today, I didn’t let the irritation take root. I just tucked a strand of Ray’s hair behind her ear, crossed the street, and headed toward the small park two blocks away.
We reached the gates, and Ray took off running the second her shoes hit the playground woodchips, squealing like she’d been released from toddler prison.
I followed at a slower pace, watching her climb the tiny plastic slide. “Look, Mama!” she shrieked before launching herself down.
“I see you!” I called. “Very graceful!”
She didn’t know the word graceful, but she knew praise, and she lit up like a sparkler.
I sat on a nearby bench. The same paparazzo hovered about twenty yards away, pretending to check his phone. I ignored him.
Instead, I pulled out my own phone to check the time , and Jordan’s next message blinked up.
“He asked if I’m staying. I told him yes. I meant it.”
Another.
“I talked to a lawyer. If Kaya fights back, I’ll fight harder. I won’t lose him.”
My chest tightened.
Not in a bad way.
Not in a romantic way.
Just… something warm. Something proud. Something relieved.
He was trying.
He was actually trying.
And not for me ,for his son.
I didn’t text back, but my lips lifted anyway.
“Ma-maaaa!” Ray called suddenly.
I put the phone away and waved. “Yes, honey?”
“I climb BIG slide!”
“That’s a very big slide,” I said, walking to stand near her. “Do you want help?”
She shook her head hard. “No. I big girl.”
I bit back a laugh. “Alright. Big girl. Go ahead.”
She climbed one step at a time, tongue sticking out, tiny legs wobbling, determination blazing in her little face. When she reached the top, she sat down, looked at me for approval, then pushed herself forward.
She hit the bottom with a soft thud and burst into victorious laughter.
“I BIG GIRL!” she screamed.
