“Yes,” I agreed, scooping her up and twirling her. “You’re definitely a big girl.”
And for a moment, it was just us.
No cameras.
No texts.
No heartbreak.
Just joy.
......
After the park, we wandered into a small boutique I liked ,not too fancy, not too loud , the kind of place where the staff smiled warmly instead of staring at you like you didn’t belong.
Ray immediately ran to a rack of dresses, touching the fabric like it was treasure.
“This!” she said, holding up a lavender dress two sizes too big.
“That is very pretty,” I admitted, “but it’s too big.”
Ray considered that, then carefully put it back and grabbed another one.
“This?”
“That one is perfect,” I said.
She pressed it to her chest and twirled. I took a picture. Not for the internet. Not for anyone else. Just for me ,and maybe for her, years from now, when she wanted to see moments that weren’t touched by noise.
We bought the dress and a pair of sparkly socks she insisted she “needed,” then grabbed lunch at a tiny café with outdoor tables where pigeons dared to come a little too close.
Ray fed one a piece of bread before I could stop her.
“Sweetheart, no—”
Too late. A whole flock descended like a feathery apocalypse.
Ray screamed in absolute delight. “MORE BIRDS!”
“No, less birds! Much less!”
I swept her up and ran inside, laughing like a maniac while the barista chuckled behind the counter.
This .. this ...was the kind of chaos I missed.
The simple, beautiful, exhausting kind.
.....
By the time the sun started dipping low, we were both tired. Ray’s curls were sticking to her forehead, and my feet ached, but neither of us wanted to go home yet.
So we walked to the riverfront, bought two scoops of ice cream , strawberry for her, vanilla-brownie for me , and sat on a wooden bench facing the water.
Ray swung her legs, humming to herself between bites. A smear of pink ice cream painted one side of her mouth.
“Good?” I asked.
She nodded hard. “Berry-berry good.”
I smiled and wiped her cheek. She leaned into my hand instinctively.
Moments like this were dangerous. Soft. Raw. The kind that made honesty slip out before you could stop it.
“Sweetheart,” I started quietly, “Mama has to tell you something important.”
Ray looked up immediately, serious as a judge. “’Bout bed-time?”
I snorted. “No, not about bedtime.”
She relaxed, eating another spoonful.
I exhaled slowly, the words settling heavily in my chest before I let them leave my mouth.
“You know how it’s always been you and me?” I asked.
Ray nodded.
“Well… there’s someone else. Someone who wants to know you.”
She took another bite. “Who?”
“Someone special,” I whispered. “Someone who’s been gone a long time but wants to come see you now.”
She blinked. “A friend?”
I hesitated, then gently shook my head. “No. Not a friend. Someone more important.”
Ray studied my face, as if trying to find a picture in my expression.
“His name is Isaiah,” I said softly. “And he… he’s your daddy.”
Ray paused mid-bite.
“My daddy?” she echoed.
“Yes,” I whispered. “Your real daddy.”
She stared at me for a long moment, almost suspiciously, like she was trying to make sure I wasn’t joking.
Then: “He nice?”
A knot formed in my throat.
“Sometimes,” I said honestly. “He wants to be. He wants to meet you. He wants to see you smile. He wants…” I swallowed. “He wants a little time with you.”
Ray licked her spoon thoughtfully.
“He live far?”
“No,” I murmured. “Very close.”
She seemed to accept that, nodding slowly. “He give hugs?”
My heart cracked open.
“If you want him to,” I whispered.
Ray looked down at her ice cream, then up at the sky, then back at me with that tiny, earnest face that always managed to undo me.
“Mama?”
“Yes, honey?”
“You be there?”
My vision blurred.
“Always,” I whispered. “Every second.”
Ray nodded, leaning her head on my arm. “Okay.”
Just like that.
Okay.
I pressed a kiss to the top of her head, breathing in the warm scent of her curls. The river shimmered gold. The last rays of sunlight stretched across the water. And for a moment, even with the complicated weight of everything ahead, I felt steady.
Ray would know her father.
Jordan would build something real with his son.
And I… I would keep standing.
For her.
For both of them.
For myself.
Ray finished her ice cream, climbed into my lap, and sighed.
“Adventure tomorrow?” she asked.
I wrapped my arms around her and held her close.
“Yes,” I whispered. “Tomorrow, too.”
And as the sun dipped below the skyline, I let myself believe, just for tonight ,that maybe everything broken could still be put back together, piece by fragile piece.
Thirty Nine
Isaiah
I wasn’t expecting my phone to ring that morning.
Most mornings lately passed the same way: I woke up before the painkillers kicked in, stared at the ceiling until the tremors calmed enough for me to sit up, then dragged myself through a routine that felt like a bad imitation of the man I used to be.
But that morning… the ringtone sliced through the quiet of my apartment like a blade.
Alyssa.
Her name on the screen nearly stopped my heart.
I almost dropped the phone trying to answer it.
“Hello?” My voice cracked embarrassingly on the single word.
There was a pause , not cold, not hostile, just cautious. Like she was holding something fragile in her hands and didn’t trust me not to break it.
“I’m ready,” she said softly.
My breath snagged. “Ready… for what?”
“For you to meet her.”
The world tilted.
I had to grab the back of a chair just to steady myself. My knees nearly buckled.
“Are— Are you sure?” I whispered, because the idea of hoping for this had always felt dangerous.
“Yes,” she murmured. “It’s time.”
My hand shook so hard the phone clicked against my ear. “Alyssa… thank you.”
“You don’t have to thank me,” she said gently. “I’m doing it for Ray.”
“I know,” I breathed. “But I’m still grateful. More than you know.”
There was a tiny inhale on the other end, like maybe that meant something to her, even if she didn’t say it.
“I’ll text you the time,” she added. “Be home. Don’t… don’t disappear.”
A weak laugh escaped me. “I’m not going anywhere.”
Not anymore.
The call ended. I stared at the screen for several seconds, letting my pulse catch up to reality.
She was letting me meet my daughter.
My daughter.
My stomach twisted, equal parts joy, terror, and the kind of aching guilt no apology could ever fix.
I pressed a trembling hand to my face and inhaled slowly.
Today… mattered.
Today was everything.
....
Leo flew in the night before, insisting I “shouldn’t be alone right now.” Which was his polite way of saying he didn’t trust me not to collapse in a grocery store aisle again.
He was in my kitchen now, leaning against the countertop with a mug of coffee and a stare sharp enough to cut concrete.
“You’re pacing,” he said.
“I’m aware.”
“You’re shaking.”
“That too.”
“And you haven’t eaten.”
“I’ll eat after.”
“That’s what you said yesterday.”
I glared halfheartedly. “Leo, please. Not today.”
His expression softened immediately. “I’m just worried.”
“I know,” I said, quieter. “But I’m… this is different.”
“Yeah,” he murmured. “It is.”
He didn’t push again. Instead, he moved around the kitchen without needing to ask where anything was , he’d taken care of me enough times to know exactly which cabinets held what.
When he placed a small plate of toast in front of me, I forced myself to take a few bites.
I needed to be steady when Ray saw me.
No shaking.
No collapsing.
No looking like a ghost she should be afraid of.
“I called my doctor,” I said quietly, staring at the plate. “Asked if I should take an extra dose today.”
“And?”
“He said no. Said I need clarity more than comfort.”
Leo nodded, sliding into the chair across from me. “He’s right.”
I let out a shaky breath. “I don’t want her first memory of me to be… sick.”
Leo reached out and squeezed my arm. “Isaiah. You’re her father. She’s not going to see ‘sick.’ She’s going to see you.”
I swallowed hard.
I hoped he was right. God, I needed him to be right.
The Waiting
Alyssa texted an hour later.
“We’re on our way.”
Three words.
My pulse spiked so violently I thought I might faint.
Leo handed me water. “Sip. Not chug.”
I managed half a swallow before setting it down.
Then…
Then came the longest twenty minutes of my life.
I kept walking to the window, looking across to her building, then pacing back and forth like a caged animal. My tremors were back, stronger with every passing second, and Leo had to steady my hands twice.
I ran to the bathroom at one point just to splash cold water on my face. My reflection looked like a man preparing for a funeral.
Hair shorter.
Eyes sunken.
Cheeks hollow.
Skin pale.
But somehow, beneath all that, hope still flickered.
A knock sounded at the door.
My heart stopped.
Literally.
Stopped.
Leo squeezed my shoulder once before opening it , slow, gentle, careful.
And then there they were.
Alyssa.
And her..
She peeked shyly from behind her leg, clutching a small stuffed bunny.
My throat closed instantly.
Alyssa cleared hers softly. “Isaiah… this is Ray.”
Ray.
My daughter.
She looked exactly like her pictures — curls, big eyes, tiny frame — but real. Warm. Breathing. Standing three feet from me.
I couldn’t move.
I was scared if I blinked, she’d vanish.
Alyssa gave her a small nudge. “Honey, say hello.”
Ray stepped forward, tiny fingers curling into the hem of her dress.
“Hi,” she whispered.
I choked on a sob.
God, no. Not yet. Don’t fall apart yet. Breathe.
“H-Hi,” I managed. “I… it’s really nice to meet you.”
Her eyes flicked up curiously. “You cryin’?”
I let out a broken laugh. “A little.”
“Why?”
“Because I’m… happy.”
It sounded strange even to me. But it was true — painfully, overwhelmingly true.
Ray stared for another second before stepping closer and touching my knee with the tiniest hand.
“You okay?” she asked.
I bowed my head, unable to hold back anymore, tears falling freely.
“I’m okay,” I whispered. “I promise. I’m okay.”
....
Ray crawled onto the couch beside me with the caution of a toddler meeting a stranger , but not fear. More like curiosity.
Alyssa stayed standing nearby. Leo leaned against the wall, already misty-eyed.
Ray tilted her head. “Mama say… you wanna see me.”
I nodded, swallowing a lump. “I did. Very much.”
She considered that, chewing her bottom lip thoughtfully. “Why?”
A simple question.
An impossible answer.
I inhaled slowly. “Because… I should’ve been here sooner. Because you’re important. Because you’re mine.”
Ray blinked. “I yours?”
My voice cracked. “Yes.”
She processed that for several seconds with the seriousness of a queen judging a servant.
Then — “What your name?”
“Isaiah,” I said softly. “But you… you can call me whatever you want.”
She swung her legs. “What Mama call you?”
Alyssa cleared her throat. “Isaiah.”
Ray nodded, then looked back at me. “’Saiah,” she repeated.
My entire body shook.
I didn’t try to hide it.
......
Ray played with the string bracelet on her wrist for a moment before glancing up again. “What my name?”
“You want me to say it?” I whispered.
She nodded.
Alyssa watched me closely, as if she wasn’t sure I’d hold together long enough to answer.
I swallowed hard, forcing the words past the tightness in my throat.
“What’s your full name?” I asked gently.
Ray straightened like she was being tested. “Race Faith… Mar-tins.”
Martins.
My name.
She had my name.
A sob tore straight out of my chest. I didn’t even try to stop it.
Alyssa turned away for a second, wiping her eyes. Leo openly sniffled.
Ray scooted closer and put her little hand on my cheek.
“Don’t cry, ’Saiah,” she whispered.
I covered her hand with mine. “I’m sorry. I’m just… happy.”
She frowned softly. “You cry when happy?”
“Sometimes,” I said.
She thought about that, then leaned in and rested her head on my shoulder with zero hesitation.
Zero.
And that… that destroyed me.
I buried my face in her curls, breathing her in, letting every emotion I’d been swallowing for two years break free at once.
Alyssa’s quiet sniffle broke somewhere behind us, and I didn’t have to look to know she was crying too.
Leo muttered, “Jesus,” under his breath, wiping his face shamelessly.
....
We sat there a long while ,Ray tucked against me, her legs dangling off the couch, her bunny clutched in one hand.
Eventually she lifted her head and poked my chest. “Mama say… you sick.”
I froze.
Alyssa opened her mouth, maybe to redirect, but Ray plowed ahead.
“You okay?”
I forced a small smile. “Some days are hard. But I’m trying.”
She nodded seriously. “I take care you.”
The laugh that escaped me was half-sob. “I’d like that.”
She hesitated, then asked softly, “You… my daddy?”
My heart slammed against my ribs.
Alyssa inhaled sharply. Leo straightened, eyes wide.
“Yes,” I whispered. “I am.”
Ray blinked once.
Twice.
Then she wrapped her tiny arms around my neck and whispered, “Hi, Daddy.”
I broke.
Completely.
I held her ,not too tightly, careful not to frighten her , but enough to finally feel the reality of her in my arms.
“Hi, sweetheart,” I choked out. “Hi.”
Alyssa broke into silent sobs near the window. Leo covered his face with both hands.
Ray pulled back enough to look at me. “Daddy?”
“Yeah?” My voice was barely sound.
“You hungry?”
A broken laugh erupted from my chest. “A little.”
“I share my snacks,” she declared.
That did it for Alyssa , she turned away, trembling.
Leo’s glasses fogged.
Ray patted my cheek again. “Daddy no cry. I here.”
God.
How could a two-year-old undo a lifetime of mistakes with four simple words?
......
After a long while, Alyssa finally came over, kneeling beside the couch. Her eyes were red, mascara smudged, but her smile… it was soft. Genuine. Painful in its tenderness.
“You okay?” she asked me quietly.
I nodded, wiping my face uselessly. “Not even a little. But in the best way.”
Ray turned to her. “Mama, Daddy sad-happy.”
“Yeah, baby,” Alyssa whispered, stroking her curls. “He is.”
Ray switched her attention back to me. “We play?”
“If it’s okay with Mama,” I said.
Alyssa nodded. “It’s okay.”
Ray slid off the couch, grabbed her bunny, then tugged my hand.
I rose carefully , slower than I wished I had to , and followed her to the floor.
We played.
Blocks.
Stuffed animals.
Pretend kitchen.
Every few minutes she’d climb into my lap again, as if testing whether I’d still be there.
I always was.
Alyssa watched from the couch, wiping her nose every so often. Leo sat beside her, his arm around her shoulders, eyes glossy.
I didn’t deserve any of this.
But God… I was grateful for it.
......
Ray eventually curled against my chest, fighting sleep. Her little fingers twisted into the fabric of my shirt like she never wanted to let go.
“Daddy?” she murmured drowsily.
“Yes, sweetheart?”
“You stay tomorrow?”
My throat tightened. “As long as Mama says yes.”
She hummed sleepily. “Want Daddy stay…”
Alyssa stepped forward then, kneeling again. “Come here, honey,” she whispered, lifting Ray gently into her arms.
Ray whimpered a little but didn’t fully wake.
I touched her cheek softly. “Goodnight, baby.”
“’Night, Daddy,” she whispered.
I nearly sobbed again.
Alyssa looked at me ,not angry, not bitter, just… tired. Emotional. Softened in a way I hadn’t seen in years.
“Thank you,” I whispered.
She shook her head. “This wasn’t for you,” she said quietly. “It was for her.”
“I know,” I said. “But I’m grateful anyway.”
Her eyes flicked briefly toward the window, toward her building across the street.
“We’ll talk tomorrow,” she said softly. “About… schedules. Boundaries.”
I nodded. “Anything you want. Anything.”
Leo touched my shoulder once more. “You did good,” he whispered.
I didn’t feel like I had.
But as Alyssa carried Ray out the door , Ray half-asleep, clutching her bunny, head resting on Alyssa’s shoulder , I felt something in me loosen for the first time in years.
I wasn’t forgiven.
I wasn’t healed.
But I’d been given a gift.
A beginning.
And for however long I had left…
I’d hold onto it with everything I had.
Forty
Ray wakes before I do.
That’s always my first clue that the day is going to run on toddler-sized lightning instead of adult logic. I feel the mattress dip, then small fingers poke my cheek with the insistence of someone who has absolutely no concept of mortality or exhaustion.
“Mommy,” she whispers loudly — somehow louder than talking. “Mommy, wake up. I want Daddy.”
My eyes open immediately.
There’s no confusion this morning , no wondering which daddy she means. No awkward clarifications. Yesterday changed that. She met him. She touched his face. She said the word daddy with this wide, wholehearted sincerity that left both of us ruined.
I sit up slowly. “Sweetheart, it’s still early.”
She climbs onto my stomach like a kitten with elbows. “But Daddy might be lonely.”
God help me.
I brush hair from her face. “Let me wake up first, okay? Then we’ll get ready.”
She nods, bouncing on her knees because nodding normally is apparently impossible for her.
While she hums to herself and arranges her stuffed rabbit family across the bed, I slide out and make breakfast. She follows me everywhere, practically vibrating with the excitement of a child who has discovered an entirely new human to adore.
She eats two bites of food, then insists she’s “full.” I give her a look because she is very much not full ,she’s plotting.
“Ray,” I warn gently.
She presses her lips together like she’s storing secrets behind them. “Okay,” she whispers. “But can we go now?”
I pack her a snack anyway, because toddlers can go from “full” to “starving” in half a breath. After we get dressed , her in the little pastel dress she chose “because Daddy likes pink,” even though she has absolutely no evidence of this ,we step into the hallway.
And her tiny hand clamps around mine, warm and urgent.
“Come on, Mommy. He’s right there.”
Across the street.
Where he’s always been.
Where I didn’t want to look for days.
The elevator ride feels longer than usual. Ray hops in place, gripping her rabbit, clutching my fingers, asking every ten seconds if I think Daddy is awake. By the time we walk across the lobby and step outside, my heart is pounding hard enough I can hear it.
Paparazzi are around, they always are ,but they keep their distance today, maybe because my building has started getting stricter. Maybe because I’m with Ray. Maybe because I look like I’d bite them.
Across the street, the guard already recognizes us and lets us through without question. Ray runs ahead , not far, but far enough that she keeps turning back to make sure I’m still following.
When we reach his door, she presses the elevator button herself.
“Common ’vator,” she murmurs like a prayer.
The doors open. We get in.
And Ray starts bouncing again.
“He’s gonna smile when he sees me.”
“I think he will,” I say quietly.
“He’s gonna hug me.”
“Yes.”
“And I’m gonna say ‘surprise’ even though he knows I’m coming.”
I laugh softly. “I think he’ll still like it.”
When we reach his floor, something in my chest tightens so sharply I have to take a breath before I knock.
But Ray doesn’t wait.
She knocks with her little fist like she’s trying to wake a sleeping giant.
There’s shuffling behind the door, a cough, footsteps.
And then Isaiah opens it.
His eyes find Ray instantly , instantly , like his brain has been rewired since yesterday. His face lights up, softens, breaks, rebuilds itself in one expression.
“There’s my girl,” he whispers.
Ray squeals like he just transformed into a Disney character. She throws her arms up, demanding to be carried.
He lifts her without hesitation.
He struggles ,I see it. That tremor in his hand, the slight tightening in his jaw. But he holds her anyway, like it’s the most important thing he’ll do today.
“Daddy, I’m hungry,” she announces.
I blink. “Ray—”
He doesn’t even question it.
“Oh,” he says quickly, nodding as if receiving royal orders. “Of course. What do you want? Anything. I’ll make it.”
I raise my eyebrows.
He flashes me a little smile , tired but real.
“Let me spoil her,” he murmurs, somehow both joking and achingly sincere.
Ray pats his cheek. “Pan-cape.”
“Pancakes?” he repeats, breathless with affection.
“Yes. The circle ones.”
He laughs, soft and stunned, as if he genuinely cannot believe she exists.
“She just ate,” I say quietly but gently.
Ray whips her head around and glares at me.
I give her a look.
She looks back at Isaiah. “Daddy, I’m starving.”
He doesn’t hesitate. “Then pancakes it is.”
He carries her to the kitchen like she’s this fragile, sacred treasure. When he sets her on the counter, she swings her feet and watches him like he invented breakfast.
I help chop strawberries, mostly because I don’t trust him to handle a knife with the tremors. He doesn’t argue. He just smiles in a way that tells me he knows, and he’s grateful.
When the stack is ready, Ray claps her hands. He watches her eat like he’s witnessing a miracle.
And maybe he is.
---
After breakfast, she drifts toward the couch on wobbly full-belly legs and climbs onto him again. He settles back, lifting her onto his chest, and within minutes she’s asleep.
He freezes, barely breathing.
I sit across from them, knees tucked under me on the armchair.
His hand shakes slightly as he keeps it on her back. Not to hold her , she’s already secure , but to feel her warmth. To memorize her weight.
“She slept on me,” he whispers.
I nod.
“She’s so… she’s so small.”
“She’s two. They make them small on purpose.”
His laugh is quiet but real.
Then he exhales. “Thank you for letting this happen.”
I look at Ray’s soft cheek, pressed against his shirt. “She deserves to know him. Whatever happens with us, she deserves this.”
He swallows. “There’s something I wanted to tell you yesterday, but I didn’t want to dump everything on you at once.”
My stomach tightens again.
“What?”
“I’ve been getting second opinions. Quietly. A lot of them.” He wets his lips. “There’s a hospital in Boston testing a slightly different protocol. It’s aggressive. Risky. But… it might slow things down. Or buy time.”
The tiniest flicker of hope stirs in my chest.
“You started treatment already?”
He nods. “Last week.”
I rub my hands together slowly. “Isaiah…”
He studies my face. “I’m not naïve. I know what the doctors in LA told me. I know the likelihood. But if there’s even a small chance I get to be around longer for her… I’m taking it.”
I glance at Ray again — her little fingers curled into his shirt, her breaths soft and even.
“You’re trying,” I whisper. “And that matters. It really does.”
He lowers his head, eyes glassy.
“I don’t deserve support from you,” he says quietly. “But I’m grateful for it.”
“I’m doing this for her too,” I breathe. “But… part of me is doing it for you. I know what we had was broken, but not everything was. You mattered to me for a long time.”
His eyes soften. “You still do.”
The words hang in the air, heavy and complicated. I don’t respond. I don’t trust myself to.
To change the subject, I ask, “Did your neurologist say anything new?”
“Nothing encouraging. But I’m focusing on the parts I can fight.”
Ray shifts in her sleep and he freezes again, terrified he’ll wake her.
I stand and take the blanket Leo draped over the back of the couch. I place it over her gently. He gives me a grateful look.
For a few minutes, we stay like that , him holding her, me watching them, all of us wrapped up in a soft, strange peace.
---
Dinner is quiet, soft, awkward in a gentle way. Ray sits on his lap eating pasta, getting half of it on his shirt. He doesn’t care.
She feeds him one noodle at a time.
He pretends each one is the best thing he’s ever eaten.
And then she suddenly gasps.
“Mommy! Daddy’s nose is leaking!”
I look up sharply.
There’s a thin streak of blood trailing down Isaiah’s upper lip.
He grabs a napkin instinctively. “It’s okay. I’m okay.”
He stands , too fast , swaying slightly. I’m on my feet immediately, Ray clinging to me as I lift her onto my hip.
We follow him to the bathroom.
Ray is near tears. “Daddy hurt? Daddy hurt?”
“No, sweet girl,” I murmur into her hair. “Just a little blood. It happens.”
Isaiah rinses his face, hands trembling. The blood keeps coming but slower. He presses a towel to his nose and tries to smile at her.
“I’m okay, princess. Look — all clean.”
Ray refuses to let go of my shirt, eyes huge with worry.
When the bleeding finally stops, he steps back, exhausted. Pale. Unsteady.
I guide him to sit on the closed toilet lid before he can argue. “You need a minute.”
He nods weakly.
Ray leans forward from my arms and presses her forehead to his.
“No more leaking,” she commands in toddler-logic hospital terms.
He laughs shakily. “I’ll try.”
She kisses his cheek ,soft, hesitant , and rests her head on my shoulder again, still holding Isaiah’s sleeve with her tiny fist.
I swallow down a wave of emotion so strong it nearly takes me out.
When we walk back out to the living room, Leo stands up from the kitchen island, worry etched across his face.
“Everything okay?” he asks.
Isaiah nods. “Just a nosebleed.”
But Leo isn’t looking at him — he’s looking at Ray, then at me. He knows how fragile all of this is. How easily moments like these can break someone.
We stay a little longer. Ray lets Isaiah read her a book, even though he stumbles through the lines with a few shakes in his voice. She corrects his animal sounds. He acts offended. She giggles.
And I watch it all with a soft ache spreading through my chest.
When it’s finally time to go, Ray grips his shirt again.
“Come back tomorrow?” she asks.
Isaiah’s voice cracks. “I’d love that.”
She hugs his neck tight, like she’s known him her whole life instead of twenty-four hours.
And he closes his eyes, inhaling the moment like he can store it somewhere permanent.
When we leave, he walks us to the door. He touches Ray’s cheek one last time, then looks at me , quietly, gratefully, painfully human.
“Thank you,” he whispers again.
I nod.
We step into the hallway.
Ray waves. “Night night, Daddy.”
His breath catches audibly.
“Goodnight, baby.”
The door closes softly.
Ray sighs happily against my shoulder.
“Mommy?”
“Yes, sweet girl?”
“I like him.”
My throat tightens.
“I know,” I whisper. “I liked him too,once.”
But the ache in my voice is something she’s too young to understand.
And something I can no longer ignore.
Forty One
Isaiah
Six months
.
It’s strange how half a year can feel like an entire lifetime when you’re dying… and somehow also when you’re learning how to live for the first time.
Every morning I wake up, I check two things:
1. If my hands are shaking worse than yesterday.
2. If Ray is still calling me Daddy.
She is.
Every single day.
The first month after meeting her, I kept expecting her interest to fade. I thought she’d get bored, or overwhelmed, or confused.
But no ,she latched onto me with this ferocious toddler loyalty that cracked something in my chest wide open.
And that was the least surprising thing that happened...
I always thought six months was enough time for a man to ruin his own life, but apparently… it’s also enough time to rebuild one,just not in the way he expected.
Six months ago, I opened my front door and found a tiny girl with my eyes, my old smile, and a bravery I’ve never owned in all my three decades of living.
Six months ago, she called me daddy for the first time, and something inside me,something dry and brittle and dying,cracked open like a dam.
And somehow since that day, every sunrise has belonged to her.
...
The first month had been chaos. Beautiful chaos.
Ray wanted to do everything with me. Eat breakfast on my lap. Watch cartoons leaning against my shoulder. Brush my hair,even though she always yanked too hard and then apologized by kissing my cheek like she was bandaging invisible wounds.
Alyssa always hovered nearby, not intruding, not inserting herself, but ensuring Ray never felt overwhelmed. She watched quietly, like a mother guarding her daughter’s heart.
And mine too, in a way.
We agreed to announce Ray publicly a month after meeting her.
“I can't stop you from telling the world that she is yours, the Internet is talking ” Alyssa said quietly, arms crossed, gaze steady. “It’s not fair to her.”
And she was right.
My whole career had trained me to hide things. To hide my addictions. My relapses. My insecurities. My marriage problems. My cancer. My shakes. My fears. My humiliation.
But Ray wasn’t something to hide.
She was light.
“She’s my daughter,” I whispered, like I was saying the words for the first time.
Alyssa nodded once. “Say it again.”
“She’s my daughter.”
I repeated it until tears blurred my vision, and Alyssa squeezed my hand gently , a gesture that shouldn’t have meant as much as it did, but God, it did.
We told the world a week later. Not with a dramatic reveal. Just two photos,one Ray asleep on my chest, my hand on her back. Her curls tangled against my shirt.I remember sitting on my once-white couch,Ray doodling on my arm with washable markers,then the second photo of her little hand gripping my finger.
The caption was simple:
Meet my daughter, Ray.
The world lost its mind.
That picture broke the internet.
But none of that mattered. What mattered was how she ran into my arms every morning like she’d been waiting her whole life for the door to open.It also fixed something in me.
Alyssa didn’t want to be seen, so she kept a careful distance from cameras, the press, the red carpets. But she said yes when I asked if Ray could come with me to premieres. So long as she wasn’t overwhelmed.
And God, did she steal the show every single time.
She would wave shyly, then hide behind my leg, then laugh when reporters cheered for her. I’d hold her hand like she was breakable glass. Alyssa always walked behind us, sunglasses on, head down, but I knew she watched everything,watched me, watched Ray, watched the world’s reaction...
She wasn’t doing it for me.
She was doing it for our daughter.
The first red carpet we did together , God, I was a wreck.
She wore a tiny pastel dress with sparkles because she insisted she wanted to “match Daddy’s shiny jacket.” She held my hand the whole time… actually, no. She clung to my fingers like I was going to float away.
Flash after flash went off. People cheered her name. Someone shouted, “Ray, look here!”
She immediately stomped her foot. “NO. Don’t tell me.”
I burst out laughing, right there in front of twenty photographers.
Ray sat on my shoulders at one point. Press covered it like a divine second coming.
The next day the headline read:
ISAIAH MARTIN MELTS HEARTS AS DEVOTED GIRL DAD
And I didn’t hate it.
I took Ray with me everywhere I could ,interviews, sets, charity events. If someone even leaned too close to her, I’d position myself between them, possessive in a way I’d never been with anything in my life.
I had something worth protecting now.
Someone.
........
Two months in, we flew to Boston.
I had an appointment.
They came with me , Alyssa and Ray, without hesitation.
Alyssa booked the hotel herself. Said she wanted to make sure they were close. Ray held my hand on the plane and demanded cookies from the flight attendant (“for Daddy”).
The treatment was harsher than I expected. I slept most days. Ray woke me up with hugs and sloppy kisses and little drawings she taped to the wall. Alyssa kept track of my medication, timing them better than any nurse ever had.
Boston was quieter, gentler. People cared less about paparazzi and more about their Dunkin’ orders. Alyssa seemed more relaxed there. Ray too. Leo, exhausted and barely holding himself together, slept most days on the guest bed like an overworked parent.
Half my days were spent in that cold oncology wing. The nurses adored Ray,she’d hand out stickers to everyone, even the grumpy ones. She’d hold my hand when the chemo made my fingers tremble and whisper, “It okay, daddy. I’m here.”
God, those words.
Those words were medicine.
Alyssa would squeeze my shoulder whenever the spasms from the Parkinson’s hit. She pretended it didn’t bother her, but her eyes always softened, just for a second.
Not love.
Not romance.
Just compassion for the father of her child.
And that was enough.
We stayed three weeks.
Three weeks where Ray slept curled with me in the hospital bed. Three weeks of Alyssa brushing my hair back when I got feverish. Three weeks of me pretending I wasn’t falling apart all over again.
But some things didn’t need pretending.
Like how I watched Alyssa read to Ray at night and remembered why I fell in love with her the first time.
Like how she looked at me when she thought I was asleep , with sadness but never regret.
....
The traveling came next.
Twenty-seven countries in three months.
It wasn’t a rich-man bucket list thing. It was… desperation. Hope. A father choosing memories over misery. Moments over medicine.
Ray saw snow for the first time in Switzerland and screamed because she thought it was raining sugar. In Paris, she insisted I carry her everywhere,even though my bones ached and the chemo made my legs feel like rubber,but I carried her anyway. Alyssa tried to take her from me, but Ray glued herself to my neck like a koala and I couldn’t say no.
In Seoul, paparazzi swarmed us, and Alyssa stared them down like a lioness. Ray had fallen asleep on my chest, and Alyssa tucked my jacket around our daughter the same way she used to tuck her own blankets around me when I pulled all-nighters years ago.
In Nairobi, Ray chased giraffes and called them “long-neck horsies,” and Alyssa laughed so hard she cried.
We became a strange little trio,broken man, careful mother, fearless child,but it worked.
It worked better than anything in my life ever had.
.....
It’s been nearly six months since Ray first said “daddy.”
Almost a month until her third birthday.
Today… today is the day that decides everything.
My review.
My results.
My life expectancy.
My palms haven’t stopped sweating.
I sit in the clinic waiting room in Boston. My leg shakes—Parkinson’s tremor mixed with anxiety. Ray sits on my lap, tiny fingers tracing patterns over the bracelet Alyssa bought her. Alyssa sits beside us, her arm brushing mine every so often when Ray wiggles. Leo stands near the door, looking like he hasn’t slept since 1998.
“Daddy?” Ray whispers, pressing her forehead to mine. “You scared?”
That alone almost undoes me.
“I’m okay, peanut,” I manage, voice rough. “Why? Do I look scared?”
She nods solemnly. “Your hand is doing the shakey-shake.”
Alyssa’s breath hitches beside me.
I exhale shakily. “Yeah… it does that sometimes.”
She pats my cheek with both hands. “I hold it for you.”
And she does,folding my trembling fingers into her tiny palms.
I nearly break.
Alyssa’s eyes glisten. She looks away quickly, like if she watches this moment too long she’ll crumble with me.
“Isaiah,” she murmurs, barely audible, “whatever happens… we’ll handle it.”
Not I’ll handle it.
We.
For Ray.
Never for us.
But still… it’s enough to steady me.
The nurse finally calls my name. I shift Ray to my hip and stand. Alyssa rises too. Leo follows, like a silent shadow.
We walk together.
Like a family.
A complicated, broken, healing family.
....
The oncologist, Dr. Whitmore, greets me with a warm nod. He looks like someone who has delivered far too many bad truths.
We sit.
Ray climbs into my lap.
Alyssa’s knee touches mine.
Leo stands behind us.
“Isaiah,” the doctor begins gently, “I’ll start with the good news.”
My heart slams.
“The chemo worked.”
Alyssa releases a breath so shaky it’s almost a sob. Ray smiles because she thinks good news means ice cream.
Leo sinks into the nearest chair like someone took fifty pounds off his spine.
I close my eyes for a moment. Relief washes through me like cool rain.
“But…” the doctor continues softly.
My stomach drops.
Alyssa’s hand instinctively finds my forearm.
“The cancer has slowed… significantly,” he says. “But it hasn’t stopped. It’s still spreading, just at a reduced pace.”
I swallow hard.
“So what does that mean?” my voice cracks despite me.
Dr. Whitmore folds his hands. “It means the timeline has shifted. With continued treatment and the right medications to manage discomfort… you likely have two and a half years. Possibly more, if the next regimen responds well.”
Two and a half years.
Not the death sentence I feared.
But not a life, either.
I nod slowly. Numbly. My throat tightens until every breath burns.
Alyssa wipes her cheek. “Two years… that’s something. That’s time. That’s—” she breaks off and covers her mouth.
Ray hugs my neck, sensing the shift in the room. “Daddy… are you okay?”
I force a smile for her. “Yeah, sweetheart. I’m okay.”
She presses her forehead to mine. “You promise?”
I swallow. “I promise I’m here with you.”
She beams and kisses my nose. Alyssa looks away again, but I see the tear escaping her lashes. She loves this little girl so fiercely it hurts to witness.
I look at her. Really look at her.
“Alyssa,” I murmur.
She meets my eyes.
“I’m sorry.”
Her brows pinch. “Isaiah—”
“No,” I whisper. “Let me say it. Let me say it right.”
“I’m sorry. For everything. For the affairs. For letting myself fall apart. For making you feel alone. For not being the husband you deserved.”
Her eyes soften. I continued,
“I’m sorry for the man I became,” I continue, voice low but steady. “For choosing fame over us. For letting addiction swallow me. For breaking your heart. For making you carry everything alone. You deserved so much better than who I was.”
A tear slides down her cheek. She doesn’t wipe it.
“And I’m sorry,” I add, voice cracking, “for making you raise our daughter alone. I should have been there. I should have protected you. I should have protected her.”
She inhales slowly. “Thank you… for saying it. Really saying it.”
“I forgave you a long time ago,” she whispers. “Before I even knew it. You’re Ray’s father, Isaiah. And you’re trying. That means more than what came before.”she added
I nod, my throat burning.
“And Isaiah?” she whispers. “You’re doing better now. For Ray. And that’s what matters.”
“Everything I’m doing is for her,” I rasp. “She’s the best part of me. The only good part I have left.”
Alyssa shakes her head gently. “You still have good in you. Ray just brings it out.”
I don’t deserve that grace,but she gives it anyway.
....
Later, back at the hotel, I make a decision.
A terrifying one.
A necessary one.
I call a major media outlet and agree to an interview.
I never imagined I’d tell the world my truth.
But Alyssa was right.
“Speak up,” she said. “Someone needs to hear it.”
So here I am , on camera, Ray in my lap , doing a live interview with the biggest media outlet in the country.
The interviewer leans forward. “Isaiah, you’ve asked for this segment to be raw. Honest. Completely unfiltered. Why?”
I look down at Ray. She smiles up at me, and something inside me snaps open.
“Because I spent too many years pretending,” I say quietly. “Pretending my marriage was perfect. Pretending my addictions didn’t exist. Pretending I wasn’t the one who broke everything.”
I swallow hard.
“I lost Alyssa because of me. I hurt her. I failed her. And the world called her cold or distant, but the truth is she saved me more times than I deserved.”
Ray leans into my chest.
I wrap an arm around her.
“And now,” I continue, voice trembling, “I’m facing something bigger than career mistakes. I have cancer. And Parkinson’s disease. And I don’t know how many years I have left.”
The silence in the room is painful.
“But the past six months have been the most meaningful of my life. Because I finally met my daughter. And she… she makes me feel brave.”
Ray pats my face. “Daddy cry?”
“Yeah,” I whisper. “But good tears.”
I look into the camera.
“I’m telling my story because I want people to know the truth ,not the tabloids, not the PR version, not the shallow headlines. I want them to know that love can still exist after destruction. That forgiveness is real. That my daughter saved my life even while I’m losing it.”
The interviewer wipes a tear.
The crew is silent.
And I hold my daughter like she’s the only thing tethering me to the earth.
Maybe she is.
Forty Two
Jordan
I used to think heartbreak was loud.
The kind of pain that comes crashing into your ribs like a car wreck, all sound and shock and sharp edges.
But this…
This thing I’m living through now?
It’s quiet..
Cruelly quiet.
I scroll through my phone for the hundredth time today. Not because Alyssa texted,she hasn’t,but because her silence is somehow louder than any goodbye.
And there it is again. Another notification from a gossip account.
A blurry paparazzi photo.
Alyssa in a baseball cap.
Isaiah holding Ray on his hip.
All three of them walking into a Boston hospital.
Family.
It shouldn't make my stomach twist like a torn wire, but it does.
It always does.
I lock my phone and press the heels of my palms against my eyes, willing the sting away.
“Daddy?”
That tiny voice pulls me back.
Colton stands in front of me,my son,his blond curls messy, the dinosaur pajamas I bought him last week already too small at the ankles. He stares up at me with the same blue eyes I see in the mirror.
The same eyes that caused him so much pain long before he even knew what pain was.
I swallow hard and force a smile. “Yeah, buddy?”
“You okay?” he asks, head tilted.
I reach out and ruffle his hair. “I’m fine. Just tired.”
He nods like he completely understands, even though he’s only six.
He shouldn’t have to understand sadness this young.
He shouldn’t have to watch his father learn how to be one.
“What you want for breakfast?” I ask.
“Pancakes!” he beams. “With strawberry. Not too much syrup. Mommy always put too much syrup. Make my tummy hurt.”
I pause for a second. He says it so casually, like he’s reciting a weather report.
Not too much syrup.
Mommy made his tummy hurt.
Kaya used to drown everything in syrup…and vodka.
And sometimes she mixed them.
“Alright,” I say softly. “No tummy aches today.”
He climbs onto the chair while I mix the batter. The kitchen smells like sugar and childhood, and it hits me how much time I’ve lost. How much time I willingly threw away because I wasn’t man enough to face the mess I’d created.
The first few months after he was born, I didn’t even want to look at him.
Not because of him.
Because of her.
Because of everything she’d done to me—taken from me—stolen from me.
Because a part of me thought if I held him, I’d be acknowledging what she forced me into.
I flip a pancake and close my eyes.
I remember the way she used to whisper in my ear:
“No one will ever believe you over me. You’re a drunk, Jordan. A druggie. A disappointment.”
She wasn’t wrong.
Not then.
And when I found out she was pregnant, I lost it.
Spiraled.
Fell so far into addiction I barely crawled back out alive.
I didn’t want the kid.
But I didn’t want to kill myself either.
So I ignored them both.
Until that night.
The night she threatened to go public.
Threatened to drag my father’s name through the mud.
Threatened to take everything from me unless I paid her more.
I’d been drunk.
Barely standing.
I wanted her to shut up. To go away. To stop breathing the same air I was suffocating on.
But then Colton waddled in.
He looked right at me.
Right at my broken, angry, useless mess of a self.
And he said, “Dada?”
My knees gave out.
Not because he recognized me.
But because I didn’t recognize myself.
I went to rehab the next morning.
And now… years later, I flip another pancake while he laughs at some cartoon on the TV.
Now I get to wake up with him in my apartment.
Now I get to make him breakfast in peace.
Because I fought.
Because I got clean.
Because I took Kaya to court and proved she wasn’t fit,drug tests, erratic behavior, police reports.
Because I stood in front of a judge and said, for the first time in my life:
“I want my son.”
And the judge believed me.
Temporary full custody.
Supervised visitation for her.
It’s not perfect.
But neither am I.
“Daddy?” he calls between bites. “You made a face.”
I blink. “What face?”
“This one.” He squishes his cheeks with his hands, pulling his mouth down in an exaggerated frown.
I laugh. It feels rusty.
“Was I sad?”
He nods.
“I’m not sad,” I lie gently. “Just thinking.”
He shrugs, accepting it. “Okay. Can we go park after? And can we play soccer? And can we get chocolate milk?”
He lists his wants like tiny wishes.
“Yeah,” I say softly. “We can do all that.”
---
Later, while he naps on the couch, I sit at the dining table with my phone.
Alyssa’s chat is pinned at the top.
JORDAN:
Morning. Took Colton to the park. He kicked the ball without falling this time. Big improvement.
No reply.
JORDAN:
Appointments went well today. Lawyer thinks full custody is likely.
I’m trying, Lyssa. I’m doing everything you said I should.
No reply.
JORDAN:
Kaya screamed at me during visitation. Colton cried. I held him until he slept.
I don’t want to be like her. Or like the man I used to be.
No reply.
She reads the messages.
I can see the little “seen” indicators.
And I don’t blame her for not responding.
She told me we needed time.
She told me she needed space.
She told me to fix my house,fix my life,fix my son.
And I'm trying.
Every goddamn day I’m trying.
But every time I look at another headline about Isaiah and Alyssa and Ray… my chest feels like it’s caving in.
I watch them from afar.
Not in person.
Online.
On news videos.
On fan clips.
Isaiah looks like a resurrected man,fragile but alive in a way he hadn’t been in years.
And Ray loves him.
God, she adores him.
Alyssa smiles more now too.
Soft, gentle smiles she used to have back in college.
Smiles she used to give me sometimes before everything fell apart.
It twists something inside me.
I know—deep down—that she isn’t doing this for him.
She’s doing it for Ray.
For their daughter’s heart.
But still…
Still I lie awake some nights wondering:
What if being around him brings back old feelings?
What if she remembers why she loved him first?
What if I lose her,not because of what I did,but because of who he was to her?
That’s my greatest fear.
Not death.
Not failure.
Not relapse.
Losing Alyssa.
Again.
Permanently.
But I can't ask her for reassurance.
I can’t demand answers or guarantees.
She asked for time.
She asked for patience.
She asked me to become the man I should have been years ago.
And I owe her that much.
So I sit.
And I wait.
And I grow.
For her.
And for the little boy sleeping under my blanket.
---
Court finally gives me a date.
One more hearing.
My lawyer calls me at noon.
“Jordan, the judge is impressed. The drug tests, the stable home, the therapy, the parenting classes—you’re doing
everything right. I’m confident the temporary order will become permanent.”
Permanent custody.
For the first time in a long time, hope doesn’t scare me.
After the call, I sit beside Colton and stroke his hair.
“I’m trying, buddy,” I whisper. “I’m trying to be a dad you can be proud of.”
His eyes flutter, and he mumbles, “Love you, daddy.”
And I break.
Quietly.
Silently.
The same quiet heartbreak I've been carrying for months… but different now.
This one is hopeful.
This one is healing.
---
A week later, after hours of testimony, medical records, drug screenings, and Kaya’s meltdown in the courtroom, the judge grants me temporary full custody again,renewed and extended this time.
Kaya gets monitored visits.
She leaves the courtroom screaming at me, but I don’t react.
Colton holds my hand tightly.
“Can we go home now?” he whispers.
“Yeah,” I breathe. “Let’s go home.”
---
That night, I pack our bags.
I haven’t told Alyssa.
She hasn’t asked.
But I need to go back to New York.
Not for her.
For my son.
For me.
For whatever future I’m trying to build.
As I zip the suitcase, Colton runs in.
“Where we going?” he asks.
“New York,” I say. “Daddy got work there. And… someone I care about lives there.”
He tilts his head. “A friend?”
I swallow. “Yeah. Something like that.”
He nods seriously, then asks, “Do she have toys?”
I laugh. “Probably.”
He thinks for a moment.
“Okay. I like New York then.”
I pull him into a hug.
And for the first time in months… maybe years…
I feel like a man who still has a chance.
Not a guarantee.
Not a promise.
But a chance.
We head to the airport before sunrise.
Colton clings to my hand.
I breathe in the cold morning air.
New York glows faintly in my mind—bright, messy, intimidating, hopeful.
Alyssa is there.
Ray is there.
My future is somewhere in that city too.
As we board the plane, I exhale and whisper to myself:
“I’m coming back, Lyssa. Not to take you away. Not to rush you. Just… to be the man you can trust again.”
Colton yawns and leans against me.
The plane doors close.
A new chapter waits for us in New York.
And for the first time in a long time…
I don’t feel unworthy of it.
Forty Three
By the time I step into the backyard, the early-morning sun is already warming the concrete tiles, turning the whole space soft and golden. I inhale sharply, trying to convince myself I’m breathing in excitement and not nerves. It’s Ray’s third birthday ,my baby is three , and somehow that truth hits me harder than any moment these past months.
She’s twirling around in her nightgown with Emeric while Isaiah pretends to be a dragon behind them, roaring dramatically. He’s breathing too hard though, even from here.
I can see it,that slight collapse of his shoulders, the tremor he tries to disguise with a laugh.
He’s healthier in some ways, but the cancer still shadows him. It feels like every day something inside him flickers just a little dimmer.
Amy whistles from the ladder beside me, stretching up to tape a pink, glittery banner that reads PRINCESS RAY in big curly letters.
“You’d think we were decorating Buckingham Palace,” she mutters, shaking the tape dispenser aggressively. “She’s three, Alyssa. Three. Kids don’t even remember stuff like this.”
“Yeah,” I answer distractedly, fluffing the edge of one of the rose-gold table runners. “But I’ll remember. And so will the pictures. Plus you literally bought a Maybach  for Emeric a week ago,he is only three.”
Amy climbs down and lands in front of me. “And that is exactly why I can’t trust you right now. You look like you’re about to throw confetti and cry into it.”
I roll my eyes at her, but the worst part is she’s right. I’m stretched thin ,between joy, fear, nostalgia, and this strange ache that’s been chewing at my ribcage for months.
Amy lowers her voice. “So… when are you going to call Jordan?”
There it is. She didn’t even sugarcoat it.
I stiffen, rearranging a tray of cupcakes like that was the question they’d been waiting to hear all morning.
“I’m not… dealing with that right now.”
“You’ve said that for SIX months.”
“I’ve had other priorities.” I glance at Ray, now giggling as Isaiah lifts her into the air despite the shake in his hands. “Ray comes first.”
“I know that,” Amy says gently. “But don’t pretend this is only about Ray.”
I don’t respond. Mostly because I can’t.
Amy sighs loudly. “Alyssa. You’ve punished Jordan enough. He messed up, yes. He hid something huge, yes. But you broke up with him because Isaiah is DYING. Not because you stopped loving Jordan.”
The name feels like a bruise pressed too soon.
She studies me, tilting her head. “Unless…”
Her eyes narrow.
“Unless you are falling for Isaiah again.”
My breath catches, way too fast to hide it.
“Alyssa?” she presses. “Do you still love him?”
“No.”
It leaves me instantly, instinctively , too quick.
“No, Amy. I don’t. Not like before.”
She waits.
Amy never pushes unless she knows I’m lying to myself.
I swallow. “I love him… but not the way I used to. It’s different. It’s—”
“Guilt,” she offers.
I nod once, painfully.
“It’s guilt,” I whisper. “And fear. And maybe… gratitude. He’s trying. He’s really trying now. He’s a good father to Ray. And I want her to have these memories. I want her to have him.”
Amy softens. “And Jordan scares you.”
My eyes sting. “Jordan makes me feel like I’m finally alive again. And then Isaiah… gets worse. How am I supposed to choose?”
“You’re not choosing,” Amy says gently. “You’re hiding.”
I open my mouth to deny it, but she touches my shoulder.
“You deserve happiness too, Lys.”
I look away, because if I look at her too long, I’ll break.
Before I can answer, Ray shrieks happily from across the yard. “MAMA! DADA IS A DRAGON AGAIN!”
Isaiah grins at me over her shoulder, sweat dripping down his forehead but refusing to let her down. My chest tightens.
Amy whispers, “And THAT is why you’re scared to let Jordan in.”
I don’t respond. I can’t.
---
An hour later, the yard is filled with neighbors, friends, kids tearing around in princess dresses and plastic crowns. Music floats through the speakers, Ray insisted on the Encanto soundtrack,and the smell of grilled food mixes with lemony frosting.
Isaiah sits on one of the lounge chairs, breathing steadily but looking exhausted. He’s doing that thing again,pretending he isn’t winded, pretending the tremor in his left hand isn’t worsening. Ray climbs onto his lap with a tiara sliding sideways on her head.
“Daddy,” she says seriously, “today I’m the princess, so you must obey me.”
Isaiah chuckles. “Yes, Your Majesty. But only if you promise not to tell anyone my dragon secrets.”
She giggles and kisses his cheek.
Every part of that moment is beautiful and heartbreaking.
I’m carrying a tray of juice boxes when I hear the back gate click open.
Amy nudges me with her elbow. “Don’t freak out. But… that’s not a delivery guy.”
I turn.
And my heart punches my ribs.
Jordan stands there,sweater, jeans, hair pushed back by the wind.
And beside him, holding his hand, is a little boy with a shy smile and curly dark hair.
Colton.
My breath stutters.
Jordan’s eyes find mine instantly.
And it’s like six months compress into a single, painful heartbeat.
He walks toward me slowly, like he isn’t sure he’s allowed to approach.
“Alyssa,” he says softly.
My stomach twists.
“What are you doing here?” It comes out breathy, like my lungs forgot how to function.
Jordan rubs the back of his neck. “Uh… Isaiah invited me.”
“What?”
And then Isaiah appears behind me, carrying a tray of empanadas like he didn’t just drop a bomb.
“Alyssa,” he says with a sheepish smile, “Ray has been begging me for weeks. Every five minutes it’s ‘Where Jowdan?’ ‘Where Jojo?’ ‘I want Jojo!’” He shrugs. “So I called him.”
I blink. “Oh.”
Jordan bites his lip. “I didn’t want to miss Ray’s birthday.”
Before anything else can be said, Ray spots him.
“JOJO!!!!”
She sprints across the yard, barreling into him so hard he stumbles and laughs. She wraps her arms around his neck.
“You came! You came! I missed you!”
He hugs her tight, eyes closing briefly. “I missed you too, princess.”
Amy whispers beside me, “That’s it. I’m adopting him.”
Jordan kneels and gently pulls Colton forward. “Ray, this is my son, Colton. Colton, this is Ray — the girl I told you about.”
Ray gasps dramatically. “Your baby?! He’s so small! Hi small baby!”
“I’m six, you are the small one,” Colton says with quiet dignity.
Ray nods thoughtfully. “Okay small baby six. I no small. I big girl. Right daddy”
"Yes baby. You are a big girl"
Within seconds, Emeric runs over, and the three of them merge into one chaotic child tornado.
Isaiah smiles as he watches them. “That’s adorable.”
Jordan smiles too, but then his gaze flicks back to me.
And everything inside me gets unbearably still.
......
I spend most of the party doing everything except being in the same space as Jordan.
I refill drink coolers.
I check on the food.
I carry plates.
I rearrange decorations that absolutely do not need rearranging.
Amy eventually corners me behind the dessert table.
“You are a chicken.”
“I’m not avoiding him.”
“You’re literally hiding behind a cake.”
“It’s a big cake.”
“And you’re a big coward.”
“I’m not ready,” I whisper sharply. “I don’t know what to say. What if I open my mouth and everything comes out wrong?”
Amy softens, then nudges my shoulder. “He came. He showed up. With his kid. That means something.”
I swallow hard.
Across the yard, Jordan is crouched with Ray on his shoulders while Colton and Emeric pretend he’s a magical horse. He laughs , really laughs , and the sound hits me somewhere deep.
It hurts.
It warms.
It confuses.
Isaiah walks by, overhears us, and says quietly, “He’s still waiting for you, you know.”
I stiffen. “Isaiah—”
“I’m not blind, Lys.” His voice is calm, not accusing. “I see how he looks at you. And I see how you look at him when you forget you’re scared.”
My throat tightens painfully.
“I don’t want you to feel guilty for moving on,” he says. “Especially when… my time is limited.”
“Please,” I whisper. “Don’t talk like that.”
He smiles sadly. “I’m just being honest. Don’t… let fear run your life.”
I press my palm to my chest. “Isaiah—”
“Go,” he whispers. “Talk to him.”
But I don’t.
Not yet.
Because then Ray climbs onto Isaiah's lap again, holding a half-eaten cupcake, and she kisses his cheek.
“Daddy, when I grow up, I want to be strong like you.”
Isaiah’s face crumples. Tears fill his eyes instantly.
“Oh sweetheart,” he breathes, voice breaking, “I wish I was strong enough to carry you forever.”
Ray wipes his cheek with her tiny hand. “It’s okay, Daddy. I carry you now.”
And he breaks.
He pulls her into his chest and sobs quietly.
Everyone pretends they don’t see it , but every parent in the room feels it.
I hold my breath until my ribs hurt.
......
The party winds down slowly. Kids nap on blankets. Adults chat softly. The backyard smells like melted frosting and warm candles.
Jordan approaches me as he prepares to leave,Colton sleeping heavily in his arms, his little head tucked into Jordan’s neck.
“Alyssa,” he says softly.
I force myself to meet his eyes.
“I just wanted to say…” He shifts Colton gently and lets out a breath. “I’m still waiting for you.”
My heart slams painfully.
“You don’t owe me anything,” he adds. “I’m not asking for answers right now. I just… wanted you to know I meant it. Six months ago. And today.”
He steps back, offering a small, tired smile.
“Happy birthday to her. And… take care of yourself too.”
I open my mouth , to say something, anything, but the words tangle and choke.
He nods once, turns, and walks toward the gate.
I stand there gripping the edge of the table until my fingers ache.
And for the first time in months…
I’m terrified.
Hope and heartbreak feel exactly the same.
Forty Four
The city smelled like warm asphalt and street pretzels, and my legs felt like they were held together with rubber bands and leftover adrenaline from rehearsal. Sixteen run-throughs of the opening sequence in one afternoon , my lungs were filing complaints with HR. If my director had asked for one more pass, I might’ve faked my own fainting spell. Dramatically. Broadway-worthy.
I pushed through the theatre’s side exit, tugging my hair up into a ponytail, ready to collapse into my ,crawl home, and sleep for fourteen years. But then I saw him.
Jordan.
Standing on the curb with Colton perched on his hip.
And at their feet ,a tiny blur of curls, pink backpack, and sparkly shoes.
Ray.
My heart tripped, stumbled, then flat-out face-planted.
“Mommy!” she squealed, abandoning her hopscotch square and sprinting across the pavement so fast I briefly considered
enrolling her in track. Jordan barely had time to set Colton down before she was barreling straight toward me.
I braced myself and caught her mid-air. “Baby,” I breathed into her curls. “What are you doing here?”
Ray pointed back at Jordan dramatically, her curls bouncing with indignation. “berry brought me!”
“Berry???” I arched a brow.
Jordan lifted one shoulder, a soft smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. “In my defense, I didn’t give myself the nickname.”
Ray wiggled out of my arms and launched herself right back into his, like gravity was permanently rigged in his direction. My stomach twisted , not painfully, just… achingly.
I hadn’t told Ray she couldn’t spend time with Jordan. I’d never do that. But I had kept myself at a distance , quietly, gently, cowardly , trying to give him space to move on from me.
Except he hadn’t.
He’d waited.
And I’d let him wait.
For months.
Colton toddled up beside Ray, holding a pack of sidewalk chalk almost as big as his head. “Chawk?” he asked Jordan, tugging on his sleeve.
Jordan bent down. “Yeah, buddy. Let’s show Ray how to draw a dragon.”
Ray gasped. “A real dragon?”
“A blue one,” Colton announced confidently.
Ray accepted this seriously, as if blue dragons were the only reasonable kind. The two of them plopped down on the
pavement and immediately began scribbling furious toddler masterpieces.
Jordan straightened, rubbing the back of his neck, eyes meeting mine for the first time tonight.
It hit me like a punch to the chest.
He looked… tired. But hopeful. And gentle. And the kind of patient that could break a woman in half.
“I’m sorry if this is… too much,” he said quietly. “Isaiah called me this afternoon and asked if she could see us. I didn’t want to overstep.”
“You didn’t,” I whispered. “She adores you.”
He swallowed, eyes flicking to Ray, then back to me. “I adore her too.”
There it was ,the ache again.
I lowered myself onto the curb beside him, tucking my skirt under my thighs. For a few minutes we watched the kids argue over whether their dragon needed wings or roller skates.
Jordan’s knee brushed mine.
I didn’t pull away.
He exhaled slowly. “It’s been a long day for you.”
“Mhm. Rehearsal is trying to kill me.”
He nudged me lightly. “But you’re shining in that show, Lys.”
My chest squeezed at the sound of that nickname ,soft, familiar, spoken like he still wasn’t sure he was allowed to use it.
I blinked hard. “Jordan…”
He stared down at his hands, rubbing his palms together, like he was gathering courage grain by grain.
“I told you I’d wait,” he murmured finally.
The words went straight through me. A punch and a caress all at once.
A memory flashed , him in my doorway months ago, voice cracking as he said I’ll wait for you, Alyssa. Take all the time you need.
And I had.
Months of time.
Months of fear.
Months of avoiding myself more than him.
I swallowed, my throat suddenly thick. “You… did. And I’ve been so unfair.”
He looked up sharply. “No.”
“Yes,” I insisted, voice trembling. “I’ve been keeping you at arm’s length. Letting you get close to Ray but not… me.”
Jordan’s jaw flexed, but he didn’t interrupt. That alone cracked me open ,most men rush to defend themselves. Jordan stayed still so that I could speak.
“I wasn’t trying to hurt you,” I whispered. “I was just overwhelmed. After Isaiah got sick… I felt like I was grieving him long before anything actually happened. And I felt guilty for wanting a future that didn’t revolve around hospitals and treatments.”
A tear slid hot down my cheek before I could stop it.
Jordan brushed it away with the softest touch.
“I was terrified of choosing happiness,” I confessed. “Terrified of losing it the second I had it. Terrified of hurting you. Of hurting Ray. Of hurting… everyone.”
Jordan’s breath hitched, but he stayed quiet. His eyes weren’t sad ,they were patient. Gentle. A little broken, but whole enough to hold both of us.
When he finally spoke, it was barely above a whisper.
“Alyssa… you don’t have to choose between loving Ray’s father and building a life with me.”
I shut my eyes.
He continued, voice steady, warm, grounding:
“I know where I stand. I’m not trying to take his place. I’m not here to rush you. I’m here because… I want a place with you. However small or slow or complicated.”
My breath shuddered out.
He shook his head softly. “You could’ve pushed me away completely. You could’ve told me to leave. But you never did.”
“I tried,” I admitted. “I tried so hard.”
He smiled, a real one this time. “Yeah, I noticed. And I just… stayed. I don’t know how not to care about you.”
Something inside my chest ,something stiff and scared and barricaded ,finally loosened.
I opened my eyes and looked at him fully.
Really looked.
The way his lashes trembled when he blinked.
The way he held himself like he was afraid to breathe too loud and scare me off..
The way he watched both kids with a tenderness that felt like a prayer.
I whispered, “Jordan…”
He leaned in slightly, not touching, but close enough that I felt the warmth of his breath on my cheek.
“I haven’t stopped loving you,” I said, voice cracking. “I just… didn’t know if I deserved you.”
His eyes darkened, soft and devastated. “Hey. Don’t ever say that. Not to me.”
My fingers moved before my brain did, sliding into his hand.
His breath caught.
I squeezed gently. “I’m tired of running from you.”
For a long second, he didn’t move. His lashes fluttered. His thumb brushed the inside of my wrist, slow and reverent, like he wasn’t sure I was real.
“Alyssa…” he whispered.
And then he cupped my face with both hands.
His palms were warm. His fingers trembled.
He leaned in, slowly, giving me every chance to pull back. I didn’t.
Our lips met , soft, steady, a sigh shared between two exhausted hearts. Not desperate. Not rushed.
Home.
Jordan’s forehead pressed to mine when we broke apart. “God,” he breathed, voice shaking. “I missed you.”
A small sob escaped me before I could catch it.
Ray, who had been completely ignoring us in favor of her blue dragon, suddenly gasped loudly. “MOMMY KISSED JOJO!”
Colton looked up, eyes wide. “Kiss!”
Ray clapped. “Do it again!”
Jordan let out a strangled laugh. “We have an audience.”
I wiped my face quickly. “A very demanding one.”
The kids abandoned their chalk and ran toward us at full speed, shrieking with excitement. Ray leaped into Jordan’s arms; Colton climbed up on my lap like he’d known me his whole life.
I laughed ,really laughed ,until my ribs hurt.
Jordan wrapped one arm around me and one around Ray, pulling us all close with a careful, trembling touch, like he was terrified I’d slip away if he held me too tightly.
For the first time in months, I let myself lean fully into him.
Ray pressed her cheek to mine. “Mommy happy?”
I kissed her curls. “Yeah, baby,” I whispered. “Mommy’s happy.”
Jordan’s thumb brushed my shoulder , a silent promise. Not a claim. Not pressure.
Just warmth.
The kind that stays.
The kind that waits.
The kind that feels like the start of something safe.
Something earned.
Something finally ours.
Forty Five
Jordan
I’ve been on a lot of red carpets in my life,mostly because my father forced me into them like some kind of show pony , but tonight feels… different.
Maybe because Alyssa is the one holding my hand.
Maybe because I can’t stop staring at her like I’m a lovesick teenager who never learned subtlety.
Or maybe because I know what she fought through to stand here , not just the roles, not just the rejections, not just the comeback…
But the grief, the divorce, the pregnancy alone, the public humiliation, the nightmare years she’s spent trying to rebuild her life piece by piece.
And now she’s here.
On Broadway’s biggest night.
Nominated for Best Lead Actress.
My heart is basically in my throat.
“Jordan, breathe,” she laughs under her breath, nudging me with her shoulder as cameras flash around us.
“Can’t,” I whisper. “I think I forgot how a few months ago.”
Alyssa rolls her eyes, but she’s smiling,that soft, shy smile she only gives me when she’s genuinely happy.
Behind us, Ray tugs at my sleeve with all the impatience of a tiny queen demanding attention.
“Jojo,” she says, waving her sparkly purse at me, “Mama said I’m the luckiest charm.”
“Lucky charm,” Alyssa corrects her.
“That too,” Ray says, proudly.
I crouch and kiss her cheek. “You’re more powerful than luck. You’re like… magic.”
I tap the purse. “This thing is just decoration.”
Ray grins like she’s convinced she could win a Tony herself tonight.
Honestly? I wouldn’t bet against her.
Colton wanders over, wearing his miniature tux like he stepped straight out of a wedding catalog. He’s seven now , bigger, bolder, and always glued to Ray like she’s the hero in his personal superhero movie.
“Ray, did you know we might be on TV?” he says, bouncing on the balls of his feet.
Ray gasps. “Coltie, we’re famous.”
“Um,” I interject, “let’s set realistic expectations—”
But they’ve already run off, hands clasped, beelining toward Isaiah, who’s waiting just off-camera with a security team.
I watch them go.
I watch Isaiah , holding himself together with more strength than half this industry combined , lift Ray into his lap like she weighs nothing.
He’s paler. Thinner. His tremors harder to hide.
He looks like a man fighting against a clock he can’t outsmart.
When Ray leans in to whisper something in his ear, he closes his eyes like it’s the only thing keeping him grounded.
Alyssa sees it too.
Her breath hitches, barely audible, but I feel it.
I slide my hand to her back.
“You okay?” I ask softly.
She nods, though it’s a lie. “He insisted on coming. I told him he didn’t have to—”
“But he did.”
I squeeze her hand. “For Ray. And for you.”
She looks up at me, eyes glistening. “I hate knowing he’s hurting.”
“I know,” I whisper, brushing her cheek with my thumb. “Me too.”
---
Inside the theater, the energy is electric, buzzing like champagne bubbles under the skin.
We take our seats: Alyssa between me and Isaiah, Ray on Isaiah’s lap, Colton next to Ray.
Ray whispers commentary the entire time in a volume she thinks is “quiet.”
“That dress is pink. And sparkly.”
“He looks like a prince.”
“I’m hungry, Mama. Can I have a snack? Please? Please-please?”
Alyssa laughs softly and gives her the emergency gummy bears tucked in her clutch.
Isaiah leans toward me, his voice low. “You still nervous?”
I stare at him. “Terrified.”
He smirks. “Good. Means you care.”
I narrow my eyes. “You enjoying this?”
“Deeply.”
We both grin.
It feels strange , being this close to him. A year ago, I thought he hated me.
Hell, I thought he might punch me.
Now we occupy this strange, fragile peace built on Ray, honesty, and the brutal reality that time is running out.
And I respect the hell out of him.
As a father.
As a man fighting for every day he still gets.
The host finishes a joke, and then—
“And the nominees for Best Lead Actress in a Musical are—”
Alyssa’s category.
My chest tightens like someone tied a rope around it.
Isaiah goes still, jaw clenched, eyes forward.
Ray freezes mid-gummy-bear with her mouth open.
Alyssa grips my hand so tightly my fingers might never work again.
The presenters open the envelope.
I swear the whole room holds its breath.
“And the Tony Award goes to…”
A beat.
A lifetime.
“Alyssa Turner — The Last Song of the Lighthouses!”
Ray shrieks. “MAMA WON!”
The entire audience bursts into laughter.
Alyssa covers her mouth with her hands, tears spilling immediately , not pretty, not controlled , just real.
Isaiah hugs her with shaking arms, whispering something to her I can’t hear.
And I’m just… gone.
Absolutely gone.
My eyes blur.
My throat burns.
When she stands, I stand with her ,because she looks like her knees might give way.
She squeezes my hand once before letting go.
Then she walks to the stage.
People cheer like she’s the second coming of Broadway itself.
Her dress glitters under the lights.
She’s crying before she even reaches the microphone.
And then she laughs , that soft, nervous laugh that's been my undoing since the first day I met her.
“Wow… I didn’t prepare a speech,” she says, voice trembling. “I didn’t think— I still don’t think— this is real.”
The audience chuckles.
She breathes. Lets herself settle.
“I want to thank the cast and crew. You were my family long before tonight.”
She wipes a tear.
“I want to thank my daughter, Ray, for being my brightest light. You remind me every day what bravery looks like.”
Ray waves from the aisle and shouts, “HI MAMA!”
More laughter.
Alyssa smiles through tears. “I also want to thank Isaiah… for teaching me perseverance, even when life tries to break you.”
The cameras cut to him. He’s crying — not subtly. Not quietly.
“And Jordan…”
Her voice falters.
She finds me in the crowd..
The room feels like it tilts.
“…thank you for believing in me when I didn’t know how to believe in myself.”
My vision breaks completely.
She finishes, beautiful and emotional and humble, and when she returns to her seat, I pull her close the second she sits.
“I’m so proud of you,” I whisper against her ear.
“I’ve never been prouder of anyone in my life.”
She turns her face and kisses my cheek, still shaking.
Isaiah lifts his phone to take a picture of us ,Alyssa leaning into me, my lips pressed to her temple, Ray holding the Tony like she’s queen of the universe.
He types something into his notes app, but I see the title of the photo before he tucks the phone away:
“My girls. My joy. My peace.”
---
The after-party is loud, glamorous, overflowing with champagne and dancers and a hundred congratulatory speeches.
But we find a small, quiet corner near the balcony.
Alyssa steps into the cool night air to breathe, clutching her Tony like she’s afraid someone might steal it.
I wrap my arms around her from behind, resting my chin on her shoulder.
“You did it,” I murmur.
She exhales, her body melting into mine.
“It feels unreal,” she whispers. “Like this isn’t my life.”
“It is,” I tell her gently. “You earned all of it.”
A moment passes.
Wind moves through the city like a sigh.
She turns in my arms, looking up at me with eyes still shining.
“Jordan?”
“Yeah?”
“I… I couldn’t have done this without you.”
“You would’ve,” I tell her instantly.
“No,” she says, firmer. “You helped me stand up again. You helped me breathe again. You helped me…”
Her voice breaks.
“…remember who I used to be.”
I tuck a strand of her hair behind her ear. “Lys…”
She puts her forehead against my chest.
“I don’t deserve you,” she whispers.
“And I don’t deserve you,” I counter, cupping her face. “So I guess we’re even.”
She laughs softly, watery and warm.
Before I can kiss her, Ray barrels onto the balcony, dragging Colton by the hand.
“Mama!” she shouts. “We found cake!”
Alyssa giggles. “Of course you did.”
Isaiah steps out behind them, slower, careful, leaning slightly on the railing.
“You two okay out here?” he asks.
Alyssa nods. “Yeah. Just breathing.”
He studies her for a long moment ,something soft but tired in his eyes.
“I’m proud of you, Lys,” he says quietly.
She walks to him and hugs him without hesitation.
He closes his eyes. Holds her tight.
When they pull away, he glances at me, a small smile tugging at his lips.
“Take care of my girl,” he says.
“I’ve been trying,” I answer softly. “I’ll keep trying.”
He nods, satisfied, then turns back toward the kids.
“Come on. Show me this cake.”
Ray squeals and drags him inside.
Alyssa watches him go, her expression flickering , joy, sorrow, gratitude, grief. All tangled.
She leans back against me, releasing a breath that sounds like a confession.
“I just want him to have a little more time,” she whispers.
I wrap my arms around her again.
“Then we’ll make the time he has… beautiful.”
She turns her head to look at me.
“Stay with me tonight?” she asks quietly.
“I’m not going anywhere.”
She smiles — soft, tired, full of love.
The kind of smile people cross oceans for.
Then she lifts her Tony, brushes her fingers over the engraved letters, and whispers:
“I think… this is the beginning of something good.”
I kiss her forehead.
“No,” I say gently.
“This is the beginning of everything.”
Forty Six
Isaiah
I’ve always hated the red carpet. Too many flashes, too many smiles, too many people pretending the world is perfect. But tonight, the carpet isn’t the problem. Tonight, I’m the problem.
Because I can barely walk. My hands shake so badly I can’t hold my cane without the fear it will drop. My legs feel like they’re moving underwater, slow and unresponsive, every step a reminder that the cancer is winning. Parkinson’s tremors have grown worse , subtle at first, now obvious even to strangers. I feel like a brittle sculpture they’re propping up with each breath.
But then I look down, and my heart stops in the best way possible.
Ray.
My little girl. My miracle.
She’s holding my hand in one, Jordan’s in the other, spinning in her miniature gown like she owns the world. Curly hair bouncing, eyes sparkling, laughter spilling into the cameras’ lenses, and suddenly , I don’t feel weak. Not for a second.
“She’s going to steal the show,” I whisper to no one in particular.
Jordan glances down at me and nods, smirking. “She’s got the charm. The style. The power. But don’t worry, Dad, I’ve got your camera work covered.”
I try to lift my own camera for a selfie with her, and my hands shake so violently it’s hopeless. Jordan catches it before it falls. He’s always been better at this ,steady, unflinching. I don’t tell him, but every time he does something like this, it cuts straight to my chest. He’s perfect in ways I can’t ever compete with, yet I’m grateful beyond words.
Alyssa’s beside me, one hand on my back, the other on Ray. She doesn’t need to check the time; I know she’s scanning me every five minutes. Every slight tremor, every hitch in my breath,
every wince when I step. She sees me. She always sees me. Even when the world thinks I’m strong, she knows I’m fragile.
“They think I’m strong,” I murmur quietly to myself.
“They don’t see my bones screaming. They don’t see me dying. But she sees it. Alyssa always sees it.”
Ray tugs on my sleeve. “Daddy, carry me!”
I groan, forcing a smile, and she giggles. She’s small but heavy, and she loves that she can make me do things I normally can’t manage. I lift her onto my lap, feeling every ache, every tremor, every failing muscle. But she doesn’t notice. She only cares about the view, the sparkle, the attention , and her laugh is worth every painful second.
Alyssa crouches to whisper, “Do you need me to hold you up?”
“No,” I murmur, almost bitterly, “I’m fine.”
She ignores me and slides an arm under my elbow, steadying me without a word. That’s all I need , her presence, not her judgment. I close my eyes for a moment, breathing her in, remembering every time she saved me, every time she stayed when no one else would.
The ceremony begins. I can barely hear the music over the sound of my own heartbeat. My body aches. My hands shake. But Ray’s curls, her tiny fingers, her sparkling eyes ,they make me feel like I’ve survived centuries. That, somehow, I’ve earned a place in the sun despite everything.
When they announce my nomination for Best Actor, my heart flutters like it did the first time Alyssa and I kissed, the first time Ray said “Daddy,” the first time I realized I was going to be a father. The old adrenaline rush, now paired with every fear I’ve ever had, every failure I’ve ever lived with.
Alyssa whispers, “You’re going to be okay.”
I nod, because I have to. I take her hand briefly, squeeze it like it’s my lifeline.
Jordan leans in beside me. “Whatever happens, they’re going to see who you really are.”
I laugh softly, but it’s more of a sigh. “I hope so.”
Ray claps her tiny hands. “Go, Daddy! Win!”
Her excitement is infectious. I can’t help but grin, though my muscles ache, though the tremors betray me. I adjust my lap, holding her closer.
Then the name is called: Isaiah Martins.
I feel the weight of the world crush me.
A few more words, then the applause.
The standing ovation.
People clapping for me.
I try to step onto the stage. My legs are heavy, and the tremors nearly topple me. Jordan’s steadying hand at my back. Alyssa’s supportive gaze. Ray gripping my shirt like I’m her anchor. And I know, somehow, I’m not falling. Not tonight. Not ever, if I can help it.
At the podium, I take a shaky breath. The microphone is unforgiving, but my voice carries.
“Thank you,” I begin, and it’s almost a whisper, “thank you… to everyone who’s believed in me.”
I pause. Tremor in my hand as I clutch the podium. Look down at Ray, seated on my lap, her tiny fingers clutching the base. She beams.
“This… this is for my daughter. Ray Faith Martins. My miracle. The child I didn’t deserve, but somehow was given the greatest gift of my life.”
I swallow, my throat tight. “And to Alyssa… for giving me a second chance at fatherhood, at being better than I was. For letting me love, for letting me be loved.”
My voice cracks. I blink hard. “I also want to publicly apologize… to Alyssa. For the mistakes, the heartbreak, the years I caused pain… that was all on me.”
