The audience is silent, and my eyes flick to Jordan. He’s staring at me like I might break, but with a calm intensity. He saved her, and in doing so, he saved me too. I nod toward him. “And to Jordan… for loving her the way she deserved. For showing me what family can be.”
I close my eyes for a moment. Breath in, breath out. Tremors shaking my hands against the podium. “If this is my last award… if this is my last moment on a stage like this… I’m glad it’s one Ray will remember.”
The applause swells, a tide that lifts me up without easing the ache inside.
Backstage, my body finally gives out a little, but Alyssa is there. Always. Jordan is there. Always. Ray, my miracle, holding my hand, laughing, sparkling, dancing. I feel like I’m seeing everything I love in one frame, condensed, perfect, untouchable.
Backstage, reporters swarm. Ray steals the attention. Her curls bounce. Miniature gown spinning. She twirls for the cameras, over and over, giggling, radiant, fearless. She’s stealing hearts left and right, but that’s okay. She’s mine, in the best ways that matter.
Three different children’s film producers approach Alyssa, offering her roles for Ray. She stiffens. I know her rules already,only commercials, only when she’s ready. And only films produced by Jordan when she’s older. No exploitative
pressure. No quick fame. I nod, approving silently. She catches my gaze, and I see gratitude there, a shared understanding.
Ray hops into my lap again, curling into me like I’m her favorite pillow. “Daddy, I like the lights,” she whispers.
“I know, baby,” I murmur, brushing her curls. “You get to keep them in your eyes forever.”
Alyssa kneels beside me. “How are you holding up?”
I force a smile. “Fine,” which is really code for, barely holding it together, but she’ll see that I’m okay because I don’t want to worry her.
She rests her hand over mine. “You’re stronger than anyone knows. But it’s okay to let me see you.”
I squeeze her fingers back. “I know. Thank you.”
Jordan crouches beside us, whispering, “She’ll be loved when you’re gone. And fiercely.”
I glance at him, realizing he means not just Ray, but Alyssa too. And somehow, despite everything, I believe him. Somehow, despite all the fear, the jealousy, the uncertainty, I trust him. And I trust Alyssa.
Because she always chooses love. Even when it’s messy, even when it hurts, even when it’s terrifying.
The ceremony ends. Awards are carried, speeches said. Cameras flash until my eyes ache. Ray refuses to leave my lap. Jordan lets her. Alyssa keeps a protective hand on my back. My chest swells with love, pride, and grief all at once. A sharp, sweet mix that tastes like home, like family, like every chance I ever got to do better.
I rest my head against Alyssa’s shoulder. She leans into me. Jordan’s arm snakes around her, his hand brushing mine. Ray’s
tiny body curled in mine, and for the first time in a long time, I don’t feel alone.
“Good,” I whisper, barely audible. “She’ll be loved when I’m gone.”
Alyssa brushes my hair back, pressing her lips to my temple. “She will. And so will you, Isaiah. You’re part of that love too.”
I close my eyes. Smile. Tremble. Laugh. Cry. Every emotion at once, perfectly messy, perfectly human.
And I know, deep down, with my lungs and my heart and my failing body… that even if this is the last big moment I experience, it’s the one that will outlast me. In Ray. In Alyssa. In Jordan. In the love we built together.
And that, for once, is enough.
Forty Seven
Two Years Later
The morning light spilled softly into the apartment, brushing across Isaiah’s pale face as he lay in the wheelchair by the window. His body was failing him faster than any of us could
have imagined,his tremors harsher, his breathing shallow and uneven. Ray climbed onto the chair’s small footrest and leaned against him, pressing her tiny cheek into his shoulder. “Daddy… are you okay?” she asked, her voice quivering, eyes wide as she traced the contours of his hand with hers.
Isaiah tried to lift a hand to smooth her hair, but it shook violently. A small cough rattled in his chest, and then he vomited again. I handed him the basin quickly, my stomach twisting as I watched him lose strength with every breath. Leo hovered behind me, steady and calm, cleaning the mess and adjusting his pillows. “He’s so brave,” I whispered, more to myself than anyone. “So small, but… still fighting.”
Ray’s hand never left his, squeezing it tightly. “You’re okay, Daddy. You’re okay.” She didn’t fully understand, but she wanted to make it true. Isaiah’s lips trembled into a weak smile. “I’m… okay… baby,” he rasped, voice barely audible, but the effort it took to speak made my chest ache. He hated that we saw him this fragile, this diminished.
I helped him sip water, careful not to spill, and brushed the sweat-damp hair from his forehead. “You’ve done enough today,” I whispered, though he shook his head weakly. “No… I want… to see it,” he said, his eyes brightening just a little, trying to hold onto the small spark of hope that life had left him. “The sunset… please.”
I glanced at Ray, whose eyes shone with excitement. “Can we go, Mommy? Please?”
I nodded, swallowing the lump in my throat. “Yes. We’ll go, just the three of us.” I helped Isaiah into his wheelchair and wrapped a blanket around his shoulders, careful not to jostle him. Ray took my hand on one side and Isaiah’s on the other. Her little fingers were warm, firm, anchoring him to this world.
The elevator ride felt endless, and every corner of the apartment building smelled faintly of antiseptic and sun-warmed brick.
Outside on the balcony, the city stretched beneath us, the skyline painted in orange, pink, and gold. Ray let out a squeal, pointing at the streaks of light. “Look, Daddy! Look at the sky!” Isaiah’s hand quivered as he reached for hers, his thumb brushing against hers in a weak grasp. “It… it’s beautiful,” he whispered. “Magic,” he added, his voice raw with fatigue but filled with something tender, something alive.
Ray leaned against him, bouncing on the balls of her feet. “It’s magic, Daddy! Look! Look at the clouds! They’re pink and gold, just like me!” She laughed, her curls bouncing. Isaiah’s lips curved into the faintest of smiles, eyes glistening. “Pink and gold,” he repeated, almost as if memorizing it. I knelt beside him, holding his other hand, feeling the frailty beneath my palm, the tremor in his fingers, and wishing desperately that the sun could freeze, that this moment could stretch into forever.
When the wind brushed against our faces, carrying the hum of the city below, I saw the ache in his eyes—the knowledge that these small joys were fleeting. I wanted to tell him it was okay to rest, to let go of the fight, but the words caught in my throat. Ray looked up at me, wide-eyed. “Mommy… is Daddy… gonna be okay?”
I forced a smile that didn’t reach my eyes. “Yes, baby. He’s okay. We’re all right here.” Ray’s small hands squeezed mine, grounding me in the fragile present.
We stayed there long enough for the sun to kiss the horizon. Isaiah leaned slightly against the railing, murmuring about the colors, about the warmth, about the way Ray’s laughter reminded him of all the times he had thought he couldn’t breathe and yet had survived. I held his hand, brushing my
thumb along the back, memorizing the feel of it. I pressed a gentle kiss to the top of Ray’s curls, trying to hold the moment still.
Back inside, he was exhausted. Every step back to the apartment left him pale, tremors increasing with fatigue. I settled him back into the wheelchair, adjusting blankets around him. His small frame slumped against the cushions, so delicate and frail that my chest ached with the need to protect him.
“Stay… please,” he whispered, voice hoarse, eyes on mine. Not romantically—never that—but as if he needed home, needed the place where he had always felt safe. I slid beside him, brushing my fingers through his hair. Ray pressed close against his chest, murmuring comfort that only a child could offer, a pure, unfiltered attempt to soothe the incomprehensible.
I hummed softly, the melody quiet, just a whisper, as if the sound could hold him there with us. Every breath of his was a struggle, each one sharp and unsteady. My hand remained intertwined with his, memorizing every shiver, every tremor, every small flicker of muscle that remained strong.
Ray adjusted, pressing closer. “Daddy… it’s okay. I’ll be strong,” she said, voice small but determined. Isaiah’s eyes glimmered as he looked down at her, a fragile warmth breaking through the exhaustion. “My… my girl…” he whispered, his voice cracking as the words barely left him.
We spent hours that afternoon like that—holding, breathing together, silent conversations of love and memory passing between us. I brushed his hair back constantly, murmuring encouragements, prayers, fragments of memories we shared, as though keeping him anchored in the world of love surrounding him could slow the creeping shadow of sickness.
He dozed lightly, eyelids fluttering, chest rising and falling with a soft rasp. Ray pressed her small hand over his, as if willing him to stay. “Don’t go… stay,” she whispered, and I kissed the top of her curls, tears slipping freely, whispering to her that we were all here.
“Mommy… tell me a story,” he mumbled softly, voice cracked and dry. I leaned in, taking his hand, feeling the coolness against my own warmth. “Do you want the one about the day we saw the stars and thought the city was glowing?” I asked. His lips curled faintly in recognition. “Yes… that one…” he breathed, his fingers tightening slightly around mine. I told it slowly, carefully, every detail, pausing when he winced or cough shook his chest. Ray’s giggles occasionally filled the pauses, her laughter like fragile glass against the weight of sorrow pressing around us.
Hours passed. The sunlight faded into golden amber, shadows stretching across the apartment floor. Ray’s curls rested against his chest as he drifted into a light sleep, eyelids twitching, chest rattling softly. I kept brushing his hair, humming, whispering, softly repeating, “You’re safe. You’re loved. You’re so, so loved.”
He shifted slightly, murmuring in his sleep, and I felt him lean just a little closer into the warmth of Ray’s body. “My girls… my girls,” he muttered faintly, almost to himself. My chest ached as I pressed my lips to his shoulder, holding the hands of the two most important people in my life, silently promising that we would hold him in memory, that we would honor every breath, every laugh, every fragile moment.
As the city lights began to twinkle below us, Isaiah slept, still holding Ray’s small hand, mine pressed against his other. We didn’t speak, we didn’t need to. The room was filled with a fragile serenity—the quiet before the inevitable storm. I let my
tears fall freely, my cheeks pressed to his frail shoulder. Ray slept lightly, murmuring occasionally in her dreams, clutching him with the pure innocence only a child can give.
I held both of their hands in mine, my mind breaking and mending in the same instant. Every tremor, every weak exhale, every faint pulse of life was a treasure I wanted to hold onto forever. I whispered to both of them, “We are here. We love you. We are never leaving.” The soft rhythm of our breaths mingled with the evening city’s hum, a quiet cocoon of love and desperation.
Later, when he stirred again, groaning softly in pain, I leaned closer. “I’m here, love,” I said. “We’re right here.” Ray pressed a small kiss to his temple. “It’s okay, Daddy. I’m not going anywhere.” His lips trembled in a small smile, weak but real, as he closed his eyes, drawing what little strength he had from the two of us.
It was in these final days, in these twilight hours before the world would tilt irreversibly, that I realized how much love could carry a soul, even as the body failed. Isaiah rested, cradled by the warmth of Ray and me, while the world outside continued unaware of the tiny, luminous miracle of a father and daughter, a mother and her charge, holding onto the edges of life with every ounce of love left.
And I sat there, breathing slowly, letting the silence speak for all of us, knowing that every moment we had together was a gift, every laugh and sigh a precious currency against the unstoppable march of time. Ray shifted again, hugging him tighter, and I kissed her curls, whispering, “We’ll stay with him. Always.” And in that soft evening glow, we stayed.
Forty Eight
The room smelled faintly of antiseptic and warm blankets, but beneath it all, the lingering scent of Isaiah’s cologne still clung to the sheets, and it made my chest ache. He looked impossibly small, curled against the pillows like a fragile bird, his skin pale and mottled, veins like faint blue ink under the surface.
His hands, always so strong, now trembled violently whenever he tried to move them. His eyes, though still burning with life, were clouded with exhaustion. The once proud, towering figure I had loved now seemed a child, frail and wan, every breath a labor, every heartbeat a fragile triumph.
Ray clung to him, her small curls pressed against his chest, one hand in his, the other in mine. “Daddy… please don’t be sad,” she whispered, her voice trembling. I pressed a hand over hers, trying to reassure both of them. “We’re right here, baby. We’re not going anywhere.”
Outside the soft glow of the early evening, Isaiah’s mother, Hudson, Kaylin, and Maddie had arrived, all flown in to see him one last time. My throat constricted as I watched them enter.
His mother’s face was etched with grief; she moved slowly to the bed, every step careful as if she were approaching a sacred space.
“Isaiah…” she whispered, voice breaking, “my son…” She pressed her trembling hands to his frail ones, tears spilling down her cheeks. “Mom,” he rasped, voice cracked and weak, “I love you.” It was a struggle for him to even speak, but the words came, a fragile offering of everything he had left to give. She kissed his forehead, a soft, quivering kiss, and sat back on the edge of the bed, holding onto his hands like he was all she had left.
The first to step close was Isaiah’s mother. She approached slowly, hands trembling, eyes red and wet. “Isaiah… my son…” Her voice broke, and I could feel the weight of every year she had spent worrying, loving, and hoping. She leaned down, pressing her lips to his frail hand.
“I’m so proud of you, even now. You’ve given me more than I ever imagined. More than I deserved.” Isaiah’s weak fingers twitched at her touch. “Mom… I love you,” he rasped. I brushed a tear from her cheek and whispered, “We’re all here, safe with you.” She nodded, gripping his hand so tightly it was almost painful to watch.
Hudson was next, kneeling at the edge of the bed. His broad frame seemed almost too large for the delicate space, yet he shrank into it, careful not to crush the fragile man before him.
“Big brother… I don’t know how to say this,” he choked, voice thick, “but I love you. So much. You’ve always been my hero.” Isaiah’s lips trembled, and his hand, weak though it was, found Hudson’s.
“I… love you too,” he whispered. “Always… always proud of you.” Hudson buried his face in his hands, a soft sob breaking free, and I felt my own chest twist with grief.
Kaylin came next, gently brushing his hair back. “Isaiah… you’ve always been the heart of this family. I’ll never forget your laugh, your stubbornness… your love.”
Her words were tender, shaky. Isaiah attempted a smile, but it was fleeting, worn thin by fatigue. “Kaylin, My… girl…” he whispered, his voice breaking. I brushed my hand across his shoulder, silently encouraging him, reminding him that we were holding him up.
Maddie pressed close as well, soft fingers grazing his arm. “You’ve been the best brother… the best father… the best person I know. Don’t worry, we’ll keep loving her… your girls… always.”
Isaiah’s eyelids fluttered, his lips moving in a faint, effortful smile. “Mads” he whispered again, as if repeating it aloud anchored him in the love that surrounded him.
Tasha and Julian hovered quietly, but their grief was no less profound. Julian bent forward, voice cracking.
“Isaiah… it’s been an honor. Truly. You’ve made our lives brighter, our work… meaningful.” Tasha pressed her hand against his, eyes wet.
“You’ve changed so many hearts, Isaiah. And you’ve been ours too.” His fingers twitched weakly, trying to respond with the faintest acknowledgment, a ghost of his usual strength in the face of total exhaustion.
Leo leaned down, his calm presence a steady anchor. “It’s okay, Isaiah,” he said softly.
“You’ve loved, fought, and protected. You’ve given her a life full of light.” Isaiah’s eyes softened at him, filled with gratitude.
He nodded faintly, a small tremor in his lips, acknowledging everything without speaking.
Jordan was already kneeling by the bed, silent vigil kept all day. He leaned forward now, holding Isaiah’s hand gently.
“I’ve got her. I’ve got Ray. Always,” he said, voice thick with emotion. Isaiah’s eyes flickered to him, and in a weak but determined gesture, he squeezed Jordan’s hand.
“Promise… promise me… she’ll be safe… happy… loved,” he murmured. “I promise,” Jordan replied firmly, tears spilling freely. “I promise, Zay. Always.”
Amy moved quietly to the edge, kneeling beside Isaiah. “You’ve been amazing,” she whispered. “And I forgive you… for everything… always.” Isaiah’s fingers twitched faintly, a fragile smile brushing his lips.
“Thank… you…” he murmured, eyes glistening. “Love… you.” She pressed her forehead against his, soft sobs shaking her frame.
Each goodbye was a dagger to the chest, a collection of fractured hearts trying to make him feel safe, cherished, and remembered. One by one, they stepped back, each lingering glance a farewell, until only us remained,me, Ray, and Isaiah.
Ray’s small hands clutched his frail fingers tightly. “Daddy… please don’t leave me,” she whispered. His eyes met hers, the love in them burning brighter than the pain. “I… want… to stay,” he whispered, voice faltering. I pressed my cheek against his shoulder. “We’re here. Always,” I whispered back.
He turned his gaze toward me, frail, trembling. “Lie to me… one more time,” he murmured softly. I leaned close, my tears falling freely, brushing my hand through his thinning hair. “I love you.
Forever,” I whispered. His lips trembled, forming the faintest, most peaceful smile.
Ray pressed closer, whispering, “Daddy… it’s okay. I’ll be strong.” My hand held his, tracing the fragile lines of his face, committing every detail to memory. I hummed softly, rocking him gently as he struggled to breathe, every sound a reminder of how fragile this moment was.
I hummed softly, the melody delicate and trembling, brushing his hair back over and over as his chest rose and fell unevenly, every breath a laborious treasure. I felt him gaze at us, really look at us, memorizing, holding on to the warmth and love we offered him. “My girls…” he whispered, voice breaking, and my chest constricted so sharply I could hardly breathe.
And then… he exhaled.
It was long, fragile, trembling,a sound that seemed to take all the air from the room with it. And then silence. Absolute, suffocating, hollow silence. No inhale followed. Nothing. The weight of absence settled over us like a storm, crushing and infinite.
Ray’s small body pressed against mine, crying and calling his name.
“Daddy… Daddy… no!” I held her, rocking gently, trying to absorb the rawness of her grief. Jordan appeared immediately, lifting her into his arms.
“It’s okay, baby. We’re here. It’s okay,” he murmured, voice thick with emotion.
I kissed Isaiah’s pale forehead, lingering, memorizing, whispering, “Thank you for loving her. Thank you for loving me.”
Minutes passed, and the room remained still, the weight of loss pressing into every corner. Jordan comes back in quietly with a sobbing Ray, eyes red and swollen, moving to my side and wrapping me in his arms. He held me upright as I shook, pressed a gentle kiss to Ray’s curls, and stayed, silent, unwavering.
We remained there, tethered to the memories, the love, the life Isaiah had left us with. In that silence, I promised silently that we would move forward, together, carrying him in every heartbeat, every breath, every action. And for the first time, though grief clawed at me, I felt a fragile thread of hope,that love, in its purest form, could survive even the most unimaginable loss.
Forty Nine
It was barely a week since Isaiah had left us, yet the air felt impossibly heavier than the world itself. The apartment, the city, everything,it had hollowed out where his laughter had lived. I held Ray close, her small body pressed to mine, and she
clung to me as if I were the only thing keeping her tethered to the world.
“Mama… will we see him?” she whispered, voice small, trembling. I swallowed hard, forcing my own trembling back. “Yes, sweet girl. We’ll see him one last time. And then… we’ll carry him in our hearts forever.”
The night before the funeral, the world held a vigil. Thousands of messages, thousands of flowers, candles lit across the city. Isaiah had been only forty-one. Forty-one years of life, love, and brilliance, now gone far too soon. He had lived fully, fought relentlessly, yet his body had betrayed him cruelly.
The idea that people,strangers, friends, colleagues,had gathered to remember him shook me. Jordan stayed close, holding my hand as we stood silently, watching the world honor the man we loved most.
That evening, the foundation in his name was announced,a promise that his legacy would continue. Funds would go to Parkinson’s research and the type of lung cancer that had stolen him from us. I saw the way Jordan’s eyes glistened, the same raw emotion mirrored in mine.
Ray curled against my side, holding her small hand over mine. “Daddy… helping kids?” she asked softly. I nodded, tears sliding freely. “Yes, baby… so much good will come from him, even now.”
Morning came with a quiet hush over New York, as if the city itself knew of our sorrow. The funeral was public, but the burial would remain private, reserved for family and those who had walked intimately with him through life.
We arrived at the church, the building monumental yet somehow comforting, its walls heavy with history, with echoes of laughter and prayer. Outside, people gathered, many holding
flowers, others simply holding their breath. They came to say goodbye, to honor the man who had touched their lives as he had touched ours.
The sky was a dull gray that morning, as if the world itself were mourning Isaiah with us. I held Ray’s hand tightly as we approached the church, the scent of lilies and fresh rain mingling in the air. She clutched her small purse, the one she insisted was her “lucky charm,” her other hand tightly wrapped in mine.
“I want to see him,” she whispered. I nodded, swallowing the lump in my throat. “We’ll see him, Ray. And we’ll be strong together.”
Inside, the church was hushed, the soft murmur of grieving voices wrapping around us like a fragile cocoon. Photographs of Isaiah lined the aisle, each one capturing the vibrancy and warmth he had carried throughout his life.
Friends, colleagues, and family had gathered,some still in disbelief, others holding back sobs that threatened to spill over. Jordan walked beside me, quiet, protective, his arm brushing mine for support. Leo was here too, steady and somber, watching over Ray.
The casket was open, and Isaiah lay before us. The sight was unbearable—he looked so small, so frail, the vibrant man we had known reduced to delicate, still perfection. His face, usually animated with energy, rested in peace, though even in death I could see the remnants of struggle etched into the skin, the lines that had carried both laughter and pain. I held Ray’s hand tightly, leading her slowly toward him.
Ray’s small fingers trembled in mine as we approached the casket. She looked up at me with wide eyes. “Mama… he looks…
peaceful.” I nodded, tears slipping down my cheeks. “Yes, baby. He looks peaceful.” I guided her closer.
Her tiny hand reached for the edge of the casket, tracing the smooth wood as if trying to hold on to him one last time. I placed a gentle kiss on her head and whispered, “It’s okay, Ray. You can say goodbye in your own way.”
She whispered his name softly, “Papa… I’ll miss you.” Her voice broke, but she held herself together, wanting to be brave. I stepped closer and laid a hand on the casket, pressing my lips lightly against the cool wood.
“I’ll always love you, Isaiah.Always remember you. Thank you for our life, for our girl, for everything.” My voice cracked as I spoke, and I could feel the grief pooling inside me, heavy and unrelenting.
Ray's eyes filled with tears. I knelt beside her, voice breaking. “It’s okay, baby… it’s okay. We’re here with him. We love him so much.”
Ray leaned closer, whispering against the casket, “I love you, Daddy.” My own tears slid down my cheeks unchecked.
I leaned over, pressing my lips to his forehead, soft, reverent. “I’ll always love you, Isaiah,” I whispered, the words catching in my throat.
“Always… and I’ll remember you… every single day.” Ray mirrored me, resting her cheek against the edge of the casket, murmuring her love again, softly but fiercely. I wrapped her small body into mine, letting her cry freely, my own grief a tide that threatened to swallow me.
The congregation began to speak quietly, offering memories, blessings, and prayers. Friends, colleagues, fans, all sharing stories of Isaiah’s kindness, humor, and brilliance.
Jordan stood close, hand on my shoulder, whispering steady encouragement. “You’re doing wonderfully, Lys. He’s hearing you.” His voice trembled slightly, and I leaned into him for support.
One by one, family stepped forward. Isaiah’s mother knelt at the casket, pressing her lips to his hand, murmuring prayers through quiet sobs. Hudson placed a hand on the casket, shaking slightly, whispering, “Big brother… I love you… I always will.”
Kaylin and Maddie, tears streaming down their faces, whispered their farewells, telling him he had been the best brother, father, friend. Amy, too, knelt, brushing her hand across his arm, whispering forgiveness and love.
Julian and Tasha offered their heartfelt tributes, their words muffled by emotion. Each farewell carved deeper into our hearts, every voice a reminder that he had been loved, profoundly, by so many.
Finally, Leo leaned down, holding back tears. “Isaiah… you’ve done everything right. You’ve loved deeply, you’ve fought relentlessly. We’re going to carry that forward.” His voice cracked, and I pressed my hand over my mouth, shaking, unable to speak.
Jordan was next, kneeling at the casket, trembling. “I’ve got her, Ray… always,” he whispered. “I promise.” He kissed the side of the casket gently. “I promise to carry him in our hearts.”
The service moved slowly, deliberately, each moment stretching time, each breath heavy with grief.
I kept my arm around Ray, feeling her small frame shudder as she whispered, “I don’t want him to go, Mama.” I pressed my
cheek to hers. “I know, baby. I know. But we have to let him rest. And we will carry him with us, always.”
When it was my turn again, I knelt, pressing my lips to Isaiah’s forehead one last time. “Zay… I’ll always love you. Always. And I’ll never forget you.” My voice cracked, tears falling freely, and Ray echoed me softly, her small voice almost a breath. “I love you, Daddy.” I brushed my hand across his face, memorizing every line, every contour, every freckle and curve, committing him to memory.
We all stepped back as the service drew to a close, the priest offering a prayer for peace and light. The organ played softly, a melancholy hymn that seemed to carry the weight of every broken heart present.
I glanced around, seeing friends, fans, and family holding each other, crying silently, hands clasped tightly. Each tear reflected the depth of loss that had entered our lives in the span of mere days.
Ray clutched my hand as we left the building, the air crisp against our wet faces. I lifted her into my arms, holding her close, whispering soothing words. “He’s in our hearts now, baby. And we’ll carry him there forever.
That’s where he lives, in every laugh, every hug, every word we say.” She pressed her head against my shoulder, trembling, and I kissed her curls, holding her tight.
At the cemetery, the private burial felt like the world had shrunk to a single, sacred point. The casket was lowered slowly, gently, into the earth, surrounded by the few who had known Isaiah most intimately.
Every person present held a piece of their heart in that moment, a collective mourning so deep it almost seemed physical, almost like the weight of grief itself could pull us under.
I stayed beside Ray, kneeling on the grass, whispering stories of her father, repeating his words, his jokes, his tenderness. She listened, eyes wide, tears tracking down her cheeks. “He loved you so much, baby,” I whispered. “Every single day.”
Jordan held my hand firmly, his presence a lifeline. “He would want us to live fully,” he murmured. “To love fiercely. To never forget.”
I nodded, though the weight of emptiness pressed into me like a physical force. Ray hugged me tightly, whispering, “Mama… he’s in the sky now, right?” I nodded through tears. “Yes, baby. He’s watching us, always.”
As the final dirt fell onto the casket, each shovelful a reminder of finality, my heart broke into a thousand pieces. I pressed my forehead to the ground, whispering,
“Goodbye, Isaiah… my first love, my first partner, my first home. I’ll always carry you with me. I’ll always love you.” Ray pressed her tiny hand over mine, and together we wept, a silent chorus of grief and remembrance.
The service concluded with soft words of blessing, a prayer for peace, and a promise that his memory would endure,not just in us, but in the lives he had touched, in the foundation that would carry his name, in the love that would continue to ripple outward.
As we walked away, I held Ray close, every step heavy with grief and memory. Jordan stayed close, his arm around me, silent, mourning in tandem.
We were left with the ache of loss, but also with the light of love Isaiah had given us, the tangible warmth of his memory, and the quiet promise that his spirit would guide us, watch over Ray, and remain indelible in our hearts.
The night fell quiet, and as we returned home, I felt the crushing emptiness alongside the fragile hope. Isaiah was gone, yes,but his love, his life, his laughter, his courage,those would remain with us forever.
And as I held Ray, her small hand in mine, I whispered once more into the quiet: “We’ll never forget you, Zay. I’ll always love you.” And in that moment, it was as if he was there with us, in the room, in the breeze, in the beating of our hearts.
Fifty
One Year Later
Morning comes softly.
Not the way it used to , not the kind that bursts into the room in golden streaks like it’s impatient with me. No. This light tiptoes, as if it already knows what today is. As if even the sun has learned to be gentle with us.
I wake before my alarm, lying in the quiet that never fully leaves now, not even on good days. My eyes open to the ceiling I’ve stared at for a full year , a ceiling that has seen grief, healing, nightmares, and the slow rebuilding of something like hope.
Today marks one year.
One year since the worst day. One year since I held the hand of the man who once held every dream I had. One year since Ray lost her father.
I sit up, brushing my thumb across the indentation in the mattress where she curled beside me last night. She said she wanted to “sleep close, just in case Daddy comes in my dream and says hi.” She’s six now , big girl, big words, but a heart that still trips over loss like a stone in the dark.
My little girl. My brave, breakable, healing little girl.
She's still asleep now, her curls a soft halo around her face, her small chest rising and falling in that steady rhythm that saved me a thousand times this year.
I slip out of bed quietly. The floor is cool beneath my feet as I walk toward the kitchen where Jordan is already making tea. He turns when he hears me, offering me the kind of smile you give someone when you love them and you’re worried for them at the same time. He doesn’t say You sure you’re okay? or Do you want to cancel today? He knows better.
He sets the mug in front of me. “Morning.”
“Hey,” I whisper.
He presses a kiss to the side of my head, letting his hand rest at the back of my neck for a lingering second. “I packed the basket. Sandwiches for us, strawberries for Ray, and… the flowers you bought last night.”
I nod, my throat tightening. “Thank you.”
He stands there a moment like he wants to say something else, but he doesn’t. He just gives my shoulder one last squeeze before stepping away. He understands that this morning belongs to me and Ray,no one else.
By the time Ray wakes, I’ve braided her curls and picked out her soft blue dress, the one she insists is her “Daddy color.” When she’s ready, she takes my hand, walking with the big, determined steps of a child who thinks bravery is something you do with your chin up.
“Do you think he misses us?” she asks as we drive.
I swallow. “I think he loves us the same way he always did.”
She nods, looking out the window. “Then I’ll tell him everything today. I made a list.”
I smile through the ache. “Of course you did.”
The cemetery is quiet when we arrive, the morning still fresh, dew clinging to the grass. Birds chatter softly somewhere in the distance, as if filling the silence with kindness.
Isaiah’s grave is impossible to miss ,simple but beautiful, marked with a picture of him smiling the way he rarely did in the last months. It’s a version of him Ray remembers more clearly than the frail man she visited at the end. I’m grateful for that.
We spread out the small blanket. Ray sits cross-legged, placing the bouquet of white lilies gently at the base of the stone.
“Hi Daddy,” she whispers, her voice trembling just a little. “It’s been a long time.”
I sit beside her, touching the picture with my fingertips. He looks so alive in it ,warm, handsome, unbroken. It’s cruel, how still he remains now.
Ray pulls out her list,a sheet of paper covered in the big, loopy handwriting of a child who hasn’t yet learned how to write quietly. “Okay,” she breathes, clearing her throat dramatically. “Update number one… I’m in the Bluebird class now. That’s the smart class.”
I laugh softly. “All the classes are smart classes.”
“Yeah but mine is the smartest.”
She lifts her chin proudly.
“And update number two… I can read whole books now. Like whole books with no help. Mommy cries every time.”
“I do not cry every time,” I protest.
She gives me a look. “Yes you do.”
She’s right. I absolutely do.
Then she looks at the headstone again, her expression softening. “I miss you all the time. I miss when you read to me, and when you carried me on your shoulders, and when you called me your sunshine.” She bites her lip, blinking quickly. “I’m trying to be brave. Mommy says I’m the bravest girl she knows. But sometimes I’m not.”
Her tiny voice cracks. I pull her into my lap and she curls against me instantly, gripping my arm.
“You don’t have to be brave today,” I murmur into her hair. “Not today.”
“I know,” she whispers. “I just wanted him to know I’m trying.”
I hold her tighter, pressing a kiss into her curls before setting her gently beside me again.
We eat quietly for a while ,Ray nibbling strawberries, me taking small bites of a sandwich I don’t truly taste. But that’s okay. Today isn’t about eating.
After a long moment, Ray sets down her food. “Mommy?”
“Hm?”
“You said you had something to tell Daddy too.”
I exhale slowly. “I do.”
I smooth my dress and look at the picture of the man who was once my best friend, my husband, my heartbreak, my comfort, my undoing, and my beginning again.
“I’m getting married,” I say softly.
Ray smiles , a small, proud smile. “To Jordan.”
“Yes. To Jordan.”
A breeze sweeps through the trees then ,soft, warm, perfectly timed. Ray gasps. “Did you feel that?!”
I nod, tears gathering in my eyes. “Yeah. I did.”
“He heard you,” she says confidently, as though it’s the most obvious truth in the world. “He’s happy.”
The breeze lifts again, brushing through her curls like a hand. She giggles. “Daddy says congratulations.”
I let out a breath that shakes, my tears finally spilling. “I hope he would,” I whisper.
“He does,” she insists. “Because you’re happy. And Jordan loves you. And he loves me. Daddy likes that.”
I close my eyes, overwhelmed by the purity of her certainty. Children know things in ways adults forget. Maybe she’s right. Maybe spirits can reach us through the smallest movements of the world. Maybe love never stops trying to be heard.
“I wish he could’ve been here,” I say, barely audible.
Ray scoots closer, leaning her head on my shoulder. “He is.”
We sit like that for a long, quiet stretch , sunlight warming our backs, the morning soft around us.
After a while, Ray twists her fingers together. “Can we tell him goodbye now?”
I nod. “Let’s do it together.”
She stands first, placing her small hand on the headstone. I rise beside her, laying my palm over hers.
“Bye Daddy,” she whispers. “I’ll come again next month. I’ll bring my drawings. And maybe a cupcake if Mommy lets me. I love you.”
Her voice trembles but she doesn’t shy away from it. She lets herself feel everything. It’s one of the bravest things I’ve ever seen.
Then I place both hands on the stone and lean forward until my forehead rests against the cool surface.
“Thank you,” I breathe. “For loving her. For loving me in all the ways you could. I’ll take care of her. I promise you that with everything I am.”
The breeze returns ,gentle, steady, wrapping around us as though answering.
I press a final kiss to my fingers and touch them to his picture.
Then Ray slides her hand into mine. “We’ll be back soon,” she tells him. “We’re not done talking yet.”
I smile at that. “Not even close.”
We walk away slowly, hand in hand, leaving the blanket of flowers and sunlight behind.
The air feels different now , lighter, like something has settled instead of shattered. Ray skips once, just a tiny hop, like she’s rediscovering the floor beneath her.
And me… I breathe easier. Not because grief is gone , grief never leaves , but because it has changed shape. Today it feels less like a wound and more like a memory I can hold without bleeding.
We step into the car. Ray buckles herself in and looks out the window.
“Mommy?”
“Yes, baby?”
“Do you think Daddy saw the picnic?”
I smile softly, starting the engine. “I think he was right there with us.”
She nods, satisfied. Then she reaches out her hand for mine.
“Let’s go home."
I lace my fingers with hers.
“Yes,” I whisper. “Let’s go home.”
Epilogue
The morning sun stretched across the horizon in delicate strokes of gold and peach, spilling onto the private beach where Jordan and I would finally say our vows.
I stood barefoot on the soft sand, the cool grains brushing between my toes, my heart fluttering like it had never before.
Ray tugged gently at my hand, her curls bouncing as she whispered, “Mom, I’m nervous.” I bent down, brushing a stray strand from her face. “I know, sweet girl. But today is about
happiness. Today we celebrate love.” She nodded solemnly, clutching Colton’s hand tightly.
Colton, my little step-son, squeezed her hand back, his shy smile brightening the morning like a sunbeam.
Emeric, insisting on being a flower boy despite his protests just an hour ago, stomped his tiny feet and crossed his arms. “I don’t want to throw petals,” he huffed, glaring at the basket in his hands.
Ray shot him a look and puffed her cheeks. “You’re supposed to, silly!” Emeric groaned but eventually acquiesced, muttering under his breath while holding the flowers like a tiny treasure he had no intention of sharing.
I chuckled quietly, tucking a strand of hair behind my ear and taking a deep breath, feeling the rhythm of the waves beneath the gentle breeze.
My parents were nearby, my mother fussing over the edge of my veil, whispering encouragements in my ear. My father, quiet but proud, gave me a small, firm nod that said more than words could.
My siblings were scattered along the shore with their families,Alec and Daisy holding Ophelia and baby Jayce, beaming as they looked at us, Jayce gurgling happily at the sight of the ocean. Amy, radiant in her bridesmaid dress, leaned close and whispered, “You’re glowing, Aly. He’s going to lose his mind when he sees you.” I smiled, holding back tears. I already knew Jordan’s reaction would make the world around us disappear, as it always did.
Sammy, the director who had watched me grow from trembling understudy to Broadway lead, gave me a thumbs-up from the small seating area they’d arranged with white linens and soft cushions.
A few close friends and family sat quietly, their eyes glimmering with emotion, the sound of waves mingling with faint, instrumental music drifting from hidden speakers. It was intimate, understated, perfect.
Ray tugged my hand again, pointing toward the aisle we’d made of driftwood and flowers. “Mom… it’s almost time.” I knelt, brushing the sand from her legs. “You’re going to be amazing, sweet girl. Remember, it’s your brother and your best friend beside you. Just smile.” She nodded, glancing at Colton, who offered a tiny thumbs-up.
Jordan appeared on the edge of the shore, his dark hair catching the sunlight, the familiar warm intensity in his eyes. He looked… breathtaking.
The world seemed to tilt ever so slightly as my heart leaped. I inhaled, steadying myself, and Ray whispered softly, “He looks happy, Mom.” I nodded, trying to hold myself together, but my chest ached. This was the culmination of so much love, loss, fear, and joy.
We began our walk down the aisle, Emeric in his stubborn, huffing glory, Ray walking beside Colton with an elegance only a six-year-old could muster. Petals scattered, soft and fragrant, marking our path.
Emeric threw his with a dramatic flair, nearly missing the mark entirely, and Ray giggled, tugging Colton along in her laughter. I caught Jordan’s eye as we neared him, and he dropped to one knee with a soft, almost inaudible laugh. “You’re gorgeous,” he whispered, voice thick with emotion.
I took his hands in mine, feeling the warmth, the steadiness, the unwavering presence I’d always leaned on. He smiled, a little shaky, but it didn’t matter,none of it mattered,because we were here, now.
Around us, the soft waves lapped against the shore, the sun casting a golden halo over the scene, and I felt the presence of Isaiah in that moment, as if the breeze whispered that he was happy for us, that he was here, silently cheering us on.
Ray leaned against me, whispering, “Mom… do you think Daddy would like this?” My throat tightened. I nodded, tears threatening to spill. “He would, sweet girl. He’s smiling, I promise.” Colton wrapped his small arms around her waist, and I felt a quiet, steadying joy in the way they held onto each other. They were inseparable, two halves of a perfect little world we’d created.
We reached Jordan, and he took our hands as the officiant began the ceremony. The words blurred into the background at times,I heard the promises, the soft vows, the tears in voices both ours and those around us,but the only thing I focused on was the man in front of me and the family we’d built together.
Ray and Colton looked on, wide-eyed, fully understanding that this was monumental, that today marked a turning point for all of us. Emeric, still petulant, now smoothed his flower-strewn hands on his pants, secretly proud he’d fulfilled his role.
Jordan’s voice broke slightly as he recited his vows. “Alyssa… you are my heart, my home, my every day. I promise to walk beside you, to laugh with you, to comfort you in every sorrow and celebrate every joy. I promise to love you fiercely, honestly, and endlessly.”
My eyes blurred with tears as I squeezed his hands. I could feel the weight of the past,the grief of Isaiah’s loss, the fear of loving again,lifting, replaced by something solid, something unshakeable.
I spoke softly, the words catching in my throat: “Jordan… you are my anchor. My laughter, my courage, my love. I promise to
nurture this family with you, to protect it fiercely, and to walk beside you, come what may. I promise to love you as fiercely as I’ve loved anyone in this life, and to honor the love that brought us here.”
The breeze shifted, and for a moment, I swear the world paused. A soft whisper of wind tickled my cheeks, ruffling Ray’s curls and Colton’s hair. I felt it deep in my chest,a quiet, supernatural acknowledgment that all the people we’d loved, all the ones we’d lost, were there in spirit, smiling, happy. Isaiah, I felt, was smiling at us, approving, at peace.
We exchanged rings, their weight familiar, grounding, full of meaning. Ray and Colton watched intently as we placed them on each other’s fingers. “They’re shiny, Mom!” Ray exclaimed, her voice brimming with excitement. Emeric puffed out his chest. “I did my job,” he muttered proudly, almost a growl, and Colton nodded, agreeing without words.
The officiant smiled, his voice soft over the ocean’s whispers. “By the power vested in me, I now pronounce you husband and wife.” Jordan and I leaned in, pressing our lips together, gentle, unhurried, full of all the love we had carried and all that we hoped to build. Ray clapped, Colton cheered, and even Emeric grinned through a reluctant smirk.
We lingered in that moment, the ocean a perfect backdrop, the sky a canvas of warmth and light. My parents came forward, hugging me and Jordan, whispering words of pride and love.
Alec and Daisy held Ophelia and Jayce close, sharing smiles and quiet congratulations. Amy kissed my cheek, whispering, “You’ve earned this, Aly. You truly have.” Sammy from Broadway clapped softly, eyes glistening. Everyone was crying,soft, happy, bittersweet tears.
Afterward, we spread a picnic blanket on the sand. Ray and Colton dug into sandwiches, giggling over little bits of chocolate they’d smuggled from the dessert basket. Emeric refused dessert at first, standing proudly over his crumbs, until Ray shoved a piece into his hand, and he finally relented with a small, victorious smile.
I leaned against Jordan, taking in the scene,the family we’d built, the love we’d nurtured, the memories we honored and carried with us.
Ray tugged at my hand, glancing toward the ocean. “Mom… can we tell Daddy about today?” I smiled, heart tight. “Of course, sweet girl.” She closed her eyes, her small voice full of awe and sincerity.
“Daddy… we got married! Mom and Jordan! And it was the most beautiful thing ever. I hope you’re smiling at us.” Colton added, “And we threw petals, and Emeric helped.” She giggled, opening her eyes to see the ocean breeze ruffle her hair.
I whispered softly, “He’s smiling, Ray. He’s so proud of you, of all of us.” She nodded, her eyes glistening.
We left small flowers at the edge of the blanket, a quiet homage to those who could not be with us physically, but whose love guided every step.
Jordan’s hand found mine, thumb brushing gently over the back of my hand. “We did it,” he murmured, voice thick with emotion. I nodded, leaning into him, feeling every heartbeat, every sigh, every soft laugh of our children playing in the sand.
This was the life we’d fought for, the life we deserved, the love that had survived loss, that had grown through grief, that had flourished in its own quiet, resilient way.
As the sun began to dip lower, painting the horizon with streaks of pink and gold, we stood together, a family intertwined.
Ray and Colton held our hands, their small fingers gripping ours tightly, solid, grounding. Emeric wiggles  from Amy's arm and he marched proudly beside them, our little trio of chaos and joy.
Alec and Daisy smiled from nearby, Ophelia’s laughter ringing, Jayce cooing, the sounds of life threading into the quiet serenity of the beach.
We watched the sun dip fully below the horizon, the ocean glowing softly in the last light. Jordan pressed a kiss to my forehead.
“I love you, Alyssa. All of you. Always.” I let myself cry, silently, openly, freely. “I love you too. All of you. Always,” I whispered back.
Ray leaned her head against my arm, Colton nestled close, Emeric beside them, and for the first time in a long time, I felt completely, achingly, beautifully whole.
The waves whispered against the shore, the breeze shifted, and I felt a quiet presence that made my heart swell and ache at once.
It was a reminder that love endured, that family endured, that even through grief and loss, there could be joy, laughter, and the deep, sustaining peace of togetherness.
I looked at Jordan, then at our children, then out at the endless expanse of ocean. The future stretched ahead, unknown but full of hope, full of love, full of the unspoken promises that had carried us here.
Today, we had made new memories, we had honored old ones, and we had pledged ourselves to each other fully. And in that promise, I felt the weight and the wonder of everything we had survived,and everything still to come.
We walked along the beach as the sky deepened into twilight, hand in hand, children trailing joyfully, and for the first time in years, I felt the ache of grief softened, held gently by the steady warmth of love and family surrounding us.
The sun had set, but a new light had risen, illuminating the life we would continue to build, the family we would nurture, and the love that would forever guide our hearts.
.....
Two Years Later
Jordan's POV
It’s been two years since Alyssa and I stood on that private beach, our vows carried away by the wind, our lives bound together in ways I never dared hope for. And now, as I sit on the edge of our bed, watching her breathe through the exhaustion of her pregnancy, I can hardly believe how full our lives have become, even with the shadows of the past still hovering.
She’s carrying our son,our boy,and the doctors have made it clear it’s high risk. Every night I check on her, tracing the curve of her stomach with one hand, holding her hand in mine with the other, counting the soft movements of life that remind me that, for now, she is okay.
Ray is eight now. Eight. And every day she becomes more like Isaiah,the green eyes, the laugh, the stubborn determination that lights up every room she enters. She still calls me Daddy Jordan, and I don’t think I’ll ever grow tired of that.
She has inherited his spirit, too, fiery and unrelenting, the kind that refuses to be small, refuses to bend, refuses to be broken. And watching her with Colton, my twelve-year-old, is a sight I treasure. Preteen hormones are slowly taking hold of him; mood swings, eye-rolls, sudden bursts of independence.
It takes effort to convince him to leave his room, to agree to go visit Kaya, to make time for traditions he doesn’t want to keep,but we manage, one promise at a time. Ray has become his constant companion, and their bond is unshakable. They bicker, argue, laugh, cry, and in all of it, they remain inseparable.
Our house is loud, messy, filled with the chaos of a family that refuses to be quiet. Ray has starred in a handful of films since she was six, her talent blossoming as she balances auditions with school, with life, with the memory of the father she lost.
Alyssa and her still continued their tradition of visiting him month to tell him about their progress in life. I sometimes join with Colton.
Colton has become a sought-after model, his blue eyes and blonde hair drawing attention from every agency in New York. He carries himself with a maturity beyond his age, and I see pieces of myself reflected in him,the stubborn protectiveness, the careful loyalty, the love for those he calls family.
Alyssa’s career has flourished too. Broadway is her home, her stage, her heartbeat. She chooses her projects carefully now, balancing the demands of her art with the demands of this life we’ve built. She’s radiant even in exhaustion, glowing with anticipation and love, a woman I never stop being grateful for.
I watch her sometimes, tracing the line of her jaw, the curve of her shoulders, the way she laughs with Ray over breakfast cereal, and I am endlessly thankful that we found each other again, that we were given this second chance.
It was a Saturday afternoon, the sunlight spilling through the windows in warm streams. Alyssa lay beside me, the soft rise and fall of her chest marking the rhythm of our lives together..
We had just made love, the tenderness of it intended to help induce labor, and now we were tangled in the sheets, talking quietly.
“I’m worried about Amy,” she said softly, her eyes fixed on mine. There was that familiar crease of concern on her forehead. “She’s been so… withdrawn. Always crying. And with everything Yulis is causing her… I feel like she’s shutting herself off from everyone, even from me.”
I brushed her hair back, careful not to disturb the baby, feeling the warmth of her skin beneath my fingers. “She’s strong,” I said gently. “She’s scared, but she’s stronger than she knows. She’ll come around, Alyssa. You’ll see.”
“I hope so,” she murmured, voice thick with worry. “I just… I wish she’d open up. Maybe writing would help her, like… like a journal, a book. Something to pour everything into.”
I nodded, letting her words sink in. “Maybe that’s exactly what she needs,” I said. “A safe space to say everything she can’t say aloud.”
She smiled faintly, a small, tired curve of her lips, and leaned into me. “You always know what to say,” she whispered.
We lay in silence then, the kind that hums with anticipation and tension, until her hand tightened in mine.
“Jordan…”
I looked down to see the fear and excitement mingling in her eyes. “I think… I think it’s starting.”
I was on my feet in an instant, pulling the bag we had prepared weeks ago from the closet, helping her into it, feeling the life within her shift with each contraction. We moved quickly but carefully, my mind racing, my heart hammering.
The drive to the hospital was quiet except for her soft, measured breathing and the occasional sharp gasp as the waves of labor rolled over her. I held her hand the entire way, murmuring encouragements, counting with her, reminding her how strong she was.
The hospital room was calm, almost sacred, bathed in soft light. Alyssa’s labor was long, arduous, the kind that breaks you and builds you at the same time.
I never left her side, holding her, whispering through each contraction, feeling every tremor, every shiver, every tiny cry of pain that tore through her. “You’re amazing,” I kept saying. “You’re doing this. You’re strong. You’re everything.”
She looked at me through sweat-slicked hair, pain etched in every line of her face, and I saw the love in her eyes, fierce and unwavering. “I… can’t do it without you,” she whispered. “Please, don’t leave me.”
“You’ll never be alone,” I promised. “I’m here. Always.”
Hours passed. The world outside became distant, irrelevant. There was only her, only the baby, only the unrelenting tide of emotion that filled the room. And then, finally, the cry. The first sharp, perfect cry of a life newly begun. I was sobbing before I even saw him, tears falling freely down my cheeks.
“He’s perfect,” I whispered, barely able to form the words. Alyssa’s own tears mingled with mine as she reached out, her voice cracking. “Oh my God… he’s perfect.”
I leaned down, pressing my forehead to hers. “We did it,” I sobbed. “We… we did it together.”
A nurse handed the baby to Alyssa, and I watched as her arms cradled life itself. His tiny fists curled, his soft skin warm against hers.
The room was filled with a sacred quiet, the kind that reverberates through your bones and leaves you trembling with gratitude.
Ray entered the room with Colton behind,he was old enough to care for Ray and this was a private wing.
I reached down, holding Alyssa’s hand as she held him. “Ray, come here,” I said softly. “Colton, come here.”
Ray came closer, her green eyes wide, hands trembling. “Is he… really here?” she whispered, awe and tears in her voice. Colton followed, more controlled, his blue eyes bright, his blonde hair slightly mussed, but every inch of him shaking with anticipation.
I guided them forward, each kneeling beside their new brother, their faces a mixture of reverence and love. “He’s your baby brother,” I said, voice breaking. “Our family… just got bigger.”
"His name is Arden Kai Campbell" Alyssa says
Ray reached out first, tiny hands brushing against his soft cheeks. “Hi… hi little guy,” she whispered. “I’m Ray. I’m your sister. I’ve missed you already.”
Colton leaned in next, careful, almost protective, and murmured, “I’m Colton. I’ll always look out for you.”
Alyssa lifted the baby higher so they could see him, cradled safely in her arms.  “ He’s ours. He’s everything we hoped for.”
Ray kissed his tiny hand gently. “I love you already,” she whispered. Colton, quieter, pressed his forehead to the baby’s, a solemn promise that he would be there, always.
I held both of them close, feeling the weight and wonder of the life we had created together. Tears slid down my cheeks as I brushed Alyssa’s hair from her face. “This is everything,” I
whispered. “Every hard moment, every heartbreak… it all led us here.”
Alyssa nodded, cradling him closer, whispering something to him I couldn’t hear, her lips trembling with the intensity of her emotion. Ray and Colton, still kneeling, exchanged a look,an understanding that their family had grown, that they would all protect this little life together.
And in that moment, the chaos of the world, the shadows of loss, the echoes of grief,they all faded. There was only us. There was only this small, perfect boy nestled in Alyssa’s arms, and the promise that we would face the future together, no matter what it held.
Bonus Chapter
Ray's POV
I step onto the familiar path to Dad’s grave, my hands clutching the small bouquet I’ve picked from the garden this morning. Even after Mom’s visits have dwindled,from monthly to just once a year on his anniversary,I never stop coming.
The first time I went to Daddy’s grave by myself, I was fourteen. I’m fifteen now, and I know I’ll keep visiting every month, no matter what. Mom has so much on her plate: managing her career, taking care of Colton, Arden, and me. I don’t blame
her for not always being here. I’ve learned that life has its responsibilities, and she does her best.
Two security guards flank me as I approach the headstone. It’s a strange sight, carrying the weight of tradition and memory alone, but I feel Dad’s presence anyway. It’s comforting in a way only he can manage.
The headstone read;
Isaiah Martins
Beloved son
Father and husband
Age 15
I kneel, brushing my fingers over the cool stone, and whisper, “Dad, I have a boyfriend.” My voice is light, but my heart pounds. I explain that he seems genuine, though I know Mom and Jordan would frown. Jordan already disapproves, and I wonder what Dad would have thought. Would he have warned me, laughed it off, or simply hugged me and said he trusted my judgment? I smile faintly at the memory of his warm, patient eyes. “I hope you think I’m making the right choice,” I murmur, a single tear rolling down my cheek.
Age 16
This time, I tell him about my new freedom: I’ve passed my driver’s test. “Dad, I can drive now! Can you believe it?” I laugh softly, though my throat tightens. I tell him about the songs I’ve started writing, about the music videos I’ve filmed, about the first flickers of a dream I’m chasing. I can almost hear him hum approvingly in my head. “I’m following your footsteps, Dad. You always wanted me to chase my dreams.”
Age 17
The wind stirs as I sit cross-legged by the grave. “Dad, I did my first gig at the street carnival. People actually listened to me, Dad. Can you believe it?” I sniffle, brushing the tears from my cheeks. “I miss you so much. And… the boy who broke my heart? He doesn’t matter. You’d tell me the same thing, right?” I laugh through the tears, letting my voice rise and fall with the memory of his laughter echoing in my mind.
Age 18
I trace the letters of his name, letting my fingers linger on the carved stone. “Dad, I got into Yale. I’m following your path.” My chest swells with pride, and then I whisper about the record label that wants to sign me. “I think you’d be proud. I’m doing it, Dad… I’m really doing it.”
Age 19
The grave is quiet today, the autumn leaves swirling around my feet. “Dad, I finally got a place of my own. It’s small, but it’s mine. And Colton—he’s helping me settle in. We argue sometimes, but we laugh too. Just like you’d expect.”
Age 20
Winter is fading as I kneel before him, the sun casting long shadows across the cemetery. “Dad, I had my first real tour. It was exhausting, but incredible. I feel alive, Dad. Alive like you wanted me to. I wish you could have been there.”
Age 21
I brush the dirt from a flower beside the headstone, my voice low. “Dad, I met someone… he’s kind, he listens, he respects me. I hope you’d approve. I wanted to tell you first. I still miss you every day, and I think about you constantly.”
Age 22
The spring air is crisp as I sit, knees tucked to my chest. “Dad, I found someone amazing. He’s sweet, he treats me right. I want you to meet him one day. And… I miss you so much. I’ll never forget you, Dad. You’re my best memory.”
Age 23
I reach up to feel the tattoos on my arms: one says “Zay,” another “Ray,” and a third is his face. I whisper, “Dad, I’m pregnant. I wanted to tell you first before Mom and Jordan. My tour went so well, I’m… I’m making my dreams real, like you always wanted for me. I’ll never forget you.” My tears fall freely, but there’s pride, too, pride in being his daughter, in carrying a part of him with me.
Age 24
The summer breeze carries the scent of flowers as I cradle my newborn son in my arms. “Dad… this is him. He’s yours, in a way. I named him Isaiah, just like you.” I smile, though tears blur my vision. “I wanted you to meet him. I wanted you to see the life you’ve left behind, the love we carry for you.” I glance at my fiancé, Callum, and whisper, “He’s wonderful, Dad. You would have liked him.” I lay my hand gently on the baby’s tiny chest. “And look, Dad… this is your grandchild. He has your green eyes.” My heart aches with a fierce love that only loss and memory can create.
I take a deep breath, smoothing the baby’s hair and looking at the sky. “I’m getting married soon, Dad. I’ll walk down the aisle with Jordan, but I’ll carry your picture. You’ll be there with me, always.” I feel the breeze lift my hair and brush my face, and I smile through the tears. “I’ll come see you every month, Dad. I’ll tell you everything, every little thing.”
The carousel spins, each memory a photograph pinned to the walls of my mind: music videos, tours, laughter, tears,
heartbreaks, accomplishments, milestones, birthdays, graduations, first loves, and my wedding day,all shared with the one person who taught me how to live and how to love.
Even as the years pass, even as the world moves forward and changes, this ritual remains. I kneel one last time, brushing a hand over the stone. “I’ll see you soon, Dad. I’ll tell you everything. You’ll always be my guide, my heart, my anchor.”
......
In the afterlife
And then, in the quiet,  Isaiah’s presence lingers. The sun dips behind the clouds, and for a fleeting second, a shimmer of him stands at the edge of the graveyard, watching his daughter. His eyes are proud, bright with the same warmth they had in life, and a faint smile graces his lips. He watches her, not with longing, but with contentment, seeing the life she has built, the love she has nurtured, the family she has grown. And then, like a soft breeze, he fades,finally at peace, finally proud of the woman she has become.
…………The End……….
“In the end, life did what it always does , it broke what needed breaking, mended what dared to heal, and left behind a quiet proof that love doesn’t vanish with the people who gave it. It lingers in the child who arrived as both goodbye and beginning, in the heart that learned to rise again, and in the soft unseen places where grief and hope learn to hold hands.”
ACKNOWLEDGEMENT
To the ones who carried me through the storm of this story,thank you.
Writing this book was not a straight line; it was a climb through heartbreak, healing, and the quiet bravery it takes to start over. Every page exists because of the people who kept reminding me to breathe, to rest, and to keep going even when the words felt too heavy.
To my family,your love is the ground I stand on.
To my friends,thank you for listening to every plot twist at unreasonable hours.
To every reader who picked up this book,you are the heartbeat of these stories. Your messages, your tears, your support, and your belief make all the difference. Thank you for choosing these characters, for letting them live in your mind, and for letting their pain and hope speak to you.
Thank you to every woman who has ever had to rebuild herself from the dust,this book carries a piece of your strength. And to anyone grieving someone they loved: may you find softness in these pages, and may you remember that even the deepest loss can bloom into something beautiful.
And finally, to the part of me that didn’t give up,thank you for staying.
DEDICATION
For the ones who loved deeply, lost painfully, and still chose to live.
For every mother rebuilding herself with trembling hands.
For every daughter learning to breathe in a world without the parent she needed.
For every heart that shattered and still found the courage to open again.
For the people we grieve,
the people who save us,
and the people we become in the aftermath.
And for you
whoever you are,
holding this book in your hands
may you find a piece of yourself in these pages
and the strength to keep going in your own story.
ABOUT AUTHOR
Cree Say  is a student midwife,a storyteller, and a writer who believes in the power of messy, complicated, deeply human love. Her stories explore women who rise after being broken, men who learn to love gently, and the quiet wars we fight behind closed doors. When she isn’t writing, she’s usually creating new characters, letting music guide her imagination, or dreaming up the next story that will keep her awake at night.
She writes with her whole heart, and she hopes her books remind readers that they are not alone ,that healing is possible, that love can be reborn, and that even the darkest moments can lead to the brightest chapters.
WHAT'S NEXT
Every story leaves behind a shadow—a question, a heartbeat, a secret waiting to be touched.
And though this chapter of the Martins’ world has closed, the echoes of everything they endured will continue to ripple outward.
The next book in the series turns its gaze toward another life running parallel to everything you’ve just read. Another heart scarred, guarded, and on the edge of unraveling. Another truth that’s been waiting years to be told.
A new voice is stepping forward.
A new story is demanding to be heard.
And it arrives with its own storm.
Because healing doesn’t end here.
Love doesn’t end here.
Family doesn’t end here.
There is another secret humming beneath the surface,one that deserves the spotlight next.
